### [1] [TabletCraft: Bridging a 4,000-Year Cultural Gap with Bidirectional Akkadian NMT and Cuneiform Rendering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02609)
*Zhaohui Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: TabletCraft is an open-source toolkit that enables both reading ancient Akkadian cuneiform tablets and composing new cuneiform tablets from English, using neural translation, sign conversion, and tablet rendering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although hundreds of thousands of cuneiform tablets exist, very few people can read or write Akkadian, and prior NLP tools only support one-way, scholar-focused translation from Akkadian to English. This leaves non-specialists unable to create new content in cuneiform and limits engagement with Mesopotamian culture. The authors aim to remove this barrier by enabling accessible, bidirectional interaction with the writing system.

Method: The authors build TabletCraft, a full pipeline that (1) trains a ByT5-based neural machine translation model on 116K bidirectional Akkadian–English sentence pairs, (2) implements a cuneiform sign converter containing 14,240 mappings that map transliterations to cuneiform signs with high coverage, and (3) creates a visual tablet renderer that turns cuneiform text into images resembling clay tablets. They package everything as an open-source Python toolkit with a CLI and web interface for end users.

Result: On a held-out Akkademia validation set of 2,812 samples, TabletCraft achieves 49.1 BLEU for Akkadian-to-English and 48.5 BLEU for English-to-Akkadian translation, representing the first reported quantitative results for the reverse (English-to-Akkadian) direction. The sign converter achieves 95.3% coverage over the sign inventory, demonstrating broad applicability to cuneiform text.

Conclusion: TabletCraft successfully demonstrates that high-quality bidirectional translation between Akkadian and English is feasible and can be integrated into an end-to-end system that lets users both read and create cuneiform tablets. This transforms interaction with Mesopotamian writing from a passive, expert-only activity into a more accessible, creative experience for non-specialists, and provides a foundation for future cultural and educational applications.

Abstract: Half a million cuneiform clay tablets survive in museums worldwide, yet modern users can neither read nor write in the world's oldest writing system, leaving a 4,000-year cultural barrier that existing NLP tools have only partially addressed.
  Prior work enables one-way, scholar-oriented translation from Akkadian to English, but offers no path in the reverse direction: non-specialist users cannot compose new content in cuneiform, and therefore remain passive consumers of ancient culture rather than active participants.
  We present TabletCraft, the first open-source system that enables bidirectional interaction with Mesopotamian writing. Users can read ancient tablets (Akkadian to English) and compose new messages as cuneiform clay tablets (English to Akkadian to cuneiform to rendered tablet).
  The system integrates a ByT5-based translation model trained on 116K bidirectional samples, a cuneiform sign converter with 14,240 mappings (95.3% coverage), and a visual tablet renderer, packaged as a pip-installable toolkit with CLI and web demo.
  On the held-out Akkademia validation split (2,812 samples), we report 49.1 BLEU for Akkadian-to-English and 48.5 BLEU for English-to-Akkadian, the first published quantitative result in the reverse direction.

</details>


### [2] [BBOWP-Bench: Evaluating LLMs on Black-Box Optimization Word Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02612)
*Yutaro Yamada,Kei Hiroshima,Nozomu Yoshinari,Kento Uchida,Shinichi Shirakawa*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces BBOWP, a benchmark where systems must infer both search spaces and optimization algorithms for black-box optimization problems described only in natural language.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Good optimization performance depends heavily on how problems are formulated, but formulating them—especially black-box optimization problems—requires expert knowledge. Existing work on deriving optimization problems from text mainly assumes that objectives and constraints can be expressed in explicit mathematical form, which misses many real-world black-box settings. There is a need to study how LLMs can automatically design search spaces and choose algorithms directly from natural-language descriptions of black-box tasks.

Method: The authors define a new task, Black-Box Optimization Word Problems (BBOWP), where a system receives a natural-language description of a black-box optimization problem and must propose both (i) a search space and (ii) an optimization algorithm. They build BBOWP-Bench, a benchmark suite in which each instance includes: a natural-language description, an executable evaluation environment for the black-box objective, and a human-designed baseline formulation. This setup enables systematic evaluation of both search-space design and algorithm selection. They then evaluate existing large language models on this benchmark, analyzing their performance under different evaluation budgets and problem types.

Result: LLMs show promising capability in selecting appropriate optimization algorithms given an evaluation budget, indicating they can reason about algorithm suitability from textual descriptions. However, they often perform poorly in designing search spaces: they may fail to identify key variables or choose appropriate ranges, especially when the description is sparse or when the optimal search-space structure is highly problem-specific.

Conclusion: The BBOWP setting and the BBOWP-Bench benchmark expose a challenging but important capability gap for LLMs: robust search-space design for black-box optimization from natural language. While LLMs can already pick algorithms reasonably well, their difficulty in constructing effective search spaces limits end-to-end performance. The benchmark, code, and dataset aim to facilitate future research on improving LLM-driven formulation and solving of black-box optimization problems described in natural language.

Abstract: Formulating an optimization problem strongly affects the quality of the final solution, yet good formulations usually require substantial expertise. Recent studies have therefore examined how to automatically derive optimization problems from natural-language descriptions, but existing benchmarks focus on settings where objectives and constraints can be written explicitly as mathematical expressions. Many practically important problems are naturally treated as black-box optimization (BBO) problems, in which only objective values are observable, and the functional form is unavailable. In BBO, the search space design, a part of the problem formulation, and the selection of the optimization algorithm are crucial for problem-solving. Automating these processes with large language models (LLMs) is a significant challenge. This paper introduces Black-Box Optimization Word Problems (BBOWP), a novel problem setting in which a system must infer both a search space and an optimization algorithm from a natural-language description of a black-box optimization task. To support research on this setting, we establish the BBOWP Benchmark Suite (BBOWP-Bench), a dataset and evaluation framework for BBOWP. Each instance combines a natural-language problem description, an executable evaluation environment, and a human-designed baseline formulation, allowing evaluation of both search-space design and algorithm selection. Using this benchmark, we provide the first evaluation of LLMs and show that current LLMs are capable of selecting suitable algorithms based on the given evaluation budget. However, they sometimes struggle with search space design, particularly in identifying important variables and balancing their ranges when the problem description is less informative or the search space is highly problem-specific. Our code and dataset are available at https://github.com/shiralab/bbowp-bench.

</details>


### [3] [MemArena: An Ego-Centric Benchmark for On-Device Agentic Personal Memory Assistants at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02613)
*Jiadong Zhang,Xiaosong Ma*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MemArena is a new benchmark for testing edge-deployed personal memory assistants in realistic, ego-centric, multi-session conversational settings, evaluating different memory backends and revealing that backend choice strongly impacts accuracy, permission-aware access is problematic, and search latency is usually acceptable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory benchmarks for conversational agents do not sufficiently capture scenarios where an on-device assistant must manage dense, long-term, ego-centric interpersonal interactions over many sessions within a coherent world. There is a need to evaluate how open-weight models handle realistic personal memory tasks, including recall, reasoning, and trustworthiness, in such settings and to understand the trade-offs of different memory backends and retrieval strategies on edge hardware.

Method: The authors build MemArena, a single-world conversational benchmark generated via their MASim agent simulator, modeling 50 agents interacting over 15 days. The simulator creates 10.3M dialog tokens and about 24.1K ego-observed tokens per agent per day. Alongside the interactions, they co-generate ground-truth labels across six evaluation dimensions related to recall, reasoning, and trust. They then evaluate five open-weight reader models combined with several memory backends: plain long-context (Vanilla), BM25-based retrieval-augmented generation (BM25-RAG), Oracle retrieval, Memobase, and MemSearch. They measure content accuracy, permission-aware information access, and latency on an edge device (Spark GB10 node).

Result: (1) Memory backend choice has a larger effect on content accuracy than scaling the reader model itself; for example, at Qwen3-0.6B, switching from Memobase to MemSearch yields +32.5 and +19.2 percentage-point gains, surpassing the gains from scaling the MemSearch reader (+10.6/+6.8 pp). (2) All systems perform poorly on permission-aware access: the Oracle backend tends to leak unauthorized information, while other backends are overly conservative and withhold information even when disclosure is allowed. (3) On an edge Spark GB10 node, adding memory search incurs modest, nearly constant latency overheads (87 ms for BM25-RAG, 7 ms for Memobase, 48 ms for MemSearch), which generally represent a small fraction of the total time-to-first-token (TTFT) across most reader-backend configurations.

Conclusion: MemArena provides a more realistic and challenging benchmark for assessing edge-deployed personal memory assistants in dense, ego-centric, multi-session conversational environments. The study shows that careful design of the memory backend is more crucial for accuracy than simply scaling the model, highlights systemic deficiencies in permission-aware information access, and finds that retrieval-based memory incurs acceptable latency on typical edge hardware. The benchmark, simulator, and code aim to support future work on more capable and privacy-conscious memory systems.

Abstract: Edge-deployed personal memory assistants must handle private interpersonal conversations on-device with open-weight models. Yet, existing memory benchmarks often under-test the combination of activity-dense interaction, ego-centric perspective, and coherent multi-session worlds. MemArena fills these gaps with a single-world conversational benchmark built with its MASim agent simulator, for 50 agents over 15 days (10.3M dialog-text tokens, 24.1K text-only ego-observed tokens/agent/day). With the interaction history, it co-generates ground truth over six recall, reasoning, and trustworthiness evaluation dimensions. We evaluate five open-weight readers with Vanilla context, BM25-RAG, Oracle retrieval, Memobase, and MemSearch as memory backends. Three results stand out: (1) Memory-backend choice matters more for content accuracy: At Qwen3-0.6B, Memobase-to-MemSearch gains +32.5/+19.2 pp, exceeding MemSearch reader scaling (+10.6/+6.8 pp). (2) Permission-aware access fails universally, with Oracle leaking heavily and other backends too timid to disclose. (3) Search latency bites only at very small reader: on a Spark GB10 edge node, memory-search adds a moderate and fixed 87/7/48 ms (BM25-RAG/Memobase/MemSearch) that composes a small part of TTFT for most reader-backend combinations. Code, the MASim simulator, and the MemArena-L benchmark will be released upon acceptance.

</details>


### [4] [OncoTriad-QA: A Patient-Level Radiology-Pathology-Genomics Benchmark for Pan-Cancer Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02615)
*Ahnaf Munir,Dannong Wang,Michael W. McDonald,Mubarak Shah,Pegah Khosravi,Yu Tian*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces OncoTriad-QA, a large, patient-level pan-cancer benchmark that integrates radiology, pathology, genomics, and clinical data for question answering, and proposes OncoVLM, a multimodal model that performs strongly on this benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current medical LLM and VLM benchmarks mostly test isolated modalities (e.g., only text or only images) or narrow image-text tasks. They do not adequately evaluate a model’s ability to perform holistic, patient-level oncology reasoning that requires combining radiology, pathology, genomics, and clinical metadata. The authors aim to fill this gap by creating a benchmark and reference model for integrated multi-evidence cancer question answering.

Method: They build OncoTriad-QA, a benchmark of 86.1k semantic questions over 9,281 TCGA patient cases spanning 32 cancer types. For each patient, they align multiple data streams: CT/MRI radiology, whole-slide pathology images, somatic mutations, copy-number alterations, DNA methylation, RNA-seq, and clinical metadata. Questions and answers are generated through a source-grounded, LLM-assisted curation pipeline that uses curated labels, diagnostic reports, molecular profiles, and modality-derived evidence as truths, followed by automated consistency checks and clinician review. They also design OncoVLM, a multimodal model that connects modality-specific encoders (for radiology, pathology, DNA methylation, RNA-seq) to a language model via learned projection layers, enabling the LLM to consume heterogeneous evidence in a unified interface. They then benchmark general and medical LLMs and compare them to OncoVLM on multiple-choice and open-ended QA in different modality settings (radiology-only, pathology-only, all-available).

Result: Existing general-purpose and medical LLMs perform poorly on comprehensive pan-cancer QA, particularly on questions that require fusing imaging, morphology, and molecular data. After fine-tuning on OncoTriad-QA, the proposed OncoVLM model substantially outperforms MedGemma-4B, achieving an average improvement of 10.7 points on MCQ accuracy and BERTScore-F1. These gains are consistent across question formats and across radiology-only, pathology-only, and full multimodal settings.

Conclusion: OncoTriad-QA provides a large-scale, patient-level, multi-evidence benchmark that exposes current limitations of LLMs in integrated cancer reasoning, and enables the development and evaluation of models designed for multimodal oncology QA. The OncoVLM model demonstrates that aligning heterogeneous imaging and omics data into an LLM interface via learned projectors, and fine-tuning on this benchmark, can significantly improve performance on pan-cancer question answering tasks.

Abstract: Cancer diagnosis and characterization require integrating complementary evidence from radiology, pathology, genomics, and clinical metadata. However, most medical large language model (LLM) and vision-language model (VLM) benchmarks focus on isolated modalities or narrow image-text tasks, leaving patient-level oncology assessment across multiple evidence streams largely untested. We introduce OncoTriad-QA, a patient-level radiology-pathology-genomics benchmark for pan-cancer question answering. OncoTriad-QA contains 86.1k semantic questions across 9,281 TCGA patient cases from 32 cancer cohorts, aligning CT/MRI radiology, whole-slide histopathology, somatic mutations, copy-number alterations, DNA methylation, bulk RNA-seq, and clinical metadata. Case-specific annotations are constructed through a source-grounded LLM-assisted pipeline using curated labels, diagnostic reports, molecular profiles, and modality-derived evidence as primary sources of truth, with automated consistency checks and clinician review. We also introduce OncoVLM, a reference multimodal model that maps modality-native radiology, pathology, DNA methylation, and RNA-seq evidence into an LLM interface through learned projectors. Experiments show that existing general-purpose and medical LLMs remain limited on comprehensive pan-cancer QA, especially when questions require integrating imaging findings, tumor morphology, and molecular evidence. After fine-tuning on OncoTriad-QA, OncoVLM exceeds MedGemma-4B by an average of 10.7 points when using MCQ accuracy and BERTScore-F1, with consistent gains across multiple-choice and open-ended questions under radiology-only, pathology-only, and all-available settings. These results demonstrate the benchmark's value for training and evaluating models for integrated cancer question answering.

</details>


### [5] [Evaluating OpenAI's Privacy Filter: Cross-Lingual, Cross-Domain PII Detection Across 42 Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02616)
*Rohith Uppala*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Paper evaluates OpenAI’s 1.5B-parameter Privacy Filter (OPF) for detecting PII across many languages, domains, and benchmarks, comparing it to Presidio, XLM-R, and GPT-4o.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is growing deployment of automated systems to detect and filter personally identifiable information (PII), but little independent, systematic evaluation of such systems, especially across multiple languages, domains, and text styles (structured vs narrative). The paper aims to understand how well OpenAI’s Privacy Filter actually works, where it excels or fails, and how it compares with other detectors.

Method: The authors run OPF zero-shot on 42 synthetic PII benchmarks that span 22 languages, 5 domains (e.g., medical, legal, financial, customer support, structured synthetic data), and include both PII-annotated datasets and multilingual NER benchmarks. They compute standard detection metrics (F1, precision, recall) and compare OPF’s performance with Presidio, XLM-RoBERTa, and GPT-4o. They analyze performance by language/script, domain, and PII type (e.g., email, phone, person, address), and conduct an error analysis focusing on structural regularity vs cultural variability of PII and precision–recall tradeoffs.

Result: OPF performs strongly on some PII tasks: it beats Presidio and XLM-R on PII-annotated synthetic benchmarks (F1=0.855 vs 0.431 and 0.269 on AI4Privacy; 0.464 vs 0.273 and 0.111 on SPY medical). On multilingual NER, however, XLM-R outperforms OPF on all 13 Indic and non-Latin languages. GPT-4o is best for medical, legal, and financial PII (e.g., SPY avg F1=0.643 vs Gretel 0.527), while OPF leads on structured synthetic PII (0.71 avg) and customer support (0.60). OPF’s performance drops sharply when PII is embedded in narrative prose, especially for non-Latin scripts (F1=0.04–0.57 overall; Arabic 0.04, Cyrillic 0.03). OPF is best on structurally regular PII types (email F1=0.78, phone F1=0.76) and worst on culturally variable ones (person 0.40, address 0.49). In customer support and medical/legal PII, it is recall-heavy (precision 0.31–0.54, recall 0.70–0.85), and its global precision varies widely (0.31–0.86) across domains.

Conclusion: OPF is a strong PII detector for structured, pattern-like identifiers and certain synthetic and customer-support settings, outperforming some baselines. However, its effectiveness drops significantly in realistic narrative text and for non-Latin languages, and it struggles with culturally variable PII categories such as people and addresses. It tends toward high recall but often at the cost of low precision, with performance highly uneven across domains, languages, and PII types. These results highlight both the promise and current limitations of OPF for robust, multilingual, real-world PII protection.

Abstract: We present the first independent, systematic evaluation of OpenAI's Privacy Filter (OPF), a 1.5B-parameter bidirectional PII detector, across 42 synthetic benchmarks spanning 22 languages and 5 domains. Zero-shot, OPF achieves F1=0.855 on AI4Privacy and 0.464 on SPY medical, outperforming Presidio (0.431, 0.273) and XLM-RoBERTa (0.269, 0.111) on PII-annotated benchmarks; on multilingual NER, XLM-RoBERTa leads OPF on all 13 Indic and non-Latin languages. GPT-4o leads on medical, legal, and financial PII (SPY: 0.643 avg, Gretel: 0.527), while OPF leads on structured synthetic PII (0.71 avg) and customer support (0.60). OPF degrades sharply when PII is embedded in narrative prose: F1=0.04--0.57 on NER benchmarks and collapse for non-Latin scripts (Arabic: 0.04, Cyrillic: 0.03). Error analysis shows OPF is strongest on structurally regular PII types (email: 0.78, phone: 0.76) and weakest on culturally variable ones (person: 0.40, address: 0.49), and is recall-biased on customer-support and medical/legal PII (P=0.31--0.54, R=0.70--0.85); global precision spans 0.31--0.86 across all domains.

</details>


### [6] [Preferred, Not Safer: Pairwise Preference Is a Poor Proxy for Clinical Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02617)
*Fay Elhassan,David Sasu,Alexandra Kulinkina,Lars Henning Klein,Mary-Anne Hartley*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies whether clinicians’ pairwise preferences between LLM outputs are a reliable indicator of clinical safety and finds they are not; safety needs explicit, rubric-based evaluation and adjusted rankings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM evaluations in healthcare often rely on overall preferences or single-number leaderboards that may conflate what clinicians like with what is actually safe and accurate. There is concern that models that are preferred in aggregate may still commit serious clinical errors, especially in specific specialties. The authors want to test if clinician pairwise preferences are a reliable safety signal and to understand what factors drive these preferences.

Method: They use MOOVE, a clinician-led platform where experts from many countries provide blinded pairwise comparisons of LLM outputs along with structured rubric ratings on a discrete scale from -2 to +2 for multiple dimensions like Harmlessness and Accuracy. They analyze 26,804 pairwise judgments across 13 LLMs. They quantify safety-critical failures (scores ≤ -1), examine how these failures vary across specialties, and decompose what features (safety-critical vs surface-level attributes, refusal/escalation, prompt length) explain preference behavior. They also derive an adjusted ranking that combines Bradley–Terry preference strengths with rubric-based safety signals.

Result: Clinician pairwise preference rankings do not reliably track safety-critical performance. Models that rank high on preferences may still have substantial rates of clinically meaningful failures on Harmlessness and Accuracy. These failures cluster by specialty, forming hidden “no-go zones” that are not visible in aggregate metrics. A large portion of preference votes carry no positive safety information, and feature analysis reveals surface-level aspects slightly better explain preferences than do differences in safety rubric scores. They also construct a clinically adjusted preference ranking that is more aligned with safety considerations than raw preference-based rankings.

Conclusion: Clinician overall preferences are an unreliable proxy for clinical safety in LLMs, so evaluation frameworks should explicitly separate preference from safety. Safety-critical failure rates should be reported directly, and rankings for clinical use should incorporate clinically grounded, rubric-based adjustments instead of relying solely on pairwise preferences or single-number leaderboards.

Abstract: We evaluate whether clinician pairwise preferences provide a reliable signal of clinical safety in large language model (LLM) evaluation using expert feedback from MOOVE (Massive Open Online Validation and Evaluation), a clinician-led platform collecting blinded pairwise preferences alongside multi-criterion rubric ratings. Clinicians assign scores on a discrete $[-2, +2]$ scale, where negative values indicate clinically unsafe or misleading content. Using 26{,}804 pairwise judgments across outputs from 13 LLMs, contributed by more than 736 clinicians across 28+ countries, we find that clinician preference is a poor proxy for safety-critical performance. Models ranking highly under pairwise preference can still exhibit substantial rates of clinically meaningful failures ($\leq -1$) on dimensions such as \emph{Harmlessness} and \emph{Accuracy}. These failures are unevenly distributed across specialties, creating domain-specific ``no-go zones'' not visible in aggregate rankings or single-number leaderboards. We further analyze contributing factors including prompt length, refusal and escalation behavior, and the relative contributions of safety-critical versus surface-level features. A substantial fraction of preference votes carry no positive safety signal, while feature decomposition shows that surface-level characteristics explain slightly more preference variation than safety-critical rubric differences. Finally, we introduce a clinically adjusted preference ranking combining pairwise preference with rubric-derived feedback, producing a more safety-aware ordering than raw Bradley--Terry strength alone. Our findings support evaluation practices that separate preference from safety, report safety-critical failure rates directly, and incorporate clinically grounded adjustments when ranking LLMs for clinical decision making.

</details>


### [7] [JudgeArena: A Unified Framework for Reproducible LLM-Judge Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02620)
*Erlis Lushtaku,Bora Kargi,Ali Elganzory,Fabio Ferreira,Alejandro R. Salamanca,Julia Kreutzer,David Salinas*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces JudgeArena, a unified, open-source framework for LLM-as-a-judge evaluation with swappable judges and strong open-model judges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-as-a-judge benchmarks are fragmented: each uses its own codebase, a fixed (often closed) judge model, and a single protocol. This fragmentation makes it hard to compare results, understand how design choices (benchmark, judge, prompt, backend) affect conclusions, and reduces transparency and reproducibility.

Method: Develop an open-source framework (JudgeArena) that (1) unifies several major LLM-judge benchmarks under one interface, (2) allows interchangeable judge and candidate models via common backends (vLLM, llama.cpp, OpenRouter), (3) logs rich metadata for transparency, (4) provides tuned configurations for open-weights judge models, and (5) combines existing human annotations with LLM-judge outputs to statistically simulate LMArena Elo scores.

Result: JudgeArena supports AlpacaEval, Arena-Hard, MT-Bench, and m-Arena-Hard in a single system with swappable judges and full logging. Using tuned configurations, open-source judges can match or outperform closed judges on human preference datasets in English and multilingual settings. The framework can accurately approximate LMArena Elo scores for a target model by fusing limited human annotations with LLM-judge evaluations, substantially reducing the need for new, large-scale human evaluation campaigns.

Conclusion: A unified, extensible evaluation framework makes LLM-as-a-judge studies more transparent, reproducible, and systematic. Open-weight judges can be viable or superior alternatives to closed models, and LMArena-style leaderboards can be approximated cheaply and accurately with a combination of existing human data and LLM-judge outputs.

Abstract: LLM-as-a-judge evaluation has become a dominant paradigm for ranking language models, yet the ecosystem remains fragmented: most benchmarks ship their own code base, hardcode a specific closed-model judge, and support a single evaluation protocol. This fragmentation makes it difficult to study how design choices--the benchmark, the judge model, the prompt, the inference backend--affect the conclusions we draw about model quality. We introduce JudgeArena, an open-source framework that unifies major LLM-judge benchmarks (AlpacaEval, Arena-Hard, MT-Bench, and m-Arena-Hard) under a single interface with swappable judges and comprehensive metadata logging for increased transparency in reporting and reproducibility. It enables systematic studies of judge choices, as any model accessible via vLLM, llama.cpp, or OpenRouter can serve as both candidate and judge. Furthermore, JudgeArena ships with tuned judge configurations for open models that match or outperform closed-model judges, validated on human preference datasets in both English and multilingual settings, reducing the reliance on opaque closed models. Finally, by combining existing human annotations with LLM-judge evaluations of a target model, JudgeArena can simulate LMArena Elo scores with high accuracy offering a practical, open, and low-cost alternative to large-scale human annotation campaigns.

</details>


### [8] [Knowing the Form, Not the Function: Automatically Auditing Answer--Authority Decoupling in Legal Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02621)
*Hsien-Jyh Liao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper finds that getting the right legal answer is not a reliable proxy for citing the correct legal authority, and proposes that legal benchmarks should evaluate both answer correctness and statutory grounding together.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing legal NLP benchmarks mainly grade whether a model’s final answer is legally correct, often ignoring whether it is grounded in the correct legal authority (e.g., statutes). This is problematic for statute-grounded tasks where verifiable provisions exist, such as bar-exam questions, because models could guess correct answers without proper legal grounding, or cite correct statutes while giving wrong answers. The authors aim to empirically test how aligned answer correctness is with proper statutory grounding and to argue for more robust evaluation protocols.

Method: The authors use 238 Taiwan bar-exam multiple-choice questions where each item has a verified governing statutory provision. They run four large language models under standard prompts that do not ask for citations and observe how often the models spontaneously include authority markers (citations or references to statutes). They then automatically compare each model output along two axes: (1) whether the final answer is correct; and (2) whether the cited authority matches the known governing provision (gold authority). They quantify mismatches in both directions (correct answer but wrong/missing authority; incorrect answer but correct authority). Additionally, they run a separate statutory-retrieval probe and an intervention that allows models to abstain from citing statutes, to show that answer generation and citation behavior can vary independently at the output level. They also conduct a small extension using PRC civil-law questions to see if similar spontaneous authority-marking occurs cross-jurisdictionally.

Result: They find a consistent dissociation between answer correctness and authority grounding. In Taiwan criminal-law questions, 24.0–42.4% of legally valid responses are correct in their answers but fail to cite the gold statutory authority, while 15.2–21.7% are answer-incorrect yet do cite the correct gold authority. The retrieval and abstention experiments further demonstrate that answer and citation behavior can shift separately, confirming that one cannot infer citation quality from answer correctness alone. The preliminary PRC civil-law extension also shows spontaneous authority marking even when citations are not requested, suggesting the phenomenon generalizes beyond a single jurisdiction.

Conclusion: Answer-only scoring on legal benchmarks is insufficient because it systematically overestimates model grounding in the governing statutes: many outputs that appear successful by answer accuracy alone in fact miss the correct authority. Since statutory authority is structurally available and automatically checkable, benchmarks should move to joint evaluation of (a) answer correctness and (b) authority correctness. The observed behavior across Taiwan and preliminary PRC settings motivates building cross-jurisdictional legal benchmarks that routinely perform this joint answer–authority audit for statute-grounded tasks.

Abstract: Legal benchmarks typically score final answers even when models also state legal authority. We test whether answer correctness can serve as a proxy for authority grounding. Under ordinary reasoning prompts that did not request statutory citations, four LLMs spontaneously produced authority markers across 238 Taiwan bar-examination items. Because each item has a verified governing provision, we automatically audit answer correctness and authority grounding jointly. The two dimensions dissociate in both directions. In criminal law, 24.0--42.4\% of valid responses were answer-correct but missed the gold authority, while 15.2--21.7\% were answer-incorrect but cited it. A separate statutory-retrieval probe and a permissive citation-abstention intervention further show that answer and citation behavior can move separately at the output level. Because this mismatch arises without adversarial or inconsistency-inducing prompting, answer-only scoring treats naturally occurring gold-authority misses as complete benchmark successes. Because statutory authority is structurally extractable and externally verifiable, the failure can be measured automatically. A preliminary PRC civil-law extension also observes citation-unrequested authority marking, motivating a full cross-jurisdictional joint audit. We therefore propose joint answer--authority evaluation for statute-grounded legal benchmarks.

</details>


### [9] [Speculative Correction: Draft-then-Refine Decoding for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02625)
*Brian K Chen,Chong Wu,Kenji Kawaguchi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies draft-and-refine decoding for diffusion language models, showing that letting a DLM first generate a full draft and then globally refine it with bidirectional diffusion improves accuracy and/or speed over standard block-autoregressive decoding, and that a smaller model can draft for a larger model to achieve better quality-latency trade-offs without extra training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard decoding for diffusion language models forces them into left-to-right, block-autoregressive generation, underusing their inherent ability to revise tokens bidirectionally. The authors want to know whether a simple draft-then-refine procedure can better exploit this property to improve both quality and efficiency, and whether a small model can serve as a fast drafter for a stronger DLM refiner without additional training.

Method: They propose a plug-and-play inference pattern with two stages: (1) a drafting stage that generates a complete response; and (2) a refinement stage where a DLM performs bidirectional diffusion over the entire draft. They instantiate this in two configurations using LLaDA2.1-Flash (Flash) and LLaDA2.1-Mini (Mini). In Flash-Flash, the same Flash model drafts and then refines its own output to test self-improvement via global refinement. In Mini-Flash, Mini drafts and Flash refines, analogous to speculative decoding but with "speculative correction" instead of rejection sampling. They compare against tuned block-autoregressive baselines on GSM8K, MBPP, and MATH; perform causal ablations (e.g., refining from a fully masked span vs using the completed draft, global vs local refinement) to isolate what aspects of initialization matter; and analyze quality-latency trade-offs to understand Pareto frontiers between accuracy and speed.

Result: Flash-Flash draft-and-refine improves GSM8K-384 accuracy from 0.848 to 0.899 while being 1.20× faster than the tuned Flash block-autoregressive baseline, and raises MBPP-384 accuracy from 0.545 to 0.693. Latency-matched Flash-only controls show these gains are not just due to better tuning of block-autoregressive decoding. Ablations show that starting from a fully masked span (no draft) performs poorly, confirming that completed drafts are useful initializations; that full global refinement yields additional gains on GSM8K; and that local refinement recovers most of the improvement on MBPP and MATH. In the Mini-Flash setting, they obtain MATH-384 accuracy of 0.294 vs 0.300 for Flash alone while running 2.17× faster, illustrating strong quality-latency trade-offs but not complete quality parity with Flash.

Conclusion: Draft-and-refine decoding that leverages bidirectional diffusion is an effective primitive for diffusion language models: even a single DLM can significantly improve its own block-autoregressive outputs via global refinement with no retraining. Furthermore, speculative correction—having a small model draft and a larger model refine—offers a training-free way to accelerate DLM generation while preserving much of the quality, tracing out a Pareto frontier of speed versus accuracy rather than uniformly matching the larger model. These findings suggest that diffusion-based refinement should be treated as a central decoding operation for DLMs, and that heterogeneous draft-and-refine cascades are a practical route to faster, high-quality DLM inference.

Abstract: Diffusion language models (DLMs) can revise tokens bidirectionally, but standard decoding procedures often adapt them to left-to-right generation by producing text block by block. We study a simple plug-and-play inference pattern: first generate a complete draft, then refine the full response using bidirectional diffusion. Using LLaDA2.1-Flash and LLaDA2.1-Mini, we evaluate two configurations. In Flash-Flash, the same Flash model serves as both drafter and refiner, testing whether an existing model can improve its own block-autoregressive output through global refinement. In Mini-Flash, inspired by speculative decoding, we introduce speculative correction: Mini drafts a full response, and Flash revises it as an editable initialization. Flash-Flash improves GSM8K-384 accuracy from 0.848 to 0.899 while running 1.20 times faster than the selected Flash block-autoregressive baseline, and improves MBPP-384 from 0.545 to 0.693. Latency-window-matched Flash-only controls indicate that these gains persist after targeted tuning of block-autoregressive decoding. Causal ablations indicate that completed drafts provide useful initializations: refinement from a fully masked span performs poorly, full global refinement provides a clear additional gain on GSM8K, and local refinement captures much of the gain on MBPP and MATH. Mini-Flash provides useful quality-latency trade-offs, including MATH-384 performance of 0.294 versus 0.300 for Flash while running 2.17 times faster. These results support a Pareto-frontier interpretation rather than the claim that the heterogeneous cascade uniformly matches Flash quality. Overall, same-model draft-and-refine provides evidence that bidirectional refinement is a useful decoding primitive for DLMs, while speculative correction demonstrates a training-free route to fast DLM generation.

</details>


### [10] [Stuck on "A": Diagnosing and Repairing Interface Injury in Attention-to-KDA Linearization of a 0.6B Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02689)
*Ronglong Bao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They replace most full-attention layers in a small LLM with a linear-attention variant (KDA) and discover that traditional distillation metrics look good, but the model’s multiple-choice ‘interface’ is badly broken; a short, format-targeted KL phase largely fixes it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Linear attention promises lower memory and compute costs than full attention, but it is unclear what is actually lost when converting an existing full-attention model to linear attention under tight GPU budgets. The authors want to understand what specifically breaks (if anything) beyond standard metrics like perplexity, and how to reliably make such conversions work in practice.

Method: Starting from Qwen3-0.6B-Base, they replace 21/28 full-attention layers with KDA (Kimi Delta Attention) linear-attention layers and distill the resulting ‘student’ from the full-attention ‘teacher’ using hidden-state alignment and end-to-end KL minimization. They then probe the model using a four-permutation diagnostic on C-Eval multiple-choice questions (rotating answer labels while fixing content) to detect whether the model is using content vs. label shortcuts. After discovering label-stickiness, they add a 1,000-step, format-targeted, completion-only KL distillation stage focused on the multiple-choice interface, then apply persona SFT and one round of on-policy DPO. They also systematically debug training, uncovering pitfalls such as an FP32-master / bf16 optimizer-update failure mode.

Result: Initial distillation yields near-teacher perplexity but severely degraded multiple-choice accuracy (25–29% vs. 50.6% on C-Eval) and extreme label-stickiness (e.g., predicting option “A” 81% of the time; 106/161 questions keep the same letter prediction under all four answer-order permutations). The additional 1,000-step format-targeted KL phase improves C-Eval accuracy by +12.48 points and roughly halves label-stickiness. Persona SFT and a single on-policy DPO round maintain benchmark scores within statistical noise. They also document concrete training engineering findings, such as silent failures when bf16 updates are swallowed in an FP32-master setup.

Conclusion: Converting most attention layers of a small LLM to a linear-attention variant can preserve perplexity while catastrophically damaging the model’s task interface, particularly for multiple-choice formats. Standard distillation objectives and metrics (perplexity, hidden-state alignment, global KL) are insufficient to detect or fix such interface injuries. Lightweight, format-targeted distillation stages that directly train on the deployment interface can substantially repair performance, and careful engineering (e.g., avoiding bf16/FP32 optimizer pitfalls) is crucial for convergence under consumer-grade GPU constraints. The work highlights the need for explicit interface diagnostics—like label-permutation tests—when compressing or modifying attention mechanisms in LLMs.

Abstract: We convert 21 of 28 full-attention layers of Qwen3-0.6B-Base into KDA (Kimi Delta Attention) linear-attention layers on a single consumer-grade GPU budget, and ask a simple question: what exactly does the conversion break? After surgery, hidden-state alignment and end-to-end KL distillation drive the student close to its teacher in perplexity, yet multiple-choice accuracy stays near random chance (25-29% vs. the teacher's 50.6% on C-Eval). Using a four-permutation diagnostic that rotates answer options while holding content fixed, we show the model sticks to option labels (predicting "A" 81% of the time; 106/161 questions keep the same label under all four rotations) rather than following answer content -- an interface injury that standard distillation metrics cannot see. A 1,000-step format-targeted completion-only KL stage repairs the interface (+12.48 points on C-Eval, label-stickiness roughly halved), after which persona SFT and one round of on-policy DPO preserve benchmark scores within noise. We release code, weights, recipes, and the full audit trail, and distill the engineering lessons -- including an FP32-master failure mode in which bf16 optimizer updates are silently swallowed -- that made convergence possible at this budget.

</details>


### [11] [Crayotter: Learning Long-Horizon Video Editing Agents via Group-Relative Preference Backpropagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02694)
*Lecheng Yan,Jianze Lin,Yichong Zhang,Ben Pan,Wenxi Li,Chenyang Lyu,Liting Zhou,Cathal Gurrin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces GRPB, a method to learn video editing agents from subjective, delayed feedback by turning same-task rankings into segment-level credit, yielding a 9B model (Crayotter) that outperforms several proprietary systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon video editing requires many interdependent decisions, but available feedback is only a final judgment that is subjective, delayed, and incomparable across heterogeneous tasks. This makes standard scalar reward learning difficult and uninformative over time, limiting the ability to train effective editing agents from user preferences.

Method: Fix each editing request, source materials, and constraints so that different editing attempts for that task become directly comparable. Collect rankings among these same-task alternatives, then apply Group-Relative Preference Backpropagation (GRPB), which: (1) converts these rankings into zero-sum advantages within each group; (2) redistributes these advantages as bounded credit across semantic editing segments in the trajectory; and (3) uses a lagged allocator and guarded transmission so that current or unreliable estimates cannot directly influence the same rollout group. Train a 9B-parameter video editing agent, Crayotter, on a manually built, project-disjoint, horizon-stratified benchmark of realistic tasks using this credit assignment scheme.

Result: Using GRPB on the constructed editing task suite, the trained 9B Crayotter model shows improved editing behavior and final rendered outputs compared with matched baselines and ablations of the credit assignment mechanism. These improvements are validated through internal credit intervention experiments, external benchmarks (notably AgenticVBench), and blinded human evaluation. Crayotter surpasses several proprietary systems on AgenticVBench.

Conclusion: Transforming subjective, delayed video editing feedback into task-local, group-relative preferences and propagating them as bounded, segment-level credit enables effective training of long-horizon video editing agents. GRPB provides a practical way to learn from subjective, temporally sparse outcomes, and the success of the 9B Crayotter model—outperforming several proprietary systems—supports task-local preference reduction as a scalable strategy for similar subjective, long-horizon domains. All code and resources are released for reproducibility.

Abstract: Long-horizon video editing agents receive final-product feedback only after many interdependent decisions. Yet editing quality is subjective, admits multiple valid solutions, and is not meaningfully calibrated across heterogeneous requests, making a global scalar objective both ambiguous and temporally uninformative. Our key observation is that fixing the request, materials, and production constraints converts this subjective objective into an ordinal comparison among directly comparable alternatives. We introduce Group-Relative Preference Backpropagation (GRPB), which transforms same-task rankings into zero-sum advantages and redistributes them as bounded credit over semantic editing segments. A lagged allocator and guarded transmission prevent current judgments or unreliable estimates from directly shaping the same rollout group. We manually construct a project-disjoint, horizon-stratified suite of realistic editing tasks for training and controlled evaluation. Across matched baselines, credit interventions, external benchmarking, and blinded human evaluation, GRPB improves both editing behavior and rendered products. The resulting 9B Crayotter model surpasses several proprietary systems on AgenticVBench, supporting task-local preference reduction as a practical approach to learning from subjective, delayed outcomes. Code and all supporting materials are publicly available at https://github.com/idwts/Crayotter.

</details>


### [12] [ARCHead: Activation-Metric Residual Correction for Large Language Model Output Heads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02703)
*Şuayp Talha Kocabay,Talha Rüzgar Akkuş,Kamer Ali Yuksel*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ARCHead is a compression method for the LM-head of LLMs that achieves ~4x storage reduction with negligible perplexity loss and minor throughput impact, complementing existing weight-only quantization of transformer blocks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While weight-only quantization compresses transformer blocks of LLMs, practical systems often keep the large LM-head (final projection to vocabulary logits) in BF16/FP16 due to accuracy concerns. Naively quantizing this head significantly distorts the logit distribution and hurts perplexity, leaving a major memory bottleneck and limiting end-to-end model compression.

Method: ARCHead builds a compressed LM-head by combining three components: (1) a quantized low-rank core that approximates the main structure of the full-rank LM-head; (2) group-wise INT4 residual weights that capture finer-grained deviations from the low-rank approximation; and (3) a low-rank correction term that is optimized in a metric derived from activations, so that the approximation is tailored to actual usage patterns. The method is implemented as a packed representation that removes the need to store any dense BF16 head.

Result: ARCHead reduces persistent LM-head storage by about 3.7–3.9x compared to a dense BF16 head. On Qwen3-8B-Base, it uses only 25.6% of the BF16 LM-head storage while achieving 1.007× the original perplexity, whereas naive INT4 quantization at similar storage cost leads to 1.14–1.16× perplexity. When plugged into models already quantized with AWQ or bitsandbytes, replacing their remaining BF16 LM-head increases cross-entropy by only 0.006–0.007 and changes throughput by less than 2% in the reported measurements.

Conclusion: ARCHead enables effective compression of the LM-head in large language models, delivering about 4x storage reduction with negligible impact on perplexity and runtime. It fills a gap left by existing block-quantization methods, which typically ignore or leave the LM-head in high precision, and therefore provides a complementary technique for achieving end-to-end LLM compression without sacrificing accuracy.

Abstract: Weight-only quantization substantially reduces the storage of large language model (LLM) transformer blocks, but practical backends often retain the final language-modeling head (LM-head) in BF16 or FP16. Quantizing this projection naively can strongly perturb the vocabulary-logit distribution. We present ARCHead, a packed LM-head compressor that combines a quantized low-rank core, group-wise INT4 residuals, and a low-rank correction fitted in an activation-derived metric. ARCHead stores no dense BF16 head and reduces persistent LM-head storage by 3.7-3.9x. On Qwen3-8B-Base, it uses 25.6% of BF16 head storage while attaining 1.007 relative perplexity; storage-matched naive INT4 yields 1.14-1.16. Replacing the BF16 head left by AWQ or bitsandbytes adds only 0.006-0.007 cross-entropy, with less than 2% throughput change in our measurements. ARCHead therefore complements block quantizers by compressing the large output projection they can leave untouched. Code is available at https://github.com/suayptalha/archead.

</details>


### [13] [Learning a Vector-Symbolic Model for Socio-Cultural Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02807)
*Meera Ray,Swapnika Dulam,Christopher L. Dancy*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build a new multi-level semantic declarative memory mechanism for ACT-R using vector-symbolic autoencoding, and test it on an IAT task to capture sociocultural impacts on decision making.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing computational cognitive models and large language models represent broad semantic associations but do not explicitly integrate self representations and culturally structured associations in a way that lets us see how sociocultural structures shape decision making across multiple semantic levels. Modelers also lack guidance on which levels of representation matter most in concrete situations.

Method: They design a new declarative memory system for the ACT-R architecture that uses a vector-symbolic autoencoder (based on Holographic Reduced Representations, HRR) to encode semantic associations at multiple levels. Episodic memories are encoded with a simple HRR operation that makes them distinct from semantic memory vectors extracted from text. Memory retrieval in ACT-R then uses these representations to compute chunk activation for a given memory request. They evaluate this system by embedding it in ACT-R models of an implicit association test (IAT) that is racially contextualized, comparing how the new memory structure affects model behavior.

Result: The system can represent multi-level semantic associations, separate yet relate episodic and semantic memory, and produce meaningful chunk activations in ACT-R that influence decisions in the IAT context. The racially contextualized IAT models demonstrate that the enriched declarative memory can capture the influence of cultural associations on implicit decision processes, aligning model behavior more closely with empirical IAT effects.

Conclusion: Incorporating a vector-symbolic, multi-level semantic declarative memory into ACT-R allows computational cognitive models to more explicitly represent how sociocultural and cultural self-related associations shape decision making. This approach shows that distinguishing episodic from semantic representations and integrating them via HRR within ACT-R is a viable way to model culturally structured effects on tasks like the IAT, and it points toward richer, more socioculturally aware cognitive models.

Abstract: How can we better represent the impact of sociocultural structures on decision making in computational cognitive models? Modeling this impact requires traversing multiple levels of semantic representation, however it is not immediately clear to a modeler which levels of representation are most salient to a given situation. Though large language models and cognitively grounded corpus models can represent broad semantic associations through co-occurences, the role of self representations in memory should be accounted for to determine how cultural associations shape decision making. We propose a declarative memory system to be used in the ACT-R cognitive architecture that represents semantic associations at multiple levels via a vector-symbolic autoencoder. We use a simple HRR operation to encode episodic memories differently from semantic memory vectors extracted from text to produce a final chunk activation for a memory request. We use ACT-R cognitive models of a racially contextualized implicit association test (IAT) to test this new declarative memory system.

</details>


### [14] [BODHI: Do LLMs Branch Out and Discover Heterogeneous Inferences?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02867)
*Soumadeep Saha,Krish Sharma,Akshay Chaturvedi,Nicholas Asher*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) changes large language model behavior, finding that it reduces not only superficial variation but also genuine diversity in reasoning paths, which may explain higher sample efficiency but at a cost to exploratory reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is an open question whether RLVR truly expands LLM reasoning capabilities or simply makes them more efficient at sampling good answers. Existing evaluations often conflate stylistic output variability with real differences in reasoning, making it unclear what kind of exploration RLVR encourages or suppresses.

Method: The authors design controlled maze-solving tasks and analyze mathematical reasoning traces from RLVR-trained LLMs. They construct BODHI-Trees, a tree structure that clusters reasoning steps by semantic equivalence, to distinguish syntactic entropy (stylistic variation) from semantic branching (genuinely different inferential paths). They then measure and compare entropy and branching behaviors across models.

Result: They find that RLVR-trained models exhibit policy entropy collapse not just at the surface level but also in terms of semantic branching: the number and diversity of distinct reasoning branches is significantly reduced. At the same time, these models follow environmental constraints better and show improved backtracking behavior when exploring solution paths.

Conclusion: RLVR’s improved sample efficiency appears linked to its constriction of the space of possible continuations: it narrows the model’s exploratory behavior, reducing genuine rollout diversity even as it strengthens constraint adherence and structured search. This suggests a trade-off between efficiency and exploratory reasoning diversity in RLVR-trained LLMs.

Abstract: Although reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has improved the performance of large language models (LLMs) across a variety of reasoning tasks, there is significant debate as to whether RLVR expands the reasoning capability boundary, or just improves sampling efficiency. In this paper, we investigate the nature of test-time exploration in RLVR-trained LLMs by employing controlled maze-solving experiments and extracting a tree structure from mathematical reasoning traces (BODHI-Trees) based on semantic equivalence. This helps us delineate between entropy arising from stylistic variations and genuine inferential branching. Our findings demonstrate that the policy entropy collapse observed in RLVR models is not merely syntactic, and is accompanied by a significant reduction in semantic branching entropy. While RLVR improves adherence to environmental constraints and backtracking capabilities, it constricts the space of continuations; we provide evidence suggesting that this might be responsible for the sample efficiency gains of RLVR, albeit at the cost of genuine rollout diversity.

</details>


### [15] [FLARE: Few-shot Learning-based Adaptive Reflective Engine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02919)
*Dhanasekar Sundararaman,Bharat Gandhi,Aashna Garg,Minjie Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces FLARE, a few-shot learning-based reflective instruction optimization framework that outperforms the prior GEPA optimizer across multiple LLM benchmarks in both accuracy and data efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are embedded into complex systems, their performance increasingly depends on well-optimized prompts or instructions. Existing state-of-the-art methods like GEPA use evolutionary reflective strategies, but may underexploit the power and efficiency of few-shot learning. The authors are motivated to revisit and strengthen few-shot-based optimization to see if it can surpass purely reflective evolutionary approaches in both performance and data efficiency.

Method: The authors propose FLARE (Few-shot Learning-based Adaptive Reflective Engine), which combines reflective instruction optimization with a structured use of a small set of few-shot reference examples. FLARE adaptively refines instructions by leveraging these examples and reflective mechanisms rather than relying solely on evolutionary search. They evaluate FLARE on multiple tasks—retrieval-augmented multi-hop QA benchmarks (HotPotQA, MedQA, 2WikiMultiHopQA), tool calling, and multi-label emotion classification (GoEmotions)—using different GPT-5 series models, then compare results directly with GEPA.

Result: Across all evaluated tasks and GPT-5 model variants, FLARE consistently outperforms GEPA. For instance, on HotPotQA with GPT-5-Chat, FLARE improves accuracy by 10 points (52.2 vs. 42.2). In tool calling tasks, it achieves 87.0% accuracy versus GEPA’s 81.0%. On GoEmotions with GPT-5.1, FLARE boosts micro-F1 to 52.7%—a +15.3 improvement over baseline and more than double GEPA’s +5.7 gain—on the full 5408-example test set. FLARE also achieves near-peak performance using as few as 100 validation examples and exhibits greater stability across random seeds than GEPA.

Conclusion: The study concludes that while reflective instruction evolution is valuable, it is not sufficient alone: strategically optimized few-shot learning remains crucial for extracting maximal performance from modern LLMs. FLARE demonstrates that integrating advanced reflection with data-efficient few-shot reference examples can surpass evolutionary optimizers like GEPA in both accuracy and robustness, highlighting few-shot-oriented instruction optimization as a key direction for future LLM system design.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in complex, compound AI systems where performance hinges on the quality of prompts. Recent state-of-the-art optimizers like GEPA (Genetic-Pareto) have argued that reflective instruction evolution can outperform traditional reinforcement learning and few-shot optimization. In this work, we challenge this shift by introducing FLARE (Few-shot Learning-based Adaptive Reflective Engine), a framework that leverages advanced reflective mechanisms and a small set of few-shot reference examples to optimize instructions. We evaluate our method across a diverse suite of benchmarks -- spanning retrieval-augmented reasoning (HotPotQA, MedQA, 2WikiMultiHopQA), tool calling, and multi-label emotion classification (GoEmotions) -- using the GPT-5 series of models. Our results demonstrate that FLARE consistently outperforms GEPA, winning on every task-model pair: it achieves gains of up to +14.2 points on HotPotQA (52.2 vs. GEPA's 42.2 with GPT-5-Chat), reaches 87.0% on tool calling (vs. 81.0% for GEPA), and lifts GoEmotions micro-F1 to 52.7% (+15.3) with GPT-5.1 on the full 5408-example test split, more than doubling GEPA's +5.7 gain. Beyond raw accuracy, FLARE is also strikingly data-efficient: on GoEmotions it reaches its peak performance using as few as 100 validation examples, while remaining markedly more stable across random seeds than GEPA. Our findings suggest that while reflective instructions are powerful, the strategic optimization of few-shot learning remains a critical frontier for maximizing the potential of next-generation LLMs.

</details>


### [16] [Character Iconicity vs. Arbitrariness: An Arabic NLP Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02935)
*Dorieh Alomari,Irfan Ahmad,Maged S. Al-shaibani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper tests whether the specific visual distinctions between Arabic letters (dots and traditional rasm groupings) are actually needed for NLP, by randomly remapping characters while keeping the same reduced inventory of base shapes, and finds that arbitrary remappings work just as well.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Arabic script has many letters sharing the same base shape and differing only by dots, and historical manuscripts show that undotted text was still readable. Prior work found that dotless Arabic can work well for NLP, but it is unknown whether this depends on preserving traditional rasm-based groupings or whether any consistent remapping to the same number of symbols would perform similarly. Clarifying this addresses basic questions about the functional necessity of letter-form distinctions and has practical implications for vocabulary size, model efficiency, and script design in NLP.

Method: The authors create several writing systems for Arabic: standard dotted Arabic, a dotless version collapsed to 19 rasms, and 2,000 random but consistent character remappings that also map into the same 19-symbol inventory, under both word- and character-level tokenization. From these random mappings they select four representative ones spanning high and low entropy properties. They then train and evaluate NLP models on multiple tasks—language modeling, text classification, sequence labeling, machine translation, and restoration back to the original script—comparing performance, vocabulary size, out-of-vocabulary (OOV) rates, model size, and training cost across representations.

Result: Across all tested NLP tasks, systems trained on random remapped scripts perform competitively with those trained on standard dotted Arabic and on dotless but rasm-preserving representations. Neither the preservation of original letter distinctions nor adherence to traditional rasm groupings yields a consistent performance advantage. Random remappings substantially reduce vocabulary size and OOV rates and lead to smaller, more efficient models without significant loss in accuracy or quality on the evaluated tasks.

Conclusion: For the evaluated NLP settings, the mapping between Arabic visual letter forms and linguistic functions is largely arbitrary: models mainly exploit distributional regularities in symbol sequences rather than any intrinsic visual or historical structure of the script. Consequently, both traditional dots and rasm groupings are not functionally necessary for strong NLP performance, and arbitrary, consistent remappings to a reduced symbol set can provide efficiency gains while maintaining accuracy. This challenges assumptions about the importance of script iconicity in computational processing and suggests broader flexibility in script design for NLP applications.

Abstract: Arabic script uses 28 letters, many of which share a common base shape (rasm) and are distinguished only by dot placement. Because early Arabic manuscripts were written without dots yet remained interpretable, dot removal offers a natural test of whether these visual distinctions are functionally necessary. Prior work has shown that dotless Arabic can remain readable and effective for natural language processing (NLP), but it remains unclear whether this success depends on preserving the original rasm groupings or whether arbitrary but consistent remappings to the same reduced rasm set can achieve comparable performance. We address this question by comparing standard dotted and dotless Arabic with arbitrary character remappings constrained to the same 19 undotted rasms. We generated 2,000 random remappings under word- and character-level tokenization and selected four representative mappings with the highest and lowest entropy values. These representations were evaluated across language modeling, text classification, sequence labeling, machine translation, and restoration to the original script. The results show that neither preserving original character distinctions nor retaining traditional rasm-based groupings is necessary for strong NLP performance. Random remappings achieve competitive performance while reducing vocabulary size, out-of-vocabulary (OOV) rates, model size, and training cost. These findings suggest that, from an NLP perspective, Arabic character form-function relationships are largely arbitrary: models rely more on stable distributional structure than on the visual iconicity of letter forms.

</details>


### [17] [Aligned in Form, Not in Meaning: The Comprehension - Containment Decoupling of LLM Safety in Low-Resource Bangla Derogatory Speech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02941)
*Shadab Bin Habib,A K M Ferdous Reza Habib,Subarno Neel,Adib Sakhawat*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper audits state-of-the-art large language models on Bangla derogatory speech and shows that models often understand low-resource slurs without reliably containing or refusing to use them, because safety is tied to high-resource surface forms instead of underlying harmful meaning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety evaluations and alignment of large language models are mostly built and tested on high-resource languages like English. There is growing but insufficient evidence that these safety mechanisms do not transfer reliably to low-resource languages, especially for nuanced, culturally-specific harmful speech such as Bangla gali (derogatory slang and slurs). The authors aim to systematically test whether models’ ability to understand harmful Bangla content and their ability to contain or refuse to generate it are decoupled, and to highlight gaps in current safety benchmarks and filters.

Method: The authors audit five frontier LLMs on native Bangla derogatory speech across six experimental protocols. They define a hypothesis called Comprehension-Containment Decoupling, which posits that models’ semantic understanding of low-resource slurs and their safety-aligned containment behavior are not tightly coupled. They use a human-annotated, calibrated dataset with high inter-annotator agreement (kappa = 0.84) as ground truth. They then measure (1) comprehension accuracy in Bangla versus a high-resource language, (2) token leakage rates (how often harmful tokens are output), (3) severity calibration behavior comparing surface/anatomical cues vs compositional harm, (4) responses under orthographic perturbations to test tokenizer and filter robustness, (5) the impact of explicit Chain-of-Thought prompting on both comprehension and containment, and (6) the effect of expert-persona framing on refusal rates and keyword-based filters’ treatment of communal slurs.

Result: The audit finds a 7.92 percentage point deficit in models’ comprehension of Bangla derogatory content compared to the high-resource baseline, yet the models maintain the same high token leakage rate (92.83%) across both languages, confirming that containment does not improve in the low-resource setting. Severity calibration is skewed toward surface anatomical cues rather than true compositional harm, leading to over-penalization of mild slang (+4.00 error) and under-penalization of explicit threats (-2.00 error). Orthographic perturbations make models appear safer, but this is a "containment mirage" driven by tokenization quirks rather than genuine understanding. Explicit Chain-of-Thought prompting dramatically increases comprehension (to 94.72% pass) but simultaneously leads models to use harmful content much more (96.23% use), undermining containment. When models are framed with an expert persona, refusal rates drop sharply to 6.57%, and keyword-based filters are shown to fail completely on dehumanizing communal slurs that lack obvious high-resource lexical markers.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that current safety alignment and benchmarking, which rely heavily on high-resource surface forms and keyword-like features, cannot guarantee safety in low-resource languages such as Bangla. The evidence supports the Comprehension-Containment Decoupling hypothesis: models can understand low-resource slurs while safety mechanisms fail to recognize or block them. Apparent improvements in safety under perturbations or prompting are often artifacts of tokenization or prompt framing rather than true containment. The authors argue that safety systems must be redesigned toward meaning-grounded containment that robustly tracks harmful intent and content across languages and scripts, instead of relying on high-resource benchmarks and surface-level cues.

Abstract: We audit five frontier large language models on native Bangla derogatory speech (gali) across six protocols to test a single hypothesis: Comprehension-Containment Decoupling. We propose that contemporary safety alignment is bound to high-resource surface forms rather than harmful meaning, causing a model's capacity to comprehend a low-resource slur and its capacity to contain it to operate independently. Every protocol corroborates this hypothesis against a human-calibrated baseline (kappa = 0.84). At baseline, models exhibit a 7.92 percentage point comprehension deficit in Bangla while maintaining an identical 92.83% token leakage rate across both languages. Severity calibration tracks surface anatomical cues over compositional harm (+4.00 error on mild slang; -2.00 on threats), while apparent containment gains under orthographic perturbation prove to be a tokenizer-driven "containment mirage." Crucially, explicit Chain-of-Thought reasoning rescues comprehension (94.72% Pass) while systematically dismantling containment (96.23% Use). Furthermore, expert-persona framing collapses refusal to 6.57%, revealing that keyword-based filters ignore dehumanizing communal slurs entirely. Our findings demonstrate that high-resource benchmarks cannot certify low-resource safety, necessitating meaning-grounded containment.

</details>


### [18] [OPTD: On-Policy Transition Distillation with Consistency-Guided Adaptive Compression for Few-Step Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02942)
*Xiaocheng Lu,Hualei Zhang,Shuhan Guo,Jie Zhang,Xiaoyi Pang,Jian Liu,Haoxi Li,Bohai Gu,Haoxuan Che,Jingcai Guo,Song Guo*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes OPTD, an on-policy distillation method for diffusion language models that adaptively compresses multiple teacher steps into each student transition while maintaining final-answer consistency, yielding better quality–efficiency trade-offs on math and code benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion language models can generate many tokens in parallel but need many denoising steps, making them slow. Few-step distillation aims to compress many teacher steps into a small number of student steps, but existing approaches supervise on off-policy trajectories: they train on states from the teacher that the student never actually visits at inference. This leads to a mismatch, especially where compression is highest, harming generation quality. An on-policy approach is needed that supervises the student on its own visited states while still safely compressing multiple teacher transitions without breaking intermediate dependencies.

Method: The authors introduce OPTD (On-Policy Transition Distillation) with consistency-guided adaptive compression. During training, they roll out the few-step student and sample partial states from its own trajectories (on-policy). Using a frozen, question-only teacher, they look ahead to find future token candidates whose commitments still lead to the same final outcome as the teacher’s rollout. They order these candidates by the current state’s confidence, then select the longest prefix of future tokens whose joint commitment is guaranteed not to alter the final outcome. They then train the student with a set-bottleneck objective that pushes all verified future tokens up to the decoding-release threshold, while applying a KL regularization term to other active positions using the frozen teacher’s distribution. No gold answers are used in constructing these supervision targets.

Result: On four benchmarks involving mathematical reasoning and code generation, OPTD improves the trade-off between output quality and decoding efficiency relative to existing few-step distillation baselines. Specifically, it achieves the highest area under the quality-constrained performance curve (AUP) among evaluated methods, indicating better performance across a range of quality targets at fewer steps.

Conclusion: OPTD shows that on-policy transition distillation with adaptive compression can effectively mitigate the mismatch between training and inference in few-step distilled diffusion language models. By only committing to the longest verified prefix that preserves teacher outcomes, it safely compresses multiple steps into one while maintaining reasoning fidelity. This leads to more efficient decoding without sacrificing—and often improving—generation quality on math and code tasks, suggesting a promising direction for scalable parallel generation in diffusion-based LMs.

Abstract: Diffusion language models (dLLMs) can predict many tokens in parallel, but accurate generation still requires many iterative denoising steps. Few-step distillation accelerates decoding by compressing multiple teacher steps into a single student transition. However, existing methods construct supervision on off-policy trajectories. At inference, the student's early parallel commitments alter the context of later predictions, so the states it actually visits drift away from the supervised ones--precisely when step compression is most aggressive. On-policy distillation is a natural remedy for this mismatch, but it leaves open how far each transition should advance: matching only the teacher's next action limits compression, while indiscriminately merging future actions can violate intermediate dependencies. To address this limitation, we propose OPTD, On-Policy Transition Distillation with consistency-guided adaptive compression. It samples partial states from the few-step student's own trajectories, uses a frozen, question-only teacher to identify outcome-aligned future candidates, and orders them by current-state confidence. The method then selects the longest prefix whose joint commitment preserves the teacher's rollout outcome. A set-bottleneck objective promotes every verified future candidate to the decoder's release threshold, while a frozen-teacher KL anchor regularizes all other active positions. Neither target construction nor training uses a gold response. Across four mathematical reasoning and code-generation benchmarks, OPTD consistently improves the quality--efficiency trade-off and attains the strongest overall quality-constrained AUP among the evaluated few-step baselines.

</details>


### [19] [Every Wrong Answer Counts: Option-Level Psychometrics for LLM Multiple-Choice Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02966)
*Xiao Fei,Yang Zhang,Sarah Almeida Carneiro,Michalis Vazirgiannis*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a new psychometric model (LLM-NRM) that uses the full distribution over multiple-choice options, not just correctness, to better measure LLM abilities and item properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard MCQ benchmarks only score LLMs as correct/incorrect, ignoring which wrong options are chosen and with what probabilities, even though this could reveal systematic behavior, ability differences, and richer diagnostic information.

Method: They adapt and extend a nominal response psychometric model to LLMs (LLM-NRM), explicitly modeling the probability distribution over all options for each item and LLM. The model disentangles LLM ability from response calibration sharpness, positional biases, and difficulty-dependent fallback patterns. They fit this model on a large dataset of 189 LLMs answering 31,554 items from 14 benchmarks, compare it to binary Item Response Theory models and other nominal-response baselines, and study information contributed by distractor identities and efficient-item selection using learned item parameters.

Result: LLM-NRM predicts held-out LLM–item responses better than binary IRT and conventional nominal models. Its ability estimates correlate strongly (Spearman 0.920) with an external human-preference-based Elo leaderboard (Arena.ai).
