Considering which distractor is chosen adds 101% more Fisher Information per item beyond correctness alone. Using only incorrect-response patterns still recovers nearly full-information ability estimates (Spearman 0.943). The learned item parameters allow creation of a short 41-item subset that preserves the full-bank model ranking with Kendall 0.85, a 770× reduction in benchmark size.

Conclusion: Incorrect answers and full option distributions contain rich, structured information about LLM behavior and ability. By modeling option-level response patterns with LLM-NRM, one can obtain more accurate ability estimates, better predict responses, and design dramatically more efficient benchmarks than with standard correct/incorrect scoring, showing that not all mistakes are equivalent.

Abstract: Most multiple-choice question (MCQ) benchmarks evaluate Large Language Models (LLMs) only by whether they select the correct answers. This binary scoring treats all incorrect responses alike, even though an LLM's preferences among incorrect options may contain systematic and useful information about its behavior and ability. We introduce the LLM Nominal Response Model (LLM-NRM), an option-aware psychometric framework that models the full distribution over answer choices to jointly estimate LLM ability and option-level item characteristics, while separating model-specific response calibration sharpness, positional preference, and difficulty-dependent fallback behavior. Across 189 LLMs and 31,554 items from 14 benchmarks, LLM-NRM predicts held-out LLM-item interactions more accurately than binary Item Response models and conventional nominal-response baselines, and its ability estimates achieve the strongest Spearman correlation of 0.920 with the external human-preference Arena.ai Elo leaderboard. Distractor identity contributes +101% additional Fisher Information per item beyond correctness, and incorrect responses alone recover full-information ability estimates with Spearman 0.943. The learned item parameters also enable efficient benchmarking, where 41 selected items preserve the full-bank ranking with Kendall's correlation 0.85, corresponding to a 770 times reduction. In conclusion, we show that incorrect answers carry distinct and useful measurement information rather than representing equivalent mistakes.

</details>


### [20] [Mapping the City Through the Lens of Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02971)
*Wanqi Liu,Rong Zhao,Zhizhou Sha,Qinyu Cui,Yecheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper empirically measures what language models implicitly consider an “ordinary” city, showing a shared bias toward large, infrastructurally developed, fast-growing, less-sparse urban forms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models routinely complete references to cities with hidden assumptions about what cities are like, but these assumptions are vague and unmeasured. The authors want a systematic, empirical way to uncover and characterize the implicit urban model inside LMs without relying on named locations, and to see how consistent these assumptions are across models, geographies, and tasks like typicality vs. desirability.

Method: They construct anonymized profiles of real morphological urban centers, each described by indicators spanning 40 metrics across seven domains (e.g., size, growth, infrastructure, land use, form). Ten open-weight language-model checkpoints are asked to rate these profiles on each indicator using constrained, probability-based ratings. The study incorporates reliability checks, aggregation that accounts for model lineage, different ways of weighting urban populations, an independent replication sample, and a separate evaluation where models rate entire profiles directly (not indicator by indicator) to validate the constructed scores.

Result: Across models, there is a clear and recurring preference pattern: profiles that represent larger developed areas, faster recent urban growth, denser and more extensive mapped infrastructure, more non-residential capacity, and less spatially sparse form tend to be rated as more typical or desirable. These patterns broadly replicate in an independent sample, and direct whole-profile ratings moderately agree with the indicator-wise composite. Apparent geographic differences in ratings become smaller once city size and development level are controlled for. Paired tasks show that what models deem typical and what they deem desirable are often closely aligned.

Conclusion: The authors show that it is possible to operationalize and quantify the otherwise vague notion of what language models regard as an “ordinary city.” Their framework reveals a shared but model-dependent representation of cities that favors large, infrastructurally developed, and growing urban forms, and it provides a replicable way to audit and compare LM urban assumptions across models and contexts.

Abstract: Language models often complete an underspecified reference to a city with unstated assumptions about urban size, form, infrastructure, environment, and function. We measure those assumptions without naming places. Ten open-weight checkpoints rate anonymized profiles derived from real morphological urban centres across 40 audited indicators and seven domains. The design combines constrained probability-based ratings, prespecified reliability screens, lineage-aware aggregation, multiple population weightings, an independent replication sample, and whole-profile validation. The clearest shared tendency favours urban profiles with larger developed area, faster recent growth, greater mapped infrastructure and non-residential capacity, and less sparse form. Most eligible directions recur in the replication data, and direct ratings of complete profiles show moderate agreement with the indicator-wise construction. Geographic differences shrink after accounting for city scale and development, while reliably measured paired tasks indicate that typicality and desirability are often closely aligned. The framework makes an otherwise vague notion of what models regard as an ordinary city empirically traceable. The resulting evidence delineates a shared yet model-dependent portrait of the city through the lens of language models.

</details>


### [21] [TQLite: Multi-LLM Jury Guided Distillation for Real-time MQM Translation Quality Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02975)
*Bhavin Jawade,Cameron R. Wolfe*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how to make small language models (SLMs) good at MQM-style translation quality evaluation and proposes TQLite, a distillation framework that transfers the abilities of strong large reasoning models (LRMs) into efficient SLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current best translation quality evaluators are large language or reasoning models, which are accurate but too computationally expensive to deploy widely. Small models are cheaper but lack the complex reasoning needed for MQM-style evaluation. There is a need for a systematic benchmark of different model sizes and a way to make small models nearly as good as the best evaluators while remaining scalable and cost-effective.

Method: 1) Run an extensive empirical benchmark comparing SLMs, LLMs, and LRMs across many MQM-based translation quality evaluation setups to map out performance and best practices. 2) Propose TQLite, a distillation framework. TQLite constructs a multi-LRM jury: several strong LRMs are used to evaluate translations and generate high-quality synthetic MQM annotation data. Their responses are curated and aggregated into consensus labels/scores. 3) Train SLMs on this curated synthetic data so that they imitate the multi-LRM jury’s evaluation behavior.

Result: The SLMs trained with TQLite substantially outperform standard, off-the-shelf SLMs on MQM-based translation quality evaluation tasks. Their performance approaches that of the best LRM-based evaluators, demonstrating that high-quality multi-LRM synthetic data plus distillation can close most of the gap between small and large evaluators while retaining efficiency.

Conclusion: Carefully designed distillation from a diverse multi-LRM jury can turn small language models into strong, scalable MQM translation quality evaluators. This provides a cost-effective alternative to directly using large language or reasoning models and offers practical best practices for building evaluation systems that balance quality and efficiency.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive performance in MQM-based translation quality (TQ) evaluation, and recent advances in large reasoning models (LRMs) promise even greater improvements. However, both LLMs and LRMs are computationally expensive to deploy at scale, while small language models (SLMs)---though much more efficient---struggle with the complex reasoning required for evaluation tasks. In this work, we present an extensive empirical study benchmarking SLMs, LLMs, and LRMs across a wide range of TQ evaluation setups, providing a comprehensive view of the current landscape and establishing best practices. To address the scalability challenge, we introduce TQLite, a novel distillation framework that enables SLMs to approach the MQM evaluation performance of the best LRM-based evaluators. Our approach leverages a multi-LRM jury to generate high-quality synthetic training data via practical data curation techniques and aggregation of evaluation responses across a diverse panel of models. Our results demonstrate that SLMs trained via TQLite achieve strong MQM evaluation performance that far exceeds off-the-shelf evaluation capabilities of standard SLMs, offering a scalable and cost-effective alternative to LLM- and LRM-based evaluators.

</details>


### [22] [On the Non-Specificity of Statistical Measures Used in Script Decipherment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02999)
*Nikhil Raghavendra*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that many statistical tests used to argue that the Indus script encodes language are not specific to language, by constructing a non-linguistic but structured sign system (SIGIL) that passes those same tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In debates over undeciphered scripts, such as the Indus script, researchers often use statistical regularities (e.g., entropy profiles, frequency distributions) as evidence that a sign system encodes language. However, this reasoning is only valid if these statistical properties are specific to language rather than to structured symbol systems in general. The paper aims to test whether commonly used statistical diagnostics actually distinguish linguistic from non-linguistic sign systems.

Method: The authors design SIGIL, an artificial generative emblem system comprising about 3,000 texts. SIGIL has explicit compositional meanings but no phonological values—i.e., it is not a writing system for speech. Before evaluation, the authors build a registry of 54 methods from the existing literature on the Indus script and related analyses. For each method where they can reproduce the published Indus result and a clear decision rule, they apply the method to the SIGIL corpus and score it in the same way. They examine repetition, directional asymmetry, lexical distributions, entropy, frequency, positional and predictive statistics, classifier usage, and network measures. They also run a sequential decipherment stress test, trying to map SIGIL to lexicons of English, Sanskrit, and Tamil and evaluating coverage and stability of the resulting “keys.”

Result: Across all methods that could be exactly scored, SIGIL falls into the same categories as the Indus corpus for repetition, directional asymmetry, and lexical-distribution tests. Moreover, reconstructed measures of entropy, frequency profiles, positional and predictive patterns, classifier-like behavior, and network structure all reproduce the characteristic “Indus-like” signatures previously cited as evidence of language. The decipherment stress test attains high dictionary coverage for multiple natural languages using the SIGIL corpus, yet performance collapses on held-out groups and the inferred keys are unstable, indicating that such apparent decipherment success is largely spurious.

Conclusion: The constructed SIGIL system demonstrates that the statistical measures commonly used in the Indus script debate are sensitive to structural organization but not specific to language. Because a non-phonological emblem system can match the Indus script on these diagnostics and even yield misleadingly high “decipherment” coverage, these methods alone cannot establish that a sign system encodes spoken language. Therefore, statistical regularities of the types tested should not be taken as decisive evidence that the Indus signs (or similar undeciphered systems) represent linguistic writing.

Abstract: Statistical regularities are routinely offered as evidence that undeciphered sign systems encode language; the Indus script debate is the canonical example. Any such inference rests on specificity: the reported outcome must be unusual among plausible structured non-languages. We test that premise constructively with SIGIL, a purpose-built generative emblem system whose 3,000-text core corpus carries explicit compositional meanings although no sign has a phonological value. A literature registry compiled in advance of evaluation records 54 methods and admits a method to exact scoring when both the published Indus outcome and a source-defined decision rule can be reproduced. SIGIL receives the same category as the Indus corpus on every criterion scored this way, across repetition, directional-asymmetry, and lexical-distribution tests. Declared reconstructions of entropy, frequency, positional, predictive, classifier, and network measures reproduce the familiar Indus-like signatures as well. A sequential decipherment stress test then reaches high dictionary coverage for English, Sanskrit, and Tamil on the same corpus, while grouped held-out declines and unstable keys reveal how little that coverage identifies. The construction does not decide what the Indus signs encode: it shows that the evaluated measures detect organization without being specific to language, and therefore cannot, on their own, establish encoded speech.

</details>


### [23] [Language Models Encode the Contextual Truth of Propositions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03035)
*Rupak Sarkar,Pritika Ramu,Rachel Rudinger*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how large language models internally represent context-dependent truth, showing it is encoded linearly in activations and can be causally manipulated, especially in dialog settings that induce sycophancy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While prior work shows LLMs encode static factual truth along linear directions in activation space, it is unknown whether and how this extends to contextual, in‑conversation truths that depend on in‑context evidence and interaction partners. Understanding this is key for diagnosing phenomena like sycophancy and for aligning LLM behavior in multi‑agent or conversational settings.

Method: The authors analyze model activations associated with propositions in a collaborative vision‑language task involving two LLMs that must maintain common ground. They search for linear directions corresponding to contextual truth, test their robustness across different output policies, and run causal steering experiments where they intervene on activations. They compare internal truth representations against both the available evidence and the partner model’s assertions to study how representations shift, particularly near the decision boundary between true and false.

Result: They find a linear representation of contextual truth that is stable across varied output behaviors, even when truth is not required for generating the response. Partner assertions substantially influence the internal truth representation, sometimes overriding available evidence, with propositions near the model’s truth decision boundary being most easily shifted. They also find that when the model agrees with a false claim by explicitly restating it, its internal representation is 2.59x more likely to cross from “false” to “true” than when agreement is only implicit.

Conclusion: Contextual truth in LLMs is encoded linearly and can be causally steered, and these internal representations are highly sensitive to conversational partner assertions, especially for borderline propositions. By separating internal representation from output, the authors distinguish between sycophantic behaviors where the model outwardly agrees but still represents a claim as false, and those where its representation genuinely flips to encode the claim as true. This sheds light on the mechanisms behind sycophancy and suggests that alignment work must consider both internal truth representations and output behavior.

Abstract: Prior work has shown that LLMs encode the truth of factual propositions along linear directions in activation space. It's unclear how these representations extend to contextual truth: propositions whose truth is determined by in-context evidence rather than world knowledge. We show that LLMs maintain a linear representation of contextual truth that persists across structurally different output policies, even when the output doesn't require the model to determine a proposition's truth, and show causal evidence via steering experiments. Using the transcripts from a collaborative vision-language task that requires two LLMs to maintain a shared common ground, we show that truth representations of a proposition are significantly swayed by partner assertions about that proposition, even when the LLM has enough evidence to determine its truth. We find evidence that propositions near the decision boundary are more susceptible to having their truth shifted through partner assertions. Separating representation from output distinguish two forms of sycophancy that output behavior alone cannot: the model may accommodate a false proposition while continuing to represent it as false, or shift its representation across the boundary. The latter is 2.59x more common when the model agrees by restating the false claim explicitly than when it agrees implicitly.

</details>


### [24] [Beyond Accuracy: A Multidimensional Evaluation of Statistical Reasoning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03038)
*Monnie McGee,Mateo Langston Smith,Julian Cabrera*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates large language models’ statistical reasoning using multiple dimensions beyond accuracy, revealing shared conceptual structures but vendor-specific stylistic differences in explanations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of LLMs in statistics focus mostly on whether answers are correct, ignoring how models structure and express statistical reasoning. The authors aim to show that statistical reasoning is multidimensional and that a richer evaluation can uncover important patterns in models’ conceptual organization and explanatory style.

Method: They design a multidimensional evaluation framework that combines: (1) response accuracy; (2) response behavior; (3) structural topic modeling of model-generated explanations to uncover latent conceptual structures; and (4) lexical similarity analysis to quantify stylistic similarities and differences. They apply this to explanations from 15 contemporary LLMs answering 90 questions taken from four statistics exams covering high school, undergraduate, and graduate levels.

Result: Accuracy across the 15 LLMs ranged from 55% to 78%, showing substantial performance differences. Despite this, structural topic modeling revealed that all models shared a similar high-level conceptual organization of statistical reasoning. Lexical similarity analysis showed modest but systematic vendor-specific stylistic clustering: models from the same vendor (e.g., Anthropic, OpenAI) produced explanations that were more similar to each other than to models from other vendors.

Conclusion: Statistical reasoning in LLMs cannot be adequately captured by accuracy metrics alone. By integrating analyses of response behavior and the content and style of explanations, the proposed multidimensional framework offers a more comprehensive characterization of how LLMs reason statistically and how different model families resemble or differ from one another.

Abstract: Statistical reasoning is multidimensional, yet evaluations of large language models (LLMs) typically emphasize response accuracy while overlooking how models construct and communicate statistical explanations. This study demonstrates the value of a multidimensional evaluation by combining response accuracy, response behavior, structural topic modeling, and lexical similarity analysis. The framework is applied to explanations generated by 15 current-generation LLMs responding to 90 questions drawn from four statistics examinations spanning high school, undergraduate, and graduate levels. Accuracy varied substantially across models, ranging from 55\% to 78\%. In contrast, structural topic modeling revealed a common conceptual organization of statistical reasoning across all models, while lexical similarity analysis identified modest but consistent vendor-specific differences in explanatory style. Models developed by the same vendor (e.g. Anthropic, OpenAI) produced explanations that were slightly more similar than models from different vendors. These findings demonstrate that statistical reasoning in contemporary LLMs cannot be characterized by accuracy alone and illustrate how complementary analyses of response behavior and model-generated explanations provide a more comprehensive evaluation of statistical reasoning in generative AI.

</details>


### [25] [Emulate or Estimate? The Divergent Strengths of Base and Post-Trained Language Models for Opinion Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03044)
*Seth Grief-Albert,Jessica Bo,Difan Jiao,Ashton Anderson*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper distinguishes two tasks in using large language models (LLMs) to simulate human opinions—emulation and estimation—and finds that base models are better at emulation, whereas post-trained models are better at estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on using LLMs to simulate human opinions shows conflicting findings: some studies report that LLMs align well with human survey data, while others find failures like persona collapse and poor demographic sensitivity. The paper aims to resolve this tension and clarify when and how LLMs can reliably simulate populations.

Method: The authors conceptually separate human-simulation tasks into (1) emulation, where the model generates many individual responses and these are aggregated into a distribution, and (2) estimation, where the model directly predicts the population distribution. They then empirically evaluate six matched base and post-trained LLMs on the Pew American Trends Panel, comparing how well each model type matches human ground-truth distributions and demographic structure under both task formulations.

Result: Base models outperform post-trained models on the emulation task: their generated responses, when aggregated, produce distributions that more closely match the human survey data and better preserve demographic patterns. Post-trained models, however, perform better on the estimation task, giving more accurate predictions when directly asked for the population distribution. This shows that different training regimes produce complementary strengths for human simulation.

Conclusion: The apparent contradictions in prior work largely arise from mixing up two distinct tasks—emulation and estimation. Because base and post-trained LLMs excel at different tasks, practitioners should choose models based on whether they need realistic individual-level text generation (emulation) or accurate aggregate distribution predictions (estimation). Model selection and evaluation for human opinion simulation should therefore be explicitly aligned with the target task.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to simulate human opinions, but prior work reports conflicting results: some studies find promising alignment with human survey data, while others find persona collapse and weak demographic sensitivity. We show that much of this conflict stems from conflating two distinct tasks. We call the first task emulation, in which models generate individual responses that aggregate into a population distribution. We call the second task estimation, in which models directly predict the population distribution. Evaluating six matched base and post-trained models on the Pew American Trends Panel, we find that base models are stronger emulators: they produce response distributions closer to human ground truth and better preserve demographic structure. Post-trained models are stronger estimators, producing more accurate distributional predictions when asked directly. We propose that model selection for human simulation should be guided by whether the task requires generating text or predicting distributions.

</details>


### [26] [PI-Mem: Pushing Long-Context Reasoning to 3.6M Tokens with Parallel-Iterative Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03048)
*Dawei Liu,Haixu Song,Shuang Cheng,Shijie Wang,Haozheng Hou,Kaifeng Liu,Ermo Hua,Zhonghang Yuan,Zhijie Zhong,Yuchen Fan,Biqing Qi,Bowen Zhou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes PI-Mem, a parallel-iterative memory mechanism that improves both accuracy and speed for long-context, multi-hop reasoning in LLMs compared to recurrent-memory baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing recurrent-memory methods for long-context reasoning update memory sequentially over chunks. This causes two main problems: (1) early important evidence can be overwritten by later irrelevant information, and (2) processing chunks serially creates inter-chunk dependencies that hinder parallelism and make inference latency grow with context length. The authors want a method that scales to extremely long documents while maintaining both accuracy and efficiency.

Method: They introduce PI-Mem (Parallel-Iterative Memory). The long context is divided into chunks, and instead of processing them sequentially, all chunks are read in parallel in each iteration, conditioned on a shared memory state. For each turn, the model selects new or complementary evidence from every chunk and merges these into a compact shared memory for the next turn. The process is iterative but bounded to a small number of turns. To avoid unnecessary iterations, they use reinforcement learning with an auxiliary turn-efficiency reward so the model learns to stop early once enough evidence has been gathered.

Result: On HotpotQA with extremely long contexts (up to 3.6M tokens), PI-Mem applied to Qwen3.5-35B-A3B and Qwen2.5-7B yields +6.25 and +7.81 absolute accuracy gains over a recurrent-memory baseline. It also speeds up inference by 6.1× and 2.1×, respectively, demonstrating both better performance and efficiency at large context lengths.

Conclusion: PI-Mem effectively breaks the apparent trade-off between accuracy and efficiency in long-context reasoning. By parallelizing chunk processing and iteratively refining a compact shared memory, it scales to multi-million-token inputs and improves multi-hop QA performance while reducing inference time, offering a practical approach for complex reasoning over extremely long documents.

Abstract: Long-context reasoning remains a critical bottleneck for large language models, as recent recurrent-memory approaches face two inherent challenges: sequential chunk-wise updates can overwrite early critical evidence with later irrelevant content, and serial inter-chunk dependencies limit parallelism and cause latency to increase with context length. To address these issues, we propose PI-Mem (Parallel-Iterative Memory), a mechanism that processes all chunks in parallel and iteratively refines a shared memory over a bounded number of turns. In each turn, PI-Mem reads all chunks in parallel conditioned on the current memory, selects new or complementary evidence from each chunk, and merges the selected evidence into a compact shared memory for the next turn. To discourage redundant turns, we optimize the workflow through reinforcement learning with an auxiliary turn-efficiency reward, enabling the model to adaptively exit once sufficient evidence has been accumulated. We evaluate PI-Mem with Qwen3.5-35B-A3B and Qwen2.5-7B on the HotpotQA benchmark across context lengths up to 3.6 million tokens and find that it outperforms the recurrent-memory baseline by +6.25 and +7.81 absolute points while achieving 6.1$\times$ and 2.1$\times$ inference speedups, respectively. These results demonstrate that PI-Mem breaks the accuracy--efficiency trade-off in long-context reasoning and provides a scalable approach to complex multi-hop question answering over extremely long documents.

</details>


### [27] [SeqLLM: Augmenting LLMs with Behavioral-Sequence Modeling for High-Stakes Decisions at WeChat Pay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03063)
*Guilin Li,Jiaxing Zhang,Matthias Hwai Yong Tan,Bo Wang,Weiran Huang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SeqLLM augments pretrained LLMs with behavioral-sequence modeling for risk control and recommendation, greatly improving precision and recall while preserving language ability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In large payment platforms, accurately screening millions of merchants is difficult because it requires jointly understanding textual information and long, complex behavioral histories. Existing LLMs handle text well but cannot directly model long behavior sequences, and naive adaptations risk catastrophic forgetting of language abilities. There is a need for a method that lets LLMs use behavioral sequences effectively without losing their core language skills, and that can also generalize to recommendation tasks.

Method: SeqLLM extends a pretrained LLM using three key components: (1) it designs a compact discrete vocabulary that encodes behavioral events (e.g., actions, transactions) as tokens that can be fed to the LLM like normal words; (2) it adds a lightweight projector that maps these behavior tokens into the LLM’s embedding space, trained with a two-stage alignment curriculum so that behavior embeddings align semantically with language embeddings; and (3) it uses prefix-guided capability injection, i.e., supervised fine-tuning with task-specific prefixes, to teach the LLM sequence-modeling capabilities without doing continual pre-training, which mitigates catastrophic forgetting of language skills.

Result: Deployed at WeChat Pay, SeqLLM screens millions of merchants daily and improves precision from 92.0% to 97.5% compared to a strong DeepSeek-based LLM baseline. Its pretrained behavior-token embeddings also boost Precision@Top-0.01% by 26.8 percentage points in a large-scale fraud detection system processing billions of transactions. On public recommendation benchmarks (MovieLens, Amazon), SeqLLM achieves state-of-the-art performance, outperforming a strong User-LLM baseline by up to 32% relative Recall@5 while maintaining substantially better language performance. On the RecIF benchmark, it improves Pass@32 by 14.2% over the OneRec-8B pipeline while using only one-fifth of the GPU-days.

Conclusion: SeqLLM shows that behavioral sequences can be effectively integrated into pretrained LLMs via discrete behavior tokens, an aligned projector, and prefix-based fine-tuning, yielding strong sequence-modeling performance without sacrificing language ability. The framework scales to real-world industrial settings (WeChat Pay risk control and fraud detection) and attains state-of-the-art results on multiple public recommendation benchmarks with improved efficiency, suggesting a general recipe for augmenting LLMs with behavior modeling capabilities.

Abstract: Merchant risk control at large payment platforms screens tens of millions of merchants daily, where false positives harm legitimate merchants and false negatives leave harmful activity undetected. The hardest cases require jointly understanding a merchant's textual profile and long behavioral sequence. Large language models (LLMs) excel at text but cannot natively model such sequences, while adapting them often causes catastrophic forgetting. We present SeqLLM, a framework that adds behavioral-sequence modeling to a pretrained LLM while preserving its language ability. SeqLLM combines three components: a compact discrete vocabulary that represents behavioral events as native tokens; a lightweight projector, trained with a two-stage alignment curriculum, that grounds these tokens in the LLM's semantic space; and prefix-guided capability injection, which acquires sequence-modeling ability through task-prefixed supervised fine-tuning rather than continual pre-training. SeqLLM is deployed at WeChat Pay, screening millions of merchants daily. Against the production DeepSeek-based LLM baseline, it raises screening precision from 92.0% to 97.5%. Its pretrained behavior-token embeddings also improve Precision@Top-0.01% by 26.8 percentage points in a production fraud detector serving billion-scale transaction traffic. Beyond payments, SeqLLM achieves state-of-the-art results on public recommendation benchmarks. On MovieLens and Amazon, it surpasses the strong User-LLM baseline by up to 32% relative Recall@5 while retaining markedly stronger language ability. On RecIF, it improves Pass@32 by 14.2% over the full OneRec-8B pipeline using only one-fifth of its GPU-days.

</details>


### [28] [Activation-Guided Neuron Intervention to Induce Alzheimer's-Related Computational Language Phenotypes in a Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03067)
*Rui He,Ercong Nie,Hong Jiang,Iris E. Sommer,Philipp Homan,Wolfram Hinzen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces an activation-guided intervention framework to test whether LLM neurons associated with Alzheimer's disease (AD) language actually influence model behavior, showing that manipulating these neurons produces AD-like cognitive and linguistic changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although LLMs can detect Alzheimer's disease from spontaneous speech, it is unclear whether the internal representations they use are functionally related to cognitive deficits or are just correlational detectors. The authors aim to causally probe whether specific neurons linked to AD language patterns affect behavior across cognitive domains, and to move toward a computational phenotype of AD.

Method: Using Qwen3-8B, the authors identify feed-forward neurons that are more active on AD than control speech transcripts. During generation, they intervene on these neurons by scaling their down-projection weights, creating nine edited model variants that differ in the direction (amplify vs attenuate), magnitude, and scope of intervention. All model variants, including the original, are then run through a 12-turn neuropsychological test battery, and their outputs are evaluated with blinded human ratings and linguistic metrics (e.g., surprisal, idea density, syntactic complexity, discourse quantity).

Result: Amplifying AD-associated neurons induces graded impairments in multiple cognitive-like tasks, including story recall, verbal fluency, working memory, procedural discourse, scene construction, and coreference resolution. Attenuating these neurons mostly preserves performance and even improves some outcomes. Amplification also decreases lexical surprisal, idea density, syntactic complexity, and discourse quantity, in line with known alterations in human AD speech.

Conclusion: Neurons discovered purely from clinical language contrasts are not just diagnostic markers; they can causally shape the model's behavior across various cognitive domains. This supports the notion of an AD-related computational phenotype in LLMs and establishes a controlled intervention framework to experimentally study how language representations relate to broader cognitive dysfunction.

Abstract: Changes in spontaneous speech provide an early signal of cognitive dysfunction in Alzheimer's disease (AD) that large language models (LLMs) can detect. However, detection alone cannot establish whether the underlying model representations contribute functionally to behavior. We introduce an activation-guided intervention framework using Qwen3-8B. The framework identifies feed-forward neurons with higher activation rates for AD than control transcripts and modulates their output contributions during generation by scaling the corresponding down-projection weights. This yielded nine edited variants differing in intervention direction, magnitude, and scope. The original and edited models completed the same 12-turn neuropsychological battery, assessed through blinded human ratings and computational linguistic measures. Amplifying AD-associated neurons produced graded impairments in story recall, verbal fluency, working memory, procedural discourse, scene construction, and coreference resolution. Attenuation largely preserved performance and selectively improved several outcomes. Amplification also reduced lexical surprisal, idea density, syntactic complexity, and discourse quantity, broadly paralleling changes reported in human AD speech. These findings show that neurons identified solely from clinical language differences can influence behavior across multiple cognitive domains, providing proof of concept for an AD-related computational phenotype and a controlled framework for experimentally examining links between language and broader cognitive dysfunction.

</details>


### [29] [CVPO: Enhancing LLM Reinforcement Learning Reasoning via Value-Variance Adaptation and Dynamic Curriculum Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03068)
*Ziqi Jia,Yalu Ouyang,Bo Pang,Panpan Li,Hangfei Xu,Shengzhao Wen,Shiyong Li,Yanpeng Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes CVPO, a curriculum-guided value-variance policy optimization method that improves RL training for LLM reasoning by using trajectory value-variance for better exploration control and a dynamic difficulty curriculum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL methods for enhancing LLM reasoning give coarse or imprecise feedback at the trajectory level and suffer from problem difficulty drift, where the model trains on questions that are mismatched with its current ability. This leads to suboptimal exploration and unstable or inefficient learning in mathematical reasoning tasks.

Method: CVPO introduces two key ideas. (1) At the response-trajectory level, it analyzes token-level value-variance as a measure of exploration intensity and proves that this variance bounds the magnitude of policy updates. It estimates trajectory value-variance to characterize intrinsic randomness in generation and uses it to design a variance-aware advantage adjustment mechanism tailored to different reward types. (2) At the question level, it proposes a dynamic curriculum weighting scheme that automatically adjusts the sampling or weighting of questions by difficulty, so that at each training stage the model is exposed mainly to problems aligned with its current skill level.

Result: Across multiple math reasoning benchmarks, CVPO achieves better performance than strong value-based RL baselines such as VAPO. It yields stronger and better-calibrated exploration, leading to higher accuracy and more stable training behavior for LLMs on various math tasks.

Conclusion: Incorporating trajectory value-variance into policy optimization and combining it with a difficulty-aware curriculum can substantially improve RL-based training for LLM reasoning. CVPO provides more precise feedback, controls exploration via variance-aware advantages, mitigates difficulty drift, and thereby produces LLMs with more accurate and robust mathematical reasoning abilities.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as an effective method for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). However, existing methods suffer from insufficient precision in feedback on generated answer trajectories and exhibit the phenomenon of problem difficulty drift. To address these challenges, we propose CVPO - Curriculum-guided Value-Variance Policy Optimization. At the response trajectory level, we find that token-level value-variance correlates with exploration intensity. Our theoretical analysis shows this variance bounds policy update magnitude. We then use the estimated trajectory value-variance to quantify the intrinsic randomness in generation. Based on this, we design a variance-aware advantage adjustment mechanism for different reward types. At the question level, we introduce a dynamic curriculum weighting method that adapts to question difficulty. This helps the model focus on tasks matched to its current ability during each training stage. Experimental results show our method outperforms strong value-based baselines like VAPO. It achieves better performance and stronger exploration, enabling more accurate and robust reasoning in language models across various math tasks.

</details>


### [30] [PAMT: Process-Aligned Reinforcement Learning for Multi-Domain Machine Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03077)
*Yongshi Ye,Biao Fu,Chongxuan Huang,Yidong Chen,Xiaodong Shi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose PAMT, a training framework that aligns the intermediate reasoning steps of large reasoning models for multi-domain machine translation, using combined step-level and sequence-level rewards to improve domain-sensitive translation quality across domains and languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-domain MT needs not only fluent but also domain-sensitive translations (correct disambiguation, terminology, style). Large reasoning models with explicit step-by-step translation reasoning can help in hard and long texts, but they often fail in terminology-heavy and stylistically strict domains. Existing training only optimizes the final output or coarse reasoning traces and cannot tell which intermediate steps are actually helpful, creating a credit-assignment bottleneck. The authors want a way to align and train the explicit reasoning process itself so that each step contributes positively to the final translation.

Method: They introduce PAMT, a process-aligned training framework for MDMT. First, they provide cold-start domain-aware Long-CoT (long chain-of-thought) supervision to teach the model explicit multi-step translation reasoning that is sensitive to domain. Then they apply reinforcement learning with a combination of: (1) sequence-level format rewards to ensure outputs follow desired structure, (2) sequence-level outcome rewards to improve final translation quality, and (3) a step-level process reward that evaluates each intermediate reasoning step by how much it increases the likelihood of the reference translation, effectively assigning credit or blame to specific steps. This jointly optimizes both reasoning process and final translation outputs.

Result: Using two different backbone models, PAMT consistently improves over the base (non-PAMT) models, and on average outperforms machine-translation-specialized baselines. It also remains competitive with strong large language/reasoning models across a wide range of scenarios: multiple domains (15), in-domain and out-of-domain test conditions, different translation directions, and multilingual settings.

Conclusion: Aligning and training the explicit reasoning process in MDMT via PAMT mitigates the credit-assignment bottleneck of existing approaches. By combining domain-aware CoT supervision with both sequence-level and step-level RL rewards, PAMT yields better domain-sensitive translations and makes large reasoning models more robust and competitive for multi-domain, multilingual MT tasks.

Abstract: Multi-domain machine translation (MDMT) requires more than fluent generation: it demands domain-sensitive translation decisions such as domain disambiguation, terminology control, and stylistic adaptation. Large reasoning models (LRMs) make such decisions explicit through intermediate translation steps, but our analysis across 15 domains and four translation directions shows that this explicit reasoning is double-edged: it improves long-form and high-difficulty translation, yet often drifts in terminology-intensive and stylistically constrained settings. We trace this failure to a credit-assignment bottleneck: existing methods optimize final outputs or coarse trajectories, but cannot identify which translation steps actually help the final translation. To address this, we propose PAMT, a process-aligned training framework that combines cold-start domain-aware Long-CoT supervision with reinforcement learning. PAMT uses sequence-level format and outcome rewards for the final translation, together with a step-level process reward that measures how much each explicit translation step increases the likelihood of the reference translation. Across two backbones, PAMT improves over base models, outperforms MT-specialized baselines on average, and remains competitive with strong LLMs/LRMs across in-domain, OOD, and multilingual settings.

</details>


### [31] [Scalable Frequency- and Length-Aware Subdocument Deduplication for Large Language Model Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03089)
*Hai Wang,Chenhao Wang,Qifeng Cai,Yixiu Liu,Miao Peng,Nuo Chen,Yuanlin Tu,Chengcheng Xu,Feng Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They introduce a scalable framework for subdocument-level deduplication in pretraining corpora that separates detecting duplicates from deciding how many copies to keep, improving model performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large pretraining corpora contain many repeated spans of text below the document level. Existing document-level deduplication misses these, while current subdocument methods either miss cross-shard duplicates (suffix arrays per shard) or use rigid, non-adaptive rules for retaining duplicates (hash-based methods). This leads to inefficient use of data and suboptimal model performance.

Method: They design a scalable framework with two decoupled stages: (1) duplicate detection using natural-boundary segmentation of documents into chunks, normalized exact hashing of these segments, and distributed aggregation to form duplicate groups across the entire corpus; (2) copy-retention control via an explicit policy that accounts for both frequency and length of each duplicate group, assigning an adaptive copy budget that keeps more instances of rare or short duplicates and removes more instances of common or long duplicates.

Result: On benchmarks using FineWeb-Edu and a code-containing web corpus, language models trained on data processed with their framework outperform models trained on data deduplicated by competing settings. Their approach yields the best overall performance in the reported experiments.

Conclusion: Explicit, policy-based control over how many copies of each duplicate group are retained—separated from the detection step—is crucial for effective subdocument-level deduplication at scale. Their framework provides a scalable way to do this, leading to better downstream model performance and demonstrating that both duplicate frequency and span length should inform retention decisions.

Abstract: Large-scale pretraining corpora contain substantial duplicate content. Although document-level deduplication is widely used, removing subdocument-level redundancy remains challenging. At corpus scale, suffix-array-based methods are commonly applied independently within shards, leaving cross-shard duplicates undetected and making the resulting retention behavior sensitive to the sharding configuration. Hash-based methods enable global exact duplicate counting, but often rely on fixed copy-retention policies that cannot accommodate heterogeneous repetition patterns. We propose a scalable subdocument deduplication framework that decouples duplicate detection from copy retention. It identifies duplicate groups through natural-boundary segmentation, normalized exact hashing, and distributed aggregation, and then applies an explicit frequency- and length-aware retention policy that allocates an adaptive copy budget to each group, retaining more copies of low-frequency or short repetitions while more aggressively deleting high-frequency or long ones. Experiments on FineWeb-Edu and a code-containing web corpus show that models trained on data processed by our method achieve the best overall performance among the evaluated settings. These results underscore the importance of explicit copy-retention control.

</details>


### [32] [VIVID: A Culturally Grounded Benchmark Exposing the Figurative Language Gap in Vietnamese NLP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03095)
*Tu Tran Do,Nhat Ngoc Nguyen,Khanh-Tung Tran,Hoang D. Nguyen,Tu Minh Phuong,Long Hoang Dang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces VIVID, the first large-scale benchmark for Vietnamese idiom and proverb understanding, and shows current LLMs lack cultural and figurative competence on it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing NLP and LLM benchmarks focus mainly on literal, often English-centric language and ignore culturally grounded figurative expressions, especially in low-resource languages like Vietnamese. There is no systematic way to measure how well models understand Vietnamese idioms, proverbs, and culture-specific nuances, which are crucial for real-world communication and applications. The authors aim to fill this gap with a dedicated benchmark and evaluation framework.

Method: The authors construct VIVID, a benchmark of 1,636 Vietnamese idioms and proverbs, each annotated with five complexity traits (literalness, pragmatic nuances, Sino-Vietnamese terms, uncommon vocabulary, folk knowledge) and seven semantic themes. They design an evaluation framework that includes both generative and discriminative tasks, and they use an LLM-as-a-Judge setup with aspect-based prompting to score model outputs, validating this automatic judging method against human annotations (achieving Cohen’s kappa of 0.792). They then evaluate eight state-of-the-art models, including Vietnamese-specialized and multilingual LLMs, under zero- and few-shot settings.

Result: Vietnamese-specialized models perform significantly worse than strong multilingual models on VIVID (e.g., VinaLLaMA-7B scoring 0.13 vs. GPT-4o scoring 2.46 on their metric), and even the best systems achieve under 50% of the maximum possible score. Few-shot prompting is not consistently beneficial and can even hurt performance; GPT-4o, in particular, shows degraded scores due to stylistic overfitting. The analysis reveals recurring error patterns: models tend to interpret idioms literally, fail on rare or Sino-Vietnamese vocabulary, and miss pragmatic subtleties, indicating shallow cultural and figurative understanding.

Conclusion: VIVID is a novel, culturally grounded benchmark that exposes substantial weaknesses in current LLMs’ ability to understand Vietnamese idioms and proverbs. The work demonstrates that even leading models lack robust cultural competence and nuanced figurative interpretation in Vietnamese, and provides a validated evaluation framework and dataset that can drive future model development, training, and analysis for figurative language understanding in culturally rich, low-resource settings.

Abstract: We present VIVID (Vietnamese Idioms for Validation and Interpretation Depth), the first systematic benchmark for evaluating culturally grounded figurative language understanding in Vietnamese. VIVID comprises 1,636 idioms and proverbs annotated with five complexity traits (literal expressions, pragmatic nuances, Sino-Vietnamese terms, uncommon vocabulary, folk knowledge) and seven semantic themes. We establish an evaluation framework combining generative and discriminative tasks, proposing an LLM-as-a-Judge approach with aspect-based prompting validated against human judgment (Cohen's kappa = 0.792). Evaluating eight state-of-the-art models reveals critical gaps: Vietnamese-specialized models drastically underperform multilingual systems (VinaLLaMA-7B: 0.13 vs. GPT-4o: 2.46), and even top models achieve less than 50% of maximum scores. Notably, few-shot prompting does not universally improve performance, with GPT-4o exhibiting degradation due to stylistic overfitting. Our analysis exposes systematic failures including literal over-interpretation, lexical gaps, and pragmatic flattening, demonstrating that current models lack cultural competence for nuanced figurative interpretation. VIVID provides an essential tool for advancing figurative language understanding in culturally rich contexts.

</details>


### [33] [What Language Does and What the Evidence Supports: A Functional Role Taxonomy and Evidence Audit of Language Grounding in Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03099)
*Yifan Guo,Chenghao Li,Zhu Wang,Wei Xu,Yu Li,Yulong Zhu,Zhuo Sun,Bin Guo,Zhiwen Yu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Survey of how language is functionally used inside embodied agents with foundation models, and how well those uses are actually evidenced and grounded.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Foundation models are increasingly embedded into embodied agents (robots, interactive systems), but it is unclear what language inside these systems really does and how strongly its supposed roles are supported by empirical evidence. Prior work often treats architectures as the main comparison unit, making it hard to isolate language’s specific contribution to behavior and grounding.

Method: Conceptual survey and framework building: the authors define five non-exclusive functional roles that language can play in embodied agents—Specification, Embodied Representation, Action Orchestration, Grounding Regulation, and Execution Coupling. For each role, they analyze how linguistic information propagates to the embodied components and what empirical observations or experimental interventions could test whether language truly fulfills that role. They then re-interpret existing literature through this lens, evaluating each grounding claim at the level of individual role assignments rather than full architectures.

Result: The analysis shows a systematic mismatch between how language is claimed to be used and the actual evidence provided. Linguistic intermediates that are touted as interpretable or revised often turn out to be inaccurate, ignored by downstream modules, or have no measurable impact on behavior. In many systems, even when actions are directly conditioned on language, reported task success does not clearly demonstrate that language is performing the claimed functional role or that it is properly grounded.

Conclusion: Grounding should be evaluated at the level of specific functional claims about language rather than at the level of architectures or overall task success. By focusing on role-specific evidence—how language is consumed and what behavioral differences it makes—researchers can more accurately assess and compare modular and end-to-end embodied agents without overgeneralizing from limited or ambiguous results.

Abstract: Foundation models place language throughout embodied agents, but its presence does not show what it contributes or how well that contribution is grounded. This survey separates these two questions. We define five non-exclusive functional roles for language: Specification, Embodied Representation, Action Orchestration, Grounding Regulation, and Execution Coupling. For each role, we trace the path from linguistic content to its embodied consumer and identify the observations or interventions that can test the claimed responsibility. Applying this framework to the reviewed literature reveals a recurring gap between functional use and evidential support. Interpretable or revised linguistic intermediates may be incorrect, go unused, or fail to affect later behavior. Even when actions are directly conditioned on language, system-level success does not by itself isolate language's contribution. We therefore evaluate grounding claim by claim, asking whether the reported evidence supports the specific responsibility assigned to language. Using role claims rather than architectures as the unit of comparison allows us to compare modular and end-to-end embodied agents without extending conclusions beyond the reported evidence.

</details>


### [34] [HomoEnsNER: Does Language Alignment Outperform Architectural Complexity in Gujarati Named Entity Recognition?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03105)
*Chandrakant K. Bhogayata*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes HomoEnsNER, a homogeneous ensemble of GujaratiBERT models, and shows it outperforms heterogeneous ensembles and a single model for Gujarati NER under the same training budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gujarati NER is challenging and underexplored due to lack of capitalization, rich morphology, lexical ambiguity, and free word order. Existing ensemble approaches focus on mixing diverse architectures or multilingual encoders rather than exploiting multiple copies of a strong language-aligned monolingual model. The authors want to test if such a homogeneous, language-specific ensemble is actually better and more budget-efficient for low-resource, morphologically rich languages like Gujarati.

Method: They fine-tune five independent instances of GujaratiBERT on a Gujarati NER dataset and combine predictions using simple majority voting, forming the HomoEnsNER ensemble. They compare this to a single GujaratiBERT baseline and six heterogeneous ensemble/architectural variants that mix GujaratiBERT with MuRIL-base, MuRIL-large, IndicBERT, mBERT, BiLSTM, CRF, and a stacked BiLSTM-CRF-GujaratiBERT model. All models are trained with the same computational budget and evaluated using entity-level F1 on the Naamapadam Gujarati test split.

Result: HomoEnsNER obtains the best entity-level F1 score of 0.8442. This surpasses the single GujaratiBERT baseline (0.8347) and all six heterogeneous alternatives, whose worst performance is 0.7855. Thus, the homogeneous GujaratiBERT ensemble consistently yields the strongest NER performance under equal training conditions.

Conclusion: For low-resource, morphologically rich languages like Gujarati, ensembling multiple instances of a strong monolingual encoder is more effective and budget-conscious than building architecturally complex or multilingual heterogeneous ensembles. Language alignment in the encoder appears to matter more than architectural diversity for improving NER performance under constrained resources.

Abstract: Named Entity Recognition (NER) for Gujarati remains underexplored, hindered by the absence of capitalization cues, rich morphology, lexical ambiguity, and free word order. Prior ensemble work has emphasized architectural diversity by combining heterogeneous classifiers, multilingual encoders, or classical sequence models, rather than exploiting language-aligned monolingual pretraining. This study asks whether, for a low-resource, morphologically rich language like Gujarati, a homogeneous ensemble of a single monolingual encoder outperforms such architectural diversity. We propose HomoEnsNER, a homogeneous ensemble of five independently fine-tuned GujaratiBERT models combined via majority voting, evaluated against a single GujaratiBERT baseline and six heterogeneous alternatives, including combinations with MuRIL-base, MuRIL-large, IndicBERT, mBERT, BiLSTM, CRF, and a stacked BiLSTM-CRF-GujaratiBERT architecture. All eight models were trained under a consistent budget and evaluated using entity-level F1 on the Naamapadam Gujarati test split. HomoEnsNER achieved the highest F1 (0.8442), surpassing the baseline (0.8347) and every heterogeneous alternative (lowest: 0.7855), indicating that language alignment is a more effective, budget-conscious ensembling strategy than architectural complexity for low-resource Indian language NER.

</details>


### [35] [From SQL Errors to Concept Gaps: An AI-Powered Knowledge Graph Analytics Platform for Personalized Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03118)
*Abdulrahman AlRabah,Weijian Zhou,Xing Gao,Abdussalam Alawini*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents an AI-powered knowledge graph platform that links SQL errors in student work to underlying conceptual misunderstandings in database courses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Students often make semantic SQL errors that stem from deeper conceptual gaps rather than simple syntax mistakes. Existing autograding tools check correctness but fail to diagnose which course concepts students misunderstand. While educational knowledge graphs have been used for curriculum analysis and adaptive learning, they have not been applied to systematically diagnosing SQL misconceptions from large collections of student submissions.

Method: The authors build a platform that (1) automatically extracts database course concepts and their relationships from instructional materials, (2) stores these concepts and relations in a graph database as a knowledge graph, (3) links nodes in this graph to traces of student SQL submissions, and (4) classifies student errors at the level of specific concepts. They evaluate the platform in two database courses at two universities—one with real student submissions and one with simulated submissions—using both an expert study with five instructors and an automated evaluation using an LLM as a judge.

Result: In the expert evaluation, 95.7% of automatically extracted concept nodes were judged at least somewhat valid, and 63.8% of extracted relational triplets were rated fully correct. Experts reported that the knowledge graphs mirrored their own mental models of course structure and that the mappings from SQL errors to course concepts were useful for diagnosing student difficulties. The LLM-based evaluation supported these findings, suggesting the platform’s representations are largely accurate and meaningful.

Conclusion: The AI-powered knowledge graph platform can validly model course concepts and connect student SQL errors to specific conceptual misunderstandings, providing instructors with actionable diagnostic insight beyond traditional autograders. The generated graphs align well with instructor thinking, though the ultimate effect on student learning has yet to be empirically measured and is left for future work.

Abstract: This innovative practice full paper describes an AI-powered knowledge graph platform that connects SQL errors to conceptual gaps in undergraduate and graduate database systems courses. Students learning Structured Query Language (SQL) frequently struggle with semantic errors that reflect conceptual misunderstandings rather than syntax mistakes. A query may execute yet return incorrect results due to gaps spanning related concepts; misusing NATURAL JOIN in place of an explicit subquery reflects intertwined misunderstandings of JOIN, GROUP BY, and HAVING. Autograding systems detect correctness but provide surface-level feedback without connecting errors to the conceptual structure of the course. Educational knowledge graph research has shown the value of structured concept representations for curriculum analysis and adaptive learning, but these approaches have not been applied to diagnosing SQL misconceptions from student submissions. We present a platform that automatically extracts course concepts and relations from instructional materials, links them to student submission traces through a graph database, and classifies errors at the concept level. We evaluate the platform across two database systems courses at two universities, one using real student submissions and one using simulated submissions, through an expert study with five participants and an automated evaluation using an LLM as a judge. Results show that 95.7% of extracted nodes were rated as at least somewhat valid and 63.8% of triplets were rated fully correct. Expert feedback confirmed that the generated graphs align with instructor mental models and that mapping errors to course concepts provides actionable diagnostic insight; evaluating impact on student learning remains future work.

</details>


### [36] [Internalizing Academic Writing Workflows for Introduction Generation via Struct-Aware Policy Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03138)
*Meicong Zhang,Tiancheng Su,Jiahao Cheng,Guoxiu He,Xinqi Tao,Dejia Song*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes StructPO, a struct-aware policy learning framework that turns multi-stage LLM-based paper introduction writing into a single-pass process controlled via stage tokens, improving coherence, structure, and efficiency over workflow-based baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Writing rigorous academic introductions with LLMs is hard because it requires coordinating several components—background, gap, method, and contributions—into a coherent narrative. Existing methods handle this via multi-stage prompting or agent workflows, which are expensive, slow, and suffer from error propagation and inconsistency between stages. There is a need for an approach that keeps the multi-stage structure while avoiding cross-stage drift and inference inefficiency.

Method: The authors introduce StructPO, a struct-aware policy learning framework that embeds the whole multi-stage introduction-writing workflow into a single-pass policy guided by explicit stage tokens (e.g., tokens indicating background, gap, method, contribution). They design a struct-aware credit assignment mechanism that separately evaluates local quality of each stage while also considering global coherence. Additionally, they apply refinement-guided optimization so that revision behavior (normally done via iterative edits) is absorbed into the first-pass policy, allowing the model to generate a refined draft in one go.

Result: Experiments show that StructPO improves semantic alignment with the intended content, structural rationality of the introduction (i.e., better organization and logical flow), and inference efficiency (fewer calls, lower latency) compared to multi-stage workflow baselines. It generalizes well to out-of-domain settings and, when implemented with Qwen3-32B, achieves human-evaluated quality competitive with GPT-5.1 on this task.

Conclusion: Internalizing academic writing workflows into a single-pass, structure-aware policy via fine-grained policy optimization can match or surpass complex external orchestration pipelines, reducing cost and inference complexity while preserving or improving coherence, structure, and semantic alignment. StructPO represents a promising alternative to agent-style multi-stage prompting for structured generation tasks like paper introductions.

Abstract: Generating a rigorous paper introduction with large language models (LLMs) remains challenging, since it requires coordinating background, gap identification, method and contribution within a coherent narrative. Existing solutions externalize this process as multi-stage prompts or agent workflows which are expensive and vulnerable to cross-stage drift. We propose StructPO, a struct-aware policy learning framework that internalizes the entire multi-stage writing workflow into a single-pass policy controlled by explicit stage tokens. StructPO introduces struct-aware credit assignment to decouple local stage quality from global coherence and refinement-guided optimization to internalize revision behavior into the first-pass policy. Experiments show that StructPO improves semantic alignment, structural rationality and inference efficiency over workflow-based baselines, generalizes to out-of-domain settings, and remains competitive with GPT-5.1 in human evaluation when scaled to Qwen3-32B. These results show that internalizing academic writing workflows through fine-grained policy optimization offers a viable alternative to costly external orchestration.

</details>


### [37] [ANCHOR-RE: An Agentic Neuro-Symbolic Framework for Grounded Biomedical Relation Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03154)
*Shufan Ming,Yikun Han,Gibong Hong,Rui Zhang,Halil Kilicoglu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ANCHOR-RE is a neuro-symbolic framework that combines ontologies, external knowledge, and verification rules with LLMs to improve biomedical relation extraction without fine-tuning, achieving higher precision and F1 across multiple benchmarks and on newly published articles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biomedical relation extraction is crucial for building knowledge bases and supporting hypothesis generation from the rapidly growing biomedical literature. Existing symbolic systems like SemRep achieve high precision but miss many valid relations (low recall), while LLM-based methods capture richer context but produce many false positives. There is a need for a method that leverages the strengths of both symbolic and neural approaches to obtain reliable BioRE results without costly task-specific fine-tuning and while remaining robust to pretraining data contamination and dataset biases.

Method: The authors propose ANCHOR-RE, a neuro-symbolic framework layered on top of large language models. It integrates: (1) ontology-guided reasoning using biomedical ontologies to constrain and guide candidate relations; (2) external knowledge grounding to cross-check LLM predictions against existing structured resources; and (3) data-driven verification rules that filter or validate predicted relations based on patterns learned from data. The framework operates as an inference-time wrapper around both proprietary and open-weight LLMs, requiring no parameter updates. They evaluate ANCHOR-RE on three biomedical relation extraction benchmarks (SemRepGS, DDI, ChemProt) and conduct a temporal evaluation on 100 biomedical articles published after the LLMs' training cutoff (2026) to reduce pretraining contamination and assess generalization.

Result: Using a proprietary LLM backbone, ANCHOR-RE improves micro-F1 over direct prompting from 0.654 to 0.676 on SemRepGS, from 0.769 to 0.872 on DDI, and from 0.939 to 0.941 on ChemProt. On DDI and ChemProt, it outperforms prior inference-only approaches and approaches the performance of fine-tuned or instruction-tuned systems, all without updating model parameters. Similar improvements are observed when replacing the proprietary model with open-weight LLMs, indicating model-agnostic benefits. On a post-cutoff temporal test set of articles published in 2026, manual evaluation of 500 sampled predictions shows 69% precision, suggesting that the method maintains its reliability on unseen, contemporary biomedical literature.

Conclusion: Neuro-symbolic reasoning, implemented in ANCHOR-RE, can significantly enhance the reliability of LLM-based biomedical relation extraction at inference time, without any fine-tuning. By integrating ontological constraints, external knowledge grounding, and verification rules, the framework reduces false positives while preserving or improving recall across multiple benchmarks. Its strong and consistent performance across proprietary and open-weight LLMs, standard datasets, and temporally held-out literature demonstrates that ANCHOR-RE is a practical, training-free solution for large-scale biomedical literature mining and knowledge base construction.

Abstract: Biomedical relation extraction (BioRE) extracts structured knowledge from biomedical literature for applications such as knowledge base construction and hypothesis generation. Traditional symbolic systems such as SemRep provide high precision but limited recall, while large language models (LLMs) offer stronger contextual reasoning but remain prone to false-positive predictions. We developed ANCHOR-RE, a framework that integrates ontology-guided reasoning, external knowledge grounding, and data-driven verification rules into LLM inference. We evaluated it on three BioRE benchmarks (SemRepGS, DDI, and ChemProt) using both proprietary and open-weight LLMs. To assess generalizability beyond benchmark datasets while reducing potential evaluation bias from LLM pretraining contamination, we conducted a temporal evaluation using 100 biomedical articles published in 2026. With the proprietary backbone, ANCHOR-RE outperformed direct LLM prompting, improving micro-F1 from 0.654 to 0.676 on SemRepGS, from 0.769 to 0.872 on DDI, and from 0.939 to 0.941 on ChemProt. On DDI and ChemProt, it also outperformed previously reported inference-only methods and approached fine-tuned or instruction-tuned systems without parameter updates. Similar performance gains observed with open-weight LLMs indicate that the benefits were not limited to the proprietary backbone. On the post-cutoff set, manual assessment of 500 randomly sampled predictions yielded a precision of 69%, maintaining consistent precision on previously unseen biomedical literature. Neuro-symbolic reasoning can improve the reliability of LLM-based BioRE without fine-tuning. Results across multiple benchmarks, model families, and post-cutoff literature support ANCHOR-RE as a practical training-free approach to biomedical literature mining.

</details>


### [38] [Aligning Large Vision-Language Models at Test Time: A Trajectory-Guided Structured Sampling Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03204)
*Tianbao Jiang,Weicong Ni,Gerard de Melo,Linlin Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a test-time alignment method for large vision-language models that uses trajectory-guided structured sampling to refine reasoning during inference, improving visual grounding and logical consistency without expensive post-training RL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL-based alignment methods for large vision-language models are computationally expensive and often misaligned with the actual inference-time data distribution. There is a need for a more efficient way to align models with human intent and visual reasoning requirements, particularly for complex multimodal reasoning, that operates at inference time instead of through costly post-training.

Method: The authors construct a reasoning memory bank using a trajectory learning algorithm that decomposes complex questions into ordered sequences of predefined reasoning patterns (trajectories). At test time, they first sample trajectories from this memory bank to form a global structural prior over the reasoning process. Then they apply an iterative Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) procedure that locally refines the reasoning trace according to multiple objectives, such as visual grounding and logical consistency, effectively performing structured sampling for alignment during inference.

Result: Across several multimodal reasoning benchmarks, the proposed trajectory-guided test-time sampling method leads to notable accuracy gains compared with baseline LVLMs and traditional RL-based alignment approaches, while adding only modest inference overhead. The experiments show better visual grounding and more coherent reasoning without additional post-training.

Conclusion: Trajectory-guided structured sampling at test time can serve as a scalable, efficient alternative to post-training RL alignment for large vision-language models. By leveraging a learned memory bank of reasoning trajectories and MCMC-based refinement, the method achieves improved accuracy and alignment on complex visual reasoning tasks without prohibitive computational cost, indicating a promising direction for alignment via inference-time control rather than heavy training-time optimization.

Abstract: Post-training reinforcement learning (RL) algorithms are commonly used to align large vision-language models (LVLMs) with human intent and the requirements of visual reasoning tasks. However, existing RL-based alignment methods are often resource-intensive and encounter mismatches between training objectives and inference-time distributions. To bridge this gap, we propose a novel test-time alignment approach that leverages trajectory-guided structured sampling for dynamic inference-time refinement, achieving better alignment with visual grounding and ensuring logical consistency. Our approach begins with curating a reasoning memory bank via a trajectory learning algorithm, which decomposes complex question solving into ordered sequences of predefined reasoning patterns. It subsequently accomplishes inference-time alignment by first collecting trajectories from reasoning memory bank to establish a global structural reasoning prior, and then using an iterative Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) algorithm for localized multi-objective refinement of the reasoning trace. Experiments across multiple multimodal reasoning datasets demonstrate that our approach significantly improves accuracy without incurring prohibitive inference overhead. These results establish trajectory-guided test-time sampling as a scalable and effective alternative to traditional post-training alignment, particularly for complex visual reasoning tasks.

</details>


### [39] [ICO: Enhancing Semantic-Shift Jailbreaks via Iterative Context Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03210)
*Hujian Zhu,Yihao Huang,Felix Juefei-Xu,Xinfeng Li,Peng Zeng,Simeng Qin,Qing Guo,Geguang Pu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies why existing semantic-shift jailbreak attacks on foundation models are weak and proposes an optimized, context-aware black-box jailbreak framework (ICO) that significantly boosts attack success rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Foundation models are widely used but still vulnerable to adversarial attacks. A new jailbreak style—semantic-shift jailbreaks—tries to evade safety filters by swapping harmful terms for benign ones and relying on context to make the model map them back to harmful meanings. Yet, current methods often work poorly, and the reasons are not well understood. The authors want to analyze what makes these attacks succeed or fail and then use that insight to design more powerful black-box attacks.

Method: The authors first perform systematic empirical analysis of different contextual prompts to measure how well they can induce a model to reinterpret benign substitutes as harmful concepts (their semantic-shift capability). They show that contexts vary greatly in this capability. They then extract and formalize key properties of high-performing contexts. Using these properties, they design ICO (Iterative Context Optimization), a black-box framework that, in multiple rounds, queries the target model, gets feedback (e.g., whether the jailbreak worked or how the model responded), and modifies the context to strengthen its semantic-shift capability while keeping the surface tokens benign.

Result: Across three benchmark datasets and eight different foundation models, the proposed ICO framework consistently beats eight strong existing jailbreak baselines. It attains an average attack success rate of 74.6%, indicating a large improvement in the ability to bypass safety mechanisms using only black-box access.
