Conclusion: The key bottleneck of prior semantic-shift jailbreaks lies in ignoring how powerful the surrounding context can be in driving semantic reinterpretation. By characterizing and iteratively optimizing such contexts in a black-box manner, attacks become much more effective, substantially raising the bar for defending foundation models against semantic-shift jailbreaks. The work suggests that future safety efforts must explicitly account for and mitigate context-driven semantic shifts, not just filter harmful tokens directly.

Abstract: Foundation models have achieved remarkable success across diverse tasks, but they remain vulnerable. To investigate such vulnerabilities, semantic-shift jailbreaks have recently emerged as a promising attack paradigm. They bypass explicit safety mechanisms by replacing harmful terms in original harmful questions with benign alternatives and leveraging contextual information to induce the target model to reinterpret these alternatives as their corresponding harmful concepts. However, existing semantic-shift jailbreaks often achieve limited effectiveness. In this work, we reveal that this limitation arises from overlooking the semantic-shift capability of contexts. Through systematic analysis, we find that contexts exhibit substantially different abilities in inducing semantic shifts: contexts with stronger semantic-shift capabilities are more likely to guide models toward recovering harmful meanings and achieving successful jailbreaks. Based on this finding, we systematically identify and distill the characteristics of effective contexts and propose a black-box context-aware semantic-shift jailbreak framework with Iterative Context Optimization (ICO). In each iteration, ICO leverages these characteristics and feedback from the target model to optimize contexts. Extensive experiments on three datasets and eight target foundation models demonstrate that ICO consistently outperforms eight state-of-the-art baselines, achieving an average attack success rate of 74.6%.

</details>


### [40] [On the Diversity of Analogy Making in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03233)
*Yuanhao Shen,Daniel Xavier de Sousa,Caio César Sifuentes Barcelos,Hongyu Guo,Xiaodan Zhu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how diverse analogies generated by modern LLMs actually are and finds they are often drawn from narrow domains, with diversity–quality trade-offs and model-specific internal mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Analogy making is central to creativity and innovation, and LLMs are promising analogy generators. However, prior work has focused on whether LLMs can make analogies and how, not on how diverse those analogies are across domains. Diversity is crucial for broad, cross-domain scientific discovery, so the authors want to systematically measure and understand it.

Method: The authors benchmark ten state-of-the-art open- and closed-source LLMs on analogy-generation tasks, quantifying both inter-query (across prompts) and intra-model (within a model) diversity of analogy target domains. They test existing diversity-enhancement techniques and perform a causal analysis of internal information flow in the models to identify regions that control analogy diversity and how perturbing them affects outputs.

Result: They find strong domain homogeneity: models repeatedly draw analogies from a limited set of familiar domains, leading to low diversity both across prompts and within a given model. Attempts to increase diversity typically reduce analogy quality, revealing a clear diversity–quality trade-off. Causal analysis shows that analogy diversity is governed by model-sensitive internal regions that differ substantially across architectures, providing a plausible mechanism for the trade-off.

Conclusion: LLMs, despite being capable of analogy making, are biased toward a narrow set of analogy domains, which constrains creative and scientific use. There is an inherent-seeming trade-off between diversity and quality in current diversity-boosting techniques, likely rooted in model-specific internal information pathways. The work establishes one of the first systematic frameworks for studying output diversity in LLM-based analogy making and points toward the need for new methods that break the diversity–quality trade-off.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable potential for analogy making, a core cognitive capability that drives novelty and creativity. While prior research has extensively investigated the applications and underlying mechanisms of LLM-based analogy making, its output diversity remains largely unexplored, despite being essential for broadening cross-domain connections and fostering scientific innovation. In this work, we present a comprehensive evaluation of analogy diversity across ten state-of-the-art open- and closed-source LLMs. Our findings highlight a concerning issue of domain homogeneity, a prevalent tendency for LLMs to generate analogies from a narrow set of target domains, limiting both inter-query and intra-model diversity. Furthermore, our analysis reveals a fundamental trade-off in existing LLM diversity-enhancement methods: increasing output diversity often comes at the expense of output quality. Finally, our causal analysis of LLM information flow reveals substantial differences in the model-sensitive regions governing analogy diversity across LLMs, suggesting a potential mechanism for the observed diversity-quality trade-off. To our knowledge, this is among the first studies to systematically investigate output diversity in LLM-based analogy making.

</details>


### [41] [Relational Priors as Convergence Pressure in LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03239)
*Ming Shen,Chao Shang,Sadat Shahriar,Devang Kulshreshtha,Yi Zhang,Sandesh Swamy,Yanjun Qi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how explicitly specifying social relationships between agents in large language model multi-agent systems affects their behavior, finding that positive relational priors mainly increase coordination and agreement but do not reliably improve accuracy, especially in objective tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based multi-agent systems are commonly built with implicit social roles and interaction rules, but the impact of making inter-agent relationships explicit is not well understood. Since such relational framing could change how agents trust, challenge, or defer to each other, there is a need to rigorously examine whether these relational priors help or hurt performance, especially when correctness or safe governance is important.

Method: The authors formalize relational priors as signed networks over agents (positive, negative, neutral links) and translate these into natural-language prompts integrated into otherwise fixed multi-agent task protocols. They then run controlled experiments in two settings—commons-governance simulations and multi-agent debate for question answering—using different model backbones, relation types, and network topologies, and compare outcomes against baselines without explicit relational priors.

Result: Explicit relational priors act mainly as a convergence force: more positive relations lead agents to coordinate or agree more readily. This improves alignment in contexts where utility is tied to behavioral convergence (e.g., sustainable resource governance, subjective consensus), but does not consistently enhance factual accuracy. In objective QA debates, relational positivity can increase agreement even when correctness-conditioned agreement does not improve and can worsen in some configurations. Effects are sensitive to model choice, relation semantics, and network structure, and explicit neutrality behaves differently from having no relational framing at all.

Conclusion: Relational priors should not be treated as a standard, harmless enhancement for LLM-based multi-agent systems. Instead, they should be used sparingly and evaluated task-by-task: always benchmark against a no-prior baseline, track correctness-conditioned metrics when truth matters, and avoid including relational layers when empirical validation does not clearly support their benefit. Their most appropriate role is as a diagnostic tool rather than a default design element.

Abstract: Large language model-based multi-agent systems (LLM-MAS) are designed through roles, debate protocols, and aggregation rules. These choices create implicit social expectations: agents may be expected to trust, challenge, defer to, or collaborate with peers. We study the effects of making inter-agent relation semantics explicit. We use a minimal signed-network formulation of relational priors and inject natural-language renderings into agent system prompts while holding the task protocol fixed. Across a commons-governance simulation and multi-agent debate, relational priors primarily act as convergence pressure: increasing relational positivity tends to make agents coordinate or agree more readily. This pressure can help when utility rewards behavioral alignment, as in sustainable resource governance and subjective consensus. It does not, however, reliably improve accuracy. In objective QA debates, higher positivity can increase agreement even when correctness-conditioned agreement does not improve and may decline in some settings. Effects vary by model backbone, relation type, and topology; explicit neutrality is not equivalent to omitting relational framing. We argue that relational priors should not be a default add-on for LLM-MAS. Their safer use is diagnostic and task-specific: compare against a no-prior baseline, monitor correctness-conditioned metrics when truth matters, and omit the relational layer when validation does not justify it.

</details>


### [42] [MoEGen: Mixture-of-Experts for Instance-Adaptive LoRA Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03275)
*Yiming Zeng,Lei Lu,Zexin Li,Zhuochun Li,Shuoqiu Li,Shuyi Liao,Xidong Wu,Zeyu Zhang,Minmei Wang,Yu Zhao,Tingting Yu,Shangqian Gao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MoEGen is a MoE-based, parameter-efficient fine-tuning framework that replaces multiple stored LoRA experts with small expert codes and a hypernetwork that generates instance-specific low-rank updates, improving performance while keeping adapter storage small.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MoE-based PEFT methods increase capacity by storing many full LoRA experts, making adapter storage grow linearly with the number of experts and limiting flexibility to a fixed expert pool. The authors want to know if it is possible to get instance-specific, mixture-of-experts-style adaptations without having to store a separate LoRA module for each expert, thereby decoupling capacity from storage.

Method: They propose MoEGen, which shifts from selecting among pre-stored experts to generating parameters conditioned on experts. Each expert is represented as a small learnable vector (expert code) rather than a full LoRA adapter. For a given input, a routing mechanism assigns weights over these expert codes; the weighted combination of codes then conditions a lightweight hypernetwork that generates low-rank updates (similar to LoRA) for the backbone model. This yields input-specific adapter parameters on the fly, with expert capacity determined by the code space and hypernetwork rather than by stored full adapters.

Result: On eight commonsense reasoning benchmarks and three different backbone language models, MoEGen consistently outperforms strong PEFT baselines, including both static adapters and traditional MoE-based PEFT that store full LoRA experts. It also shows strong performance when adapting jointly to medical and legal domains, indicating its effectiveness in multi-domain settings.

Conclusion: MoEGen demonstrates that MoE-based PEFT can achieve instance-conditioned adaptations without explicitly storing a full adapter for each expert. By representing experts as compact codes and using a hypernetwork to generate low-rank updates, it decouples expert capacity from adapter storage, leading to improved performance and scalability across diverse tasks and domains.

Abstract: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) enables efficient adaptation of large language models, but existing MoE-based PEFT methods typically improve capacity by storing multiple full LoRA experts, causing adapter storage to grow linearly with the number of experts and restricting adaptation to a fixed expert pool. We ask whether MoE-based PEFT can produce instance-specific adaptations without explicitly storing a separate LoRA module for each expert. To address this gap, we propose MoEGen, an adaptation framework that shifts MoE-based PEFT from expert selection to expert-conditioned parameter generation. Instead of storing each expert as a full LoRA adapter, MoEGen represents each expert as a small learnable vector, termed an expert code. It routes each input over these vectors and uses their weighted combination to condition a lightweight hypernetwork that generates input-specific low-rank updates. This design decouples expert capacity from adapter storage while enabling instance-conditioned adaptation. Experiments on eight commonsense reasoning benchmarks show consistent improvements over strong static and MoE-based PEFT baselines across three backbones. MoEGen also performs strongly in joint medical and legal-domain adaptation.

</details>


### [43] [Benchmarking the Benchmarks: Testing the Predictive Validity of Commonsense Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03340)
*Ine Gevers,Walter Daelemans*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper examines whether standard commonsense benchmarks reliably predict large language models’ performance on real-world tasks requiring implicit reasoning, finding that their predictive power is limited and task-dependent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Commonsense benchmarks are widely used as proxies for real-world reasoning ability in LLMs, but it is unclear how well scores on these benchmarks translate to performance on practical downstream tasks. This gap makes it hard for practitioners and researchers to know whether benchmark improvements reflect meaningful gains in applied commonsense reasoning. The paper aims to systematically test the criterion validity of popular commonsense benchmarks as predictors of downstream capability across diverse model families.

Method: The authors evaluate 23 models from six different model families on: (1) four standard commonsense benchmarks, (2) four revised variants of these benchmarks, (3) three non-commonsense control benchmarks, and (4) eight downstream tasks involving implicit social, pragmatic, temporal, and physical reasoning. They then analyze model rankings across datasets, compute controlled correlations between benchmark scores and downstream performance, and apply leave-one-family-out cross-validation to test how well benchmark scores generalize as predictors of performance on unseen model families.

Result: Reworked versions of commonsense benchmarks preserve almost the same model rankings as the originals and do not significantly improve prediction of downstream performance. Commonsense benchmarks exhibit consistent, cross-family predictive validity only for a limited subset of downstream tasks. For other tasks, any predictive gains tend to be smaller, model-family-specific, or dependent on particular evaluation metrics. Overall correlations between commonsense benchmark performance and downstream reasoning ability are modest and far from uniformly strong.

Conclusion: Standardized commonsense benchmarks do not serve as broad, reliable indicators of real-world commonsense reasoning ability in LLMs. Their utility as predictors is strongly task-dependent and often narrow, providing robust guidance only for certain downstream tasks while failing to generalize across others or across model families. The paper implies that relying solely on such benchmarks to assess or compare LLMs can be misleading for many practical applications, and motivates the development or adoption of more targeted, task-aligned evaluations.

Abstract: Predicting LLM's capabilities on real-world tasks is essential, yet the extent to which performance on commonsense benchmarks predicts downstream performance remains underspecified. To establish the practical usability of widely adopted commonsense benchmarks, we evaluate 23 models from six families on four established commonsense benchmarks, four reworked variants, three non-commonsense controls, and eight downstream tasks requiring implicit social, pragmatic, temporal, or physical reasoning. We compare model rankings, compute controlled correlations, and use leave-one-family-out cross-validation to assess the criterion validity of commonsense benchmarks. Our results show that revised benchmarks largely preserve original model rankings and do not improve downstream predictive power. Commonsense benchmarks show consistent cross-family predictive validity for only a narrow subset of downstream tasks, with smaller or metric-specific gains elsewhere. Overall, standardized commonsense benchmarks provide task-dependent rather than broad evidence of downstream commonsense competence.

</details>


### [44] [ArtECulture: Benchmarking Culture-Conditioned Visual Emotion Understanding in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03358)
*Xiaolin Chen,Xuemeng Song,Wenhao Shi,Xianjing Han,Mong-Li Lee,Wynne Hsu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new culture-aware visual emotion understanding task, introduces the ArtECulture benchmark, shows current MLLMs perform poorly, and presents a retrieval-augmented framework that improves culture-conditioned emotion prediction and explanations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most visual emotion understanding models overlook how different cultures perceive emotions differently and existing datasets have inconsistent individual annotations and imbalanced cultural coverage, making it hard to derive reliable culture-level emotion labels and to fairly evaluate models across cultures.

Method: The authors define a culture-conditioned visual emotion understanding task and build ArtECulture, a balanced benchmark of 6,792 artworks annotated with culture-specific emotion labels and textual explanations for English, Chinese, and Arabic cultures. They evaluate 16 open- and closed-source MLLMs in a zero-shot setting, then propose a retrieval-augmented framework: a concept-based cultural emotion knowledge base is constructed and used to retrieve relevant culture-specific emotional concepts for an image, which are then fed into MLLMs to inject explicit cultural knowledge without additional training.

Result: Benchmarks show that existing MLLMs perform poorly on the new task, with the strongest model achieving under 50% accuracy, confirming the difficulty of culture-conditioned visual emotion understanding. The proposed retrieval-augmented framework yields performance gains in both accurate, culturally aligned emotion prediction and in generating better-grounded, culturally aware explanations, compared to vanilla MLLMs.

Conclusion: Culture-specific perception is crucial for visual emotion understanding, and current MLLMs are not yet capable of handling it well. ArtECulture provides a balanced, multi-culture benchmark to study this problem, and retrieval-based injection of cultural emotion knowledge is an effective, training-free way to improve both predictions and explanations. The resources are intended to support future research on culturally aware multimodal models.

Abstract: Existing visual emotion understanding methods typically ignore cultural variations in emotional perception. We introduce culture-conditioned visual emotion understanding, a task that predicts the culture-specific emotional perception of a given image and explains the underlying rationale. Although related benchmarks exist, they are limited by inconsistent individual annotations, which hinder the derivation of majority-supported culture-level emotion labels, and imbalanced cultural coverage. Thus, we present ArtECulture, a benchmark containing 6,792 artworks with culture-specific emotion labels and explanations across English, Chinese, and Arabic cultures, with balanced Western and non-Western content. Evaluations of 16 open- and closed-source Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) under a zero-shot setting reveal that the task remains challenging, with the best model achieving below 50\% accuracy. To address this limitation, we introduce a retrieval-augmented culture-conditioned emotion understanding framework, which leverages a concept-based cultural emotion knowledge base to inject explicit cultural knowledge into MLLMs without additional training. The framework improves both culturally aligned emotion prediction and grounded explanation generation. Our benchmark and code will be publicly released.

</details>


### [45] [FACTWASH: Catching AI Rewrites That Wash Hearsay into Fact](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03372)
*Alex Kwon*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces 'factwashing', a failure mode where AI rewrites preserve claims but strip away checkable context, and proposes 'factwash', a deterministic write-time gate to detect this using surface cues and minimal LLM help.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI systems transform inputs (conversations, documents) into stored memories or answers, they risk keeping factual claims while losing crucial meta-information like source, certainty, and time, which makes verification difficult. The authors aim to precisely define this failure mode, quantify it, and build a practical guardrail that can be run cheaply and deterministically instead of relying entirely on expensive, opaque LLM-based judges.

Method: They define factwashing as rewrites that drop cues of negation, hedging, or attribution. They construct deterministic detectors based on surface-cue inventories (word/phrase lists and patterns) to catch these cues at write-time. For properties with open-ended realizations (hedging, attribution), they add a minimal LLM 'witness' that only ever strengthens the detector’s conservativeness (can only lower a confidence/verdict, not raise it). They evaluate cue detection on 105,596 annotated sentences and apply it to a blind-labelled corpus of memory writes from systems like mem0 2.0.7, measuring where and how often failures occur in different domains (conversational hearsay vs business email).

Result: Negation cues are near-enumerable, allowing a purely deterministic vocabulary-based detector to achieve 0.91 F1 on unseen text. For hedging and attribution, deterministic vocabularies plateau around half recall; adding a one-question LLM witness boosts recall by 17 and 15 points respectively at the same precision. In deployment analysis, 55% of bad memory writes occur in conversational hearsay vs 7% in business email (p < 0.001), indicating domain-dependent risk. On unmodified mem0 2.0.7, the gate correctly flags 5 of 8 problematic hedged-hearsay memory writes.

Conclusion: Factwashing is a measurable, practically important failure mode in AI rewriting, especially in conversational memory systems. A deterministic, write-time gate based on surface cues can reliably detect negation loss and, with minimal and carefully-scoped LLM assistance, substantially improve detection of hedging and attribution failures while prioritizing precision. The key deployment question is first whether factwashing occurs in a given use case, and only then which combination of deterministic and LLM-based detectors to employ.

Abstract: AI systems rewrite information constantly: conversations become stored memories, documents become answers. The rewrite can keep a claim while washing away what made it checkable, who said it, how sure they were, when it held. We call that failure factwashing, and release factwash, an open-source write-time gate that catches it deterministically, with named flags and evidence rather than an LLM judge. Building it answers a practical question: when does a cheap check suffice, and when do you need a model? What decides is whether the property has a bounded surface-cue inventory. Explicit negation cues are close to enumerable, so a word list finishes and transfers, reaching 0.91 F1 on untuned text. Hedging and attribution have open-ended realizations, so vocabulary plateaus near half recall, and a one-question LLM witness recovers +17 and +15 points of cue-detection recall at equal precision. Deployed, that witness may only lower a verdict, so it buys precision rather than coverage. We measure cue detection on 105,596 independently annotated sentences. A blind-labelled corpus of memory writes then locates the failure: 55% of bad writes in conversational hearsay, 7% in business email (p < 0.001), so the first deployment question is not which detector to use but whether the failure occurs at all. On unmodified mem0 2.0.7, the gate flags 5 of 8 hedged-hearsay writes.

</details>


### [46] [Don't Let Me Ask for It: LLMs Show Deficiencies in Active Multi-Turn Information Acquisition for Abductive Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03388)
*Shahrukh Mohiuddin,Chalamalasetti Kranti,Sherzod Hakimov,David Schlangen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Alien Abduction game, an interactive benchmark to probe how LLMs perform abductive reasoning, focusing not just on correctness but on evidence acquisition, hypothesis updating, and stopping decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of abductive reasoning in LLMs mostly measure whether models arrive at the correct hypothesis, but they do not examine the underlying process: how models gather evidence, revise their beliefs, and know when to stop reasoning. Understanding these process-level behaviours is important for deploying LLMs in settings where stepwise reasoning and decision-making matter.

Method: The authors design the Alien Abduction game, an interactive reasoning task with multiple interaction modes that differ along two axes: (1) whether evidence is given all at once or spread over multiple turns, and (2) whether the model actively selects queries to obtain evidence or passively receives examples from an oracle. They then evaluate multiple LLMs in these different modes, tracking success rates, how models use available turns, and how their hypotheses evolve relative to the evidence observed.

Result: Providing all evidence upfront yields higher success rates across models than distributing evidence over turns. In multi-turn settings, some models prematurely commit to hypotheses before incorporating all available evidence, while others continue querying without converging on a final answer. Models perform better when the oracle supplies examples than when they must choose queries themselves, though in the latter case their final hypotheses tend to align more closely with the specific evidence they selected.

Conclusion: LLMs show systematic process-level weaknesses in abductive reasoning: they tend to form hypotheses that fit the particular evidence they have queried without adequately discriminating among alternative explanations, and they have difficulty validating and refining hypotheses or deciding when to terminate the reasoning process. These behaviours highlight gaps between surface-level success on abductive tasks and robust, disciplined reasoning over evidence across time.

Abstract: Abductive reasoning requires forming hypotheses that explain observed evidence and revising them as new evidence becomes available. While large language models (LLMs) are often evaluated on whether they solve abductive reasoning tasks correctly, less is known about how they acquire evidence, update their hypotheses, and decide when to stop. We introduce Alien Abduction game, an interactive probe for studying these behaviours under different interaction modes. The modes vary in whether evidence is provided upfront or across turns, and whether queries are selected by the model or examples are provided by the oracle. Across models, providing evidence upfront leads to higher success rates than distributing it across turns. In multi-turn settings, some models commit before using the available evidence, while others exhaust the turn budget without converging. Models also achieve higher success rates when examples are provided by the oracle than when they select their own queries, although their final hypotheses are more consistent with the evidence they selected. These findings suggest that models may form hypotheses that fit self-selected evidence without sufficiently distinguishing them from alternatives, and may struggle to validate and refine their hypotheses or determine when to stop.

</details>


### [47] [DUD: Decoupled Update Dynamics for Reliable Uncertainty Quantification in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03411)
*Yixin Bu,Runze Xia,Guanyun Zou,Yupeng Ji,Haodong Liu,Piji Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new mechanistic uncertainty quantification method for LLMs that decouples attention and FFN update dynamics, yielding better calibrated and more generalizable uncertainty estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing uncertainty quantification methods for LLMs rely mainly on probabilistic outputs or on mechanistic metrics that aggregate all residual stream updates. These approaches fail to distinguish between the different functional roles of attention (contextual processing) and feed-forward networks (parametric memory). As a result, they miss fine-grained conflicts such as misalignment between what the model has memorized and what the current context suggests, which are key indicators of epistemic uncertainty and model unfaithfulness. The paper aims to capture these subtle internal conflicts for more faithful and robust UQ.

Method: They introduce Decoupled Update Dynamics (DUD), a mechanistic UQ framework that explicitly separates the contributions of FFN and attention modules. Using noise-based causal interventions on the residual stream, they independently probe how well each module can restore or reconstruct internal states when perturbed. From these interventions, they compute module-specific restoration scores and combine them into a dual-stream dynamic profile that characterizes internal fragility, conflict, or alignment between memory (FFN) and context (attention). This decoupled profile is then used as a signal for uncertainty estimation and calibration.

Result: Across extensive experiments, DUD achieves higher accuracy in detecting uncertain or unreliable model predictions and yields better calibration metrics than state-of-the-art UQ baselines, including probability-based and other mechanistic methods. Furthermore, the DUD-based uncertainty signal transfers well across datasets and tasks, indicating strong cross-distribution generalization and robustness.

Conclusion: Decoupling attention and FFN dynamics through noise-induced causal interventions provides a powerful mechanistic lens on LLM internal states. The resulting dual-stream dynamic profile captures memory-context conflicts that correlate strongly with epistemic uncertainty. This leads to more faithful and better calibrated uncertainty estimates that generalize across datasets, suggesting that decoupled update dynamics are a robust proxy for model uncertainty and reliability.

Abstract: Accurate Uncertainty Quantification (UQ) is critical for reliable deployment of Large Language Models (LLMs), yet traditional probability-based metrics often fail to capture the model's true epistemic state. While recent mechanistic approaches leverage hidden state dynamics, they typically aggregate residual stream updates, conflating the distinct roles of parametric memory (Feed-Forward Networks) and contextual processing (Attention). We argue that this aggregation obscures fine-grained mechanistic conflicts, such as memory-context misalignment, that are fundamental indicators of uncertainty. To address this, we introduce \textbf{D}ecoupled \textbf{U}pdate \textbf{D}ynamics \textbf{(DUD)}, a framework that explicitly decouples FFN and Attention contributions via noise-induced causal interventions. By quantifying the independent restoration capabilities of each module, we construct a dual-stream dynamic profile that captures the model's internal fragility. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DUD significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in both uncertainty estimation and calibration, while exhibiting superior cross-dataset generalization, validating decoupled dynamics as a robust proxy for model faithfulness.

</details>


### [48] [Dynamically Allocating Evaluation Effort for Model Ranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03437)
*Vilém Zouhar,Julia Kreutzer,Alon Lavie,Tom Kocmi,Matt Post,Ondřej Bojar,Mrinmaya Sachan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes an adaptive, bandit-based framework to reduce the human evaluation cost needed to identify the best NLP models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human evaluation is the most reliable way to assess NLP systems, but it is very expensive and does not scale to many models and large benchmarks. Existing practice evaluates every model on all items, which wastes annotation effort when only the top models matter, such as in competitions or leaderboards. The authors want a principled way to identify the best model(s) with fewer human judgments.

Method: The authors cast multi-model human evaluation as a best-arm identification problem in a multi-armed bandit setting with correlated arms, where each arm is a model and pulling an arm is obtaining a human judgment for that model. They design adaptive sampling algorithms that use intermediate model rankings from the collected samples to decide which models to evaluate next, concentrating annotations on promising, competitive models. They provide theoretical analysis proving optimality properties of their algorithms.

Result: The proposed bandit-based evaluation algorithms are shown, both theoretically and empirically, to more efficiently distinguish top-performing models than standard exhaustive evaluation. For a given annotation budget they achieve better discrimination among strong models, and for a target level of discrimination they require fewer human annotations, making evaluations faster and cheaper.

Conclusion: Adaptive, bandit-style human evaluation can replace exhaustive human scoring in multi-model NLP comparisons when the main goal is to identify the best systems. By exploiting correlations and focusing annotation effort on competitive models, the approach reduces cost and improves the effectiveness of large-scale model evaluations and shared-task style competitions.

Abstract: While human evaluation is the gold standard in many NLP tasks, it suffers from prohibitive costs and poor scalability. When identifying top-performing models, typical evaluation protocols waste effort by exhaustively evaluating all models on the entire benchmark, a safe but inefficient approach. In this work, we formalize multi-model human evaluation as a best-arm identification problem in a multi-armed bandit setup with correlated arms, where pulling an arm corresponds to human-evaluating a model. By sampling adaptively based on the intermediate model rankings obtained on the samples so far, we can focus the annotation budget on the most competitive models. We prove the optimality of the proposed algorithms and show that it improves discrimination between top-performing models. This makes evaluations faster, cheaper and more aligned with large-scale competition evaluation goals.

</details>


### [49] [Predicting Multilingual Classification and Translation Performance of LLMs with Cross-Lingual Alignment $\unicode{x2013}$ Is English Enough?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03446)
*Adnan Al Ali,Kathy Hämmerl,Jindřich Libovický,Alexander Fraser*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes and compares many ways to measure how well multilingual LLMs align non-English languages with English, and shows that alignment with English predicts not only classification but also translation quality, suggesting English is an internal pivot language.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work shows that multilingual LLMs do better on non-English classification when those languages are well aligned with English inside the model, but there are many different cross-lingual alignment (CLA) scores and embedding extraction methods, and it is unclear which are best or how they relate to performance, especially for generative tasks like translation. There is also a lack of target-language-agnostic translation metrics for correlational studies across many languages.

Method: The authors systematically compare 27 variants of cross-lingual alignment scores for multilingual LLMs, using different ways of computing alignment between languages, and evaluate how well these scores correlate with downstream task performance on three classification tasks. They extend the analysis to translation by designing and using a PMI-based, target-language-light translation metric that correlates well with chrF, allowing cross-language correlation analyses. They then compare how alignment with English versus direct source-target alignment predicts translation quality.

Result: Across tasks, different CLA score variants differ in how strongly they correlate with downstream performance, and several are strong predictors. For translation, the proposed PMI-based metric strongly correlates with chrF, validating it as a proxy. Importantly, CLA scores measuring alignment between non-English languages and English predict translation performance as well as or better than CLA scores measuring direct source-target language alignment, across target languages.

Conclusion: Cross-lingual alignment quality, especially alignment with English, is a good predictor of multilingual LLM performance not only on classification but also on translation. The strong predictive power of English-centric CLA and the success of the PMI-based translation metric provide evidence that multilingual LLMs internally rely on English as a pivot language, and offer practical guidance on which alignment measures are most informative for evaluating and improving multilingual LLMs.

Abstract: Multilingual large language models (LLMs) have been shown to perform better on non-English classification tasks when the representations of the given language are more aligned to English within the model. Several cross-lingual alignment (CLA) scores have been proposed for use with LLMs, along with multiple approaches for extracting embeddings from the models. We provide a comparative analysis of 27 CLA score variants, examining how they differ and how well each predicts downstream performance across three tasks. Crucially, while LLMs are widely used for generative tasks such as machine translation, prior work has focused almost exclusively on classification. We therefore investigate whether CLA scores are similarly predictive of translation performance. To enable computing correlations across target languages, we propose a PMI-based translation metric, which is less dependent on the target language and correlates strongly with chrF. We find that CLA with English predicts translation quality comparably to or better than source-target CLA, providing new evidence that LLMs use English as an internal pivot language.

</details>


### [50] [Probing Character-level Transformers for the Spanish L-shaped Morphome](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03452)
*Akhilesh Kakolu Ramarao,Kevin Tang,Wiebke Petersen,Dinah Baer-Henney*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates what internal representations transformers learn when acquiring a highly irregular Spanish verb pattern (the L-shaped morphome) and finds they encode an item-specific, lexically stored morphomic class rather than generalizable rules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While prior work shows character-level transformers can output correct irregular forms like the Spanish L-shaped morphome, it remains unclear whether they internally represent abstract morphomic classes or merely surface alternation patterns. Understanding this helps clarify what kinds of linguistic generalizations neural models truly learn and whether they mirror human-like morphology learning and generalization.

Method: The authors study the Spanish L-shaped morphome, where certain verbs exhibit a stem alternation only in 1st person singular indicative and all subjunctive forms, with no predictive phonological, semantic, or syntactic cues. They train 12 models for each of five transformer-based architectures under lemma-disjoint cross-validation, ensuring no verb lemmas overlap between train and test. They employ probing classifiers on hidden states, with several control conditions and surface-pattern baselines, to test whether the models’ internal representations encode membership in the L-shaped class itself. They analyze where in the network this information is localized, especially around the stem-final consonant position in the decoder, and test cases where surface alternation is absent and where probes trained on alternating verbs must classify non-alternating ones.

Result: Across architectures, probes can decode L-shaped morphome class membership from model representations above all surface baselines, demonstrating that models learn an abstract class rather than just visible alternations. This encoded class persists even when verbs do not show overt stem alternation (all forms share the same stem). Probes trained only on alternating verbs can still correctly classify non-alternating verbs as class members. The morphomic information is localized around the time and position in the decoder where stem choice is determined—specifically, the stem-final consonant position in middle decoder layers. Moreover, variation across verb items is far more important than variation across architectures: which verbs the model sees strongly shapes the learned encoding, highlighting item-specific learning.

Conclusion: Transformers trained on Spanish verb morphology do not merely memorize surface alternations; they form internal, item-specific lexical abstractions corresponding to the L-shaped morphome class. These representations are localized to positions where stem selection occurs and are robust even without overt alternation. However, this knowledge appears item-bound rather than rule-like: it suffices to reproduce the irregular pattern for seen verbs but does not support human-like productive generalization of the morphome to new items.

Abstract: When a transformer learns an irregular morphological pattern, what has it learned? Our test case is the Spanish \emph{L-shaped morphome}, a complex irregular pattern in which the verb's stem alternates in exactly the first-person singular indicative and all subjunctive forms, and whose membership no phonological, semantic, or syntactic feature predicts. Prior studies have shown that character-level transformers can reproduce this pattern, but that evidence describes what models produce, not what they represent. Probing five architectures, twelve trained models each, under lemma-disjoint cross-validation with controls and surface baselines, we show that the models encode the L-shaped class itself, not just its visible alternations. It is decodable above every surface baseline, survives instances in which every form shows the same stem, and probes trained on alternating instances still classify non-alternating ones. The encoding is localized where the stem choice is made, at the stem-final consonant position of the middle decoder, before the alternant is read. And it is item-specific: which verbs a model learned matters far more than which architecture it is. The models store the morphome as an item-specific lexical abstraction, sufficient to reproduce the pattern but not to generalize it as humans do.

</details>


### [51] [Efficient Multilingual Neural Machine Translation via Corpus-Driven Vocabulary Pruning: An English-Arabic Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03480)
*Ahmed Amine Aliane,Nasredine Semmar,Hassina Aliane*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They shrink overly large vocabularies in multilingual NMT models and fine-tune them, cutting memory by ~60% while keeping or improving translation quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large pre-trained multilingual NMT models (e.g., M2M100, NLLB-200, mBART-50) have huge vocabularies and embedding layers, causing high memory and compute costs. Standard compression techniques (pruning, quantization, distillation) don’t alter the vocabulary structure, leaving a major inefficiency source untouched. There is a need for a method that directly optimizes the vocabulary for a specific language pair without sacrificing translation performance.

Method: They introduce a general optimization framework for multilingual NMT that (1) prunes the vocabulary aggressively to keep only tokens useful for the target language pair, and (2) applies a targeted fine-tuning protocol to adapt the pruned model and recover/boost performance. They test this on three large multilingual models (M2M100, NLLB-200, mBART-50) for English–Arabic translation, comparing against bilingual baselines. Details likely include criteria for vocabulary pruning and specific fine-tuning strategies, though not fully described in the abstract.

Result: Using their framework, they reduce the vocabulary size from over 128k tokens to about 10k, which yields around 60% memory savings. Despite this aggressive reduction, translation performance is maintained or improved compared with the original large multilingual models, and can match or surpass strong bilingual baselines on some metrics. For example, a pruned and fine-tuned M2M100 reaches BLEU 42.04 vs 44.59 for the OPUS-MTen-ar bilingual model, but on COMET it scores 0.8730 vs 0.7911, indicating better semantic adequacy and fluency.

Conclusion: Vocabulary structure is a major, previously underexploited source of inefficiency in multilingual NMT. By jointly applying vocabulary pruning and targeted fine-tuning, large multilingual models can be made much more memory-efficient without performance loss, and can even rival or exceed specialized bilingual systems on quality metrics. This suggests that properly optimized multilingual models can be both compact and high-performing for specific language pairs.

Abstract: The adoption of large pre-trained multilingual models for neural machine translation (MNMT) faces a major challenge: excessive memory and computational consumption due to overly large vocabularies and embedding layers. Although existing compression methods like pruning, quantization and knowledge distillation reduce parameter redundancy, they mainly preserve the structure of the original vocabulary, thereby leaving a major source of inefficiency unresolved. We propose in this paper a general optimization framework that combines a vocabulary pruning method with a targeted fine-tuning protocol for MNMT models. We evaluate the proposed framework using three models (M2M100, NLLB-200, mBART-50) on the English-Arabic language pair. Our approach reduces the vocabulary size from over 128,000 to approximately 10,000 tokens, enabling a 60% memory saving without any loss in performance. Results show that optimized multilingual models can match or exceed the performance of dedicated bilingual baselines. In particular, the pruned and fine-tuned M2M100 model achieves a competitive BLEU score of 42.04 (against 44.59 for the OPUS-MTen- ar bilingual model) while it significantly outperforms it on the COMET metric (0.8730 vs 0.7911) revealing superior semantic adequacy and fluency.

</details>


### [52] [Beyond Initialization Loss: A Systematic Study of Token Embedding Initialization Strategies for LLM Vocabulary Extension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03494)
*Raviraj Joshi,Utkarsh Vaidya,Sanjay Singh Chauhan,Niranjan Wartikar*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper systematically evaluates over 20 ways to initialize new token embeddings when extending an LLM’s vocabulary to Hindi, finding that carefully designed subword-composition-based initialization dramatically speeds up continued pretraining and that quick, lightweight training is a better way to pick strategies than static metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adapting large pretrained language models to new languages often requires adding many new tokens, but how these new token embeddings are initialized heavily influences training cost and final performance. Existing practice relies on simple heuristics (e.g., mean of existing embeddings) or opaque external methods, with little systematic comparison or understanding of which strategies work best and why, especially for large models and low-resource languages like Hindi. The paper aims to identify initialization strategies that yield faster, more stable continued pretraining and better downstream accuracy, while also clarifying which early metrics actually predict long-run convergence.

Method: Using Nemotron-3-Nano-30B-A3B as a base model, the authors extend its vocabulary to include Hindi and systematically compare 20+ initialization strategies for the newly added token embeddings. These strategies include: (1) simple vocabulary-averaging baselines (e.g., mean of all embeddings); (2) external or learned approaches such as FOCUS, top-k semantic retrieval, and residual MLP mappings that map from existing to new token embeddings; (3) subword composition schemes that construct new token embeddings from their constituent subword embeddings, including symmetric and asymmetric variants for input versus output embeddings; (4) norm calibration techniques tailored to Hindi; and (5) experiments on input-output asymmetry where embedding and LM head initializations are decoupled. They measure early validation loss, continued pretraining steps needed to reach certain performance, MILU-Hindi benchmark accuracy, and compare these to simple proxies like initialization loss and bits-per-byte, while also testing very short CPT runs (as few as 50 steps) as a selection signal.

Result: Subword composition consistently outperforms both simple averaging baselines and more complex external/learned initialization strategies in terms of early validation loss and downstream performance. Asymmetric subword composition, where input and output embeddings are initialized differently, performs best and shows that the optimal strategies for input and output embeddings are not the same. The best-performing configuration initializes the input embedding matrix using uniform subword averaging combined with a Hindi-specific norm calibration, and initializes the output LM head using character-length-weighted subword averaging. Compared to the common Mean-all baseline, this full initialization pipeline reaches comparable validation loss with more than a 6× reduction in required CPT steps, and surpasses the baseline’s MILU-Hindi accuracy at 3,500 steps after only 500 steps. The study further finds that static metrics like initialization loss and Init BPB do not reliably predict which initialization will converge best, whereas doing a very short, lightweight CPT run (around 50 steps) provides a much more reliable signal for selecting among initialization strategies at low cost.

Conclusion: For extending LLM vocabularies to new languages such as Hindi, subword-composition-based initialization, especially with asymmetric treatment of input and output embeddings plus language-specific norm calibration, offers a substantial efficiency gain in continued pretraining, reducing the training steps needed by more than a factor of six without sacrificing performance. The work also cautions against relying on static initialization metrics like Init BPB as proxies for eventual convergence and instead advocates for brief, low-cost CPT trials to empirically choose the best initialization scheme. These insights provide a practical recipe and evaluation protocol for practitioners who need to efficiently adapt large models to new token vocabularies in multilingual or domain adaptation settings.

Abstract: Vocabulary extension is an efficient way to adapt pretrained large language models (LLMs) to new languages, but the initialization of newly added token embeddings can strongly affect continued pre-training (CPT) efficiency. We present a systematic study of more than 20 initialization strategies for Hindi vocabulary extension in Nemotron-3-Nano-30B-A3B. Our comparison spans vocabulary-averaging baselines; external and learned initialization methods, including FOCUS, top-k semantic retrieval, and residual MLP mappings; subword composition; norm calibration; and input-output asymmetry. We find that subword composition methods outperform both vocabulary averaging and external/learned initialization approaches. Within subword composition, asymmetric variants achieve the lowest observed early validation loss and reveal distinct preferences for input and output embedding initialization. The best observed configuration initializes the input embedding matrix with uniform subword averaging and Hindi-specific norm calibration, and the output language modeling head with character-length-weighted subword averaging. Relative to the standard Mean-all baseline, this full initialization pipeline reaches comparable validation loss with over a 6x reduction in CPT steps and exceeds the baseline's 3,500-step MILU-Hindi accuracy after only 500 steps. Finally, we show that initialization loss and initialization bits-per-byte (Init BPB) are unreliable predictors of downstream convergence, whereas lightweight CPT, as few as 50 steps, provides a cost-effective and reliable signal for selecting the best initialization strategy.

</details>


### [53] [ConlangBench: Exploring Language Knowledge and Learning in LLMs through Diverse Constructed Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03505)
*Jinhong Jeong,Seungyeop Yi,Sangah Lee,Youngjae Yu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces ConlangBench, a large-scale benchmark of constructed languages for training and evaluating LLMs, showing how different conlang design types affect learnability and translation performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Constructed languages are rich, systematically designed linguistic systems that are underused in LLM research, yet they offer a controlled way to study how models learn low-resource and artificial languages. The paper aims to fill this gap by providing a large-scale, multi-conlang benchmark for systematic investigation.

Method: The authors compile a large parallel corpus of 21 conlangs paired with English, yielding over 21M sentence pairs and hundreds of thousands of vocabulary entries. They categorize conlangs (e.g., a posteriori vs others), run bidirectional translation experiments with LLMs on all 21 conlangs, and conduct focused training experiments on eight conlangs with sufficient data to study learning dynamics and performance differences based on conlang design properties.

Result: Models achieve higher translation performance on a posteriori conlangs whose vocabularies are derived from natural languages. When trained on ConlangBench, LLMs successfully learn to translate all eight data-rich conlangs, but their learning curves and difficulty vary, aligning with differences in how each conlang was constructed and how similar they are to natural languages.

Conclusion: Conlangs, especially as organized in ConlangBench, constitute a powerful and underutilized testbed for probing how LLMs acquire low-resource and artificial languages. Design properties of conlangs, such as being a posteriori or not, significantly affect model learnability and performance, suggesting that controlled language design can be used to better understand and possibly improve LLM language acquisition.

Abstract: Constructed languages (conlangs) are intentionally created human languages with a rich tradition of linguistic creativity. Despite their potential for studying language learning in large language models (LLMs), existing conlangs remain largely underexplored in LLM research. We present ConlangBench, the first large-scale benchmark for evaluating and training LLMs on 21 existing conlangs. We collect over 21M conlang-English parallel sentence pairs (including 430K pairs across the 20 non-Esperanto conlangs) and 321K vocabulary entries. In bidirectional translation experiments, we find that models perform better on a posteriori conlangs, whose vocabularies are derived from natural languages, reflecting the design characteristics of conlangs. Training on ConlangBench also shows that models can learn all eight conlangs for which sufficient parallel corpora are available, while their learning curves vary depending on how the conlangs were created. Our findings suggest that conlangs provide a unique testbed for investigating how LLMs acquire low-resource languages.

</details>


### [54] [ChronoLens: Measuring Language Change Across Time, Languages, and Linguistic Levels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03507)
*Gagan Bhatia,Julian Schlenker,Simone Paolo Ponzetto,Steffen Eger*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces ChronoLens, a unified framework to quantify and compare historical language change across morphology, syntax, semantics, and pragmatics, showing they change at similar magnitudes within languages but along different trajectories across languages and time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing computational studies of historical language change focus on individual linguistic levels (e.g., semantics) using incompatible representations, making it impossible to assess whether and how different levels co-evolve within and across languages. The authors aim to build a single analytical space that captures multiple linguistic levels in a comparable way, so they can study the magnitude and direction of change jointly over long historical periods and across languages.

Method: They propose ChronoLens, which uses frozen multilingual language models as a base representation, then learns feature-aligned crosscoders to map model activations into a sparse, interpretable space that aligns with linguistic features. They further apply post-hoc linguistic interventions to disentangle morphology, syntax, semantics, and pragmatics within this space. ChronoLens is applied to a large diachronic corpus of ~45M parliamentary documents (~17.2B tokens) from five parliamentary traditions covering 1803–2026, and its sparse representations are evaluated against linguistic statistics and compared to dense embeddings and a pooled sparse autoencoder.

Result: The sparse ChronoLens representations correlate much more strongly with independently derived linguistic statistics than both standard dense embeddings and a pooled sparse autoencoder (Spearman ρ≈0.72 vs 0.29 and 0.28). Analyses across the diachronic corpora show that within a given language, morphology, syntax, semantics, and pragmatics tend to change by similar overall magnitudes over time. However, different languages display substantial variation in when changes occur, how extensive they are, and in which directions they move within the shared representational space.

Conclusion: Historical language change emerges as a structured, multidimensional phenomenon that cannot be characterized by magnitude alone. Even when different linguistic levels or languages exhibit similar amounts of change, their trajectories and directions in representational space can differ substantially. Therefore, meaningful cross-linguistic comparison of diachronic change requires jointly modeling both distance and direction of change across multiple linguistic levels within a unified analytical framework such as ChronoLens.

Abstract: Historical language change affects morphology, syntax, semantics, and pragmatics, yet computational studies typically examine these levels with incompatible representations and therefore cannot determine whether they evolve together across languages. We address this problem by asking how the magnitude and direction of change vary across linguistic levels, languages, and historical periods within a single analytical space. We introduce ChronoLens, a framework that combines frozen multilingual language models, feature-aligned crosscoders, and post-hoc linguistic interventions, and apply it to 44.98 million documents and approximately 17.2 billion tokens from five parliamentary traditions spanning 1803--2026. The resulting sparse representations agree substantially more strongly with linguistic statistics than dense embeddings or a pooled sparse autoencoder ($ρ=0.72$ versus $0.29$ and $0.28$), and reveal that morphology, syntax, semantics, and pragmatics generally change by comparable amounts within a language, while languages differ markedly in when, how far, and in which direction they change. These findings show that historical language change is a structured, multidimensional process: similar magnitudes can conceal different trajectories, and meaningful cross-linguistic comparison requires measuring both distance and direction.

</details>


### [55] [Consensus Measures for Unstructured Biomedical Text Annotations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03529)
*Pascal Wullschleger,Christian Kreis,Martin A. Walter,Marc Pouly,Jennifer Foster*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates how to quantify agreement between annotators who provide free-text (open-ended) labels in biomedical literature mining, comparing different semantic similarity measures and proposing natural language inference–based metrics as a practical compromise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In biomedical text mining, researchers increasingly repurpose literature to extract new knowledge, often for tasks not envisioned by the original authors. Since the exact target concepts are not known beforehand, annotation schemes prefer open-ended, free-text labels rather than fixed label sets. However, traditional inter-rater reliability metrics assume predefined, discrete labels and therefore do not work well for these unstructured annotations. There is a need for methods that can quantify how consistently annotators agree when labels are expressed as natural language text.

Method: The authors study "soft" inter-rater reliability for annotators who supply unstructured textual labels. They conduct synthetic experiments to evaluate different ways of computing semantic equivalence between annotator texts, including embedding-based similarity metrics and large language model (LLM)–based assessments. They also explore using natural language inference (NLI) models as a basis for measuring agreement, and analyze how each approach behaves, including its failure modes and scalability characteristics.

Result: The experiments show that soft inter-rater reliability can indeed be quantified using multiple semantic equivalence measures. However, each type of measure has distinct behavior: embedding-based methods scale well but struggle to distinguish closely related yet distinct concepts, while LLM-based methods provide more nuanced judgments but are computationally expensive and problematic for estimating agreement expected by chance. NLI-based measures emerge as a balanced option that addresses some of these trade-offs.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that it is feasible to measure inter-rater reliability for open-ended, free-text annotations in biomedical literature using soft, semantics-based metrics. While embeddings and LLMs each have limitations—either in conceptual discrimination or scalability—NLI-based measures offer a practical compromise between reliability, semantic sensitivity, and computational cost. The authors recommend NLI-style metrics as a sensible approach for future biomedical annotation efforts involving unstructured textual labels.

Abstract: Biomedical literature is increasingly mined for knowledge beyond the questions it was written to answer. Because the target concepts are not known in advance, annotators prefer open-ended labels, whose agreement is hard to quantify. We study soft inter-rater reliability for annotators providing unstructured texts for biomedical annotation tasks. Synthetic experiments show that soft reliability can be quantified using a variety of semantic equivalence measures, and that the choice of measure affects failure modes of the estimation. Embeddings are scalable, but limited when differentiating similar but distinct concepts. Large language models are promising, but limited by scalability for estimating agreement by chance. Finally, we suggest measures based on natural language inference as a sensible compromise.

</details>


### [56] [Cross-Lingual Bias in Large Language Models: A Comparative Analysis of English and Swahili](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03532)
*Ruolei Zhang,Teddy Njuguna,Yue Feng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies whether social biases in large language models behave the same way across languages (English and Swahili) and finds that biases change form rather than directly transfer, showing English-only safety checks are insufficient.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most safety alignment and bias evaluations for large language models focus on English, even though these models are used in many languages. The authors want to know if social biases and safety behaviors observed in English hold in another language, questioning the assumption that English-based audits generalize to multilingual use.

Method: They create 4,900 symmetric prompt pairs in English and Swahili that target nine demographic bias dimensions. These pairs are submitted to two LLMs (GPT-5.2 and Gemini 2.5 Flash), producing 19,600 responses. The authors then automatically and/or manually assess the outputs for four properties: presence of stereotypes, sentiment polarity, refusal or safety-triggered behavior, and semantic similarity between English and Swahili completions for the same prompt pair.

Result: Bias patterns differ significantly between languages. Stereotype frequencies change by up to 12 percentage points along certain demographic axes when switching from English to Swahili. Gemini shows a much higher rate of neutral sentiment in Swahili compared with English. GPT-5.2 refuses many prompts in English (169) but none in Swahili, indicating language-dependent safety triggering. More than 55% of English–Swahili prompt pairs yield semantically dissimilar answers, even though the prompts are symmetric.

Conclusion: Social biases in LLMs do not simply carry over from English to other languages; instead, their expression and the models' safety behaviors transform across languages. Consequently, conducting bias and safety audits only in English is inadequate for models deployed in multilingual settings, and language-specific evaluations are necessary for robust safety alignment.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly deployed in multilingual contexts, yet safety alignment and bias evaluation remain overwhelmingly English-centric. We investigate whether social biases generalise across languages by submitting 4,900 symmetric English--Swahili prompt pairs to GPT-5.2 and Gemini 2.5 Flash across nine demographic bias axes, yielding 19,600 completions evaluated for stereotype prevalence, sentiment, refusal behaviour, and cross-lingual semantic similarity. Our findings show that bias transforms rather than transfers: stereotype rates shifted by up to 12 percentage points on specific axes, Gemini's neutral-sentiment rate doubled in Swahili, and GPT-5.2 refused 169 prompts in English and zero in Swahili, consistent with refusal behaviour anchored to English-language surface forms at the behavioural level. Over 55% of prompt pairs produced semantically dissimilar completions across both models. These reinforce the idea that English-only bias audits do not produce adequate coverage for multilingual deployment.

</details>


### [57] [Hi-TTRL: Regulating Consensus with Hints for Test-Time Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03545)
*Kunbin Xu,Xingzuo Li,Xuefeng Bai,Kehai Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes Hi-TTRL, a test-time reinforcement learning method that stabilizes and improves majority-vote–based TTRL by adaptively steering the consensus strength of rollouts using MCMC-sampled hints, leading to better reasoning performance of LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing test-time reinforcement learning (TTRL) improves LLM reasoning using pseudo-labels obtained via majority voting, but the reward signal is highly sensitive to how strong the consensus is in a rollout group. Low consensus can over-amplify noisy pseudo-labels, and very high consensus can collapse reward contrast and cause vanishing gradients. There is a need for a mechanism that controls or regularizes consensus strength so that pseudo-labels remain both reliable and informative for learning.

Method: The authors introduce Hi-TTRL, which augments TTRL with an adaptive, hint-guided mechanism to regulate consensus strength during sampling. For each test instance, the method first forms a partial rollout group and estimates the current consensus strength (the frequency of the most common answer). If this value lies outside a target interval, Hi-TTRL invokes a Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) hint sampler. This sampler operates on a power-transformed prefix distribution and performs finite-step approximate sampling to generate rollout prefixes that serve as hints to the policy. By adjusting the power exponent of the target distribution, the method sharpens or flattens the sampling distribution, thereby encouraging higher or lower agreement among rollouts. This adaptive hint injection aims to steer consensus strength into the desired range before computing pseudo-labels and advantages for policy updates.

Result: Across multiple datasets and model backbones, Hi-TTRL consistently outperforms standard TTRL in reasoning tasks. The authors conduct ablation studies to isolate the contribution of different components and perform analyses showing that the method effectively steers consensus strength into the target range. These experiments support that adaptive hint-guided consensus regulation both stabilizes training signals and yields better performance.

Conclusion: Hi-TTRL demonstrates that explicitly regulating rollout consensus strength is an effective way to address the instability of majority-vote–based pseudo-labeling in test-time RL for LLMs. By using MCMC-sampled hints to adaptively shape the rollout distribution, the framework mitigates the issues of unreliable updates under low consensus and vanishing gradients under high consensus. The approach yields consistent gains over vanilla TTRL, suggesting that consensus-aware control is a valuable design principle for future test-time learning methods for large language models.

Abstract: Test-time reinforcement learning (TTRL) improves the reasoning capabilities of large language models without labeled data by updating the policy with pseudo-labels constructed through majority voting. While effective, the reward signal assigned from majority voting is highly sensitive to consensus strength, defined as the frequency of the most common answer within a rollout group. In TTRL, consensus strength plays a dual role: it reflects both the reliability of the pseudo-label and the distribution of advantages. Low consensus can amplify updates from unreliable pseudo-labels through disproportionately large advantages, whereas high consensus reduces reward contrast and ultimately yields vanishing gradients. In this paper, we introduce Hi-TTRL, a test-time reinforcement learning framework that utilizes hints during sampling to regulate rollout consensus strength. Hi-TTRL first estimates consensus strength from a partial rollout group. When the consensus strength falls outside a target interval, it invokes a Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) hint sampler. The sampler targets the power-transformed prefix distribution and uses finite-step approximate sampling to generate rollout prefixes as hints. By tuning the power exponent, Hi-TTRL generates hints with a sharpened or flattened power target, steering rollout consensus strength toward the target interval. Experiments on multiple datasets and backbones show that Hi-TTRL consistently improves over standard TTRL, with ablations and consensus-steering analyses validating the effectiveness of adaptive hint-guided consensus regulation.

</details>


### [58] [SFT Conflicts, RL Coexists: A Theoretical and Empirical Analysis of Multi-Task Learning for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03573)
*Kejian Zhu,Zhuoran Jin,Shangqing Tu,Hongbang Yuan,Yushi Bai,Kang Liu,Juanzi Li,Jun Zhao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper compares Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Reinforcement Learning (RL) for multi-task reasoning in LLMs, finds that RL yields sparse, near-orthogonal task updates and more stable multi-task coexistence, explains this via gradient interference theory, and introduces a Parallel-RL paradigm to exploit this property.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enhancing multi-task reasoning in large language models is challenging because standard Supervised Fine-Tuning often causes task conflicts, especially in multi-stage training where learning new tasks can degrade performance on existing ones. The authors observed empirically that RL-based training avoids many of these conflicts, motivating a deeper investigation into why RL supports more stable multi-task behavior and how to systematically leverage this advantage for more efficient and flexible training.

Method: The authors run preliminary experiments comparing SFT and RL for multi-task training, then analyze the resulting parameter updates at the task level, focusing on sparsity and orthogonality. They develop a theoretical framework for multi-task gradient interference, contrasting how SFT and RL behave. Specifically, they study how interference scales in SFT with gradient norms versus in RL with gradient variance, accounting for advantage normalization and on-policy optimization. Based on these insights, they design Parallel-RL, a training paradigm that explicitly decouples multi-task optimization while leveraging RL's variance-limited interference to maintain stable, near-orthogonal updates across tasks.

Result: Empirically, they observe that in multi-task, multi-stage settings, SFT leads to strong task conflicts and performance degradation across tasks, while RL maintains stable performance and coexistence among tasks. At the parameter level, RL updates are found to be sparse and approximately orthogonal between tasks. Theoretical analysis shows that SFT's gradient interference is norm-limited (proportional to absolute gradient magnitude), whereas RL's interference is variance-limited due to advantage normalization and on-policy sampling, leading to a tighter bound on interference and more orthogonal optimization directions. Using these properties, their proposed Parallel-RL approach achieves more efficient and flexible multi-task training, although quantitative results are not detailed in the abstract.

Conclusion: The study concludes that RL and SFT behave fundamentally differently for multi-task reasoning in LLMs due to their distinct gradient interference regimes: SFT suffers from norm-limited interference causing task conflicts, while RL enjoys variance-limited interference that promotes sparse, near-orthogonal updates and stable task coexistence. This insight justifies RL as a principled approach for scalable multi-task training and motivates new training paradigms like Parallel-RL, which decouples tasks to improve efficiency and flexibility in multi-task LLM optimization.

Abstract: Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Reinforcement Learning (RL) exhibit fundamentally different behaviors in enhancing multi-task reasoning for large language models (LLMs). Our preliminary experiments revealed a phenomenon: SFT suffers from severe task conflicts under multi-stage training, whereas RL enables stable coexistence across diverse tasks. Empirically, we trace this to the parameter level, observing that RL induces sparse and approximately orthogonal updates across tasks. We provide a theoretical explanation for this mechanism by analyzing multi-task gradient interference. Our results reveal a distinction: interference in SFT is norm-limited, scaling with the absolute gradient magnitude, whereas interference in RL is variance-limited, bounded by the gradient variance induced by advantage normalization and on-policy optimization. This small variance bound yields near-orthogonal optimization directions across tasks. Leveraging this insight, we propose Parallel-RL, a paradigm that decouples multi-task training, significantly improving efficiency and flexibility.

</details>


### [59] [Looking under the Wrong Lamppost: On the Limitations of Automated Translation Quality Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03577)
*Serge Gladkoff,Angelika Vaasa,Sue Ellen Wright,Ingemar Strandvik,Lifeng Han*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper critically evaluates current Translation Quality Estimation (QE) systems and argues they are structurally unsuitable as standalone tools for real-world translation workflows, especially at segment level.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a rapid proliferation of QE tools used to manage translation quality at scale, but this growth has not been matched by robust, transparent, and reproducible research and testing. The authors want to scrutinize whether current QE technology is actually fit for purpose in production settings.

Method: The paper conducts a theoretical and empirical review of existing QE systems and research. It analyzes how segment-level QE operates, surveys empirical studies on QE performance, and catalogs recurring limitations such as generalization failures, biases, overfitting, distribution collapse, and data-related issues.

Result: The review finds that QE systems systematically struggle to capture essential aspects of translation quality, particularly cohesion, coherence, and stylistic/rhetorical features at the text level. Empirical evidence shows major issues including lack of generalization, systematic biases, overfitting, performance gaps, annotation difficulties, and data scarcity, which have not been solved by larger datasets or improved architectures.

Conclusion: Because of these structural limitations, especially at the segment level, current QE scores should not be used alone for critical production decisions such as routing, release, or bypassing human review. The authors propose that future work should shift toward automating human evaluation processes, specifically those grounded in MQM (Multidimensional Quality Metrics).

Abstract: Automation of Translation Quality Estimation (QE) has emerged as a widely discussed approach to managing translation quality at scale, and a growing number of tools and technologies have been released in pursuit of this goal. However, the proliferation of new QE systems has not always been accompanied by robust, transparent, and reproducible research and testing. This gap deserves critical scrutiny.
