This paper examines some fundamental limitations of the QE technology from both theoretical and empirical perspectives, arguing that current QE systems are structurally ill-equipped to serve as reliable standalone tools in real-world translation workflows. The reviewed evidence suggests that QE suffers from a range of interrelated and largely unresolved limitations. Most fundamentally, the evaluation of the quality of translation at the level of isolated segments is problematic because it tends to miss out on cohesion, coherence, and stylistic and rhetorical text features. In addition, empirical research documents several other limitations and flaws, including failure to generalize, systematic biases, overfitting and distribution collapse, performance gaps, error annotation challenges, and data scarcity. These are structural limitations arising from the complexity of human language and translation as a cognitive and communicative act - limitations that more data and better architectures have so far not overcome. Consequently, segment-level QE scores should not be used as a standalone basis for routing, release, or review bypass in production; we argue future work should focus on automating human evaluation grounded in MQM.

</details>


### [60] [Disentangling Language Modeling and Boundaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03599)
*Mykola Haltiuk*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper argues that byte-level language models have a key structural advantage: they all share the same byte output space, enabling exact, tokenizer-agnostic knowledge transfer and flexible separation of capability learning from boundary (tokenization/patch) decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing language models rely on model-specific tokenizers, making it difficult to transfer knowledge or capabilities between models with different vocabularies or segmentations. Although byte-level models are usually promoted for robustness, fairness, and character-level reasoning, their deeper advantage may be that they share a universal input-output space (bytes), potentially making model interoperability and capability transfer much easier and more exact.

Method: Conceptually, the paper analyzes the distributions produced by a byte-level model: (1) the distribution over the next byte and (2) the distribution over patch (segment) boundaries. The authors hypothesize these can be disentangled and modified almost independently. They propose two key experiments: (a) transferring capabilities from a teacher model into a student while keeping the student's original boundary scheme, and (b) changing the boundary placement while preserving the model's capabilities. They also provide preliminary measurements of model properties that these experiments rely on, though full experiments are left as future work.

Result: The abstract reports only preliminary measurements suggesting that the next-byte and boundary-placement distributions may be sufficiently separable to support their hypothesis, but does not present full experimental confirmation. The primary result at this stage is a conceptual framework and early empirical indications that such disentanglement and transfer are feasible.

Conclusion: If their hypothesis is validated, byte-level models would enable cheap, routine, and tokenizer-agnostic transfer of capabilities and boundary schemes between models, simply because they all operate over the same byte space. The authors conclude that the research community should standardize on byte-level interfaces to unlock this structural advantage and remove the current bottleneck imposed by per-model tokenizers.

Abstract: Byte-level language models are usually argued for on the grounds of robustness, multilingual fairness, and character-level skills. We point to a different, structural advantage: because they read and write bytes, any two of them share an output space, so knowledge transfer between them is exact and independent of how either was originally tokenized. We hypothesize that the two distributions a byte-level model produces, one over the next byte, one over where its patch boundaries fall, can be disentangled and changed almost independently. A model could absorb a teacher's capability while keeping its own boundaries, or change how it places those boundaries while keeping its capabilities. We lay out the two experiments that would settle the hypothesis, alongside preliminary measurements of the properties they rest on. We argue that the community should move toward a byte-level interface as a shared standard: if the hypothesis holds, then once byte-level models are the norm, transferring capabilities and reshaping boundaries between them become cheap and routine, free of the per-model tokenizer that blocks them today.

</details>


### [61] [Language-Specialized Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation for Multilingual LLM-Based ASR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03610)
*Yuan Xie,Jiaqi Song,Xianliang Wang,Ming Lei,Jie Gao,Jie Wu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a new multilingual ASR training framework that uses multiple language-specialized RL teachers to distill into a single multilingual student, reducing cross-lingual optimization conflicts and outperforming all individual teachers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multilingual LLM-based ASR systems are now standard, but training a single model across languages with very different acoustic and phonological properties causes optimization conflicts, hurting per-language performance. The authors want a way to keep strong multilingual capability while still allowing language-wise specialization, and ideally even exceed the performance of independently trained language experts.

Method: They introduce Language-Specialized Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation (LS-MOPD). First, for each language they train a separate language-specialized ASR teacher via reinforcement learning, letting each optimize purely for its own language. Then they train a single multilingual student model. During student training, they use language routing to select relevant teachers and conduct token-level multi-teacher distillation so the student imitates the specialists’ behavior. The process is on-policy (student-generated data) and they also study two ways of configuring acoustic prefixes for teacher and student—static vs dynamic—to analyze how prefix consistency impacts distillation effectiveness.

Result: On datasets including Mandarin, Mandarin subdialects, Cantonese, and English, LS-MOPD significantly outperforms existing RL-based multilingual ASR baselines. Moreover, the multilingual student consistently surpasses the performance of the best RL teacher for each language, indicating successful integration and generalization beyond the individual specialists.

Conclusion: Decoupling language-specific optimization from multilingual integration via LS-MOPD effectively mitigates cross-lingual optimization conflicts in multilingual LLM-based ASR. Multi-teacher on-policy distillation with language routing enables a single multilingual model to not only match but surpass specialized RL teachers across different languages and dialects, suggesting this framework is a promising direction for robust, high-performance multilingual ASR.

Abstract: Modern LLM-based ASR systems have established multilingual capability as a standard feature, leveraging large-scale multilingual corpora and LLMs' cross-lingual knowledge to achieve competitive performance across multilingual benchmarks. However, joint modeling of languages with heterogeneous acoustic, phonological, and lexical characteristics inevitably introduces optimization conflicts, undermining language-wise specialization. To address this challenge, we propose Language-Specialized Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation (LS-MOPD), which decouples language-specific knowledge acquisition from multilingual capability integration: language-specialized teachers are independently optimized via reinforcement learning (RL), after which their expertise is integrated into a generalist multilingual student through language routing and token-level multi-teacher distillation, thereby reducing direct cross-lingual optimization conflicts. We further explore two acoustic-prefix configurations, static and dynamic, to examine how teacher--student prefix consistency influences the efficacy of on-policy distillation. Experiments on benchmarks covering Mandarin, Mandarin subdialects, Cantonese, and English demonstrate that LS-MOPD substantially outperforms RL baselines and consistently surpasses the empirical performance envelope defined by best-performing RL teachers, revealing its potential to generalize beyond all teachers in multilingual ASR.

</details>


### [62] [A machine-readable catalogue of the Tsiolkovsky papers (fond 555, Archive of the Russian Academy of Sciences), and a way to measure how well its handwriting can be read](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03617)
*Vladimir Beskorovainyi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build a structured, machine-readable catalog and evaluation method for Tsiolkovsky’s digitized archive, enabling search, classification, and quality-assessed handwriting recognition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The digitized Tsiolkovsky archive is only available as browsable images without searchable text, structured metadata, or evaluation benchmarks, which severely limits research use. The authors want to turn this passive image collection into an analyzable, searchable resource and to understand how reliable automatic handwriting recognition is when no ground-truth transcriptions exist.

Method: They construct a machine-readable catalog for all files and scans, annotate dates for nearly all files using the archive’s descriptions, classify each page as handwriting or typescript, and create a growing set of machine transcriptions. For evaluating handwriting recognition without ground truth, they exploit cases where the same text survives both as manuscript and as a typed copy; both are transcribed automatically and compared to estimate reading error, under the assumption that content and processing pipeline are identical and only page difficulty changes. They validate this estimate against two files with reliable published editions.

Result: They produce a catalog covering 2,019 files and 51,008 scans, add dates for 1,969 files, classify each scan by script type, and generate machine transcriptions for 322 files (5,454 scans). Using 294 manuscript–typescripts pairs from 27 files, they find that two independent automatic readings of the same handwritten page agree on a median of only 37% of words. Comparison with two published editions shows that this agreement-based accuracy estimate is essentially unbiased (within ~1 percentage point) and correlates strongly with true page difficulty (rank correlation 0.92 when the edition is faithful).

Conclusion: The Tsiolkovsky archive can now be used as a structured digital corpus with searchable metadata and text, but current handwriting-recognition quality is too low for fine-grained tasks like word-by-word collation of different authorial variants. Their cross-copy comparison method offers a practical, validated way to estimate recognition accuracy in archives lacking ground-truth transcriptions, and they explicitly encode the resulting quality constraints into their tools so downstream users do not overinterpret noisy transcriptions.

Abstract: The personal archive of Konstantin Tsiolkovsky (1857-1935) is held as fond 555 of the Archive of the Russian Academy of Sciences. The archive scanned the fond and published the images, but with no queryable catalogue, no full-text search and no dataset: the holdings can only be browsed one page at a time. This paper describes a machine-readable catalogue of all 2,019 files and 51,008 scans, a dating for 1,969 files taken from the archive's own descriptions, a page-level classification of every scan into handwriting and typescript, and a growing corpus of machine transcriptions (currently 322 files, 5,454 scans).
  It also reports a way to measure handwritten-text-recognition accuracy in an archive with no ground truth. Archives of the typewriter era often preserve one text twice, as manuscript and as a typed copy; transcribing both and comparing isolates the reading error, since source and pipeline are identical and only page difficulty differs. Across 294 such pairs from 27 files, two readings of a handwritten page agree on a median 37% of words. On two files that also have a published edition the estimate can be checked against ground truth: it is unbiased to within a percentage point and ranks pages as the truth does (rank correlation 0.92 where the edition is a faithful witness). This bounds use: two variants of one work here share 19% of words, below the rate at which two readings of a single page agree, so the redactions cannot be collated word by word at this quality. That negative result is reported as such, and the constraint is built into the tool.

</details>


### [63] [LoopMTP: A looped transformer guided by latent multi-token prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03624)
*Behzad Shomali,Markus Frey,David Berghaus,Joachim Koehler,Mehdi Ali*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces LoopMTP, a method that combines looped transformers with multi-token prediction to improve reasoning and parameter efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Looped transformers allow models to effectively increase depth and reasoning ability without adding parameters, but existing methods suffer from overthinking and undifferentiated computation because there is no explicit guidance for intermediate loop representations. The authors aim to provide better supervision across loops to make the iterative computation more purposeful.

Method: The authors propose LoopMTP, which leverages multi-token prediction (MTP) in looped transformers. The core idea is a structural correspondence: if the model loops T times, each loop t is trained to anticipate the token t steps ahead. Technically, they softly align the hidden state at loop t with the embedding of the token t positions in the future, and introduce a lightweight gating mechanism to preserve task-relevant information across iterations while incorporating this forward-looking supervision.

Result: LoopMTP yields up to 8.1% relative improvement in average accuracy over a non-looped transformer baseline. The approach also maintains stable training behavior for models with up to 15 loops, demonstrating scalability of the looping mechanism with the proposed supervision scheme.

Conclusion: By integrating multi-token prediction as dense, forward-looking supervision for looped transformers, LoopMTP guides intermediate representations and mitigates latent overthinking and undifferentiated computation. This leads to better reasoning performance at fixed parameter counts and enables deeper effective computation via stable looping over many iterations.

Abstract: Looped transformers have emerged as a parameter-efficient alternative to scaling depth for strong reasoning. By reusing one stack of layers across $T$ iterations, they attain the effective depth and reasoning capabilities of larger models at a fixed parameter count. Yet existing approaches suffer from latent overthinking and undifferentiated computation, largely because intermediate representations receive no guidance across loops. Multi-token prediction (MTP) supplies exactly the dense, forward-looking supervision the loop is missing. We propose \textsc{LoopMTP}, which links the two through a structural correspondence in latent space: a model that loops $T$ times can anticipate $T$ future tokens. \textsc{LoopMTP} realizes this by softly aligning the hidden state of loop $t$ with the embedding of the token $t$ steps ahead, while a lightweight gate preserves useful information across iterations. \textsc{LoopMTP} improves average accuracy by up to 8.1\% (relative) over the non-looped baseline, with training remaining stable for up to 15 loops.

</details>


### [64] [Decoupling Generation and Selection for Budget-Constrained Faithful Summarization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03655)
*Zeyu Wang,Guanghua Wang,Meng Xu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a generation-and-selection framework for sentence-budget-constrained abstractive summarization, improving factuality and grounding by selecting and combining sentences from multiple generated candidates under an explicit length budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Abstractive summarization models often generate factually inconsistent, redundant summaries and struggle with precise length control. Existing approaches tightly couple generation with decoding, making it hard to improve factual grounding without retraining large models. The authors aim to provide a flexible, model-agnostic way to improve factual consistency and control length without modifying or retraining the base summarization model.

Method: The authors propose a modular framework with two stages: (1) a pretrained generator produces multiple candidate summaries; (2) these summaries are decomposed into sentence-level candidates, and a combinatorial selector builds the final summary subject to a sentence-length budget. The selector optimizes an objective that balances relevance to the source, factuality, and non-redundancy. The framework supports several selection objectives, including MMR (maximal marginal relevance), ILP-based optimization, and a DPP-inspired log-determinant objective. Crucially, the generator is left unchanged, and only the selection stage is optimized.

Result: On benchmarks including CNN/DailyMail, Multi-News, FaithBench, and TofuEval, the framework improves factuality and source-grounding metrics, with particularly strong gains in multi-document summarization. These gains come with reduced reference-overlap scores (e.g., ROUGE) compared to baseline systems. Human evaluations show that the selected summaries are rated higher for consistency, relevance, clarity, and conciseness, although there is a small decrease in perceived coherence.

Conclusion: Decoupling generation from selection enables a practical, model-agnostic way to improve factual grounding and control length in abstractive summarization systems. By treating summarization as a combinatorial selection problem over generated sentence candidates, the framework can boost factuality and reduce redundancy without retraining the underlying generator, albeit sometimes at the cost of lower overlap with reference summaries.

Abstract: Abstractive summarization models remain vulnerable to factual inconsistency, redundancy, and weak length control. We propose a modular generation-and-selection framework for sentence-budget-constrained summarization. A pretrained generator produces multiple candidate summaries, which are decomposed into sentence-level candidates. A combinatorial selector then constructs the final summary by balancing relevance, factuality, and redundancy under an explicit budget. The framework supports MMR, ILP, and a DPP-inspired log-determinant objective without retraining the generator. Experiments on CNN/DailyMail, Multi-News, FaithBench, and TofuEval show consistent improvements in factuality and source-grounding metrics, especially for multi-document summarization, at the cost of lower reference-overlap scores. Human evaluation further indicates higher perceived consistency, relevance, clarity, and conciseness, with a small reduction in coherence. These results show that decoupling generation from selection provides a model-agnostic mechanism for improving factual grounding. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/bcfs-D05E/.

</details>


### [65] [How Closely Do LLM Reviews Align with Human Peer Review?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03659)
*Abraham Camelo-Guerrero,Jairo Diaz-Rodriguez*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper compares how well three major LLMs’ reviews align with human reviews and conference decisions on 300 ICLR submissions, finding they can separate accepted from rejected papers but miss finer distinctions and differ in what they prioritize as weaknesses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly used to generate or assist with scientific peer reviews, it is unclear whether different commercial models not only match final accept/reject decisions but also reflect human reviewers’ finer-grained judgments and priorities, all under the same controlled setting. Current evaluations rarely compare multiple providers side-by-side on the same real conference submissions and decision labels.

Method: The authors collect 300 ICLR 2026 submissions, balanced across oral, poster, and rejected decisions. They remove explicit decision information and prompt three LLMs (OpenAI GPT-5.4, Google Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview, Anthropic Claude Opus 4.6) to review each paper with identical instructions and rating scales. They then analyze: (1) how well each model’s scores align with broad (accept vs reject) and fine-grained (oral vs poster vs reject) decision categories; (2) how each provider uses the recommendation scale (e.g., systematically higher or lower scores); and (3) thematic overlap and differences in the weaknesses identified by LLMs versus humans.

Result: All three LLMs reliably distinguished accepted from rejected papers, but none could reproduce the finer oral versus poster separation present in human ratings. Gemini tended to assign higher scores overall, whereas OpenAI and Claude produced ratings closer to human reviewers for rejected and poster papers but were more critical toward oral papers. In qualitative/thematic analysis, LLMs more often flagged missing baseline comparisons, while human reviewers more frequently emphasized computational efficiency issues.

Conclusion: The study concludes that while leading LLMs can align with human reviewers and conference decisions at a coarse level (accept vs reject), they fail to capture finer-grained distinctions such as oral versus poster status and differ systematically in their scoring distributions and evaluative priorities. Therefore, broad decision alignment is insufficient as a metric: using LLMs in peer review may reproduce high-level decisions without matching the nuanced judgments or value trade-offs that human reviewers apply.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to generate scientific reviews, yet existing evaluations rarely examine whether different providers align with both conference decisions and human reviewing priorities within the same controlled setting. We compare reviews from OpenAI GPT-5.4, Google Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview, and Anthropic Claude Opus 4.6 with human reviews and final decisions for 300 topic-matched ICLR 2026 submissions, equally divided among oral, poster, and rejected papers. Each model reviewed every paper using identical instructions and rating scales after decision information was removed. Our study contributes a cross-provider analysis of three complementary dimensions: alignment with broad and fine-grained decision categories, differences in recommendation-scale usage, and thematic agreement in identified weaknesses. All three LLMs distinguished accepted from rejected papers, but none reproduced the oral versus poster distinction present in human ratings. Scoring patterns were provider-specific: Gemini assigned systematically higher ratings, while OpenAI and Claude were closer to humans for rejected and poster papers but more critical of oral papers. Human and LLM reviews also differed in emphasis, with LLMs more frequently identifying missing baseline comparisons and humans more often raising computational-efficiency concerns. These results show that broad decision alignment does not imply agreement with finer human judgments or reviewing priorities.

</details>


### [66] [VetScore: Risk-Weighted Fact Verification for Veterinary Long-Form QA with Citations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03675)
*Ivan Kartáč,Jan Tovarys,Mateusz Lango,Ondřej Dušek*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces VetScore, a risk-aware evaluation method for checking whether long-form veterinary QA answers are supported by their cited excerpts, adjusted by how harmful incorrect claims could be.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In high-stakes areas like veterinary medicine, LLMs often generate long answers with citation excerpts. While citations can increase trust, they do not ensure that each claim in the output is actually supported by the cited text, which is dangerous when incorrect claims could harm animals. There is a need for an automatic, fine-grained, and risk-aware evaluation method that checks faithfulness of each claim to its sources, rather than just presence of citations.

Method: The authors propose VetScore, a multi-step evaluation framework for long-form veterinary question answering. The system: (1) segments a generated answer into smaller units and decomposes it into individual claims; (2) for each claim, assigns a harm-potential score and evaluates how well the claim is supported by the provided citation excerpts using various judge models; and (3) aggregates these per-claim scores into a single overall risk-adjusted score that reflects both faithfulness and potential harm. They also build a meta-evaluation dataset with expert veterinary annotations to benchmark different judge models within this framework.

Result: Using the expert-annotated dataset, the authors test different LLM-based judge models inside VetScore and find that VetScore’s scores correlate highly with veterinary experts’ assessments, even when relatively small judge models are used. The framework also produces structured, interpretable outputs that break down evaluation along multiple dimensions (e.g., claim support, harm potential).

Conclusion: VetScore is an effective and interpretable approach for evaluating long-form, citation-based veterinary QA systems in a risk-aware manner. It provides fine-grained, claim-level faithfulness judgments weighted by harm potential and achieves strong agreement with domain experts, making it a promising tool for safer deployment and assessment of medical/veterinary LLM applications.

Abstract: Citation excerpts can be used to increase the reliability of generated outputs and their faithfulness to cited sources, which is especially important in high-stakes domains such as human and veterinary medicine. However, this does not guarantee that generated claims are faithful to the provided excerpts. We present VetScore, a multi-step evaluation method for veterinary long-form question answering, designed to assess how well are generated claims supported by the provided excerpts, weighing this information by each claim's harm potential. VetScore first segments the output and decomposes it into individual claims, then scores each claim with respect to its harm potential and evaluates its faithfulness to source excerpts, and finally calculates the overall risk-adjusted score. We collect an expert-annotated meta-evaluation dataset, evaluate our approach with a range of judge models, and show that it achieves high correlations with veterinary experts even with small judge models, while offering explainability across multiple dimensions.

</details>


### [67] [Predicting Deep Neural Network Training Outcomes from Early Training Telemetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03709)
*Ranjita Naik,Anh D. Nguyen,Pankaj Kumar Singh*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that the first few epochs of training already contain enough telemetry to accurately predict a deep network run’s final performance and failures, enabling early stopping and better compute allocation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hyperparameter sweeps waste large amounts of compute on runs that are doomed to perform poorly, but practitioners typically lack a reliable, per-run signal early in training to decide which runs to stop without comparing across many runs.

Method: The authors collect early-training telemetry (loss, training accuracy, gradient signal-to-noise ratio, weight-norm growth, and activation saturation) plus hyperparameters from 23,788 runs across six architecture/dataset pairs. They train gradient-boosted tree models to predict three targets—final test accuracy, relative performance ranking, and training-dynamics failures—using only the first 1–5 epochs of telemetry, and study ablations and transfer across architectures/datasets.

Result: Using telemetry from just the first five epochs, gradient-boosted trees achieve R^2 of 0.92–0.99 on final-accuracy prediction and ROC-AUC of 0.983–0.998 for relative performance classification on held-out hyperparameter configurations. Informative predictions are already possible after a single epoch. Including gradient and weight-level telemetry consistently improves prediction over using loss and accuracy alone. Models transfer well between similar architectures, while cross-dataset transfer is mostly affected by differences in accuracy scale rather than a change in underlying relationships.

Conclusion: Early-training telemetry from individual runs can reliably forecast final performance and detect failures, making it a useful decision-support tool for dynamically allocating compute in large hyperparameter sweeps. However, because predictions may vary across domains and transfer is imperfect, human oversight remains important when using such automated signals for intervention or early stopping.

Abstract: Large hyperparameter sweeps for deep neural networks spend substantial compute on configurations that are effectively doomed from the first few epochs. We study whether a single training run's own early telemetry - per-epoch loss, training accuracy, gradient signal-to-noise ratio, weight-norm growth, and an activation-saturation snapshot - together with its sampled hyperparameters, can predict that run's eventual outcome without reference to other runs. We evaluate three prediction tasks: final test accuracy, relative performance within a domain, and training-dynamics failure, including numerical divergence. Across 23,788 training runs spanning six architecture/dataset combinations, gradient-boosted trees using only the first five epochs of telemetry achieve R^2 = 0.92-0.99 for final-accuracy regression and ROC-AUC = 0.983-0.998 for relative classification on a permanently held-out set of hyperparameter configurations. Useful prediction is already available after a single epoch. A paired ablation shows that gradient- and weight-level telemetry provides a statistically consistent improvement over loss and accuracy curves alone, although the practical gain varies by domain. Transfer is strong between similar architectures, while cross-dataset transfer is limited mainly by differences in accuracy scale rather than loss of the underlying relationship. These results suggest that early-training telemetry can provide a practical decision-support signal for compute allocation while motivating human oversight for any automated intervention.

</details>


### [68] [Detecting Hallucinations and Recovering Verified Answers in Arabic Islamic Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03720)
*Khaled Ziani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper builds a system to detect hallucinations in Arabic Islamic Q&A generated by LLMs and to pick the correct answer from multiple options, achieving strong scores on a shared-task benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs often produce fluent but factually incorrect answers to Islamic questions, and such hallucinations are hard for users to notice. There is a need for reliable automatic methods that can both flag hallucinated answers and recover the correct information from trusted options, especially in sensitive religious domains and non-English languages like Arabic.

Method: The authors fine-tune the google/gemma-4-12B-it model on a shared-task dataset of 600 Islamic Q&A instances labeled as hallucinated vs non-hallucinated, each with six candidate verified answers. At inference time they use deterministic decoding to produce a unified prediction that includes both (1) a hallucination label for the LLM’s answer and (2) the index of the chosen candidate option. They then normalize the model’s textual output to extract these two structured decisions and evaluate with standard metrics.

Result: On the HalluScoring 2026 Task 2.1 dataset, the system attains a Macro-F1 of 0.928 and label accuracy of 0.935 for hallucination detection, and 0.895 accuracy for selecting the correct verified answer among six options. The combined task score is 0.912.

Conclusion: Fine-tuning a mid-sized instruction-tuned model (gemma-4-12B-it) with deterministic decoding can yield strong performance on joint hallucination detection and answer selection in Arabic Islamic Q&A. While detecting hallucinations is relatively reliable, correctly distinguishing the single verified answer from several plausible alternatives is still harder, indicating room for improvement in fine-grained factual discrimination.

Abstract: Large language models can generate fluent responses to Islamic questions while introducing factual errors that are difficult to identify. This paper presents our system for \textsc{HalluScoring 2026} Task 2.1, \textit{Islamic Hallucination Detection and Find the Truth}. The task requires a unified two-step prediction: determining whether an Arabic answer generated by an LLM is hallucinated and selecting the verified answer from six closely related candidate options. We use the Islamic knowledge dataset provided by the shared task, which contains 600 question--answer instances, including 341 hallucinated and 259 non-hallucinated answers. Our system is based on the fine-tuned \texttt{google/gemma-4-12B-it} model and uses deterministic decoding during inference. The generated outputs are normalized to extract the hallucination label and the selected option. The system achieves a Macro-F1 score of 0.928 and a label accuracy of 0.935 for hallucination detection, together with an option accuracy of 0.895 for answer selection. These results yield a combined score of 0.912, demonstrating strong performance across both stages of the task. The lower option-selection accuracy indicates that distinguishing the verified answer from plausible alternatives remains more challenging than detecting hallucinated responses.

</details>


### [69] [GPTKB 2.0: Direct Construction of Disambiguated Knowledge Bases from Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03729)
*Yujia Hu,Tuan-Phong Nguyen,Simon Razniewski*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: GPTKB 2.0 is a scalable method to build a large, disambiguated knowledge base directly from LLMs, treating the LLM as the knowledge source while explicitly canonicalizing entities, relations, and classes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs can implicitly store vast amounts of world knowledge, but they lack explicit, stable representations of entities, which leads to duplicate or conflated entries when trying to turn their knowledge into a structured knowledge base. Existing KB construction approaches often rely on external resources like Wikimedia and do not natively address the entity disambiguation and canonicalization problem at scale in an LLM-first setting.

Method: The authors design a pipeline, GPTKB 2.0, that queries LLMs to generate candidate facts and, crucially, performs on-the-fly disambiguation and canonicalization of entities, relations, and classes. The system is architected to balance scalability, disambiguation accuracy, and computational cost, with explicit design choices to manage these trade-offs while growing the KB to large scale.

Result: Using GPTKB 2.0, the authors construct a materialized knowledge base with over 1 million disambiguated entities and 38.4 million triples. This KB is the first of its kind to reach million-scale while being natively induced from LLMs and including explicit internal canonical identifiers for entities, relations, and classes, independent from Wikimedia-style resources.

Conclusion: GPTKB 2.0 demonstrates that it is feasible to construct a large, disambiguated, LLM-native knowledge base with explicit canonicalization, moving beyond prior Wikimedia-centric efforts. The work highlights practical trade-offs between accuracy, scale, and cost, and provides an operational system and resource (available at gptkb.org) for future research on LLM-based knowledge extraction and KB construction.

Abstract: Automated Knowledge Base Construction (AKBC) is a core NLP task, and recent work proposes generating knowledge bases directly from large language models (LLMs), treating the model itself as the knowledge source. However, LLMs natively possess no representation of entities, leading to duplicate entries as well as conflations. We propose GPTKB 2.0, a methodology for constructing disambiguated KBs directly from LLMs. GPTKB 2.0 incorporates on-the-fly disambiguation of entities, relations and classes, and is meticulously designed to satisfy both scalability and disambiguation accuracy. We analyze the central design decisions and characterize the trade-offs between accuracy, scale, and cost. We execute GPTKB 2.0 at scale, obtaining a materialized KB containing over 1M disambiguated entities and 38.4M triples. This represents the first million-scale LLM-native KB with explicit internal canonicalization of entities, relations, and classes, a significant departure from prior Wikimedia-centric works. GPTKB 2.0 is available at https://gptkb.org/.

</details>


### [70] [MDLMPE: Distribution Aware Positional Encoding for Masked Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03769)
*Tong Ling,Hang Lei,Feng Xiao,Changhui Sun,Jiahang Xie,Hao Liu,Lu Liu,Yanlong Du*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces MDLMPE, a new positional encoding tailored for masked diffusion language models that leverages the dynamic pattern of revealed vs. masked tokens to improve performance over standard encodings like RoPE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Masked diffusion language models generate tokens in parallel using iterative denoising, creating non-contiguous, dynamically changing patterns of visible and masked positions. Standard positional encodings such as RoPE only encode static sequence order and pairwise distances, ignoring which tokens are currently available. This mismatch suggests that current encodings under-utilize an important positional signal in MDLMs, motivating a new encoding that is explicitly aware of token availability during diffusion steps.

Method: The authors propose MDLMPE, a positional encoding that incorporates the evolving revealed/masked configuration. For each position, they encode token availability as a binary sequence over the whole context, apply a distance-aware Gaussian weighting to emphasize nearby available tokens, and then project this weighted pattern onto a cosine (spectral) basis to produce distribution-aware positional features. These features are added to the token embeddings and passed through a lightweight MLP to produce angular offsets that modulate the phases used in standard RoPE, effectively adjusting RoPE according to the current availability distribution.

Result: In experiments on LLaDA and DREAM benchmarks, MDLMPE consistently outperforms conventional positional encoding schemes (e.g., vanilla RoPE and others) across multiple regimes: supervised fine-tuning, pretraining, zero-shot evaluation, and block-diffusion scenarios. Ablation studies show that removing any key component—explicit availability state, Gaussian locality weighting, spectral (cosine) basis projection, or the embedding-injection mechanism—degrades performance, and that the full combination yields the best outcomes.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that the dynamic token-availability distribution in masked diffusion language models is a valuable positional signal. By making positional encodings explicitly aware of which tokens are revealed or masked and how they are distributed, MDLMPE improves MDLM performance across a variety of tasks and training setups, indicating that future MDLM architectures should integrate such distribution-aware positional representations.

Abstract: Masked diffusion language models (MDLMs) enable parallel generation and bidirectional context modeling, but their positional context differs fundamentally from that of autoregressive (AR) models. Whereas AR decoding exposes a contiguous prefix, MDLM denoising produces dynamic, non-contiguous configurations of revealed and masked tokens. Conventional positional encodings such as RoPE capture sequence order and pairwise displacement but remain insensitive to this evolving token-availability structure. To address this limitation, we propose MDLMPE, a positional encoding designed specifically for masked diffusion. To the best of our knowledge, MDLMPE is the first method to make positional representations explicitly aware of the changing revealed/masked configuration. It represents token availability as a binary sequence, applies distance-aware Gaussian weighting, and projects the resulting pattern through a cosine basis to obtain distribution-aware positional features. These features are added to token embeddings and mapped by a lightweight MLP to angular offsets that modulate the standard RoPE phases. Extensive experiments on LLaDA and DREAM demonstrate that MDLMPE generally outperforms conventional positional encoding methods across supervised fine-tuning, pretraining, zero-shot evaluation, and block-diffusion settings. Further ablations show that the complete combination of availability state, Gaussian locality, spectral basis, and embedding injection yields the strongest result. These results establish the evolving token-availability distribution as a useful positional signal for masked diffusion language models.

</details>


### [71] [Efficient Knowledge Distillation for LLMs: Offline Top-K Logits and a Fused Chunked KL Loss](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03796)
*Bakbergen Ryskulov,Iker García-Ferrero,David Montero,David Jansen,Ali Hashemi,Jezabel R. Garcia,Antonio Tiene,Román Orús*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how to make knowledge distillation for small language models more efficient in terms of speed and memory, proposing offline distillation with cached teacher logits and a fused, chunked KL loss to avoid large logit tensors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small language models are necessary in settings with strict latency, cost, or on-prem constraints, but they are usually obtained via knowledge distillation, which is expensive and often the main determinant of final model quality. There is a need for practical, systems-level techniques that make this distillation process faster and more memory-efficient without sacrificing performance, especially on single GPUs.

Method: The authors conduct a practitioner-focused, systems-oriented study of distillation training. They first compare online KD with offline KD, where the teacher’s top-K logits are precomputed and cached, and the student is trained only against this cache. Second, they design a fused, chunked KL divergence loss implementation that operates on chunks of the vocabulary logits without ever materializing the full vocabulary-sized tensor, keeping peak memory usage linear in sequence length. They validate these methods on real KD workloads and through a toy benchmark isolating the loss kernel over a wide range of context lengths.

Result: Offline KD with cached top-K teacher logits achieves nearly identical training loss to standard online KD, while running about 29% faster per iteration and providing up to 41% higher throughput on a single H200 GPU, since the teacher model does not need to be kept in memory or run during student training. The fused, chunked KL loss eliminates the memory spike associated with full vocabulary logits, enabling training at 4x longer context windows (up to 32,768 tokens) on a single GPU and showing good scaling from 4K to 256K tokens in an isolated output-head benchmark. Supporting ablations further detail the effects of loss design and sequence packing strategies.

Conclusion: Efficient knowledge distillation for small language models can be achieved through two key systems techniques: (1) offline distillation with cached top-K teacher logits, which preserves training quality while significantly improving speed and throughput; and (2) a fused, chunked KL loss that avoids materializing full vocabulary logits, drastically reducing memory usage and allowing much longer context training on a single GPU. These improvements make large-scale “healing” and extensive ablation studies practically affordable, and the authors provide an open-source implementation of their chunked-loss kernel for the community.

Abstract: Small language models are often the only option for deployment under tight latency, cost, and on-premises constraints, but they are rarely trained from scratch: a compressed model is usually recovered through knowledge distillation (KD). This recovery step largely decides the final quality, yet it is expensive. We present a practitioner's study of how to make distillation training efficient, organised around two systems contributions. First, we show that offline KD (caching the teacher's top-$K$ logits once and training the student against the cache) matches online distillation at near-identical training loss while removing the teacher from memory, running about 29\% faster per iteration, and reaching up to 41\% higher throughput on a single H200 GPU. Second, we introduce a \emph{fused, chunked KL loss} that never materialises the full vocabulary-sized logit tensor, making peak memory linear in the sequence length. This removes the memory spike that otherwise caps context length and lets us train at four times the context (32{,}768 tokens) on a single GPU. A separate output-head-only toy benchmark isolates the loss kernel and confirms its memory and iteration-rate scaling from 4K to 256K tokens. Together these make large-scale healing and hundreds of ablations affordable. We also report supporting ablations on loss design and sequence packing. We release our chunked-loss implementation: https://github.com/CompactifAI/Full-Chunked-KL-Loss.

</details>


### [72] [M-GATE: Multilingual Grammar, Accuracy in Translation, and Efficiency Benchmark for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03803)
*Tomáš Burkert,Angelika Peljak-Łapińska,David Zelený*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces M-GATE, a benchmark that measures multilingual models’ true linguistic proficiency (not just fluency) across 30 languages using grammar error detection, round‑trip translation, and tokenizer efficiency, revealing large gaps between translation quality and grammatical mastery, especially in low‑resource languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multilingual benchmarks mostly check if models can perform tasks in different languages (e.g., translation, QA) but do not directly test whether the models deeply command each language’s grammar and structure. Fluency in output or task success can mask shallow competence. The authors want a way to disentangle linguistic proficiency from task-specific performance, cover many typologically diverse languages (including low-resource ones), and understand how training data, reasoning features, and tokenization affect true multilingual language ability.

Method: They build M-GATE, a benchmark covering 30 typologically diverse languages, with three components: (1) Grammatical error detection on linguist-crafted, adversarial sentences focused on hard, language-specific grammatical phenomena, evaluated via metrics like MCC; (2) Round-trip translation from shared English sources into 29 target languages and back, with quality assessed by a panel of three different LLM judges that are calibrated/validated against professional human annotators; and (3) A tokenizer efficiency metric that measures how efficiently text in each language is tokenized by the model’s subword vocabulary. They then test 50+ multilingual language models in 80+ configurations, including variants with and without explicit reasoning enabled, and analyze performance patterns in relation to pretraining data quantities (e.g., Common Crawl shares).

Result: They find a sharp divergence between fluency (e.g., decent translations) and underlying grammatical proficiency: models that translate reasonably well perform near chance on the adversarial grammar error detection task, with best MCC only about 0.36, and they tend to under-flag errors—i.e., they accept ungrammatical sentences rather than falsely flagging grammatical ones. Translation quality strongly correlates with each language’s share of web pretraining data (correlation r = 0.86 with log Common Crawl share), showing a steep penalty for low-resource languages, though the penalty is shrinking as newer model generations are released. Turning on reasoning capabilities consistently improves translation performance, but its benefit for grammatical error detection is weaker and can even be negative for some models, indicating that optimal configurations differ by task type.

Conclusion: Linguistic proficiency is not well captured by standard multilingual benchmarks focused on task success or apparent fluency; models can look good as translators while lacking robust grammatical competence, especially on difficult, language-specific phenomena. Data availability is still a dominant driver of multilingual performance, disproportionately hurting low-resource languages, though this gap is slowly improving. Reasoning-style configurations help with translation but are not a universal win for grammar-sensitive tasks, so evaluation and deployment should be task- and language-aware. M-GATE, maintained as a private test set with a public leaderboard and example items, is proposed as a robust benchmark to track true multilingual language ability and guide future model development.

Abstract: Multilingual language models are deployed across a hundred or more languages, yet most benchmarks test whether a model can perform a task _in_ a language rather than whether it commands the language itself, conflating fluency with proficiency. We introduce M-GATE (Multilingual Grammar, Accuracy in Translation, and Efficiency), a benchmark of linguistic proficiency spanning 30 typologically diverse languages from high- to low-resource. M-GATE comprises three tasks: grammatical error detection on linguist-crafted, adversarially selected sentences that turn on hard, language-specific phenomena; round-trip translation of shared English sources across 29 target languages, scored by a three-provider LLM judge panel validated against professional annotators; and a supplementary tokenizer-efficiency measure. We evaluate over 50 models in more than 80 configurations. Fluency and proficiency come apart sharply: models that translate competently sit near chance on the adversarial grammar items, the best reaching a Matthews correlation coefficient (MCC) of only 0.36, and their errors lean systematically toward under-flagging, accepting ungrammatical text rather than raising false alarms. Translation quality closely tracks a language's share of pretraining data (r = 0.86 against log Common Crawl share), producing a steep low-resource penalty that is nonetheless narrowing with successive model releases. Enabling reasoning reliably improves translation, while its effect on error detection is smaller and for some models negative, so the best configuration is task-dependent. To resist contamination, test items are kept private behind a continuously updated public leaderboard, with illustrative examples released (https://m-gate.ai).

</details>


### [73] [VIBE: A VAD-Informed Benchmark for Entity-Centered Affective Profiling of Large Language Model Outputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03810)
*Andrei Chetvergov,Alexander Evseev,Timofei Sivoraksha,Stepan Ukolov,Mikhail Solovev,Danil Sazanakov,Sergey Bolovtsov*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces VIBE, a benchmark and measurement framework for profiling how language models emotionally frame specific entities using Valence-Arousal-Dominance (VAD) scores, with a strong focus on clear contracts, separated scoring, and standardized reporting via an Affective Passport.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of language models’ affective content focus on sentiment or emotion in a coarse way and often at the whole-text level, lacking precise, target-specific affective profiling and without clear methodological contracts. There is a need to systematically measure how models frame socially salient entities (e.g., groups, organizations) along multiple affective dimensions and to report this in a transparent, replicable way, including scorer details and protocol conditions.

Method: The authors design VIBE, which formalizes a measurement contract separating text generation from external scoring and clearly distinguishing (1) scalar favorability, (2) whole-response VAD, and (3) target-directed VAD. They implement an Affective Passport format to summarize profiles and run three empirical studies: H1 validates that valence is reliable and not reducible to arousal/dominance, H2 compares whole-response vs target-directed VAD to show they differ, and H3 tests how elicitation protocols (prompt/context conditions) shift affective profiles as a diagnostic of protocol drift.

Result: H1 shows that scalar favorability (valence) is highly reliable across human judges and scorers, while arousal and dominance are less precise, aligning with known annotation challenges. It also demonstrates that valence does not capture the full VAD space. H2 finds that the emotional tone of an entire response can differ from the affective stance toward a specific named target, confirming that target-directed VAD is a distinct measurement. H3 reveals that changes in elicitation conditions cause measurable shifts in affective profiles, indicating that protocol and context must be documented for meaningful comparison.

Conclusion: Entity-centered affective profiling in VAD space should follow an explicit measurement contract that separates generation from scoring, distinguishes different affective scopes (favorability, whole-response VAD, target-directed VAD), and documents scorer identity, coverage, protocol, and limitations using an Affective Passport. This approach improves reliability, transparency, and interpretability of how language models frame socially salient targets and should become standard practice for affect-related LLM evaluation and auditing.

Abstract: Large language models routinely describe socially salient targets, including political figures, countries, religions, organizations, historical events, and social groups, encoding affective framing alongside factual content: a target may appear favorable or threatening, calm or conflictual, powerful or vulnerable. Existing work captures parts of this space through sentiment, favorability, and emotion benchmarks, but none combines target-directed VAD attribution, an explicit scorer contract, and a passport reporting format. We introduce VIBE, a benchmark for entity-centered affective profiling of LLM outputs in Valence-Arousal-Dominance (VAD) space. Its core contribution is a measurement contract: VIBE separates generation from external scoring, distinguishes scalar favorability, response-level VAD, and target-directed VAD, and reports profiles through an Affective Passport. Three empirical layers support the contract. H1 shows scalar favorability does not subsume arousal and dominance: valence findings are cross-validated (rV = 0.944 judge-human, rV = 0.954 inter-scorer); arousal and dominance are single-scorer directional estimates, not point-precise, consistent with known inter-annotator difficulty on these axes (rA = 0.495, rD = 0.702 among human annotators). H2 shows whole-response and target-directed VAD are different contracts: the same text can carry one affective tone overall while representing the named target differently. H3 is a protocol-drift diagnostic: elicitation conditions shift profiles, motivating context metadata in every affective report. These results motivate entity-centered affective profiling as a documented practice: profiles should be released with scorer identity, coverage, protocol, and interpretation limits.

</details>


### [74] [Sensitivity, Causality, and Repair Dissociate: A Layer-Wise Analysis of Perturbation Robustness and Its Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03842)
*Nathan Labiosa,David Buff,Ena Nayak,Erica Donno*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper examines where in transformer language models the effects of noisy, surface-perturbed inputs (typos, OCR noise, homophones) arise and can be fixed, finding that different operational definitions of “responsible layers” disagree and revealing distinct propagation regimes and practical guidance for adapter placement and evaluation methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models often fail on inputs with minor surface noise, but it is unclear which layers are most responsible for these failures and how best to intervene. Prior work typically assumes that sensitivity, causal importance, and repairability of a layer align, but this has not been systematically tested across models and scales. The authors want to understand the internal propagation of perturbation effects, identify mechanisms behind failures, and derive robust, training-efficient intervention strategies while highlighting evaluation pitfalls, especially for chain-of-thought tasks.

Method: They compare three notions of “responsible layer”: (1) sensitivity—where hidden representations diverge most between clean and perturbed inputs; (2) causality—where replacing noisy activations with clean ones best recovers correct predictions; and (3) compensatory capacity—where small adapter modules can best repair behavior. They perform layer-wise analyses across a panel of five transformer LMs, identify propagation regimes, and study scaling behavior within families (e.g., Qwen2.5 from 1.5B to 14B). They introduce the concept of cascade disruption: testing whether inserting adapters at causally implicated layers harms downstream computation. They conduct fixed-harness layer sweeps on tasks like GSM8K chain-of-thought vs multiple-choice control, and test a representation-stability loss, carefully examining effects under different generation budgets.

Result: The three layer maps—sensitivity, causality, and adapter-based repairability—do not align and can be strongly dissociated. They find two regimes: spike-and-suppress (Phi-3.5, Gemma-2-9B) and late-accumulation (Llama-3, Mistral, Qwen2.5-7B). In models with sufficiently high identity-patch performance, sensitivity and causality are strongly anti-correlated (rho between -0.72 and -0.88). Scaling within the Qwen2.5 family shows late-accumulation behavior strengthening with model size, replicated in a second family. Adapters placed at early, causally implicated layers disrupt later computation, making such “diagnostic-flagged” sites the worst places to insert repair adapters. Layer sweeps on 3.8–8B models confirm that the diagnostically flagged layers are the most damaging adapter windows on GSM8K; effects are weaker but sign-consistent on a shorter multiple-choice control, indicating damage compounds with longer generations. Gains from a representation-stability loss vanish or reverse when using sufficiently long chain-of-thought generation, revealing that earlier improvements were artifacts of truncated, effectively empty CoT.

Conclusion: Different operationalizations of which layers are responsible for noise-induced failures point to different parts of the network, and diagnostic signals like sensitivity and causal influence can be misleading for where to place corrective adapters. The authors argue for cascade disruption as a key mechanism behind this dissociation and show that naive diagnostics may point to the worst places for interventions. They offer practical rules of thumb—such as a training-free LRD pre-screen and defaulting to deepest adapter placement—though improvements are modest. They also caution that evaluation methodologies, especially for chain-of-thought tasks with limited generation budgets, can produce spurious gains and must be designed carefully when assessing representation-level interventions.

Abstract: When a language model fails on surface-perturbed input (typos, OCR noise, homophones), "which layer is responsible" has three natural operationalizations: where representations diverge most (sensitivity), where restoring clean activations recovers the prediction (causality), and where a small adapter can repair the damage (compensatory capacity) - and we show these three layer maps dissociate. Across a five-model panel we identify two propagation regimes - spike-and-suppress (Phi-3.5, Gemma-2-9B) and late-accumulation (Llama-3, Mistral, Qwen2.5-7B) - and on the two models meeting an 80% identity-patch gate, sensitivity and causality are anti-correlated (rho = -0.72 to -0.88). Within-family scaling on Qwen2.5 (1.5B to 14B) shows the late-accumulation signature strengthening monotonically with scale, corroborated on a second family. We propose cascade disruption as the mechanism behind the dissociation: adapters placed at causally implicated early layers break intact downstream computation, making diagnostic-flagged sites the worst adapter placements. A fixed-harness layer sweep across four models (3.8-8B) confirms the core prediction on chain-of-thought GSM8K - the flagged sites are the most damaging adapter windows on every adjudicable model - and is sign-consistent but strongly attenuated on a multiple-choice control, consistent with damage that compounds with generation length. The sweep yields practical guidance: a training-free LRD pre-screen and a default-deepest placement rule, though absolute gains over no-adapter baselines remain small. Finally, apparent gains from a representation-stability loss reverse under an adequate generation budget - truncated chain-of-thought had been scored as empty - a methodological warning for any intervention evaluated on chain-of-thought tasks.

</details>


### [75] [Beyond Representational Similarity: Source-Conditioned Description-Length Gain for Generative Plagiarism Detection and Candidate Source Reranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03859)
*Peijia Guo,Wenxuan Xie,ZiGuang Li,Ming Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes SCDG, a description-length-based framework to detect generative plagiarism by measuring how much a candidate source helps a language model predict a suspicious document.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-generated-text detectors focus on whether AI was involved rather than whether specific sources were reused, and similarity-based plagiarism detection fails when text is heavily rewritten or synthesized from multiple sources. There is a need for a robust, source-specific generative plagiarism signal that works under extensive transformation.

Method: Using a frozen language model, the authors compute the description length (negative log-likelihood) of a suspicious document P with and without conditioning on a candidate source S. The difference in token-level log-likelihoods defines the Source-Conditioned Description-Length Gain (SCDG), a directional and training-free measure of how much S helps predict P. They then aggregate and calibrate these gains to detect and retrieve reused sources, and evaluate the method on standard generative plagiarism benchmarks.

Result: On a PAN 2025-derived pairwise benchmark, SCDG reaches 0.92 Precision, 0.97 Recall, and 0.94 F1, outperforming all baselines. On PAN 2026’s multi-source retrieval task, it achieves 0.83 nDCG@10 and 0.96 Recall@100, again surpassing all baselines. On a challenging Multi-News same-topic, same-event test, the calibrated SCDG classifier flags source reuse in only 0.125% of pairs, indicating robustness against mere topical overlap.

Conclusion: SCDG provides a unified, token-decomposable, and training-free signal for detecting source-specific content reuse, even after heavy rewriting and multi-source synthesis, outperforming existing baselines and showing robustness to topical similarity without true reuse.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) pose challenges to academic integrity and peer review. Yet generative plagiarism detection remains an underexplored and largely unresolved challenge. Prior work on LLM-generated-text detection targets AI involvement, which may be permissible, rather than source reuse, while similarity-based methods struggle after extensive rewriting and multi-source synthesis. Motivated by the description-length view of probabilistic prediction, in which relevant side information can reduce a target sequence's code length, we introduce Source-Conditioned Description-Length Gain (SCDG), a directional, training-free framework that contrasts a frozen language model's description length of a suspicious document $P$ with and without a candidate source $S$. This contrast yields token-level log-likelihood gains that measure the incremental predictive evidence supplied by $S$. We evaluate SCDG on the PAN at CLEF benchmarks for generative plagiarism. On a PAN 2025-derived pairwise benchmark, SCDG achieves 0.92 Precision, 0.97 Recall, and 0.94 F1, outperforming all baselines; on PAN 2026's multi-source retrieval task, it reaches 0.83 nDCG@10 and 0.96 Recall@100, surpassing all baselines. On a same-topic, same-event Multi-News test, the calibrated gain-distribution SCDG classifier predicts source reuse for only $0.125\%$ of pairs, supporting robustness to topical overlap under this evaluation protocol. These results establish SCDG as a unified and token-decomposable signal for source-specific content reuse under extensive transformation.

</details>


### [76] [SciRet: A Compute-Aware Empirical Study of Retrieval and Reranking for Scientific RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03860)
*Kaysarul Anas Apurba,Md. Hasibul Hasan,Rofiqul Alam Shehab,Asab Azad*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SciRet analyzes how corpus scale and retrieval design affect retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) for scientific QA over CORD-19, without proposing a new model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand, in a compute-aware and systematic way, how different retrieval configurations and corpus sizes impact the effectiveness and faithfulness of RAG systems for scientific question answering, rather than adding another model to the literature.

Method: The authors fix a scientific RAG pipeline and vary only the corpus scale (1K, 5K, 15K papers, corresponding to 1,034, 5,160, and 15,480 chunks). The pipeline uses sentence-window chunking, BM25 sparse retrieval, BGE-M3 dense retrieval, reciprocal rank fusion to combine them, optional cross-encoder reranking (trained on MS MARCO), and a grounded answer generator. They evaluate retrieval quality (e.g., Recall@10) and answer faithfulness (using RAGAS), using pseudo-relevance labels derived from the hybrid system’s own outputs for controlled comparisons.

Result: Hybrid retrieval (BM25 + BGE-M3 with reciprocal rank fusion) is more robust than using either sparse-only or dense-only retrieval, achieving Recall@10 of 1.000 at both the smallest (1K) and largest (15K) corpus scales. Introducing an MS MARCO-trained cross-encoder reranker actually lowers precision on this scientific corpus, indicating harmful domain mismatch. As the corpus scale increases, generation faithfulness scores (RAGAS) improve. However, retrieval scores are based on pseudo-labels from the hybrid system, so they support relative comparisons rather than absolute benchmarking claims.

Conclusion: A well-tuned hybrid retrieval strategy can remain highly effective and robust across sizable scientific corpora, and naive use of out-of-domain cross-encoder rerankers can hurt performance despite their stronger interaction modeling. Larger corpora can improve RAG faithfulness in this setup, but evaluation based on pseudo-relevance labels should be interpreted as controlled comparative evidence, not as definitive benchmarking. The authors provide code, indexes, and outputs to facilitate replication and future work.

Abstract: We introduce SciRet, a compute-aware empirical study of retrieval-augmented generation for scientific question answering over CORD-19. Rather than proposing a new model, we evaluate a fixed scientific RAG pipeline across three corpus scales: 1,034 chunks (1K papers), 5,160 chunks (5K papers), and 15,480 chunks (15K papers). The pipeline combines sentence-window chunking, BM25, BGE-M3 dense retrieval, reciprocal rank fusion, optional cross-encoder reranking, and grounded answer generation. Across these settings, hybrid retrieval is more robust than either sparse-only or dense-only retrieval in our setting, reaching Recall@10 of 1.000 at 1K and 15K. In contrast, an MS MARCO-trained cross-encoder reranker reduces precision on the scientific corpus, suggesting that domain mismatch can outweigh the benefits of stronger query-passage interaction. Generation faithfulness measured with RAGAS increases with corpus scale in our setup. Retrieval evaluation uses pseudo-relevance labels derived from the hybrid system, so we treat the results as controlled comparative evidence rather than a benchmark claim. We release code, indexes, and evaluation outputs to support replication and follow-up studies.

</details>


### [77] [MultiGlobeQA: A Multilingual and Globally Diverse Benchmark for Geospatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03882)
*Martin Böckling,Elizaveta Nosova,Heiko Paulheim,Andreea Iana*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces MultiGlobeQA, a large multilingual benchmark to test LLM geospatial reasoning, showing LLMs fail mainly at geometric computation rather than knowledge access, with worse performance on low-income regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs contain rich geographic facts but perform poorly on concrete geospatial reasoning (distances, containment, spatial relations). Existing tests are synthetic, small, monolingual, and geographically biased, so they cannot fully diagnose where and why LLMs fail. A more realistic, large-scale, multilingual, and geographically diverse benchmark is needed to analyze geospatial reasoning capabilities and biases of LLMs.

Method: The authors construct MultiGlobeQA, a benchmark of 46,060 QA pairs organized into 14 spatial-function families (e.g., distances, containment, directions, topology) and 15 answer formats, grounded in three knowledge graphs with execution-based ground truth. They sample coverage across 201 countries and territories using income- and population-density-stratified sampling, and create parallel questions in English plus 16 other high- and low-resource languages. They then evaluate multiple LLMs under parametric-only, reasoning-focused, and tool-augmented/agentic settings, including retrieval and tool use with access to gold facts, to separate knowledge from computation.

Result: Across model types and settings, LLMs perform relatively well on topological relations and directional questions but perform very poorly on tasks requiring explicit geometric computation such as grid indexing and shape computation. Retrieval and tool integration significantly improve scores but still cap performance at under two-thirds accuracy even with gold factual information, showing that reasoning/computation remains the main bottleneck. Additionally, models consistently perform worse on questions involving low-income regions, and this performance gap actually increases, not decreases, when gold facts are provided.

Conclusion: MultiGlobeQA reveals that LLM weaknesses in geospatial tasks stem chiefly from limitations in spatial computation rather than lack of geographic knowledge. Even when provided with perfect supporting facts and tools, current models cannot reliably execute the necessary geometric reasoning, and they also exhibit systematic underperformance on low-income regions. The benchmark thus both diagnoses core computational deficits in LLM geospatial reasoning and highlights inequities in geographic coverage, providing a challenging and diverse testbed for future model and toolchain improvements.

Abstract: Geospatial reasoning, i.e., computing distances, containment, and other spatial relations over real-world entities, is central to navigation and logistics, yet large language models (LLMs) struggle with the required geometric and topological computation despite storing considerable geographic knowledge. Existing benchmarks localize these failures only partially: they are synthetic or smallscale, largely monolingual, and offer limited control over geographic coverage. We introduce MultiGlobeQA, a multilingual benchmark of 46,060 question-answer pairs spanning 14 spatial-function families and 15 answer formats, with execution-based ground truth over three knowledge graphs. It covers 201 countries and territories via income- and density-stratified sampling, with parallel questions in English and 16 additional high- and low-resource languages. Across parametric, reasoning, and agentic settings, LLMs collapse on tasks requiring grid indexing and shape computation, while topological relations and directions fare best. Retrieval and tool use yield considerable gains, yet performance plateaus below two thirds even when gold facts are supplied, indicating that computation, not access to knowledge, is the bottleneck. Models also underperform on low-income regions, a gap that gold facts widen rather than close.

</details>


### [78] [DS@GT-ARC at eRisk 2026 Task 3: Sparse, Semantic, and LLM Reranking for ADHD Symptom Sentences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03883)
*David Guecha*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper reports zero-shot, staged-retrieval systems for ranking Reddit sentences by relevance to ADHD symptoms in eRisk 2026 Task 3, showing LLM-based reranking performs best.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To tackle a new shared task (eRisk 2026 Task 3) where no annotated training data are available, requiring effective ranking of Reddit sentences for each of 18 ADHD symptoms, and to explore how retrieval and reranking pipelines can work under zero-shot and weakly guided settings.

Method: The authors design multiple staged retrieval pipelines. First, BM25 is used as a sparse retriever to collect candidate Reddit sentences at scale for each symptom-related query. Then several reranking and refinement components are applied, including: (1) evidence-aware rescoring focusing on self-referential symptom reports; (2) embedding-based semantic reranking; (3) query-prototype expansion to enrich symptom queries; and (4) large language model (LLM)-based reranking to refine final rankings. They rely on zero-shot setups, manual validation, and unsupervised or weakly guided retrieval due to the lack of labeled training data.

Result: Among the submitted systems, the LLM-based reranker obtains the highest official scores in the shared task, with the query-prototype expansion run performing second best. A manual inspection of the top-10 ranked sentences for each method matches the trend in expert scores, supporting the observed performance differences.

Conclusion: Staged retrieval architectures that start with scalable BM25 retrieval and refine results via semantic or LLM-based reranking are effective for ADHD symptom sentence ranking in a zero-shot setting. The success of LLM-based and prototype-expansion reranking indicates that such multi-stage approaches are promising directions for further improvement on similar clinical or symptom-focused ranking tasks.

Abstract: This paper describes our submissions to eRisk 2026 Task 3, ADHD Symptom Sentence Ranking. The task requires systems to rank candidate Reddit sentences according to their relevance to each of the 18 symptoms in the Adult ADHD Self-Report Scale (ASRS-v1.1). Because no annotated training data were released for this first edition of the task, we relied on zero-shot experimentation, manual validation, and unsupervised or weakly guided retrieval pipelines. Our systems combine sparse BM25 retrieval, evidence-aware rescoring for self-referential symptom reports, embedding-based reranking, query-prototype expansion, and LLM-based reranking. All submitted systems follow a staged retrieval design in which BM25 retrieves candidates at scale and semantic or LLM rerankers refine the final rankings. Among our submissions, the LLM reranker achieved the strongest official scores, followed by the prototype query-expansion run. Our manual top-10 analysis aligned with the official expert scoring trend, suggesting that staged reranking is a promising direction for further development.

</details>


### [79] [ANNOTARES: A Dataset for Extracting Logical Structures from German Statutory Texts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03898)
*Ronja Schwarz,Jannik Strötgen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a new German legal dataset (ANNOTARES) with span-level labels for legal conditions and legal consequences, and benchmarks multiple models, finding BERT/LLM-based models work best.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatically understanding the internal logical structure of legal provisions is crucial for legal tech applications, but reliably separating legal conditions (Tatbestand) from legal consequences (Rechtsfolge) in statutory texts is still very hard due to complex syntax and domain-specific language. There is also a lack of annotated data and systematic benchmarks for this specific task in German law.

Method: The authors construct ANNOTARES, a span-level annotated dataset of German statutory provisions from three different legal codes, where text spans are labeled as legal conditions or legal consequences. They then benchmark a range of modeling approaches for sequence labeling: a rule-based system, classical CRFs, BiLSTMs, BiLSTM-CRF, and several Transformer-based models including BERT variants and large language model–based methods, evaluating both in-domain and cross-statute generalization.

Result: Experiments show that Transformer-based models, particularly BERT variants and LLM-based methods, substantially outperform rule-based, CRF, and BiLSTM(-CRF) baselines in identifying and segmenting the condition and consequence spans in German statutes, especially in handling complex syntactic constructions typical of legal text. The dataset also reveals differences in performance when transferring across different legal codes.

Conclusion: ANNOTARES provides a first systematic resource for span-level Tatbestand/Rechtsfolge segmentation in German statutory law and can serve as a benchmark for legal-structure parsing. The strong performance of BERT and LLM-based models suggests that modern Transformer architectures are well-suited to capture the intricate syntactic and logical patterns in legal language, and releasing the dataset should foster further advances in automated legal reasoning and legal NLP.

Abstract: The automatic structural analysis of legal texts is a cornerstone of legal technology, yet the extraction of their logical components remains a significant challenge.
