In this paper, we introduce the task of identifying and segmenting legal conditions (Tatbestand) and legal consequences (Rechtsfolge) within German statutory texts. To support this task, we present ANNOTARES (Annotations of Tatbestand-Rechtsfolge Sequences), a novel dataset comprising German law texts with span-level annotations. Spanning three distinct legal codes, the dataset is designed to evaluate both domain-specific performance and cross-statute generalizability. We benchmark diverse architectural approaches: a rule-based baseline, CRFs, BiLSTMs, BiLSTM-CRF, and modern Transformer-based models, including BERT variants and LLM-based methods. Our results demonstrate that BERT and LLM-based models achieve superior performance in capturing the complex syntactic structures of legal language. We release our dataset to facilitate further research in automated legal reasoning.

</details>


### [80] [Logic Before Language: Pre-pretraining on Formal Derivations Fosters Skill Acquisition and Compressibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03930)
*Jo-Ku Cheng,Nikolaos Aletras,Marco Valentino*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes logic-based pre-pretraining (Logic-PPT) for language models, using formal logic derivations to better initialize models, which accelerates and improves downstream natural language learning and yields more compressible internal representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing pre-pretraining tasks (like Dyck languages and simple procedural algorithms) are structurally narrow and do not capture the full expressiveness and complexity of natural language. Prior work also uses relatively small token budgets, limiting understanding of how abilities emerge and how representations evolve at scale. The authors seek a more principled and expressive symbolic pretraining regime that imparts abstract mechanisms central to language, while evaluating it at a realistic scale.

Method: They introduce Logic-PPT, where language models are pre-pretrained on formal logic derivations. These derivations require variable binding, quantifier interactions, relational dependencies, and predicate-argument composition over long contexts. Models first learn from this symbolic derivation data and are then trained on natural language. The authors scale experiments to a 100B-token training regime and compare Logic-PPT against standard initialization and other symbolic pre-pretraining baselines, analyzing both performance and internal representational geometry (e.g., rank and spectral concentration). They also conduct pruning experiments to test compressibility.

Result: Logic pre-pretraining significantly speeds up acquisition of linguistic skills: models reach 80% accuracy on linguistic benchmarks using 36B fewer tokens compared to standard initialization. Logic-PPT also outperforms alternative pre-pretraining baselines built on other symbolic tasks. Representation analysis shows that models trained with Logic-PPT develop a more structurally organized latent space, with lower rank and stronger spectral concentration. Models initialized with Logic-PPT remain robust under parameter pruning, maintaining performance comparable to dense baselines even when about one-third of weights are removed.

Conclusion: Logic-PPT is an effective, principled pre-pretraining strategy for language models. Training first on formal logic derivations instills abstract structural and linguistic biases that transfer to natural language, accelerating learning and improving data efficiency. This procedure also reshapes internal representations in a way that supports higher compressibility, allowing substantial pruning with little loss in performance. The work suggests that rich, logic-based symbolic training is a powerful complement to standard language model pretraining and a better foundation than prior narrow symbolic tasks.

Abstract: Pre-pretraining language models (LMs) on symbolic data can accelerate and improve natural language acquisition. However, existing pre-pretraining tasks, such as Dyck and procedural algorithms, rely on narrow primitives that fail to capture the expressive capacity of natural language. Moreover, prior studies remain restricted to relatively small token budgets, offering limited insight into skill emergence and representational dynamics. To address these limitations, we propose logic pre-pretraining (Logic-PPT) as a principled initialization strategy, leveraging formal derivations to impart richer structural and linguistic biases. Formal derivations require abstract mechanisms that are central to natural language, simultaneously binding variables, connecting quantifiers and relational dependencies, and composing predicate-argument structures over long contexts. Scaling our evaluation to a 100B-token regime, logic pre-pretraining substantially accelerates skill acquisition in LMs, achieving 80\% accuracy on linguistic tasks with 36B fewer tokens than standard initialization, and outperforming alternative pre-pretraining baselines. Mechanistically, formal derivations induce persistent structural reorganization, distinctively characterized by a lower-rank, spectrally concentrated representation space. Crucially, we show that this internal geometry enables improved model compressibility via pruning, matching the dense baseline performance even at $\approx$33\% sparsity.

</details>


### [81] [HalluTruthQA-4K: A Fine-Grained Corpus and Annotation Process for Arabic Hallucination Detection and Truth Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03966)
*Salah Eddine Bekhouche,Abdessalam Bouchekif,Hichem Telli,Mohammed-En-Nadhir Zighem,Abdenour Hadid*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces HalluTruthQA-4K, a 4,000-instance expert-annotated Arabic QA dataset for fine-grained hallucination analysis and evaluation in LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Arabic hallucination datasets are coarse-grained, usually assigning only a single binary label per response without pinpointing which parts are wrong, why they are wrong, or what the correct facts are. This limits research on hallucination detection, localization, explanation, and factual verification in Arabic language models, especially in knowledge-intensive domains where errors can be subtle and harmful.

Method: The authors expand a previous HalluTruthQA resource to 4,000 instances and design a detailed annotation pipeline: selecting questions in four knowledge-heavy domains, generating model answers in a controlled way, constructing candidate distractors, and then having domain experts annotate. For each QA pair, they provide the question, model response, a verified gold answer, and five plausible distractors. For hallucinated outputs, annotators mark character-level erroneous spans, write natural-language explanations, and assign hierarchical hallucination types. The process includes independent verification, adjudication of disagreements, adherence to written guidelines and taxonomy, and multiple quality-control checks. They measure inter-annotator agreement and analyze corpus statistics.

Result: The final HalluTruthQA-4K corpus contains 4,000 Arabic QA instances over Islamic knowledge, history, science, and geography: 1,643 hallucinated and 2,357 non-hallucinated responses, with 1,843 manually annotated erroneous spans, each accompanied by explanations and hallucination-type labels. The dataset includes structured metadata, the reference answers, and distractor answers, along with documented guidelines, taxonomy, data formats, and agreement metrics.

Conclusion: HalluTruthQA-4K is a reusable, fine-grained benchmark for studying hallucinations in Arabic LLMs. By providing span-level error localization, explanations, hallucination types, and strong reference answers plus distractors, it supports diverse tasks such as hallucination detection, error span identification, explanation generation, and factual verification, and more generally enables rigorous evaluation of factual reliability in Arabic language models.

Abstract: Large language models can generate fluent Arabic answers while introducing factual errors that are difficult to identify and verify. Existing Arabic hallucination resources often assign a binary label to an entire response, indicating whether it is hallucinated or non-hallucinated, but provide limited information about the exact erroneous content, the reason for the error, or the correct factual answer. We present HalluTruthQA-4K, an expanded version of the HalluTruthQA resource containing 4,000 expert-curated Arabic question-answering instances across four knowledge-intensive domains: Islamic knowledge, history, science, and geography. Serving as the official dataset for Track 2 of the HalluScoring 2026 shared task, HalluTruthQA-4K extends our original corpus to 4,000 instances. Each instance pairs an Arabic question with a model-generated response, a verified reference answer, and five plausible distractors. Hallucinated responses are additionally annotated with character-level erroneous spans, human-written explanations, and hierarchical hallucination types. The corpus contains 1,643 hallucinated and 2,357 non-hallucinated responses, with 1,843 annotated erroneous spans. We describe the resource construction and annotation methodology, including question selection, controlled answer generation, candidate construction, expert annotation, independent verification, adjudication, and quality control. We also document the annotation guidelines, taxonomy, data format, inter-annotator agreement, and corpus statistics. HalluTruthQA-4K provides a reusable resource for hallucination detection, span-level error localization, explanation generation, factual verification, and the broader evaluation of factual reliability in Arabic language models.

</details>


### [82] [string2string Studio: An Interactive, In-Browser Platform for String-to-String Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03984)
*Mirac Suzgun,James Zou,Stuart M. Shieber,Dan Jurafsky*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: An in-browser interactive platform, string2string Studio, for fast, inspectable string-to-string analysis across multiple domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: String-to-string operations like alignment, distance, similarity, and homology search are central in NLP, computational biology, and digital humanities, but existing tools are often slow, require installation, rely on server-side computation, and lack transparency and unified interfaces.

Method: Design and implement an in-browser platform composed of six modules (alignment, distance, similarity, search, generation metrics, and BLAST homology search) whose core algorithms are written in C++, compiled to WebAssembly, and run locally. Provide multi-level (character, word, token, line, residue) analysis, expose evidence such as alignments and edit paths, and validate performance and correctness via internal benchmarks and comparison to established tools like NCBI BLAST+.

Result: The platform achieves up to 2,500x speedup over its Python predecessor, outperforms a general-purpose native C aligner on global/local alignment, and matches independent reference tools exactly under the same settings. The client-side blastn implementation yields rankings and statistics closely aligned with NCBI BLAST+ when parameters are matched. A curated set of examples and a Learn mode illustrate and standardize common algorithms and metrics.

Conclusion: string2string Studio provides a fast, transparent, and installation-free environment for string-to-string analysis that unifies multiple algorithms and domains in the browser, enabling reproducible, debuggable, and educational workflows, and is openly available for broad use at string2string.org.

Abstract: We present string2string Studio, an interactive in-browser platform for string-to-string analysis across natural language processing, computational biology, and the digital humanities. The system integrates six main modules (alignment, distance, similarity, search, generation metrics, and BLAST homology search), operating at character, word, token, line, and residue levels. Its C++-based algorithms compile to WebAssembly, so core operations run locally by default without any installation or data upload. The interface reports scores with their "evidence" (alignments, edit paths, metric matches, search hits, and homology traces), making methods inspectable, debuggable, and comparable on shared inputs. Internal benchmarks show speedups of up to 2,500x over the Python predecessor, faster global/local alignment than a general-purpose native C aligner, and exact agreement with independent references under declared settings. For homology search, the scoped client-side blastn path closely matches NCBI BLAST+ rankings and statistics under matched parameters. A curated showcase and Learn mode present canonical algorithms and metrics as reusable demonstrations. string2string Studio is open-source and freely available at string2string.org.

</details>


### [83] [When Attention Goes Blind: Numerical Failure in ALiBi Positional Encodings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03994)
*Christopher Schröder,Lukas Gienapp,Ferdinand Schlatt,Martin Potthast,Gerhard Heyer*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper discovers and analyzes a numerical failure mode in ALiBi positional encodings that makes some attention heads effectively blind, evaluates its impact and mitigations, and offers practical training recommendations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ALiBi positional encoding is widely adopted in modern transformer language models because of its simplicity and strong empirical performance, especially for long-context tasks. However, systematic numerical issues and edge-case failure modes have not been fully characterized. The authors are motivated by the need to understand and improve ALiBi’s behavior, particularly in long-context token retrieval settings, and to clarify why some ALiBi-based models underperform on such tasks despite doing well on standard benchmarks.

Method: The authors perform a theoretical and empirical analysis of ALiBi’s linear bias scaling and show how it can underflow floating-point precision, zeroing out large portions of the attention matrix and making some heads partially blind. They then probe this failure mode in existing state-of-the-art pretrained models that use ALiBi. To isolate its effects, they conduct controlled pretraining experiments with 148M-parameter decoder-only models, systematically varying ALiBi configurations and applying four distinct training-time mitigation strategies. They evaluate these configurations on token retrieval tasks (e.g., passkey / needle-in-a-haystack) and on standard decoder benchmarks to compare impact.

Result: They find that ALiBi’s linear bias can indeed underflow in common floating-point formats, creating many zero attention weights and effectively disabling parts of attention heads, especially at long ranges. This significantly harms token retrieval and long-context performance while leaving standard benchmarks largely unaffected, which can mask the issue. Among the mitigation strategies tested, using log-scaled distances for the ALiBi bias provides the most consistent and robust improvement on passkey retrieval tasks. Still, default ALiBi slope choices remain surprisingly competitive, particularly on needle-in-a-haystack evaluations.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that ALiBi suffers from a nontrivial numerical failure mode due to bias underflow, which selectively degrades long-range token retrieval without clearly showing up in common benchmarks. Nevertheless, ALiBi remains a strong baseline if configured carefully. The authors recommend adopting log-scaled distance variants or other mitigation strategies when training ALiBi-based models, especially for long-context retrieval use cases, and provide concrete guidance on slope and bias design to avoid partial head blindness while preserving ALiBi’s advantages.

Abstract: We identify a previously overlooked failure mode of ALiBi positional encoding: its linear bias scaling underflows floating-point precision, which zeroes out a large fraction of attention weights and renders the affected attention heads partially blind. We analyze this failure mode, characterize its impact, and examine four mitigation strategies. We further demonstrate its occurrence in state-of-the-art pretrained models based on ALiBi. Comprehensive pretraining experiments with 148M-parameter decoder models help us to disentangle its effects from out-of-context degradation. We find that ALiBi's failure mode can substantially impair token retrieval while having only a minor effect on standard decoder benchmarks. We propose four training-time mitigation strategies and evaluate them individually and in combinations, finding that log-scaled distances yield the most consistent improvements in passkey retrieval. Despite this problem, default ALiBi slopes remain a surprisingly strong baseline, particularly for needle-in-a-haystack retrieval. Based on these findings we provide concrete recommendations on how to train models with ALiBi.

</details>


### [84] [PAST-Bench: Benchmarking the Foundations of Recursive Self-Improvement in Personal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04003)
*Shuhan Xue,Zixin Ding,Yichen Shen,Yinjie Wang,Zhenfei Yin,Yingcheng Wu,Yuxin Chen,Mengdi Wang,Ling Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces PAST-Bench to systematically test whether personal AI agents actually improve from retained experience, and proposes Hermes+ to enhance such improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although personal AI agents can store preferences, histories, and learned skills across sessions, it is unclear whether this persistence translates into measurable performance gains over time; existing work lacks a controlled, diagnostic benchmark for this question.

Method: The authors design PAST-Bench, where agents perform ordered sequences of fresh-session tasks under matched conditions with retained experience toggled on or off. The benchmark covers 26 scenarios and 204 episodes across four categories: memory, procedural reuse, information gathering, and update. They evaluate seven base models and four agent frameworks, analyze both performance gains and evidence that gains arise via intended save–retrieve–update pathways, and then iteratively design Hermes+, an enhanced version of the Hermes agent, with five interventions targeting different stages of the agent loop.

Result: Across diverse models and frameworks, agents do show performance gains from retained experience, but improvements are uneven and strongly capability-dependent. Even agents with similar overall gains differ substantially in whether their behavior actually follows the intended memory and update pathways. Hermes+, incorporating targeted interventions, increases the average benefit from retained experience and yields clearer evidence that improvements arise from the hypothesized mechanisms, with particularly strong effects on tasks that require overwriting outdated state.

Conclusion: PAST-Bench offers a controlled, diagnostic benchmark for assessing whether and how persistent agents improve from prior experience, while Hermes+ demonstrates that carefully designed agent-loop interventions can make this improvement more reliable and interpretable. Together they establish a foundation for systematic study and engineering of recursive self-improvement in personal AI agents.

Abstract: Recursive self-improvement requires agents to turn accumulated experience into better future behavior. Personal AI agents offer a concrete setting for studying this capability because they retain preferences, task histories, tool routines, and learned skills across sessions. Yet whether retained experience actually improves them over time has not been systematically tested. We introduce PAST-Bench, a benchmark designed to isolate this question. Each agent runs through ordered sequences of fresh-session tasks under matched conditions that turn retained experience on and off. It spans 26 scenarios and 204 episodes across memory, procedural reuse, information gathering, and update. We report both later-task gains and whether those gains follow the intended save, retrieve, and update pathway. Across seven base models and four agent frameworks, improvement is real but uneven across capabilities. Agents with the same headline gain can differ markedly in whether that gain is supported by evidence of the intended pathway. Guided by these findings, we develop Hermes+, which extends Hermes with five targeted interventions across stages of the agent loop. Hermes+ raises the average gain from retained experience and provides clearer pathway evidence, with its strongest improvement on tasks requiring outdated state to be replaced, although the effect remains capability- and model-dependent. Together, PAST-Bench and Hermes+ provide an evaluation and diagnostic foundation for studying how persistent agents can progress from retaining experience to systematically improving through it. Code: https://github.com/Gen-Verse/PAST-Bench

</details>


### [85] [TurnSight: Turn-Level Hindsight Self-Distillation for Tool-Integrated Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04007)
*Changle Qu,Sunhao Dai,Hengyi Cai,Yuqi Zhou,Xinran Chen,Simon,Jun Xu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: TurnSight is a hindsight-based, turn-level self-distillation framework that improves reinforcement learning for tool-integrated reasoning by providing fine-grained, reliable supervision signals derived from the agent’s own execution trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL methods for tool-integrated reasoning rely mainly on trajectory-level supervision, which makes credit assignment coarse and ineffective for long-horizon tasks. On-policy self-distillation can offer denser learning signals, but prior approaches construct teacher signals using ground-truth answers or retrieved skills that do not correspond to the actual states and tool calls visited by the agent, reducing their usefulness. Additionally, most supervision is applied at the token level, which ignores the natural turn-level structure of multi-step tool interactions. There is a need for a method that (1) grounds supervision in the agent’s own executed trajectories, (2) operates at the turn level, and (3) provides reliable dense feedback suitable for long-horizon TIR.

Method: The authors introduce TurnSight, a turn-level hindsight self-distillation framework for RL in tool-integrated reasoning. After the agent executes trajectories with tool calls, TurnSight constructs execution-conditioned hindsight branches that re-evaluate each turn using privileged future information (lookahead). It builds multiple ‘hindsight views’ with different lookahead horizons, and then selects reliable supervision by checking cross-horizon directional agreement—i.e., whether different horizons agree on improving or degrading a turn. The supervision signal chosen via this agreement test is then normalized across sibling rollouts and used to modulate the RL advantage values at the turn level. This modulation amplifies or attenuates advantages while explicitly preserving their original sign (optimization direction), effectively integrating hindsight-based self-distillation into the RL update without flipping its underlying policy-gradient signal.

Result: TurnSight is evaluated on three tool-integrated reasoning benchmarks and consistently improves performance over baselines, demonstrating better handling of long-horizon tasks and more effective learning from tool interactions. The experiments show that incorporating execution-conditioned, turn-level hindsight supervision yields higher success rates and more efficient training compared to existing trajectory-level RL and token-level self-distillation methods.

Conclusion: TurnSight provides a principled way to incorporate hindsight self-distillation into RL for tool-integrated reasoning by operating at the turn level and grounding supervision in the agent’s actual execution trajectories. By leveraging multiple hindsight horizons and selecting supervision via directional agreement, the method offers reliable, dense learning signals that refine the advantage estimates without altering their optimization direction. Empirical results on three benchmarks indicate that this framework significantly enhances LLMs’ ability to perform complex, long-horizon tool-based reasoning tasks, suggesting a promising direction for future work on fine-grained credit assignment in TIR systems.

Abstract: Tool-Integrated Reasoning (TIR) enables LLMs to solve complex tasks through iterative tool interactions. However, existing reinforcement learning methods often rely on trajectory-level supervision, limiting fine-grained credit assignment in long-horizon TIR scenarios. On-policy self-distillation offers denser signals through teacher branches with privileged context, but existing approaches typically derive such context from ground-truth answers or retrieved skills, which may not reflect the states actually visited by the agent. Moreover, token-level supervision fails to capture the turn-level structure of tool interactions. To address this, we propose TurnSight, a turn-level hindsight self-distillation framework that derives supervision directly from execution-conditioned hindsight. It then constructs multiple hindsight views with different lookahead horizons and selects reliable supervision through cross-horizon directional agreement. Finally, the selected hindsight signal is normalized across sibling rollouts and used to adaptively modulate RL advantages while preserving their original optimization direction. Extensive experiments on three benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of TurnSight. Our codes are available at https://github.com/quchangle1/TurnSight.

</details>


### [86] [WorldCup Arena: Prospective, Leakage-Free Evaluation of Frontier LLMs on a Live Tournament](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04008)
*Zhenran Wang,Zhonghan Bian,Jinsong Li,Zhangyang Qi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a leakage-free, fully prospective benchmark of LLM forecasting performance using 2026 FIFA World Cup match predictions, finding that current frontier models roughly match bookmaker favorites but share similar, systematically biased behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most benchmarks for LLM forecasting are retrospective, so models can potentially rely on web-available answers or training data memorization, complicating evaluation. The authors want a clean, future-facing benchmark where answers are truly unknown at query time, to fairly assess how well LLMs can forecast real-world events and to characterize their prediction behavior.

Method: During the 39 days of the 2026 FIFA World Cup, six frontier LLMs equipped with extended reasoning and server-side web search were repeatedly queried in real time. Before every kickoff, each model filled out a seven-market prediction card for the upcoming match (including scoreline and related markets) across all 104 matches, plus predictions for 12 group winners and an outright tournament winner. This produced 4,494 scored predictions with no information leakage. The authors then compared model accuracy to baselines such as betting-market favorites and analyzed systematic patterns in calibration and choice distributions.

Result: The models averaged 63.9% accuracy on match outcome, effectively matching the performance of naively backing the bookmaker’s favorite, which they often implicitly emulate. The models tended to agree with one another much more often than they were actually correct, so aggregating them via majority vote offered no gain. They systematically under-predicted draws and goals, and concentrated scoreline guesses on a narrow, prototypical result. Forecast accuracy correlated with match lopsidedness (one team clearly favored) rather than with how much information existed; performance dropped in closely matched fixtures even when extensive background data were available, while more aggregate tournament-level questions were answered competently. Differences between frontier systems were modest: rankings were stable at the extremes but fluctuated in the middle, and performance margins remained small.

Conclusion: Prospective, leakage-free evaluation reveals that current frontier LLMs, even with strong tools and reasoning, are not especially strong or differentiated football forecasters: they mostly mirror betting favorites and share common, systematic biases in probability allocation and scoreline selection. Collective methods like majority voting do not improve accuracy because models are highly correlated. The authors provide the dataset of dossiers, fixtures, results, and scoring code as a benchmark for future work on LLM forecasting, calibration, and debiasing in real-world, forward-looking prediction tasks.

Abstract: Benchmarks that measure the forecasting ability of large language models are almost always retrospective: the event has happened, the answer is somewhere on the Web, and the evaluation must defend itself against memorisation. We report the opposite design. Over the 39 days of the 2026 FIFA World Cup, six frontier LLMs -- all with extended thinking and native server-side web search -- were asked before every kickoff, one match at a time, to fill in a seven-market prediction card for all 104 matches, plus 12 group winners and a pre-tournament outright pool; no answer existed when the question was asked, so the evaluation is leakage-free by construction rather than by filtering, and the frozen archive holds 4,494 scored predictions. What the tournament establishes is a set of behaviours the six systems share. On match outcome they average 63.9%, level with backing the bookmaker's favourite -- which is in fact what they usually do. They agree with one another far more often than they are right, so a majority vote adds nothing. They under-commit to draws and to goals, and crowd their scoreline picks onto a single prototypical result. Accuracy tracks how lopsided a fixture is rather than how much is known about it: it collapses in the closest ties, where the dossiers are richest, while questions about the tournament as a whole are answered well. On this task the current generation of frontier systems is not sharply differentiated: the standings hold up at the top and the bottom across the run and churn in the middle, and the margins stay narrow throughout. The briefing dossiers, fixtures and official results are released as a benchmark, together with the scoring code.

</details>


### [87] [SocietyBench: Forecasting Counterfactual Social-World Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04009)
*Zhenran Wang,Zhonghan Bian,Jinsong Li,Zhangyang Qi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces SocietyBench, a benchmark to test LLMs’ ability to forecast the evolution of real-world social events using anonymized timelines and dual-axis scoring for calibration and timing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM evaluation focuses mainly on task completion (e.g., coding, browsing, GUI control) and largely ignores social forecasting: how well models understand and predict how real social events unfold over time. The authors aim to fill this gap by systematically measuring LLMs’ social event reasoning and forecasting performance, while controlling for memorization of past events.

Method: They build SocietyBench, an end-to-end pipeline: (1) start from a short description of a real social event; (2) collect news and social media data across five platforms; (3) distill this into a date-indexed timeline that separates factual events from public opinion; (4) generate, at each cutoff date, a set of auditable forecasting questions. They anonymize the data via a three-phase procedure that replaces named entities and shifts all dates by an event-specific constant to construct a counterfactual but structurally equivalent social world. Models are evaluated on two independent 0–100 axes: probability calibration and temporal accuracy. They test six frontier LLMs, three agent frameworks built on one base model, and two heuristic, non-LLM baselines on five different events (Chinese and English), with 125 prediction points in total.

Result: The best-performing LLM achieves a score of 75/100, only moderately better than a trivial 50-point baseline, revealing substantial headroom. Calibration and temporal accuracy often diverge: models can be well-calibrated but poor at timing, or vice versa. The three agent frameworks do not outperform their underlying base LLM, and the simple, model-free heuristics perform worse than all LLMs. There are large per-event performance gaps (up to 21.4 points on a single axis), indicating that performance is highly event-dependent.

Conclusion: SocietyBench exposes significant limitations in current LLMs’ ability to forecast social event trajectories, even after controlling for memorization, and shows that calibration and timing are distinct dimensions of performance. The substantial per-event variability and failure of agent wrappers or simple heuristics to close the gap underscore the need to evaluate social reasoning across diverse events. The benchmark, together with released timelines, question banks, ground truth, and scoring code, provides a standardized platform for future work on social forecasting with LLMs.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs), and the agents built on top of them, are now benchmarked heavily on whether they can finish a task -- fix a bug, drive a browser, operate a GUI. A complementary social ability, namely how well a model understands and forecasts the way real social events unfold, has barely been measured. We introduce SocietyBench, an end-to-end benchmark that takes a one-line event topic, collects Web news and social-media posts across five platforms, distills them into a date-indexed timeline that keeps factual events and a public-opinion layer separate, and then turns every cutoff date on that timeline into an audited bank of forecasting questions. Questions are scored on two orthogonal 100-point axes: probability calibration and temporal accuracy. Before any model sees a timeline, a three-phase procedure replaces every named entity and shifts every date by a per-event constant, turning a real arc into a counterfactual social world -- structurally identical to what happened, but stripped of the surface labels a model could match against pre-training memory. On five heterogeneous events and 125 prediction points in Chinese and English editions, the strongest of six frontier LLMs reaches only 75.0 out of 100, against a trivial anchor of 50. The two axes come apart: a model can be calibration-strong but time-weak, or the reverse. Three agent frameworks built on a shared base model fail to improve on that base, and two model-free heuristics trail every LLM. Per-event gaps reach 21.4 points on a single axis, which is our main argument for evaluating on several events rather than one. All anonymized timelines, question banks, ground truth, and scoring code are released.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [88] [ISEE: Interactive Semantic Enrichment for Database Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02604)
*Yuan Tian,Yiru Chen,Rakesh R. Menon,Zifan Liu,Ting Cai,Fei Wu,Anudeep Chimakurthi,Prashanthi Ramamurthy,Sridevi Aishwariya Ganesan,Kun Qian,Yunyao Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents ISEE, an interactive system that scores and improves data field descriptions by incorporating user domain knowledge, thereby boosting LLM-agent performance on downstream tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents struggle with data-related tasks when field descriptions are ambiguous or incomplete, because crucial semantic context often resides in users’ undocumented domain knowledge. This limits performance on tasks like entity linking and data sense-making, creating a need for a way to systematically capture and enrich these semantics.

Method: The authors design ISEE, an Interactive Semantic Enrichment system that: (1) evaluates the quality of a data field description via a scoring mechanism; (2) interactively elicits and organizes domain knowledge from users; and (3) guides users to collaboratively refine and enrich field semantics. They validate ISEE via a combination of user study, automated user simulation, quantitative evaluation, and case studies.

Result: Empirical results from multiple evaluations show that ISEE meaningfully improves the quality of field descriptions, reduces users’ cognitive load during the enrichment process, and leads to better performance of LLM-based agents on downstream tasks such as entity linking.

Conclusion: Interactive semantic enrichment with users’ domain knowledge is an effective way to address incomplete and ambiguous data field descriptions. ISEE provides a practical framework that both eases the user’s cognitive burden and improves LLM-agent performance on data-centric downstream tasks.

Abstract: LLM-based agents are increasingly being deployed for data-related tasks, including data sense-making, exploration, and retrieval. However, their performance heavily depends on the clarity and completeness of data semantics. In practice, many field descriptions remain ambiguous or incomplete, as much of the essential context (e.g., the meaning of a customized field) originates from users' domain knowledge and is rarely documented publicly. This gap restricts the agents' task performance in downstream tasks, such as entity-linking. To bridge this gap, we introduce a novel and comprehensive Interactive SEmantic Enrichment system (ISEE). Given a data field description, ISEE measures its quality through a scoring system, gathers domain knowledge, and collaboratively enriches the semantics with users. Through a user study, automated user simulation, quantitative evaluation, and case study, we demonstrate that ISEE significantly reduces cognitive load, improves description quality, and enhances downstream task performance.

</details>


### [89] [Self-Organising Digital Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02606)
*Marcello Barylli,Gabriel Béna,Alexander Mordvintsev,Eleni Nisioti,Sebastian Risi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a learning-based, self-organising approach to make digital circuits fault-tolerant, inspired by biological plasticity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical digital fault tolerance relies on fixed, static mechanisms like redundancy and error-correcting codes, which can be inefficient and inflexible when facing diverse or unforeseen hardware faults. Biological systems, however, can dynamically reorganise to maintain function after damage. The authors aim to bring such adaptive, self-repairing capabilities to digital circuits, enabling them to assemble and reconfigure themselves in response to failures.

Method: They model circuit configuration and maintenance as a meta-learning problem on graphs. A topology-masked Transformer takes as input the circuit graph and configures the Lookup Tables (LUTs) of Boolean gates. Building on Neural Cellular Automata concepts, the system learns a policy that explores the degenerate space of Boolean functions to satisfy a computation task. The same learned policy can both assemble functional circuits from scratch and adapt configurations in response to different fault types, including permanent hardware defects and transient soft errors, while being trained on smaller circuits and tested on larger ones.

Result: The approach can self-assemble working circuits without pre-defined configurations and can quickly reroute logic around unseen, permanent hardware faults. For soft errors, the method provides near-perfect functional recovery, achieving over 99.99% accuracy even for damage levels significantly larger than those used in training. It also generalises across scale: when applied to much wider circuit graphs than those encountered during training, performance not only holds but actually improves.

Conclusion: Self-Organising Digital Circuits, implemented via a topology-aware Transformer meta-learner, can endow digital hardware with biologically inspired self-organisation and repair capabilities. This adaptive approach offers robust fault tolerance to both permanent and transient errors and generalises to larger circuit scales, suggesting a promising new direction for resilient digital hardware design that moves beyond static redundancy and fixed error-correction schemes.

Abstract: Fault tolerance in classical computing has traditionally relied on static strategies like hardware redundancy and error-correcting codes. Biological systems, in contrast, exhibit adaptive plasticity, maintaining function through dynamic re-organisation around damage. Inspired by this principle, we introduce Self-Organising Digital Circuits, framing functional logic generation and maintenance as a meta-learning problem on graphs. Our architecture employs a topology-masked Transformer that configures the Lookup Tables (LUT) of a circuit's Boolean gates. Extending the pattern-generation paradigm of Neural Cellular Automata (NCA), it navigates the degenerate Boolean search space to satisfy a computational task, rather than regenerating a fixed target state. We demonstrate that it can self-assemble functional circuits from scratch and rapidly re-route logic around permanent, previously unseen hardware faults. For soft errors, the policy achieves near-perfect recovery (>99.99\% accuracy) from damage sizes far exceeding training conditions. We further observe generalisation across circuit scales: accuracy improves on graphs substantially wider than those seen during training. This work bridges the principles of biological self-organisation with the practical domain of digital hardware.

</details>


### [90] [Beyond the Hivemind: Escaping LLM Homogeneity via Meta-Persona Anchoring and Sequential Temperature Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02618)
*Tairan Fu,Javier Conde,Carlos Arriaga,Gonzalo Martínez,Pedro Reviriego,Javier Coronado-Blázquez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper addresses semantic collapse in LLMs and proposes a two-stage sampling framework (Meta-Persona Anchoring + Filtered Temperature Scaling) that significantly increases diversity of generated responses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs tend to exhibit an 'Artificial Hivemind' effect where outputs converge to a narrow consensus, even on open-ended questions and with high-temperature sampling. This limits creative and typological diversity, leading to high inter-response similarity (0.80-0.90). The paper aims to mitigate this by systematically increasing diversity while maintaining coherence.

Method: The method consists of two main components. (1) Meta-Persona Anchoring: before answering, the model is prompted to self-select a distinctive, idiosyncratic persona that serves as an anchor and starting point for generation. (2) Filtered Temperature Scaling (FTS): a two-stage sampling sieve where Top-p filtering is first applied to ensure grammatical and semantic plausibility, and then a very high temperature (T ≥ 4.0) is applied over the filtered candidates to aggressively diversify token choices. The system is evaluated on INFINITY-CHAT using ~20B parameter open-weight models, measuring semantic similarity between multiple responses to the same prompt via cosine similarity.

Result: Using the proposed framework, average pairwise cosine similarity across responses drops from about 0.85 to about 0.65. A majority of questions fall below a 0.7 similarity threshold, indicating substantially reduced semantic convergence and closer approximation to human-like diversity in responses.

Conclusion: Meta-Persona Anchoring combined with Filtered Temperature Scaling effectively counters semantic collapse in LLMs, leading to more diverse and creative outputs without sacrificing basic grammatical quality. The authors release an open-source implementation to facilitate adoption of their approach for deploying more varied AI systems.

Abstract: Recent studies have identified an ``Artificial Hivemind'' effect in Large Language Models (LLMs) causing models to converge on a narrow, homogenized consensus even for open questions. This semantic collapse limits the diversity of AI, resulting in high inter-response similarity ($\approx 0.80-0.90$) even under high-temperature sampling. In this paper, we propose a novel mitigation framework to increase diversity: Meta-Persona Anchoring combined with Filtered Temperature Scaling (FTS). Our approach utilizes a two-stage generation process: first, the model is prompted to self-select a unique, idiosyncratic persona to anchor its starting point; second, we apply a dual-stage sampling sieve, utilizing Top-$p$ filtering to preserve grammatical validity followed by extreme temperature scaling ($T \ge 4.0$) on the surviving candidates to explore the broadened probability distribution. We evaluate our method using the INFINITY-CHAT dataset on state-of-the-art open weight models under $\sim$20B parameters. Our results demonstrate a significant reduction in semantic convergence, with average pairwise cosine similarity dropping from ($\approx 0.85$) to ($\approx 0.65$). Our scheme achieves a majority of questions below the 0.7 threshold, effectively reducing the gap between artificial mode collapse and human-level typological diversity. We provide our implementation as an open-source framework to enable more diverse and creative AI deployments.

</details>


### [91] [PULSE: An Executable Contract Language for Spatiotemporal Knowledge Graph Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02630)
*Dongxu Yang,Ziyi Liang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PULSE is a typed runtime language that localizes operational contracts for knowledge graph execution, enforcing strong safety and trace properties, and is validated against formal proofs and large empirical datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing knowledge graph engineering spreads execution semantics and constraints across multiple artifacts (e.g., ontologies, SHACL, workflows), so the actual operational contract is implicit and external, making safety reasoning and behavior reproducibility hard. The authors want a way to localize and formalize the execution contract—how evidence, state, and events are handled—in one place, with clear guarantees.

Method: They design PULSE, a language inspired by Object-Process Methodology, where modes represent operational roles (not modal logic). The language and runtime enforce a fixed contract covering non-overwrite of evidence, branch isolation, grounded timers, guarded state changes, and ordered events. They define a core calculus and prove an effect-confinement lemma plus six safety properties. Using Lean 4, they check kernel analogues for positions, evidence, clocks, monitors, atomicity, and branch retention. They implement the runtime, generate GeoSPARQL/SOSA/SHACL as views, and evaluate via tests, bounded model checks, Lean/Python integration cases, and reproducing a cold-chain trace in other formalisms. They further run large-scale temporal-trace comparisons (including NOAA IBTrACS) against independent workflows and standards-based systems.

Result: The implementation passes 88 tests, 3,534 bounded checks, and 32 combined Lean/Python kernel/runtime cases. Two independent first-author implementations (standards composition and Sismic statechart) reproduce the same cold-chain trace as PULSE. Across 37,440 generated temporal traces, PULSE matches the behavior of a separate workflow and detects ten single-field mutant differences, showing sensitivity to contract violations. On a large real-world NOAA IBTrACS dataset, PULSE agrees with GEOS and an event sweep on over 1.47 million transition-zone pairs and thousands of events. GeoSPARQL-based probes give additional coverage metrics for the interfaces.

Conclusion: The study provides evidence that localizing the execution contract in PULSE yields strong safety properties, trace parity with independent systems, and effective detection of contract-breaking mutations, at least for the tested fragment and datasets. The authors are cautious not to claim that PULSE is superior in general or more usable than alternatives; they limit their claims to contract localization, safety reasoning, and behavioral equivalence for the examined scenarios.

Abstract: Knowledge graph engineering often distributes accepted state, observations, constraints, processes, and hypothetical scenarios across artifacts whose combined execution contract remains external. We present PULSE, an Object-Process-Methodology-inspired language that localizes four operational roles and their write effects in one typed runtime. Here, modes denote operational roles rather than modal or deontic logic. The implemented contract fixes evidence non-overwrite, branch isolation, grounded multi-subject timers, guarded state change, and declaration-ranked event ordering over time and space; an external runner still decides whether evidence becomes an authoritative move. GeoSPARQL, SOSA, and SHACL remain generated views. A core calculus gives an effect-confinement lemma and six safety properties. Lean 4 checks kernel analogues for positions, evidence, clocks, monitors, atomicity, and branch source retention; 88 tests, 3,534 bounded checks, and 32 Lean/Python runtime-kernel cases bound the implementation claim to the checked cases. First-author implementations of a standards composition and a separate Sismic statechart reproduce the tested cold-chain trace. Across 37,440 generated temporal traces, PULSE matches a separate workflow and distinguishes ten single-field mutants. On the complete NOAA IBTrACS since1980 subset it agrees with GEOS and an event sweep on 1,476,290 transition-zone pairs, including 4,800 sampled and 12,831 duration-qualified events. Project-specific GeoSPARQL probes measure interface coverage. Overall, the results support contract localization, safety arguments, and trace parity for the tested fragment; language superiority and usability remain outside the evaluation.

</details>


### [92] [HyperAgent: Planning and Acting over Tool-Schema Hypergraphs for Tool-Use LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02650)
*Zian Zhai,Xingyu Tan,Gaowang Zou,Xiaoyang Wang,Wenjie Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HyperAgent models tools and their data schemas as a hypergraph to enable more reliable, efficient tool-use planning and execution by LLM agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents need to call external tools to solve real-world tasks, but current approaches rely mainly on the LLM’s implicit reasoning over textual tool descriptions. This leads to inefficient tool selection, brittle multi-step compositions, and poor adaptation to changing execution context. The paper is motivated by the need for a more explicit, structured representation of tool relations that supports reliable planning and dynamic execution in complex environments.

Method: The authors introduce a Tool–Schema Hypergraph where each tool is modeled as a hyperedge connecting input-schema nodes to output-schema nodes, explicitly encoding preconditions and effects. On top of this, they propose HyperAgent, a framework that: (1) builds a task-relevant subgraph (tool context graph) from the global hypergraph; (2) uses this context to construct a schema-aware Task DAG for high-level planning; and (3) during execution, dynamically realizes each DAG subtask by creating a state-conditioned tool support graph via deficit-oriented expansion, which detects missing requirements and incrementally pulls in producer tools based on the current agent state. They evaluate HyperAgent on AppWorld, comparing it against existing LLM-based tool-use agents.

Result: On the AppWorld benchmark, HyperAgent achieves higher task completion rates than baseline agents while performing fewer redundant API/tool calls, fewer LLM invocations, and using fewer tokens overall. This demonstrates both effectiveness (better success) and efficiency (lower resource consumption) in complex tool-use scenarios.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling tools and their data schemas as a hypergraph enables more reliable, schema-aware planning and dynamic execution for LLM agents. HyperAgent’s hypergraph-guided planning and deficit-based expansion mechanism improves both performance and efficiency over standard LLM-centric tool orchestration, suggesting that structured tool-schema representations are a promising direction for scalable, robust tool-use agents.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on external tools to complete complex real-world tasks. However, reliable tool-use planning remains challenging due to the limitations of implicit reasoning and the evolving nature of real-world execution environments. Existing tool-use agents typically rely on LLMs to infer tool compositions from textual descriptions, which can lead to inefficient exploration and unreliable execution in complex tasks. To address these challenges, we model tool relations at the schema level and construct a directed Tool--Schema Hypergraph, in which tools are represented as hyperedges from their required input-schema nodes to their output-schema nodes. Furthermore, we propose HyperAgent, a Tool--Schema Hypergraph-guided framework for dynamic planning and execution. Given a task, HyperAgent first extracts a task-relevant tool context graph and uses it to guide the construction of a schema-aware Task DAG. During execution, HyperAgent dynamically realizes each subtask by constructing a state-conditioned tool support graph through deficit-oriented expansion, which identifies unresolved requirements and retrieves supporting producer tools according to the current agent state. Experiments on AppWorld demonstrate that HyperAgent improves task completion performance while reducing redundant API calls, LLM interactions, and token consumption compared with existing agent baselines.

</details>


### [93] [Explainable AI for the EU Right to Explanation: A Systematic Review of the Law-XAI Translation Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02699)
*Benjamin Fresz,Elena Dubovitskaya,Marco F. Huber*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Systematic review of Explainable AI (XAI) work on the EU Right to Explanation, revealing major gaps in legal-technical integration and proposing a framework and blueprint to make this right technically realizable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Consequential automated decisions in domains like finance, hiring, and healthcare trigger a legal Right to Explanation under EU law (GDPR, AI Act). However, it is unclear whether existing XAI methods can actually satisfy these legal obligations in practice, which directly affects individuals’ ability to understand and contest algorithmic decisions. There is also a new, finalized regulatory landscape after the 2024 AI Act, including Art. 86, that has not been comprehensively mapped against current XAI research.

Method: The authors conduct a systematic literature review focused on XAI in relation to the EU Right to Explanation, specifically targeting Art. 15(1)(h) GDPR, Art. 86 AI Act, and related legal instruments. Starting from 2643 records collected via a deliberately broad search, they screen and narrow down to 57 full-text papers, then further identify 19 works with substantive integration of both legal and technical perspectives. They qualitatively analyze these papers to identify trends, legal misinterpretations, and conceptual confusions, and then derive a conceptual framework and operational blueprint.

Result: The review finds that only a small subset of XAI papers truly integrate both legal and technical dimensions of the Right to Explanation. Three systematic problems emerge: (1) Most works misidentify the correct GDPR legal basis for explanations; (2) Very few engage with the CJEU’s Dun & Bradstreet judgment, likely due to its recency, even though it is central to interpreting the Right to Explanation; and (3) Many papers conflate explanation form (who the explanation is for) with explanation content (what must be conveyed according to legal purpose). The authors articulate this confusion as an Addressee/Purpose Framework, outline a four-phase operationalization blueprint, and list six open research questions.

Conclusion: Existing XAI research is not yet aligned with the concrete legal requirements of the EU Right to Explanation under the GDPR and AI Act. Misunderstandings of the legal basis, lack of engagement with key case law, and conceptual conflation of explanation form and content hinder the development of technically sound, legally compliant explanation methods. The proposed Addressee/Purpose Framework and four-phase blueprint offer a structured path toward bridging law and XAI, but significant interdisciplinary work is still needed; otherwise, the Right to Explanation will remain a largely formal, unenforceable requirement without viable technical means of implementation.

Abstract: When algorithms make or influence consequential decisions---about loan eligibility, hiring, or healthcare---EU law grants affected individuals a Right to Explanation. Yet whether (and how) Explainable AI (XAI) can satisfy this right in practice remains poorly understood, with direct implications for individuals' ability to contest automated decisions that affect their lives. This paper presents a systematic literature review of XAI in the context of the EU Right to Explanation, with particular focus on Art. 15(1)(h) GDPR, Art. 86 AI Act (AIA), and related instruments. We consider papers published from 2024 onwards, as the final version of the AIA was published in July 2024---with Art. 86 being added late. From 2643 initial records identified by a deliberately broad search, we review 57 full texts, of which only 19 papers demonstrate substantive integration of both legal and technical perspectives, showing gaps in the interdisciplinary synthesis of the current regulatory framework. We document three problematic patterns across the corpus: Most misidentify the GDPR legal basis; few engage with the CJEU's Dun & Bradstreet judgment (likely due to publication timing); and the distinction between explanation form (governed by addressee) and content (governed by legal purpose) is often conflated. We conceptualize this as the Addressee/Purpose Framework, propose a four-phase blueprint for operationalization, and identify six concrete open research questions. Without further progress, the Right to Explanation risks remaining a formal obligation without a technically realizable path to compliance.

</details>


### [94] [Predictive Set Theory: A Generative Framework for Cognitive Architecture with Operationalized Core Mechanisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02704)
*Yiyang Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces Predictive Set Theory (PST), a formal, set-theoretic framework that reconstructs cognition as a predictive, consistency-maintaining system under uncertainty, deriving core cognitive functions from minimal primitives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing predictive processing and Bayesian cognitive science lack precise operational definitions of predictions, error correction, and the generative formation of discrete cognitive objects. There is a need for a first-principles, generative formalism that specifies how a cognitive system can maintain internal consistency while acting in uncertain, risky environments, and that can address foundational paradoxes and limitations in existing theories.

Method: The paper defines a minimal cognitive architecture based on set theory: a sensor modeled as an identity function, set-theoretic state refresh operations, and three basic types of reference chains (reference, counter-reference, semi-reference). From these primitives, it formally derives higher-level cognitive constructs such as state sequences, demand, comparison, efficiency, and finite-horizon probabilistic planning, positioning the framework as a design specification rather than a neural model.

Result: The framework of Predictive Set Theory is shown to generate core cognitive functions from a small set of primitives and to provide principled treatments of classical issues like Russell’s paradox, Gödelian incompleteness in cognition, the grounding of negative feedback, and how film editing is cognitively comprehended. It yields a unified, generative account of prediction, error handling, and consistency maintenance under uncertainty.

Conclusion: PST is presented as an original and complete formal design specification for predictive, consistency-maintaining cognitive systems operating under uncertainty and irreversible risk. It complements and extends predictive processing and Bayesian approaches by offering explicit generative structure for predictions, referents, and error responses, while resolving or reframing several classical logical and cognitive puzzles. The main contribution is to establish the conceptual and formal foundations of PST in the academic record.

Abstract: Predictive processing theories portray the brain as a hierarchical prediction engine that minimizes prediction error, yet they lack operational definitions for the structure of a "prediction," the standardized response to a prediction error, and the mechanism that maintains consistency across successive updates. Bayesian cognitive science attempts to subsume all uncertainty under probabilistic belief updating, but it presupposes a closed hypothesis space and provides no generative account of how the objects over which probabilities are distributed become discrete, identifiable referents in the first place. This paper introduces Predictive Set Theory (PST), a formal generative framework that reconstructs cognitive architecture from first principles. PST anchors cognition in a minimal set of operations---a sensor formalized as an identity function, set-theoretic state refresh, and three fundamental forms of reference chains (reference, counter-reference, and semi-reference)---and rigorously derives core cognitive functions including state sequences, demand, comparison, efficiency, and finite-horizon probabilistic planning. Rather than modeling neural mechanisms, PST constitutes a design specification for any system that must maintain internal consistency while acting under incomplete information and irreversible risk. The framework offers novel resolutions to classical problems such as Russell's paradox, the cognitive status of Gödelian incompleteness, the grounding of negative feedback, and the comprehension of film editing. The primary purpose of this paper is to establish, through the public academic record, the originality and completeness of the Predictive Set Theory framework.

</details>


### [95] [Towards a new paradigm of scientific discovery with socialized artificial intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02775)
*Xinjie Yao,Xingxin Xu,Xiyuan Gao,Zhoupeng Guo,Kunlong Yang,Dengyu Zhao,Siqi Zhao,Zhihe Fan,Yichen Dong,Xin Li,Jiekang Feng,Jiahe Wu,Sen Wang,Beiming Yu,Kejia Zhao,Ruipu Zhao,Jiaqi Zhou,Heyang Li,Jianjun Chen,Anbo Dai,Xin Liu,Zhengtao Yu,Qinghua Hu,Pengfei Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes BLAZE, a new paradigm where AI serves as infrastructure that connects literature, agents, and continuous experimentation to create a socialized, traceable, and cumulative scientific discovery process.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Science has progressed through stages (observation, theory, computation, data-intensive methods), but now faces the challenge of organizing rapidly expanding knowledge, reasoning, and evidence into a coherent, cumulative discovery process. Existing approaches are fragmented and focus on isolated tasks rather than an end-to-end, socially organized research lifecycle.

Method: The authors conceptualize and define BLAZE (Bridging Literature, Agents, and Zero-gap Experimentation) as an organizational framework in which AI agents, persistent knowledge bases, empirical validation tools, and human researchers are integrated into a continuous lifecycle. BLAZE treats AI as an infrastructure for coordinating literature review, hypothesis generation, experiment design and execution, and collective verification, rather than as a collection of point tools.

Result: The abstract is conceptual rather than empirical; it claims that organizing humans and machines within a shared scientific process via BLAZE makes discovery more traceable, reproducible, and cumulative, while maintaining human creativity, judgment, and responsibility. It positions BLAZE as enabling “socialized scientific intelligence.”

Conclusion: Scientific intelligence arises from ongoing interaction among knowledge, hypotheses, experiments, and collective verification, not from computation alone. By structuring these elements through BLAZE, science can enter a new era where AI extends the scale, depth, and continuity of collective inquiry, without replacing human discovery but augmenting and coordinating it.

Abstract: Scientific discovery has advanced through successive transformations in the organization of knowledge. Observation and experimentation established the empirical foundations of science. Theory made it possible to derive general principles from particular phenomena. Computation extended inquiry into systems beyond direct observation, while data-intensive methods opened new spaces of pattern and prediction. Science now confronts a different frontier. The central challenge is no longer simply to produce more information, but to organize expanding knowledge, reasoning, and evidence into a coherent process of discovery. Here, we introduce Bridging Literature, Agents, and Zero-gap Experimentation (BLAZE), a paradigm of socialized scientific intelligence. BLAZE conceives AI not as an assistant for isolated research tasks, but as an organizational infrastructure for scientific discovery. It connects persistent knowledge, collective reasoning, empirical validation, and human judgment within a continuous research lifecycle, transforming fragmented activities into a cumulative process of inquiry, criticism, and revision. The central premise of BLAZE is that scientific intelligence does not arise from computation alone. It emerges from the sustained interaction among knowledge, hypotheses, experiments, and collective verification. By organizing humans and machines within a shared scientific process, BLAZE makes discovery more traceable, reproducible, and cumulative while preserving human creativity, judgment, and responsibility. Socialized scientific intelligence may provide a foundation for the next era of science. Its purpose is not to replace human discovery, but to extend the scale, depth, and continuity of collective scientific inquiry.

</details>


### [96] [BAP-SQL: Budget-Aware Observation Planning for Agentic Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02876)
*Chong Peng,Pin Qian,Su Wang,Yihang Chen,Varun Sah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces BAP-SQL, a budget-aware planning framework for agentic text-to-SQL systems that controls how observations are formed to improve performance under tight token and compute budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In agentic text-to-SQL, the model issues exploratory or broad SQL queries whose results and intermediate tool calls consume limited context and computation budgets. If the agent spends too many tokens or database work on low-value observations, later important evidence may not fit into context, and post-hoc compression cannot recover missing rows or wasted work. Existing approaches lack explicit budget-aware control over how and when to query, leading to inefficiencies and degraded performance under tight budgets.

Method: BAP-SQL introduces an intermediate budget-control stage that explicitly manages observation formation. It estimates the “risk” or cost-benefit profile of a candidate SQL query, rewrites the SQL when beneficial to focus on likely high-value results, and relies on an external runtime shield to enforce hard limits such as row caps or timeouts. This planning layer is integrated with several backbone models (a general 4B model, a FINER-SQL-specialized 4B model, and a 7B model) so that the agent policy can take budget-aware actions while querying databases.

Result: Across different backbone models (general 4B, FINER-SQL 4B, and 7B), BAP-SQL improves success rates under tight token budgets relative to standard supervised fine-tuned (SFT) baselines. On a primary benchmark derived from BIRD, BAP-SQL achieves gains of 3.4 and 3.6 percentage points over matched SFT models while using 4.5% and 5.0% fewer tokens, respectively. Experiments with matched retraining and transfer across tasks indicate that these improvements come specifically from policy-visible planning and budget-sensitive fallback strategies, rather than incidental factors.

Conclusion: BAP-SQL demonstrates that treating observation formation in agentic text-to-SQL as an explicit budget-control problem yields better performance and token efficiency under tight budgets. The gains are strongest when resources are constrained, decreasing as model capacity and available budget grow, and even reversing under the loosest budget setting. While BAP-SQL does not reduce the amount of underlying database work, it more effectively allocates token and observation budget, highlighting the importance of budget-aware planning in tool-using language agents.

Abstract: Tool-using agents do not merely consume observations: their actions determine what arrives next. In agentic text-to-SQL, a broad query can spend context and database work before useful evidence appears, while post-hoc compression cannot recover omitted rows or expended work. We present BAP-SQL, which treats observation formation as a budget-control stage: it estimates query risk, rewrites SQL when useful, and delegates hard limits to an independent runtime shield. Across general 4B, specialized FINER-SQL 4B, and 7B backbones, BAP-SQL improves tight-budget success. On the primary BIRD-derived setting, it gains 3.4/3.6 percentage points over matched SFT while using 4.5/5.0% fewer tokens. Matched retraining and task-level transfer associate the gain with policy-visible planning and budget-sensitive rescue. The benefit attenuates as model capability and budget increase, reverses at the loosest setting, and does not reduce database work.

</details>


### [97] [VeriTrace: Human-Like Temporal Exploration Completes Agentic Action Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02878)
*Yu-Tung Liu,Cunxi Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces VeriTrace, a multi-agent system that achieves 100% accuracy in automated Verilog RTL generation by giving its debugging agent a fully flexible way to explore signal traces over time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent systems for Verilog RTL generation plateau at about 95% accuracy because their debugging agents are constrained to limited sets of signals or fixed time windows, turning debugging into shallow pattern matching instead of true root-cause analysis. The authors aim to remove this accuracy ceiling by enabling more human-like, hypothesis-driven debugging.

Method: The authors design VeriTrace, a multi-agent system whose key component is an Inspector agent equipped with a complete debugging action space. This agent can independently choose which signals to inspect, what time intervals to query, and how many iterative steps of analysis to perform. They define this capability as Agentic Temporal Exploration, allowing iterative hypothesis formation, evidence gathering from waveforms, and refinement, all built on top of a Claude Sonnet 4.0 backbone. They compare VeriTrace against reproduced baselines on standard benchmarks, notably VerilogEval-V2.

Result: VeriTrace attains 100% Pass@1 on VerilogEval-V2, becoming the first system to reach perfect functional correctness on this benchmark. Using the same Claude Sonnet 4.0 backbone for fair comparison, it improves performance by 5.1 percentage points over the strongest reproduced baseline, indicating that enhanced debugging agency, not just model capacity, closes the remaining performance gap.

Conclusion: Giving debugging agents full control over what signals and time windows to explore, along with iterative analysis depth, enables human-like exploratory verification and eliminates the prior performance ceiling in Verilog RTL generation benchmarks. Agentic Temporal Exploration is thus a key design principle for future multi-agent code generation and debugging systems.

Abstract: Large language models have shown promise for automated Verilog RTL generation, yet state-of-the-art multi-agent systems plateau at ~95% accuracy on standard benchmarks. We trace this ceiling to an incomplete debugging action space: existing systems restrict which signals the agent can inspect, which time windows it can query, or both, reducing debugging to pattern matching on a narrow, predetermined view of circuit behavior rather than hypothesis-driven root-cause analysis. We present VeriTrace, a multi-agent system whose Inspector agent operates over a complete debugging action space, with independent control over signal selection, time-window bounds, and iteration depth. This capability, which we term Agentic Temporal Exploration, enables the agent to form hypotheses about failure causes, query the waveform for evidence, and refine its understanding iteratively, mirroring the exploratory process of human verification engineers. VeriTrace achieves 100\% Pass@1 on VerilogEval-V2, the first system to attain perfect functional correctness on this benchmark. On a shared Claude Sonnet 4.0 backbone, VeriTrace outperforms the strongest reproduced baseline by +5.1%, demonstrating that debugging agency closes the final accuracy gap.

</details>


### [98] [Interpreting Black-Box Large Language Models with Sentence-Level Energy Landscapes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02879)
*Maryam Rezaee,Pooriya Safaei,Maryam Asgarinezhad,Fatemeh Seyyedsalehi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build a sentence-level, post-hoc interpreter for black-box LLMs using an energy-based surrogate model so it can attribute which prompt sentences most influenced a chosen piece of generated text, without repeatedly querying the closed LLM API.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Closed, proprietary LLMs are only accessible via APIs and expose almost no internal information, making it difficult to interpret why a model produced a certain output. Existing attribution or explanation methods often assume access to model internals or require many extra API calls. This creates a gap for practical, efficient, model-agnostic interpretability tools tailored to commercial LLMs.

Method: They train an Energy-Based Model (EBM) as a surrogate that learns the consistency relationship between prompts and responses of the target LLM. This EBM defines an energy landscape over prompt–response pairs. Then they train a lightweight interpreter network guided by this landscape to estimate, at the sentence level, how much each prompt sentence contributes to a specific target portion of the LLM’s response. Training is global across many prompt–response examples, so the interpreter learns generalizable patterns rather than being fitted per instance. After training, the interpreter can be used offline, without additional calls to the original LLM.

Result: Experiments indicate that the EBM surrogate closely matches the target LLM’s behavior on prompt–response consistency. Using this surrogate, the interpreter can correctly rank and highlight which prompt sentences are most responsible for chosen target outputs in the LLM’s generation, outperforming baselines on attribution quality metrics.

Conclusion: A model-agnostic, post-hoc, sentence-level interpreter for black-box LLMs is feasible by learning an EBM surrogate and a dedicated interpreter network. This approach yields practical attribution explanations for closed-source LLMs with reduced dependence on repeated API queries, and it generalizes across diverse inputs by capturing global generation patterns instead of only local, instance-specific behavior.

Abstract: The widespread adoption of proprietary Large Language Models (LLMs) accessed strictly through closed APIs has created a critical challenge for responsible deployment: a fundamental lack of interpretability. To address this, we propose a model-agnostic, post-hoc attribution interpreter operating at the sentence level. Our approach trains an Energy-Based Model (EBM) as a surrogate to capture the LLM's internal conceptual consistency between prompts and responses. This energy landscape guides the training of a lightweight interpreter network. Uniquely, our interpreter operates as a standalone tool; once trained, it quantifies the influence of prompt sentences on a user-specified target output without requiring further API queries to the LLM. By globally training a local interpreter across diverse inputs, our framework captures broader generation patterns and mitigates instance-specific biases. Experiments demonstrate that our EBM accurately simulates the target LLM, allowing the interpreter to effectively identify the prompt sentences most influential in generating specific target outputs.

</details>


### [99] [Hypercubes, Hyperplanes, and Constraint-Induced Complexity Collapse in Atomic Concept Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02930)
*Irene Tsapara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how logical complexity of higher-arity atomic concepts (relations) is distributed inside the space of all ground instances, using the geometry of hypercubes and hyperplanes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In higher-arity concept learning, the instance space of all possible tuples (ground atoms) becomes very large and seems uniformly complex. The author wants to understand where the true logical complexity resides, to better characterize learnability and structure, rather than treating all regions of this space as equally hard.

Method: Model the space of ground r-ary atoms as an r-dimensional hypercube and analyze its hyperplanes (slices defined by coordinate equalities). Use tools from model theory and the author’s prior higher-dimensional framework: canonical simple concepts, minimal orderings, and representative reductions. Study elementary equivalence classes of instances on each hyperplane and how they collapse under reductions. Work out in detail the binary case and then the ternary (3D) hypercube as explicit examples.

Result: All hyperplanes except the full diagonal decompose into only finitely many elementary-equivalence classes, with a bound that does not depend on term depth, showing strong collapse of logical complexity there. In contrast, the full diagonal is exceptional: the number of classes on it grows without bound. In three dimensions, one already sees orthogonal families of hyperplanes, partial diagonals, and the uniquely complex full diagonal. The analysis yields a taxonomy of hyperplane behaviors in higher dimensions linked to the reduction machinery.
