Conclusion: Logical complexity in higher-arity atomic concept learning is highly localized rather than evenly distributed across the instance hypercube. Most hyperplanes are logically simple (finite, bounded variety of patterns), while the full diagonal concentrates unbounded complexity. This geometric-logical understanding aligns with viewing learning in terms of constrained hypothesis spaces and structured classification, suggesting that focusing on specific regions (like diagonals) is key to understanding and controlling complexity.

Abstract: We revisit higher-arity atomic concept learning through the geometry of hypercubes and hyperplanes of ground instances. Our starting point is the observation that the ambient r-dimensional hypercube of ground atoms is not structurally uniform. Its logical complexity is organized by hyperplanes: every hyperplane other than the full diagonal collapses into finitely many elementary-equivalence classes, with a bound independent of the term depth, while the full diagonal is exceptional and its class count grows without bound. This asymmetry is not merely geometric. It reflects the reduction-theoretic structure of the concepts themselves. Building on a higher-dimensional framework developed in the author's earlier work, we reinterpret these results through canonical simple concepts, minimal orderings, and representative reductions. This yields a taxonomy of hyperplane behavior in higher dimensions and shows that complexity is localized rather than spread uniformly through the instance space. The paper includes a fully worked binary case, an explicit treatment of the ternary hypercube, and an unpacked account of the reduction machinery that drives the collapse. The three-dimensional case already exhibits the essential phenomenon of orthogonal families, partial diagonals, and the exceptional full diagonal. This geometric-logical perspective clarifies where complexity is concentrated in atomic concept learning and suggests a modern interpretation in terms of constrained hypothesis spaces and structured classification.

</details>


### [100] [When Compression Scores Cannot Decide: Information Boundaries for Group-Robust LLM Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02940)
*Andrew Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies why statistically reliable pruning or compression scores for language models can still pick suboptimal model variants, and develops a framework and practical methods to reduce worst-group performance degradation when making pruning or routing decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Even when a compression or pruning metric is highly reliable (e.g., high split-half reliability), it can still systematically choose worse models or endpoints, especially for minority or hard groups. This creates hidden damage in worst-case groups despite good global averages. The authors want to understand and quantify the gap between what such statistics can distinguish and the true performance differences across groups, and then design selection procedures that protect worst-group behavior.

Method: They introduce an information-interface perspective that formally characterizes which distinctions a given statistic (e.g., pooled or group-aggregated moments) can or cannot support. Using this, they derive a conic law describing the exact ‘pooling price’—the worst-case damage incurred when using pooled statistics instead of more granular ones, for positive linear damage under fixed candidates and with certain moment constraints (diagonal and full PSD covariance). They construct three two-world counterexamples and an exact observation-fiber radius to show what remains unresolved when relying on pooled or group-local moments and curvature along a reference path. Empirically, they test several allocation and selection schemes (uniform, depth-based, model-specific masks) on dense LLMs and an MoE model (OLMoE), using perplexity and KL metrics over worst groups and router traces for routing direction prediction.

Result: They show that a highly reliable dense pruning score (0.906 split-half) still led to a predicted 16.1% gain but actually chose an endpoint that was 6.0% and 7.7% worse than two baselines, illustrating the failure of naive score-based selection. Their theory gives exact bounds on how much information is lost by pooling and what damage this can cause. A group-resolved diagonal moment statistic can recover the broad ordering of damage across candidates (Spearman 0.9239) but not the fine-grained ranking. In practice, replacing balanced uniform allocation with a simple coarse depth allocation reduces worst-group perplexity inflation by 12.6–20.9% across three dense LLMs. Further, using model-specific complete-mask endpoint selection improves over those references by 2.7–8.0%. For OLMoE, router traces can predict which singleton expert direction to move in significantly better than a strong relabeling baseline (114/192 vs. 81/192). Restricting decisions to a finite menu on one layer still achieves held-out worst-group KL reductions of 13.7% and 7.2%.

Conclusion: Compression and routing decisions built only on pooled or naively aggregated statistics can be both highly reliable and systematically wrong in selecting endpoints, particularly in their impact on worst groups. By analyzing the information interface of statistics and quantifying the pooling price, one can design selection mechanisms that either rely on full candidate endpoints or on validated uniform guarantees, with uncertainty calibrated per comparison. Simple, implementable allocation and endpoint-selection schemes, informed by this framework, substantially lower worst-group damage without needing exhaustive evaluation of every candidate.

Abstract: A reproducible compression statistic can still select the wrong candidate. A dense pruning score with 0.906 split-half reliability predicted a 16.1% gain. Its selected endpoint was 6.0% and 7.7% worse than two controls. We model the gap through information interfaces that delimit which distinctions each statistic supports. For equal-weight groups, a conic law gives the exact pooling price for positive linear fixed-candidate damage, including diagonal and full PSD second moments. Three two-world constructions and an exact observation-fiber radius characterize what pooled moments, group-local moments, and reference-path curvature leave unresolved. A group-resolved diagonal recovers broad damage order (Spearman 0.9239) while fine order remains weak. Relative to balanced uniform allocation, a coarse depth allocation cuts worst-group perplexity inflation by 12.6--20.9% across three dense LLMs. Model-specific complete-mask endpoint selection improves over those references by 2.7--8.0%. In OLMoE, router traces predict singleton direction (114/192 versus 81/192 under the strongest relabeling). Finite-menu decisions on one layer yield held-out worst-group KL reductions of 13.7% and 7.2%. Local measurements construct candidates. Selection is licensed by complete candidate endpoints or a validated uniform guarantee, with uncertainty calibrated to every comparison.

</details>


### [101] [On the missing data layer and a potential solution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02949)
*Francis F Daniel,Mauro Ibañez,Francis Perelman,Marian Basti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies missing AI data infrastructure in Latin America and proposes DataHub, a task-first, ontology-based platform to centralize and scale dataset discovery, management, and reuse.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Latin America lacks two key AI infrastructure layers—datasets and benchmarks. For datasets, there are two core issues: (1) discovery – existing Latin American AI datasets are scattered across many platforms without a unified index, making them hard to find and reuse; and (2) supply – even if all existing datasets were perfectly indexed, their total volume would still be insufficient to support frontier AI development. The authors are motivated to create infrastructure that both organizes what exists and encourages the systematic growth of high-quality, region-relevant AI datasets.

Method: The paper proposes DataHub, a task-first data infrastructure structured via a simple ontology: /<task?>/<domain?>/<language?>. This structure organizes datasets by what they are used for (task), the subject area they belong to (domain), and the language they are in. On top of this ontology, DataHub provides mechanisms for: (1) dataset discovery through a shared index and search over tasks/domains/languages; (2) rich metadata to describe datasets; (3) contribution workflows so users can upload and document new datasets; (4) licensing management so datasets can be used in a legally compliant way; and (5) tools for reuse, such as standardized formats or APIs to access the data. The approach is conceptual and infrastructural rather than algorithmic.

Result: From the abstract alone, concrete empirical results (e.g., number of datasets onboarded, performance gains in models) are not specified. The result is primarily the design and proposal of DataHub as a structured, task-first, ontology-driven data infrastructure tailored to Latin American AI needs, promising improved dataset discoverability, coordination, and eventual scaling of data supply.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that addressing Latin America’s AI infrastructure gap, starting from the dataset layer, requires a principled, task-first data hub. By organizing datasets under the ontology /<task?>/<domain?>/<language?> and providing mechanisms for discovery, metadata, contribution, licensing, and reuse, DataHub can consolidate currently fragmented resources and lay the groundwork for increasing both the quality and volume of AI datasets in the region. This is positioned as a necessary step toward enabling frontier AI development that reflects and serves Latin American contexts.

Abstract: Latin America is missing two foundational layers of AI infrastructure: the dataset layer and the benchmark layer. This paper targets the dataset layer. The dataset layer faces two compounding problems: discovery and supply. Latin American AI datasets exist but are scattered across platforms with no shared index. Even with perfect indexing, the total volume would remain far below what frontier AI development requires. We propose DataHub: a task-first data infrastructure organized through the ontology /<task?>/<domain?>/<language?>, with mechanisms for dataset discovery, metadata, contribution, licensing, and reuse.

</details>


### [102] [Neurosymbolic Reasoning with Incremental Knowledge for Sample Efficient Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02993)
*Subrat Prasad Panda,Blaise Genest,Arvind Easwaran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a neurosymbolic hierarchical RL framework that incrementally updates symbolic knowledge for planning and uses learned low-level skills, greatly improving sample efficiency in sparse-reward navigation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flat RL struggles in sparse-reward, long-horizon tasks because it needs many samples and lacks built-in structure. Standard HRL helps but typically hard-codes high-level structure and cannot leverage newly learned knowledge during training, so symbolic reasoning only becomes useful after extensive exploration, hurting sample efficiency.

Method: Introduce Incremental Knowledge (InK) within a neurosymbolic HRL architecture: a symbolic high-level planner (e.g., D* search) operates over an updatable world/knowledge representation, while low-level goal-conditioned neural policies learn motion primitives with reward shaping. They also propose Belief World Tree Search to perform optimal symbolic planning under prior (possibly uncertain) knowledge about the world.

Result: On navigation benchmarks, the proposed InK-based neurosymbolic HRL significantly improves sample efficiency compared with flat RL and conventional, fixed-knowledge HRL baselines. The framework successfully exploits both incrementally acquired knowledge and prior world knowledge for more effective planning and control.

Conclusion: Incrementally updating symbolic knowledge within a neurosymbolic HRL system allows effective symbolic planning much earlier in training and greatly improves sample efficiency in sparse-reward, long-horizon tasks. Belief World Tree Search further enables optimal planning when prior world knowledge is available, making the approach broadly useful for knowledge-aware RL and navigation problems.

Abstract: (Flat) Reinforcement Learning (RL) agents face significant challenges in environments with sparse rewards that require long-horizon reasoning. A compelling approach to improve sample efficiency is to incorporate knowledge into learning and decision-making. In standard Hierarchical RL (HRL), knowledge is encoded in a fixed, non-updatable form, such as architectural choices, and remains unchanged throughout learning. With fixed HRL, reasoning with incremental knowledge learned during exploration is impractical before sufficient environmental knowledge is acquired, leading to poor sample efficiency. In this work, we propose neurosymbolic HRL with {\em Incremental Knowledge (InK)}: symbolic high-level components perform {\em symbolic planning} (e.g. using $D^*$) on an updatable representation of current InK, while low-level goal-conditioned neural modules learn motion primitives through experience using reward shaping. Experiments on navigation tasks demonstrate that incorporating InK substantially improves sample efficiency. Additionally, to perform {\em optimal} symbolic planning given {\em prior} knowledge about the world, we develop Belief World Tree Search. The code is available at https://github.com/CPS-research-group/ink_bwts.

</details>


### [103] [On the missing benchmarks layer and a potential solution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02996)
*Francis F Daniel,Mauro Ibañez,Francis Perelman,Marian Basti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that Latin America lacks a crucial AI benchmark infrastructure and proposes EvalsHub/LatamBoard as an open, regional, task-first benchmarking platform for auditing and optimizing AI systems for local needs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Latin America currently depends on foreign AI systems and benchmarks that do not reflect regional social, economic, and institutional realities. This creates two critical problems: (1) public institutions cannot independently audit AI systems against local social and regulatory requirements, and (2) companies lack appropriate benchmarks to optimize AI systems for economically relevant, region-specific use cases. As AI becomes critical infrastructure, this absence undermines sovereignty, trust, and performance of deployed systems.

Method: The paper proposes the design of EvalsHub, with LatamBoard as its first Latin American instance: an open, task-first benchmark infrastructure where diverse stakeholders (universities, public institutions, professional communities, companies) can submit, run, and maintain evaluations. The platform supports benchmarking across models, workflows, and agents, enabling repeated re-runs as new systems or updates are released. Its design emphasizes openness, shared governance, and incentive structures to encourage continual contribution and maintenance.

Result: As this is likely a position or proposal paper based on the abstract, concrete empirical results are not described. Instead, the main result is a conceptual and architectural blueprint for a regional benchmark layer—EvalsHub/LatamBoard—that can be implemented to enable independent audits and region-relevant optimization of AI systems in Latin America.

Conclusion: Establishing a shared, open benchmark layer is presented as foundational for native AI development in Latin America. By enabling region-specific auditing and performance optimization, EvalsHub/LatamBoard aims to restore auditability and provide clear optimization direction for AI systems treated as critical infrastructure. The authors conclude that once such an infrastructure is built, it can serve as a long-term, reusable evaluation backbone—“built once, measured forever”—supporting continuous testing as new AI models and system updates emerge.

Abstract: Latin America is missing a foundational layer for native AI development: the benchmark layer. The benchmark layer does two things no other layer can - it audits AI systems against regional social requirements and it directs AI optimization in economically relevant environments. Without it, public institutions cannot independently evaluate foreign AI systems, and companies cannot optimize AI systems to solve local problems with SOTA performance. The cost of the missing layer is dual: a loss of auditability and a loss of optimization direction over a technology that is increasingly critical infrastructure. We propose an EvalsHub, with LatamBoard as its first regional instance - an open, task-first benchmark infrastructure where universities, public institutions, professional communities, and companies can publish, execute, compare, and maintain evaluations across models, workflows, and agents. Built once, measured forever - re-run by institutions as new AI systems ship and by industry teams after every system change. Open by design and incentive-driven by construction.

</details>


### [104] [ProPRL: Property-Aware Prerequisite Relation Learning in Educational Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03006)
*Xinghe Cheng,Jiapu Wang,Chaobo He,Ruihai Dong,Quanlong Guan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes ProPRL, a framework for learning prerequisite relations between concepts that adaptively fuses multiple educational evidence sources and enforces irreversibility of prerequisite direction, achieving state-of-the-art performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing prerequisite relation learning methods typically treat the task as standard link prediction and cannot: (1) adaptively combine different types of educational evidence (e.g., content structure vs. student behavioral data) at the level of each concept pair; and (2) explicitly discourage having high confidence in both directions of a concept pair, which contradicts the inherently directional nature of prerequisites. There is a need for a model that better uses complementary views of data and respects the irreversibility of prerequisite relations.

Method: The authors propose ProPRL, a Property-aware Prerequisite Relation Learning framework. It constructs a concept-resource hypergraph to model relationships between concepts and learning resources, and a directed learning-behavior graph to capture learner behavior sequences. It learns concept representations from both graphs, using direction-preserving personalized propagation on the behavior graph to aggregate multi-hop, direction-sensitive behavioral evidence. For each candidate ordered concept pair, a Pair-conditioned Gate adaptively weights and fuses the two learned views to produce a pair-specific representation. Finally, an Irreversibility Constraint acts as an anti-symmetry regularizer that penalizes the model when it assigns high confidence to both directions of the same concept pair.

Result: On multiple real-world educational datasets, ProPRL outperforms existing methods on the task of prerequisite relation learning, achieving state-of-the-art performance metrics compared to prior models.

Conclusion: By jointly leveraging a concept-resource hypergraph and a directed learning-behavior graph, adaptively fusing them per concept pair via a Pair-conditioned Gate, and enforcing an Irreversibility Constraint to model the anti-symmetric nature of prerequisites, ProPRL more accurately learns prerequisite relations and establishes a new state of the art on several educational datasets. The framework demonstrates the benefit of property-aware modeling and direction-preserving behavioral evidence aggregation for educational relation learning.

Abstract: Prerequisite relation learning is central to adaptive instruction, yet existing methods often formulate it as conventional link prediction, limiting their ability to adaptively integrate complementary educational evidence for individual candidate pairs and to discourage contradictory reverse predictions. We propose ProPRL, a Property-aware Prerequisite Relation Learning framework. ProPRL first learns complementary concept representations from a concept-resource hypergraph and a directed learning-behavior graph, where direction-preserving personalized propagation aggregates multi-hop behavioral evidence. It then employs a Pair-conditioned Gate to adaptively weight and fuse the two views for each candidate ordered concept pair. Finally, an \textit{Irreversibility Constraint} introduces an anti-symmetry regularizer that penalizes simultaneously high confidence in both directions of the same concept pair. Experiments on multiple real-world educational datasets show that ProPRL achieves state-of-the-art performance on prerequisite relation learning.

</details>


### [105] [UrbanAgent: A Tool-Augmented Agent for Cross-System Urban Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03018)
*Jiayu Cao,Xingyuan Zeng,feiyu Li,Zhijing Huang,Xujie Yuan,Rongxiang Chen,Shimin Di,Libin Zheng,Jian Yin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Urban-Agent, a tool-augmented LLM framework for executing complex cross-system urban tasks, and Urban-Eval, a benchmark to evaluate both task success and execution quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Urban digital services are fragmented and lack interoperability, making it hard for residents to complete everyday tasks that require coordinating multiple systems. Existing platforms, urban foundation models, and assistants handle only parts of these tasks and fail to reliably turn complex natural-language requests into executable, multi-system workflows. There is also no good benchmark for evaluating such cross-system urban task agents in terms of both outcome and how the task is executed.

Method: The authors propose Urban-Agent, a framework that augments a large language model with a suite of tools (code execution, API calls, Model Context Protocol). The system operates in an adaptive closed loop: it first clarifies missing information from the user, then grounds its tool use in live observations of external systems, and finally ensures that the produced answer is consistent with observed evidence and task constraints. They also design Urban-Eval, a benchmark of cross-system urban requests that measures not only whether the task is completed but also the quality of execution, including tool coverage, validity of dependency ordering among tools, and traceability of evidence used.

Result: In experiments on Urban-Eval, Urban-Agent achieves a 71% task success rate, outperforming the strongest baseline by 10 percentage points. This performance advantage is consistent across multiple underlying LLM backbones such as GPT-5-mini, Gemini-2.5-flash, DeepSeek-V4-flash, and Qwen3-235B-A22B.

Conclusion: Urban-Agent effectively bridges complex natural-language urban requests and executable cross-system workflows by combining LLM reasoning with a rich tool set and an adaptive closed loop interaction pattern. Urban-Eval provides a more realistic and comprehensive benchmark for tool-augmented urban agents by jointly assessing task outcomes and execution properties. Together, they advance the development and evaluation of practical, cross-system urban assistants.

Abstract: Modern cities rely on an increasing number of digital services to operate, but residents' daily needs are still difficult to meet. Services are fragmented and have little interoperability, placing a heavy operational burden on users. Existing digital platforms, urban foundation models, and intelligent assistants each address only isolated aspects of an urban task. But they struggle to reliably convert complex natural-language requests into executable cross-system workflows. We propose Urban-Agent, a tool-augmented agent framework for cross-system urban tasks. It couples the cognitive and reasoning capabilities of a large language model with a tool-set supporting code execution, API calls, and Model Context Protocol. Through one adaptive closed loop, it clarifies missing information before acting, grounds tool use in live observations, and aligns the final response with observed evidence and task constraints. To address the evaluation gap, we introduce Urban-Eval, a benchmark specifically designed for cross-system urban request. Unlike prior benchmarks that assess either general tool use or urban knowledge and reasoning, Urban-Eval evaluates both task results and execution quality, including required tool coverage, dependency validity, and evidence traceability. Experimental results indicate that Urban-Agent reaches a 71% task success rate, 10 points above the strongest baseline. This lead holds across GPT-5-mini, Gemini-2.5-flash, DeepSeek-V4-flash, and Qwen3-235B-A22B.

</details>


### [106] [LoCA: Forward-Only LLM Tuning after One-Shot Calibration with Local Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03020)
*Linhan Xia,Rui Liu,Zhaofeng Zhang,Yihao Wang,Binrui Shen,Shengxin Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes LoCA, a method to replace repeated backpropagation-based adaptation of LLMs with a single calibration step plus forward-only tuning, achieving lower loss and significantly reduced compute/memory vs. LoRA on multiple benchmarks and model sizes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods like LoRA reduce trainable parameters but still require full backpropagation through a frozen backbone at every training step, which needs backward-capable hardware, activation storage or recomputation, and high memory/latency. The authors want a way to adapt large models to small distribution shifts without repeated global credit assignment, enabling cheaper hardware, lower memory, and faster per-step updates.

Method: They introduce Local Credit Assignment (LoCA), a two-stage procedure. First, a one-time probe backward pass (calibration) is run: for each transformer block, they learn a low-rank linear map from the final prediction error to a local hidden-state correction, effectively approximating how global error affects that block. Second, during actual adaptation, they no longer backprop through the backbone: they use the precomputed local maps to turn forward activations into block-wise regression targets and then fit low-rank adapters via closed-form ridge regression solves. This yields forward-only training after the initial calibration, with shared, scale-normalized candidate adapter structures reused across model sizes.

Result: On five discriminative benchmarks using Qwen2.5 models from 0.5B to 14B parameters, LoCA outperforms LoRA in 16 of 25 task–scale settings in terms of evaluation cross-entropy. In addition, LoCA reduces measured full-run GPU peak memory (including calibration) by 26–29% compared to LoRA, reduces CPU steady-state memory after calibration by 36–52%, and cuts per-pass time by 43–48%. The same normalized adapter candidate set transfers across all tested Qwen2.5 sizes and to SmolLM2-1.7B.

Conclusion: LoCA shows that global credit assignment for small-shift adaptation can be effectively amortized into a single calibration step, after which efficient, forward-only tuning is possible. This approach can replace repeated backpropagation in parameter-efficient fine-tuning while achieving competitive or better accuracy and substantially lower compute and memory costs, and it generalizes across multiple model scales and architectures.

Abstract: Parameter-efficient post-training reduces the number of trainable parameters, but still requires repeated end-to-end backpropagation through the frozen backbone. Every adaptation step therefore needs backward-capable hardware and must store or recompute activations. We ask whether this repeated backward chain can be replaced by a one-time calibration. We introduce Local Credit Assignment (LoCA), a two-stage method for small-shift adaptation. One probe backward pass fits a low-rank map at each transformer block from the final prediction error to a local hidden-state correction. LoCA then reuses these maps to form blockwise regression targets from forward activations and fits low-rank adapters with closed-form ridge solves. No further backbone backward pass is required. We evaluate LoCA on five discriminative benchmarks with Qwen2.5 models from 0.5B to 14B. In 16 of 25 reported task--scale comparisons, LoCA yields lower evaluation cross-entropy than the corresponding LoRA run. Its measured full-run GPU peak, including calibration, is 26--29\% lower than LoRA's. After calibration, its CPU steady-state memory is 36--52\% lower and its per-pass time is 43--48\% lower. A shared scale-normalized candidate set is reused across all tested Qwen2.5 sizes and on SmolLM2-1.7B. LoCA thus amortizes global credit assignment into one calibration and enables later forward-only tuning when repeated backpropagation is impractical. The code associated with this paper is available \href{https://github.com/Xia12121/LoCA}{here}.

</details>


### [107] [DiffImaginE: Imagine to Verify Entity Types with Diffusio](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03025)
*Feng Zhang,Feiyu Han,Rongxin Yang,Yang Liu,Yancheng Chen,Rui Wang,Yingguang Yang,Tian Xueyun,Chongyang Zhang,Hao Zheng,Xu Kefu,Congjing Ran,Fuhai Chen,Bin Chong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes DiffImaginE, a diffusion-based probabilistic verifier for multimodal named entity recognition that replaces deterministic imagine-and-compare scoring with a likelihood-grounded diffusion scoring mechanism, achieving better performance on standard Twitter MNER benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal NER approaches that "imagine and compare" compress each (span, type) pair into a single predicted visual prototype, losing the diversity of possible visual evidence and lacking explicit probabilistic semantics for how well a type explains the observed image. The authors want a verification framework that (1) preserves uncertainty and variability in visual realisations, and (2) yields scores with a clear likelihood-based interpretation so that competing entity types can be compared more rigorously.

Method: They formulate multimodal NER type verification as conditional latent diffusion inference. For each candidate text span and entity type, they use span-localised visual features and a type-conditioned denoiser operating in a standardised latent space. The denoiser predicts the noise injected into the latent, and the denoising error is used as an ELBO-consistent surrogate for the type-conditional negative log-likelihood. Architecturally, they keep a standard multimodal encoder and replace the prior deterministic verifier with a classifier-free-guided diffusion scorer, trained with Min-SNR weighting. They directly supervise diffusion scores as classification logits, learn how to aggregate scores across noise levels, and employ antithetic sampling to reduce Monte Carlo variance when comparing type hypotheses. They also analytically study how classifier-free guidance affects the induced posterior over types and under what conditions antithetic sampling lowers variance at the same denoiser cost.

Result: On the Twitter-2015 and Twitter-2017 multimodal NER datasets, DiffImaginE consistently outperforms a strong deterministic ImaginE baseline that uses the same encoder, auxiliary losses, and evaluation setup. The improvements are validated through ablation studies that isolate the contributions of diffusion-based scoring, classifier-free guidance, Min-SNR weighting, noise-level aggregation, and antithetic sampling, as well as through paired statistical significance tests, showing the gains are robust rather than due to chance.

Conclusion: DiffImaginE demonstrates that casting multimodal entity-type verification as conditional diffusion inference yields more principled, likelihood-grounded scores than deterministic prototype-based verifiers and translates into empirically better MNER performance. The work suggests that diffusion-based, classifier-free-guided scoring with appropriate training and variance-reduction strategies is a promising direction for tasks where model predictions must reflect how well different hypotheses explain complex multimodal evidence.

Abstract: Multimodal named entity recognition (MNER) determines whether each candidate span and entity-type hypothesis is supported by joint textual and visual evidence. Existing imagine-and-compare verifiers map each (span, type) pair to one predicted visual feature, compressing diverse visual realisations into a single prototype and providing a compatibility score without explicit probabilistic semantics. We introduce DiffImaginE, which formulates MNER type verification as conditional latent diffusion inference. Given span-localised visual evidence, a type-conditioned denoiser predicts noise injected into its standardised latent. The resulting denoising error provides an ELBO-consistent surrogate for type-conditional negative log-likelihood, allowing competing type hypotheses to be ranked by how well they explain the observation. DiffImaginE retains a standard multimodal encoder stack and replaces the deterministic verifier with a classifier-free-guided diffusion scorer trained using Min-SNR weighting. We directly supervise per-type diffusion scores as classification logits, learn aggregation across noise levels, and use antithetic sampling to reduce Monte Carlo comparison variance. Our analysis shows that classifier-free guidance sharpens the induced type posterior and characterises when antithetic pairing reduces variance at equal denoiser cost. Experiments on Twitter-2015 and Twitter-2017 show consistent gains over a matched deterministic ImaginE control under the same encoder, auxiliary objectives, and evaluation protocol, supported by ablations and paired significance tests.

</details>


### [108] [Evaluating Counterfactual Sensitivity to Patient Information in Medication-Safety Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03028)
*Zhitian Hou,Yuhang Liu,Pengkai Wang,Zeyu Liu,Guanghao Zhu,Zheng Liu,Shuo Cai,Congkai Xie,Zhijie Sang,Kun Zeng,Hongxia Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces MedPIC-Bench, a benchmark to test if models correctly apply patient-specific medication safety rules, especially under counterfactual changes in patient data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing medication-safety evaluations use static, isolated scenarios where models can succeed by memorizing drug-risk associations without genuinely reasoning about whether patient-specific conditions for a rule are met. This hides whether models can adjust safety decisions when patient information changes.

Method: The authors build MedPIC-Bench, a benchmark of 467 questions with source-verifiable recommendations and expert validation. Each guideline-following question has a paired counterfactual version where controlled changes in patient information alter whether a medication-safety rule applies. Questions are annotated along six clinical and reasoning dimensions, and 28 large language models (medical-specific, general, and proprietary) are systematically evaluated on both original and counterfactual questions, including analysis of their rationales and accuracy patterns.

Result: Across all 28 models, performance consistently drops on counterfactual questions: mean accuracy decreases from 63.6% on original questions to 45.1% on counterfactuals. Models do relatively well when a clear, explicit patient attribute matches a familiar contraindication but perform poorly when reasoning requires narrowing, qualifying, or withdrawing a safety warning based on nuanced patient data. Many model explanations mention the changed patient information, yet their final answers often retain the same safety judgment as in the original scenario. Medical-specific models also show this vulnerability and, on average, perform worse on counterfactuals than general LLMs.

Conclusion: MedPIC-Bench provides a way to quantitatively measure whether models can conditionally apply medication-safety rules based on patient-specific information, especially under counterfactual changes. The findings show that static medication-safety accuracy overestimates model reliability for real-world, patient-specific decision-making, and highlight that even medical-tuned LLMs lack robust conditional reasoning about when safety rules should or should not apply.

Abstract: Applying a valid medication-safety rule when its patient-specific conditions are not met can produce an incorrect decision. Existing medical evaluations largely use isolated and fixed scenarios. A model may therefore answer correctly by recalling a drug-risk association without showing that it used patient information to decide whether the rule applies. To address this gap, we introduce MedPIC-Bench, a benchmark of source-verifiable recommendations and expert-validated questions for patient-specific medication-safety reasoning. It combines guideline-following questions with paired counterfactual questions in which a controlled change in patient information changes whether a rule applies. The benchmark contains 467 questions annotated along six clinical and reasoning dimensions. Across 28 medical-specific, general, and proprietary LLMs, every model performs worse on counterfactual questions, with mean accuracy falling from 63.6\% to 45.1\%. Models perform well when an explicit patient attribute directly signals a familiar contraindication, but struggle when patient information must narrow or withdraw a safety warning. Model rationales often acknowledge the changed patient information, yet the final answers retain the previous safety judgment. This vulnerability persists among medical-specific LLMs, whose average CF performance trails that of general LLMs. MedPIC-Bench therefore makes conditional rule application measurable and highlights the limitations of static medication-safety accuracy for assessing patient-specific reliability.

</details>


### [109] [CastFSR: A Fast--Slow--Reflect Agentic Reasoning Framework for Context-Aware Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03031)
*Xiaoyu Tao,Mingyue Cheng,Bokai Pan,Chuang Jiang,Huanjian Zhang,Tian Gao,Yaguo Liu,Qi Liu,Enhong Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces CastFSR, an agentic LLM-based framework for context-aware time series forecasting using a Fast–Slow–Reflect workflow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional time series forecasting methods and recent LLM-based approaches struggle to fully leverage rich, evolving contextual information and to explicitly reason about how such context affects future dynamics while maintaining temporal and domain consistency. There is a need for a principled, interpretable workflow that can select relevant contexts, reason over them, and refine forecasts under constraints, and that can be deployed efficiently on smaller models.

Method: CastFSR formulates context-aware forecasting as a three-stage agentic process: (1) Fast thinking: profile the observed time series and select lightweight numerical forecasters to produce a data-driven prior forecast; (2) Slow deliberation: retrieve contextual evidence, adaptively choose informative look-back windows, and use LLM-based reasoning to adjust forecasts based on how identified contexts modulate the future; (3) Reflection: iteratively refine and validate forecasts with respect to temporal structure, contextual cues, and domain-specific constraints. The framework can run training-free with off-the-shelf LLMs or be operationalized by a two-stage supervised fine-tuning plus reinforcement learning procedure that distills the orchestration policy into compact LLMs.

Result: On multiple public time-series datasets, CastFSR yields consistently better forecasting accuracy than representative baseline models, including both classical numerical forecasters and recent LLM-based or context-aware methods, demonstrating the effectiveness of its Fast–Slow–Reflect agentic design and orchestration distillation strategy.

Conclusion: By combining fast numerical forecasting, slow contextual reasoning, and reflective consistency checks in an agentic LLM framework, CastFSR advances context-aware time series forecasting, offering improved predictive performance and a practical path to deploy such capabilities in smaller, more efficient language models.

Abstract: Time series forecasting is fundamental to decision-making in complex systems, where future dynamics are influenced not only by historical observations but also by evolving contextual features. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have extended forecasting beyond numerical extrapolation toward context-aware reasoning. However, existing approaches often lack explicit mechanisms to identify relevant contexts, reason about their impacts, and validate forecasts against temporal and domain constraints. In this work, we propose CastFSR, an agentic framework that formulates context-aware forecasting as a Fast--Slow--Reflect workflow. In fast thinking, CastFSR profiles observations and selects lightweight forecasters to construct a data-driven forecast prior. In slow deliberation, it retrieves contextual evidence, adaptively determines informative look-back windows, and reasons about how contexts reshape future dynamics. In reflection, it iteratively refines forecasts to ensure temporal, contextual, and domain consistency. CastFSR supports both training-free inference with off-the-shelf LLMs and efficient deployment through a two-stage SFT and reinforcement learning strategy that transfers its orchestration capability to compact LLMs. Extensive experiments on public datasets demonstrate that CastFSR consistently outperforms representative baselines. Our code is available at https://github.com/Xiaoyu-Tao/CastFSR.

</details>


### [110] [TraceCAD: Trace-Guided Repair for Agentic CAD Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03062)
*Fengxiao Fan,Jingzhe Ni,Fan Sang,Xiaolong Yin,Yu Liu,Ruofeng Tong,Min Tang,Peng Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TraceCAD is a recovery layer for LLM-based CAD agents that uses persistent state and localized search to diagnose and repair faulty parametric CAD programs, improving geometric quality and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based CAD agents can generate parametric CAD programs, but their iterative correction loops forget which requirements are already satisfied, which operations failed, and what repairs were previously tried. This loss of evidence leads to inefficient and unreliable repairs of CAD models.

Method: TraceCAD maintains a persistent state that links user-requested features, modeling operations, failure evidence, and candidate repair outcomes. It diagnoses likely faulty operations, performs a bounded and localized search of edits within the dependency region of those operations, executes candidate repairs with checks that validate geometry and preserve constraints, and stores both successful and failed repairs in a reusable skill memory for future use.

Result: On DeepCAD-derived benchmarks including 200-model ablation studies and a 1K-model comparison, TraceCAD matches or exceeds competing methods in geometric quality metrics such as IoU, Chamfer distance, and Hausdorff distance. Ablation experiments show that removing persistent state almost halves recovery performance, and removing localized search more than doubles geometric regression errors and doubles the number of calls to the CAD code agent. Pre-initializing the skill store on separate training models further reduces retries, token usage, and latency.

Conclusion: Persistent state, localized repair search, and reusable skill memory are key to robustly repairing LLM-generated CAD programs. By explicitly tracking the relationships between requirements, operations, and repair attempts, TraceCAD improves final CAD geometry quality and repair reliability while reducing computational cost, demonstrating an effective design pattern for CAD-focused LLM agents.

Abstract: LLM-based CAD agents produce executable parametric programs, but their correction loops may lose evidence about satisfied requirements, faulty operations, and prior repairs. We introduce TraceCAD, a recovery layer that links requested features, modeling steps, failure evidence, and candidate outcomes as persistent state. TraceCAD diagnoses likely faulty operations, searches bounded edits in their dependency regions, validates candidates through execution and preservation checks, and retains successful and failed repair outcomes in reusable skill memory. On DeepCAD-derived benchmarks with 200-model ablations and a 1K-model comparison, TraceCAD achieves competitive geometric quality in terms of IoU, Chamfer distance, and Hausdorff distance. Removing persistent state nearly halves recovery score; removing localized search more than doubles geometric regression and doubles code-agent invocations. Initializing the skill store on disjoint training models further reduces retries, token cost, and latency. These results demonstrate that persistent, localized, and reusable recovery improves final CAD quality and repair reliability.

</details>


### [111] [Getting the Parameters Right: A Difficulty-Graded Benchmark and Probe-Guided Training for LLM Tool Calls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03071)
*Guoyao Yu,Xiaoqing Sun,Ziqi Huang,Shaojing Fan,Zhongyi Zhang,Xiaomeng Hu,Xiaobo Xue,Yangyang Shi,Xiong Xiao,Yang Song,Biao Lyu,Rong Wen,Xing Li,Qinming He,Shunming Zhu,Zhenguang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They discover that LLM hidden states strongly signal whether a tool-call parameter will be correct, and use a simple linear probe during training and inference to greatly improve parameter filling accuracy for tool use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tool use is crucial for LLM agents, but most work focuses on choosing tools and call order, not on accurately filling tool parameters. In complex domains like cloud networking, even top models often fail to generate correct parameters, limiting real-world reliability. The authors want a principled way to improve parameter correctness without redesigning the base model.

Method: They analyze LLM hidden states during parameter generation and train a linear probe that predicts if a candidate parameter value will be correct. Using this probe, they propose: (1) Probe-filtered Bootstrapped Training (PBT), which filters self-generated tool calls to retain only reliable ones for fine-tuning; and (2) Probe-Guided Reranking (PGR), which uses the probe at inference time to score and select better candidate parameter values. They also construct ParamBench, a benchmark of real cloud-network APIs with instances categorized into 5 difficulty levels based on structural and reasoning complexity of parameters.

Result: The linear probe can reliably distinguish correct from incorrect parameter values from hidden states. Applying PBT and PGR to 5 open LLMs on ParamBench and 6 other benchmarks significantly improves parameter generation accuracy, boosting average exact match from 19.7% to 59.6%.

Conclusion: Hidden states of LLMs encode a strong correctness signal for tool-call parameters, and simple probing can turn this into a powerful control signal. Their unified probe-guided framework, together with the ParamBench benchmark, substantially enhances parameter filling for tool use and provides a practical path to more reliable LLM-based agents in complex API domains.

Abstract: Large language model agents derive much of their capability from tool use. Existing research on tool use has largely focused on selecting the right tool and orchestrating the order of calls. However, correctly filling the parameters of a tool call is equally critical for successful execution and has received far less attention. In domains such as cloud networking, even frontier models correctly complete fewer than half of tool calls. Inspired by recent analyses showing that LLM hidden states encode rich information about model predictions, we discover that while the model generates a parameter value, its hidden state contains a strong correctness signal: a simple linear probe can accurately predict whether the value will be correct. Based on this observation, we propose a unified probe-guided framework with two complementary approaches: probe-filtered bootstrapped training (PBT), which uses the probe to filter reliable self-generated calls for fine-tuning, and probe-guided reranking (PGR), which uses the probe to select better candidates during inference. To support systematic evaluation, we release ParamBench, a benchmark built from real cloud-network APIs that categorizes every instance into five difficulty levels according to parameter nesting depth, cross-parameter dependencies, and the reasoning required to derive values from earlier calls. Extensive experiments across 5 open models on ParamBench and 6 external benchmarks demonstrate that our method substantially improves parameter generation, raising the average exact match from 19.7% to 59.6%.

</details>


### [112] [AI Agent Economics: Can Autonomous Economic Behavior Emerge among AI Agents under Minimal External Conditions?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03076)
*Lingyun Zhang,Shang Shang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether complex economic relations can emerge endogenously among LLM-based agents when they are only given executable mechanisms (for work, transfer, elections, allocation) but no social or economic strategies, showing that such relations arise from rights and resource consequences rather than scenario labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most multi-agent AI studies embed agents in predesigned games or markets, so it is unclear if observed economic structures are truly emergent or simply inherited from the scenario. The authors want to isolate how economic organization forms when agents only have access to low-level mechanisms and constraints, and to define a more principled notion of "AI Agent Economics" grounded in production, allocation, and institutional effects on future actions.

Method: They propose a two-stage experimental framework. First, a no-production boundary test checks behavior in environments where agents cannot do productive tasks, only communicate and govern resources. Second, they run 24 independent six-agent worlds instantiated on GPT and DeepSeek models, giving agents executable mechanisms for work, transfers, elections, and allocation under scarcity. They systematically vary whether productive tasks exist, how allocation authority is implemented, and whether energy is material or symbolic, while keeping some interfaces (e.g., elections) fixed.

Result: In environments without productive tasks, agents engage in communication and governance over resource provision but exhibit almost no meaningful transfers. When verified productive work exists and access to tasks is scarce, richer economic behaviors emerge: transfers, loans, promises of future access, vote-buying via access offers, and more sophisticated allocation strategies. With a fixed election interface, granting executable allocation authority to elected agents leads to greater differentiation among agents, fewer failed allocations, and less long-term exclusion. When energy resources are made symbolic (removing real continuation consequences), support for others' continuation disappears, though competition for task access remains.

Conclusion: Economic organization in multi-agent LLM systems arises from the structure of executable rights and the real resource consequences that shape agents' future feasible actions, not from narrative roles or prompt wording. This implies that understanding and auditing "AI Agent Economics" requires examining the concrete mechanisms and institutional constraints embedded in agent environments, motivating governance audits focused on the mechanisms that actually control agents' future options.

Abstract: Multi-agent studies commonly place AI agents in predefined games, markets, or roles, making it difficult to distinguish endogenous economic organization from behavior inherited from the scenario. We ask whether economic relations emerge when agents receive executable mechanisms for work, transfer, elections, and allocation but no prescribed social or economic strategy. We define AI Agent Economics as systems of production, allocation, consumption, exchange, and institutions that alter agents' future feasible actions. We develop a two-stage framework comprising a no-production boundary test and 24 independent six-agent worlds across GPT and DeepSeek. Without productive tasks, agents communicate and govern resource provision but show no substantive inter-agent transfer activity. With verified work and scarce task access, transfers, loans, access promises, vote-for-access exchanges, and allocation strategies emerge. Holding the election interface fixed, executable allocation authority increases differentiation while reducing failed allocation and prolonged exclusion. When energy becomes symbolic, continuation support disappears, yet competition over task access persists. These findings show that organization follows executable rights and resource consequences rather than role labels or prompt language, and motivate governance audits of the mechanisms that actually constrain agents' future actions.

</details>


### [113] [Don't Peek at the Answer: Outcome-Masked Group Relative Policy Optimization for Label-Free RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03119)
*Yongshi Ye,Liang Zhang,Yidong Chen,Xiaodong Shi,Biao Fu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OM-GRPO is a label-free reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards method for LLM reasoning that avoids reward collapse by masking gradients on answer tokens and using contrastive reward estimation, matching ground-truth-based training and improving test-time training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RLVR methods improve LLM reasoning but usually require ground-truth answers, limiting scalability. Label-free, voting-based RLVR removes this need but suffers from collapse because the same answer-level consensus signal is used to compute rewards and to optimize the token-level policy, leading models to overfit answer tokens instead of reasoning steps. The paper aims to design a scalable, label-free RLVR framework that decouples reward estimation from policy optimization to maintain stable training and better reasoning performance.

Method: The authors propose OM-GRPO, a label-free RLVR framework that (1) decouples reward estimation from policy optimization by masking gradients on the final answer span while still using answer-level rewards via a soft consensus signal, so that learning pressure focuses on reasoning tokens; and (2) introduces Contrast-Augmented Reward, which improves reward estimation through low-cost pairwise comparisons across existing trajectories, without needing extra rollouts. This is implemented within a GRPO-style RL algorithm for LLMs and evaluated on multiple backbones and reasoning benchmarks.

Result: OM-GRPO consistently outperforms prior label-free RLVR methods on multiple reasoning benchmarks and across three different LLM backbones. Its performance matches that of supervised RLVR using ground-truth rewards, while maintaining stable optimization dynamics. In test-time training scenarios, OM-GRPO improves over a majority-voting baseline by 4.24 points, showing particular benefits when adapting on-the-fly without ground-truth labels.

Conclusion: Decoupling reward estimation from policy optimization via gradient masking on answer spans, combined with contrast-augmented reward estimation, prevents collapse in label-free RLVR and yields stable, strong performance comparable to ground-truth-based methods. OM-GRPO thus offers a scalable, effective approach to improving LLM reasoning and is especially advantageous for test-time training where labels are unavailable.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) improves LLM reasoning but typically relies on ground-truth (GT) answers, limiting scalability. Voting-based label-free RLVR replace gold supervision with answer-level consensus from model samples. However, collapse arises when the same answer-level signal is used both to estimate rewards and to drive token-level policy optimization, encouraging the model to directly reinforce answer tokens rather than improve reasoning. We propose OM-GRPO, a label-free RLVR framework that decouples reward estimation from policy optimization. OM-GRPO masks gradients on the answer span while retaining answer-level rewards through a soft consensus signal, shifting optimization pressure away from answer tokens. We further introduce Contrast-Augmented Reward, which refines reward estimation via low-cost pairwise comparisons over existing trajectories without additional rollouts. Across diverse reasoning benchmarks and three LLM backbones, OM-GRPO consistently outperforms existing label-free RLVR methods and matches supervised GT-reward training with stable optimization. This stability is particularly beneficial in the Test-Time Training setting, where OM-GRPO surpasses majority voting by 4.24 points.

</details>


### [114] [Beyond Average Performance: Dynamic Instance Clustering and Specialized Algorithm Design in LLM-Assisted Evolutionary Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03129)
*Qinglong Hu,Qingfu Zhang,Fei Liu,Xialiang Tong,Kun Mao,Mingxuan Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DyCA is a new LLM-assisted evolutionary search framework that dynamically clusters problem instances based on their algorithmic behavior to build specialized algorithm portfolios, improving tail robustness and overall performance on heterogeneous tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-assisted evolutionary search (LES) methods focus on optimizing average performance across instances. This biases the search toward instances that dominate the mean, leaving hard or rare instances poorly served, which harms tail robustness and limits reliability in real-world heterogeneous settings. There is a need for an LES framework that explicitly handles instance heterogeneity and improves worst-case (tail) performance while preserving good overall results.

Method: DyCA introduces a dynamic, feature-free instance clustering mechanism integrated into the LES loop. Instead of relying on pre-defined instance features, it reuses evaluation results of candidate algorithms as behavioral signals to cluster instances that exhibit similar response patterns to algorithms. These clusters co-evolve with the algorithm search: as more evaluation data are collected, clusters are progressively refined. Each cluster defines a structure-aware sub-objective, guiding the evolutionary search to design specialized algorithms (or portfolio components) tailored to that cluster, thereby building a reliable algorithm portfolio over heterogeneous instances.

Result: Across four algorithm design tasks with heterogeneous instance distributions, DyCA is empirically compared to state-of-the-art LES baselines. It achieves an average improvement of 15.2% in tail robustness (performance on the hardest or worst-served instances) and a 7.1% gain in overall performance, while keeping head performance (on easier or most common instances) competitive with baselines.

Conclusion: Incorporating dynamic, behavior-based instance clustering into LES enables structure-aware, specialized algorithm design that better handles heterogeneous instance distributions. DyCA successfully trades off and improves both tail robustness and overall performance without sacrificing performance on easy instances, making LES-generated algorithms more reliable for real-world use.

Abstract: Large Language Model-assisted Evolutionary Search (LES) has emerged as a powerful paradigm for automated algorithm design. However, existing LES methods primarily optimize for average performance, inherently directing search effort toward instances that contribute most to this metric while leaving others poorly served, resulting in weak tail robustness and limited real-world reliability. To address this limitation, we propose Dynamic Instance Clustering and Specialized Algorithm Design (DyCA), an LES framework with a feature-free, structure-aware mechanism for constructing reliable algorithm portfolios under heterogeneous instance distributions. DyCA treats instance clustering as a co-evolving component within the search process, reusing accumulated evaluation data as feature-free signals to progressively partition instances with similar algorithmic response patterns. The uncovered clusters decompose the mixed objective into a set of structure-aware sub-objectives, thereby enabling finer-grained and more adaptive guidance for specialized algorithm design. Experimental results across four algorithm design tasks with heterogeneous instances demonstrate that DyCA outperforms state-of-the-art LES baselines, improving tail robustness by an average of 15.2\% and overall performance by 7.1\% while maintaining competitive head performance.

</details>


### [115] [Verifiable Memory: Learning Unified Memory Management with Local and Global Verifiers for Large Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03137)
*Xiaolong Sun,Qichao Wang,Hangyu Li,Liang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Verifiable Memory (VerMem), a unified, RL-trained memory-control framework for LLM agents that manages long-term, short-term, and episodic memory with atomic operations and verifier-based credit assignment, achieving state-of-the-art performance and efficiency on multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents operating over long horizons need to remember useful information, manage a limited active context window, and reliably retrieve earlier evidence. Existing work typically treats long-term and short-term memory separately or uses unified policies trained only with trajectory-level feedback, which makes it hard to assign precise credit or blame to individual memory operations. This leads to suboptimal memory management, inefficient use of context, and worse performance on complex interactive tasks. The authors aim to build a principled, trainable mechanism that can jointly control different memory types with fine-grained feedback.

Method: They introduce Verifiable Memory (VerMem), which models three distinct memory states: (1) long-term memory (LTM), (2) active context (the bounded working memory sent to the LLM), and (3) episodic history (past interaction traces). A single memory-operation policy controls all three via seven atomic operations: adding, revising, and soft-deleting LTM entries; retrieving LTM into active context; filtering or summarizing the current active context; and restoring selected episodic fragments. The policy is first initialized by supervised fine-tuning, then further trained using a three-stage reinforcement-learning curriculum. During training, a local verifier evaluates individual memory transitions for executability and quality, while a global verifier judges overall evidence coherence and consistency of final memory after a task ends. These verifier scores are combined with programmatic rewards that capture task success, evidence recall, efficiency (e.g., token budget), and adherence to constraints in a hierarchical credit-assignment scheme, providing dense and targeted learning signals for each memory operation. Verifiers are used only at training time; at inference VerMem runs as a learned memory controller for LLM agents.

Result: On five benchmarks and using two different LLM backbones, VerMem outperforms strong memory baselines on most reported metrics, including measures of task performance and evidence recall. In online, interactive settings with strict token budgets on three benchmarks, VerMem traces a better efficiency–performance frontier than competing methods, meaning it achieves higher performance for the same or fewer context tokens. The results demonstrate that its unified, verifier-guided memory control policy yields both accuracy gains and improved token efficiency across diverse tasks.

Conclusion: VerMem shows that treating long-term memory, active context, and episodic history as explicit and jointly controlled states, and training a single memory policy with verifier-assisted reinforcement learning, can substantially improve LLM agents’ long-horizon reasoning and interaction. The combination of atomic memory operations, local and global verifiers, and hierarchical credit assignment produces a more effective and efficient memory controller than existing approaches that separate memory types or rely only on trajectory-level rewards. This suggests that verifiable, fine-grained memory management is a promising direction for building capable and scalable LLM agents, and the released code enables further exploration and extension of this framework.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents must retain reusable information, control a bounded active context, and recover earlier evidence during long-horizon interaction. Existing methods commonly optimize long-term memory (LTM) and short-term memory (STM) separately, while unified policies are often trained primarily with trajectory-level feedback, which provides weak credit for individual memory decisions. We present Verifiable Memory (VerMem), a framework that represents LTM, active context, and episodic history as distinct states and controls them with one memory operation policy. Seven atomic operations let the policy add, revise, or soft-delete LTM entries; retrieve LTM into the active context; filter or summarize the active context; and restore selected episodic fragments. VerMem is initialized by supervised fine-tuning and trained with a three-stage reinforcement-learning curriculum. The local verifier scores executable memory transitions, and a global verifier assesses evidence coherence and terminal-memory consistency after task completion. These scores are combined with programmatically computed task, evidence-recall, efficiency, and constraint signals through hierarchical credit assignment. The verifiers are used only during training. Across five benchmarks and two LLM backbones, VerMem achieves the best result on the vast majority of reported metrics and consistently outperforms strong memory baselines. Under controlled online-token budgets on three interactive benchmarks, it also achieves the strongest efficiency--performance frontier among the compared methods. Code is available at https://github.com/Sun-SYSU-24/VerMem.

</details>


### [116] [Spatial proteomics guided by H&E-based AI reveals recurrence-risk niches in triple-negative breast cancer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03145)
*Yesung Cho,Ji Hwan Park,Chanil Kim,Hyewon Kim,Honglan Li,Yumin Lee,Geongyu Lee,Sujeong Hong,Seong Min Park,Yoonyoung Lee,Hee Sool Rho,Sumin Lee,Amos Chungwon Lee,Changhwan Lee,Hwanyoung Shim,Hyunwook Kim,Hyeji Shin,Sanha Park,Jihoon Yu,Yoon Hee Shin,Sooheon Kim,Hyunjin Park,Seung Min Park,Sangwan Kim,Yujung Kim,Sung-Im Do,Eun-Young Kim,Dongmyung Shin,Jongbae Park,In-Gu Do*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They link AI-predicted recurrence risk on H&E slides to local protein programs, showing that high‑risk morphology corresponds to proliferative, genome‑maintenance states and low‑risk morphology to immune activation, and derive a multiscale biomarker for TNBC prognosis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning can predict outcomes from histology, but it is unclear what localized molecular states these image features represent, and how to turn black‑box, slide‑level predictions into mechanistically interpretable, spatially resolved biomarkers in triple‑negative breast cancer (TNBC).

Method: They trained an outcome-informed AI model on H&E slides of TNBC to generate patch‑level recurrence risk heatmaps, aggregated high‑risk patches to derive slide‑level risk scores, and integrated these with bulk and spatial mass‑spectrometry proteomics. Using the heatmaps as spatial coordinates, they microdissected AI‑defined high‑ and low‑risk tumor regions from two patients and profiled their proteomes, then derived a protein composite signature from the spatial contrasts and validated it along with a transcript-based analog in larger cohorts. They also evaluated prognostic performance via AUC and C-index and assessed improvement when combining image and protein scores.

Result: In 156 patients, aggregation of high‑risk patches yielded an AUC of 0.77 and C-index of 0.77 in an independent test set. Bulk proteomics linked high image‑derived risk to cell‑cycle/genome‑maintenance programs and low risk to immune activation. High and low risk patches frequently coexisted within the same morphologic compartment but had distinct nuclear/architectural features, indicating intratumoral heterogeneity. Spatial proteomics of 46 AI‑selected regions from two patients showed consistent biology: mitotic programs enriched in high‑risk regions, immune and antigen‑presentation programs in low‑risk regions. A 13‑protein composite from these contrasts showed a trend toward worse recurrence‑free survival with higher scores in an expanded cohort; a corresponding transcript signature stratified recurrence‑free survival in the independent METABRIC TNBC cohort. Combining the protein composite with the H&E risk score improved out‑of‑bag C‑index from 0.679 to 0.739 and improved 3‑ and 5‑year discrimination.

Conclusion: Outcome‑trained AI models on H&E slides can serve as spatial guides to sample specific tumor regions, enabling direct linkage of prognostic morphology to localized molecular states. This strategy uncovers intratumoral heterogeneity in TNBC beyond standard compartment labels and yields integrative, multiscale biomarkers that improve risk stratification and are biologically interpretable.

Abstract: Deep learning models can predict cancer recurrence from H&E stained slides, but the localized molecular states underlying these predictions remain largely obscured. Here, we developed an outcome informed spatial pathology framework in TNBC that integrates AI generated recurrence risk heatmaps with mass spectrometry based spatial proteomics. In a cohort of 156 patients, distribution based aggregation of high scoring patches achieved an AUC of 0.77 and a C-index of 0.77 in an independent test cohort. Bulk proteomics associated high image derived risk with cell cycle and genome maintenance programs and low risk with immune activation. High and low risk patches coexisted within the same tumor compartment and displayed distinct nuclear and architectural features, revealing intratumoral heterogeneity beyond tissue compartment identity. We then used the heatmaps as coordinate level guides to physically isolate and profile 46 AI defined tumor regions from two recurrence patients. Spatial proteomic profiling revealed a concordant molecular contrast across both patients: mitotic programs were enriched in high risk regions and immune and antigen presentation programs in low risk regions. A 13 protein composite derived from these spatial contrasts showed a trend toward poorer recurrence-free survival with increasing scores in an expanded cohort, while the corresponding transcript based composite stratified recurrence free survival in the independent METABRIC TNBC cohort. Integrating the protein composite with the H&E derived risk score improved the out of bag C-index from 0.679 to 0.739 and enhanced time dependent discrimination at 3 and 5 years. Together, these findings define a new role for outcome trained AI models as spatially explicit experimental guides that connect prognostic morphology with localized molecular states and advance biologically grounded, multiscale biomarker discovery in TNBC.

</details>


### [117] [UniGD: A Unified Generative-Discriminative Framework for Industrial Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03150)
*Shujie Ji,Yawei Kong,Yilin Zhao,Li Wang,Xialong Liu,Peng Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces UniGD, a unified generative-discriminative retrieval framework for industrial search advertising that improves ad revenue, retrieval effectiveness, and latency over existing cascaded generative retrieval systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing generative retrieval systems for industrial search advertising are hard to deploy because they must meet strict relevance and latency constraints. Current practice cascades a generative retriever with a separate relevance model, which decouples the generation objective from relevance discrimination, degrading effectiveness and increasing serving cost. There is a need for a single model that can both retrieve and accurately score relevance while remaining efficient and robust across heterogeneous ad formats.

Method: The authors propose UniGD, a unified model that jointly performs retrieval and relevance scoring. To address conflicting gradients between generative and discriminative objectives, they introduce Conflict-Aware Gradient Enhancement (CAGE), which adaptively coordinates updates for the two tasks. They design a Codebook-Anchored Representation Module (CAM) that anchors item representations to frozen hierarchical codebooks distilled from a multimodal pretrained model, injecting rich semantic priors. For handling heterogeneous ad types such as short-video, product, and live-stream ads, they introduce Heterogeneous Ad-material Modeling (HAM), which uses a shared backbone to learn cross-type semantic commonalities while preserving type-specific modeling capacity.

Result: In online A/B tests on the Kuaishou search advertising platform, UniGD increased ad revenue by 5.78%, reduced inference latency by 33%, and improved discriminative relevance estimation versus the existing production system. On large-scale benchmarks NQ320K and MS300K, UniGD outperformed the strongest reproduced generative retrieval baseline, improving Recall@10 by 8.44% and 3.19%, respectively.

Conclusion: UniGD successfully unifies generative retrieval and discriminative relevance scoring within a single framework, overcoming the limitations of cascaded systems in industrial search advertising. By coordinating multi-objective training with CAGE, enriching representations through CAM, and modeling heterogeneous ad materials via HAM, the approach delivers both higher retrieval quality and better system efficiency, making generative retrieval more practical for large-scale commercial deployment.

Abstract: Generative retrieval (GR) is a promising paradigm for industrial search advertising, yet its deployment is constrained by strict relevance and latency requirements. Existing systems cascade GR with an independent relevance model, decoupling the generative likelihood objective from query-ad relevance discrimination, which compromises effectiveness and increases serving costs. We propose a Unified Generative-Discriminative framework (UniGD) that integrates retrieval and relevance scoring within a single model. To mitigate gradient interference in joint optimization, UniGD introduces Conflict-Aware Gradient Enhancement (CAGE) to adaptively coordinate the two objectives. UniGD further designs a Codebook-Anchored Representation Module (CAM) that anchors item representations to frozen hierarchical codebooks distilled from a multimodal pretrained model, thereby endowing them with rich and generalizable semantic priors. For heterogeneous short-video, product, and live-stream ads, UniGD proposes Heterogeneous Ad-material Modeling (HAM), which captures cross-type semantic commonality over a shared backbone while preserving type-specific modeling capacity. Online AB tests on Kuaishou search advertising platform show that UniGD raises ad revenue by 5.78%, reduces inference latency by 33%, and improves discriminative relevance estimation. On NQ320K and MS300K, UniGD improves Recall@10 over the strongest reproduced GR baseline by 8.44% and 3.19%, respectively.

</details>


### [118] [Evidence-Grounded Multimodal Knowledge Graph Construction for Multi-Lecture Educational Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03161)
*Sahil Al Farib,Momota Ahsana Meem,Sheikh Redwanul Islam,Md. Tanvir Raihan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build an auditable multimodal pipeline that turns lecture videos into a provenance-rich knowledge graph of concepts and relationships using transcript, OCR, and visual evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transcript-only search over lecture videos misses important information in slide text, diagrams, equations, and how material is visually presented. The authors want a way to capture and structure all this multimodal information into a form that supports reliable, evidence-backed retrieval, rather than relying solely on noisy text transcripts.

Method: They design a multimodal processing pipeline for lecture videos: (1) transcribe the lecture; (2) select semantic anchors in the timeline; (3) run OCR on slides at those anchors; (4) use a vision–language model over frames, OCR, and transcript to extract concept mentions and typed relationships, but only when explicitly supported by at least one evidence source; (5) validate and canonicalize these mentions into entities and relations, tracking provenance for each fact in a knowledge graph. They evaluate on three neural-network lectures, processing thousands of frames and transcript segments and measuring coverage and basic retrieval performance over the resulting graph.

Result: On three neural network lectures, the pipeline processed 3,118 video frames, 756 transcript segments, and 559 semantic anchors. From this it retained 1,022 concept mentions and 312 relationship mentions, which were canonicalized into 172 unique concepts and 282 unique relationships. The resulting graph achieved 90.38% coverage of endpoints (concepts involved in relationships). A small three-question retrieval test over this graph obtained 100% top-1 and top-3 accuracy and 100% mean top-5 recall.

Conclusion: The main contribution is a transparent, evidence-grounded method for constructing multimodal knowledge graphs from lecture videos, not a claim of state-of-the-art retrieval accuracy. The approach shows that combining transcript, OCR, and visual understanding with explicit provenance can effectively structure lecture content and support accurate retrieval, and it sets a foundation for more scalable and rigorous systems in the future.

Abstract: Lecture videos distribute knowledge across speech, slide text, diagrams, equations, and presentation order, which transcript-only retrieval does not fully preserve. This paper presents an evidence-grounded multimodal pipeline that transcribes lectures, selects semantic anchors, applies optical character recognition (OCR), and uses a vision-language model to extract only concepts and typed relationships supported by transcript, OCR, or visual evidence. Mentions are validated and canonicalized into a provenance-rich knowledge graph. On three neural-network lectures, the pipeline processed 3,118 frames, 756 transcript segments, and 559 anchors.
