It retained 1,022 concept and 312 relationship mentions, yielding 172 canonical concepts and 282 relationships with 90.38% endpoint coverage. A preliminary three question retrieval test achieved 100% top-1 and top-3 accuracy and 100% mean top-5 recall. The contribution is an auditable construction method rather than a state-of-the-art performance claim.

</details>


### [119] [Adversarial Stress Testing of Role-Playing Language Agents using Multi-Agent Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03166)
*Saqib Shouqi,Abdullah Nazly,Januki Wanniarachchi,Ravisha De Alwis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a modular multi-agent system that adversarially stress-tests role-playing language agents (RPLAs) in multi-turn dialogues to uncover subtle, cumulative failures in persona fidelity and ethics that static, single-turn tests miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Role-playing language agents are increasingly used in high-stakes domains like healthcare, support, and education, where they must maintain a consistent persona and ethical behavior even under adversarial pressure. Current evaluations rely mostly on static benchmarks or isolated prompts and thus fail to capture cumulative, interaction-level failures (e.g., gradual persona drift or ethical breakdown) that emerge over long conversations. There is a need for systematic, reproducible methods to probe these agents through realistic, adversarial, multi-strategy dialogues and to quantify different dimensions of robustness.

Method: The authors build a modular multi-agent evaluation platform with three coordinated agents: (1) an Interrogator Agent that adversarially probes the target using six progressively stronger strategies (e.g., authority challenge, emotional manipulation); (2) a Target Agent, i.e., the role-playing language agent under test; and (3) an automated Judging Agent that scores the Target’s responses along four behavioral axes: role fidelity, drift, ethical deviation, and consistency. They run multi-turn dialogue evaluations across three different personas and three LLM families (Llama-3.3-70B, GPT-4o-mini, Claude-3.5-Haiku) and compare results from multi-strategy adversarial testing against simpler, single-strategy setups. They also validate the automated judging by measuring its agreement with human annotators using correlation and Fleiss’ kappa.

Result: Experiments show that multi-strategy adversarial evaluation surfaces additional failure modes, leading to a reduction of 0.17–0.20 points in robustness scores on average compared to single-strategy testing. The degradation patterns are consistent across three different LLM families, indicating that the observed vulnerabilities are not model-specific. Among the adversarial tactics, Authority Challenge and Emotional Manipulation are the most effective at causing failures. The automated Judging Agent exhibits strong alignment with human judgments, with a correlation of r = 0.82 and Fleiss’ kappa of 0.71.

Conclusion: The proposed multi-agent, multi-strategy adversarial framework provides a more realistic and revealing way to evaluate the robustness of role-playing language agents than static or single-turn tests. It uncovers cumulative behavioral failures that existing methods miss and does so in a manner that generalizes across different LLM families and personas. The open-sourced platform can serve as a tool for AI safety research and standardized benchmarking of RPLAs. At the same time, the authors caution that adversarial testing itself carries ethical risks and should be used responsibly, with the goal of improving safety rather than exploiting or amplifying vulnerabilities.

Abstract: Role-Playing Language Agents (RPLAs) are increasingly deployed in high-stakes applications such as healthcare assistance, customer support, and education, where maintaining consistent personas, ethical constraints, and behavioral coherence under adversarial pressure is critical. Existing evaluation approaches rely on static benchmarks or isolated single-turn prompts that fail to capture cumulative behavioral failures emerging over extended interactions.
  We present a modular multi-agent platform for adversarially stress-testing RPLAs through structured, multi-turn dialogue. The system coordinates three agents: a strategy-driven Interrogator Agent that applies six progressive adversarial strategies, a Target Agent representing the RPLA under evaluation, and an automated Judging Agent that scores behavior across role fidelity, drift, ethical deviation, and consistency dimensions.
  Through experiments across three personas and three LLM families, we demonstrate that multi-strategy adversarial evaluation reveals failure modes invisible to single-strategy testing, reducing overall robustness scores by 0.17--0.20 points on average. Cross-model validation confirms consistent degradation patterns across Llama-3.3-70B, GPT-4o-mini, and Claude-3.5-Haiku, with Authority Challenge and Emotional Manipulation emerging as the most effective attack strategies. Automated judging achieves strong human alignment ($r = 0.82$, Fleiss' $κ= 0.71$). This work is released as an open-source platform to support AI safety and reproducible RPLA benchmarking. While the framework enables systematic discovery of failure modes, we acknowledge potential ethical risks associated with adversarial testing methodologies and emphasize responsible usage for improving AI safety.

</details>


### [120] [Surrogate Substitution Preserves PHI Detectability: A Multi-Detector Equivalence Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03172)
*Qiming Bao,Sherry J. H. Feng,Kim Chester Eugenio,Meng Fon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They test whether structure-preserving PHI de-identification (replacing PHI with realistic surrogates) harms the ability of downstream PHI detectors, and find it basically doesn’t when substitutions are well-formed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Structure-preserving de-identification is attractive because it keeps clinical text fluent and compatible with existing tools, but it’s unclear whether replacing PHI with realistic surrogates might break downstream PHI detectors precisely on the spans that get changed. Existing evaluations mix up coverage (how much PHI is found to mask) with utility (how well tools work after masking) and often rely on underpowered or misapplied significance tests. The authors want a clean, quantitative way to test whether surrogates remain detectable as PHI by existing detectors, and to characterize when and why they fail.

Method: They propose a paired evaluation protocol that compares detector performance on original vs. surrogate-masked PHI spans only. First, they restrict scoring to the spans that a de-identifier actually masked, separating coverage from post-masking utility. Second, they use two one-sided equivalence testing (TOST) with a specified equivalence margin (+/−2 recall points) instead of standard null-hypothesis testing, applied to about 57k paired PHI spans. Third, they construct a typology of surrogate failures to distinguish errors due to fixable surrogate generator issues (e.g., truncation, odd forms) from inherent detector limits. They apply the protocol across 11 PHI detectors, 7 benchmarks, and 7 languages (total 1,750 clinical documents), and compare recall and ranking before vs. after masking. They also use a redaction floor and an open-source surrogate generator as baselines.

Result: Across all settings, recall on the masked spans drops only slightly from 76.1% to 74.9%, and TOST shows this difference is statistically equivalent to zero within a ±2 percentage point margin (p≈3×10⁻⁹). Detector rankings are preserved before and after masking. The small residual recall loss is not due to detectors fundamentally being worse at recognizing PHI-like spans, but is concentrated in malformed or out-of-distribution surrogates, such as truncated locations ("Chicago" → "Illino") or surrogates that lose salient institutional cues ("Cedars-Sinai" → "Vidant"). Results with a pure redaction approach and an open-source surrogate generator suggest the observed behavior is a general property of well-formed structure-preserving substitution, not an artifact of a single proprietary tool.

Conclusion: Structure-preserving de-identification, when it produces well-formed surrogates, preserves the utility of downstream PHI detectors on the very spans that are masked: detectors recognize surrogates as PHI almost as well as they do the original entities, and comparative performance rankings remain stable. Small utility losses mainly trace back to surrogate-generation pathologies rather than any fundamental incompatibility between substitution and PHI detection. The proposed paired, equivalence-based protocol, along with released data, code, and dashboard, provides a practical way to audit and diagnose any structure-preserving transformation applied to clinical text.

Abstract: Structure-preserving de-identification replaces protected health information (PHI) with realistic same-type surrogates -- "Anna S." becomes "Maria S.", not [NAME] -- so that clinical text stays fluent and downstream tools keep working. But this only helps if the substitution does not itself corrupt the signal those tools rely on. We ask a narrow, testable question: on the spans a de-identifier actually masks, can downstream PHI detectors still find the surrogate? We introduce a paired, multi-detector evaluation protocol that (i) scores utility only on masked spans, decoupling coverage from utility; (ii) uses equivalence testing (TOST) rather than null-hypothesis significance testing, which is uninformative at our sample size (57k paired spans); and (iii) builds a surrogate-failure typology separating fixable generator defects from intrinsic detector limits. Across 11 detectors, 7 benchmarks, and 7 languages (1,750 documents), recall on masked spans moves from 76.1% to 74.9% -- a change our equivalence test shows is statistically equivalent to zero within a +/-2-point margin (p ~ 3e-9), with detector ranking preserved. The residual loss does not reflect detectors getting worse at PHI: it concentrates in malformed and out-of-distribution surrogates (truncation Chicago -> Illino, salience loss Cedars-Sinai -> Vidant). A redaction floor and an open-source surrogate baseline indicate the effect is a property of well-formed substitution, not of one tool. We release the evaluation subsets, scoring code, and an interactive dashboard at https://custodianai.pages.dev so the protocol can audit any structure-preserving transform.

</details>


### [121] [Diversity is Not Ambiguity: Toward Accurate and Efficient Ambiguity Detection for Open-Domain QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03177)
*Jiwon Lee,Yong-chan Park,Jungin Hong,U Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes ARCHIVE, a framework for efficiently detecting ambiguous questions in open-domain QA by reasoning over logical conflicts among candidate answers, and introduces a new benchmark QuireQA to evaluate ambiguity detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In open-domain QA, some user queries are inherently ambiguous, admitting multiple plausible interpretations. Misclassifying such ambiguity can lead systems either to answer the wrong meaning or to ask users for clarification when it is not needed. Existing ambiguity detection methods often equate having multiple different answers with being ambiguous, which is incorrect because multiple answers can still be mutually consistent. They also treat all queries with equally heavy processing, leading to inefficient computation. There is thus a need for a more principled and efficient way to detect true semantic ambiguity in QA queries.

Method: The authors propose ARCHIVE (Ambiguity Recognition via Cascaded Hypothesis Inspection and Conflict Verification), a two-stage framework. First, a lightweight early-exit encoder handles surface-detectable cases of ambiguity or unambiguity, saving computation. For more complex cases, a conflict reasoning module examines the logical relationships among candidate answers to the query, identifying ambiguity when valid answers cannot simultaneously be true under a single interpretation. The framework also incorporates an invariance objective to make the conflict reasoning robust to noisy and varied answer sets. Additionally, they construct QuireQA, a dataset of 4,703 queries including factoid, non-factoid, and ill-formed questions to evaluate the approach.

Result: On the QuireQA benchmark, ARCHIVE outperforms baseline methods on both ambiguous and unambiguous query detection, improving F1-amb (for ambiguous queries) by up to 10.4% and F1-unamb (for unambiguous queries) by up to 21.6%. It also achieves substantial efficiency gains, running up to 16 times faster than the strongest competing method.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that modeling ambiguity through logical conflict among candidate answers is more accurate than naively using answer diversity, and that a cascaded architecture with early-exit encoding and conflict reasoning effectively balances accuracy and efficiency. ARCHIVE sets a new state of the art for ambiguity detection in open-domain QA on the QuireQA benchmark, suggesting that future QA systems can better decide when to answer directly versus when to seek clarification by adopting similar conflict-based reasoning approaches.

Abstract: How can question answering (QA) systems determine whether a query is ambiguous? Ambiguity detection is essential in open-domain QA, as misclassification leads to answering the wrong interpretation or unnecessary clarification. However, existing methods conflate answer diversity with ambiguity, leading to inaccurate predictions. They also process queries uniformly, resulting in wasteful computation. We propose ARCHIVE (Ambiguity Recognition via Cascaded Hypothesis Inspection and Conflict Verification), an accurate and efficient framework that detects ambiguity via logical conflict: a query is ambiguous when its valid answers cannot all be true under a single interpretation. ARCHIVE combines a lightweight early-exit encoder for surface-detectable cases with a conflict reasoning module that models logical relations among answers, reinforced by an invariance objective for robustness to noisy answer sets. We present QuireQA, a 4,703-query benchmark spanning factoid, non-factoid, and ill-formed queries. Experiments show ARCHIVE outperforms competitors, improving F1-amb by up to 10.4% and F1-unamb by up to 21.6%, while operating 16$\times$ faster than the best competitor.

</details>


### [122] [TumorBoard: Evidence-Grounded Multi-Agent Decision Support for Longitudinal Neuro-Oncology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03190)
*Yantong Liu,Zheyu Zhang,Runpeng Liu,Mu Xitang,Seong-Yoon Shin,Hyun-Ae Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TumorBoard is a multi‑agent, evidence‑tracking system that coordinates specialized AI agents to make safer, more accurate neuro‑oncology treatment recommendations from complex longitudinal data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neuro‑oncology decision‑making is complex because clinicians must integrate many heterogeneous, longitudinal data sources—MRI over time, pathology, molecular markers, prior treatments, performance status, and evolving clinical guidelines. Single LLMs or loosely coordinated councils struggle to: (1) maintain a coherent case state over time, (2) explicitly tie recommendations to supporting evidence, and (3) manage safety when information is missing or inconsistent. The paper is motivated by the need for a system that can systematically coordinate specialist reasoning, provide auditable links between claims and evidence, and know when to defer rather than make potentially harmful recommendations.

Method: The authors design TumorBoard, a multi‑agent decision‑support architecture centered on: (1) a shared longitudinal case state capturing the evolving patient history, and (2) an auditable claim‑evidence ledger where each assertion is logged with provenance. Dedicated specialist agents (radiology, neuropathology, molecular diagnosis, guidelines, and therapy planning) generate atomic claims, each with explicit evidence links. An adversarial critic agent scans the ledger to surface contradictions and weaknesses. A safety governor agent decides whether to release, qualify, or defer recommendations based on evidence sufficiency and temporal validity. They evaluate TumorBoard on a 360‑case hidden benchmark, compare it to a strong "typed‑council" baseline at matched token budget, perform robustness tests under evidence deletion, and run ablations removing the ledger, critic, and governor to understand their contribution.

Result: On the 360‑case benchmark, TumorBoard achieved an action F1 of 0.772 and evidence entailment of 0.914, outperforming the strongest typed‑council baseline by 3.1 percentage points (95% CI: 1.6–4.7, adjusted p = 0.0012). Recommendation‑to‑evidence coverage reached 0.927, indicating that nearly all recommendations had explicit supporting evidence in the ledger. In adversarial robustness tests where key evidence was deleted, the system deferred in 84.2% of cases identified as unsafe and restricted harmful recommendations to 5.8%. The safety governor reduced harmful releases by 7.8 percentage points relative to a no‑governor setting, at a cost of 4.3 percentage points more false deferrals. Ablation of the ledger, critic, and governor yielded distinct degradation and failure modes consistent with the design hypotheses.

Conclusion: Structured multi‑agent coordination around a shared case state and explicit claim‑evidence ledger provides measurable benefits for complex clinical decision support in neuro‑oncology. The combination of specialized agents, an adversarial critic, and a safety governor improves both the accuracy of recommendations and their safety, especially under missing or inconsistent evidence. The empirical gains over a strong typed‑council baseline, along with ablation results, support the claim that the architecture—rather than just model scale or token budget—is responsible for the performance and safety improvements. TumorBoard illustrates a promising pattern for building auditable, defer‑when‑unsafe AI systems in high‑stakes medical domains.

Abstract: Neuro-oncology decisions require coordinated interpretation of serial MRI, pathology, molecular markers, treatment history, performance status, and evolving guidelines. We present TumorBoard, a multi-agent decision-support system built around a shared longitudinal case state and an auditable claim-evidence ledger. Specialist agents for radiology, neuropathology, molecular diagnosis, guidelines, and therapy planning produce atomic claims with provenance. An adversarial critic exposes contradictions, and a safety governor releases, qualifies, or defers recommendations according to evidence sufficiency and temporal validity. On a 360-case hidden benchmark at a matched token budget, TumorBoard achieved an action F1 of 0.772 and evidence entailment of 0.914. It exceeded the strongest typed-council baseline by 3.1 percentage points (95% CI: 1.6 to 4.7, adjusted p = 0.0012), while recommendation-to-evidence coverage reached 0.927. Under evidence deletion, the system deferred 84.2% of unsafe cases and limited harmful recommendations to 5.8%. The safety governor reduced harmful release by 7.8 percentage points at a false-deferral cost of 4.3 percentage points. Ablation studies of the ledger, critic, and governor produced the predicted failure patterns, establishing structured coordination as the source of the measured multi-agent advantage.

</details>


### [123] [When Refusal Looks Safe: The Refusal-Cue Shortcut in Safety Guard Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03201)
*Yu Feng,Chunting Zang,Chen Shen,Rui Miao,Ge Teng,Weidong Cai,Jieping Ye*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper finds that safety guards for language models exploit a “refusal-cue shortcut,” where adding refusal phrases can cause harmful content to be misclassified as safe, and proposes a lightweight internal patch that suppresses this shortcut while preserving normal performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety-guard models are critical for filtering harmful outputs from language models, but they are usually trained on supervised datasets that may contain annotation artifacts. The authors audit popular safety-guard training sets and discover that refusal phrases almost always co-occur with safe labels in harmful-prompt scenarios. This suggests that guards may be learning to trust superficial refusal cues rather than truly detecting harmful content, which creates a potential vulnerability attackers can exploit by wrapping harmful text with refusals. The motivation is to understand and mitigate this shortcut so that safety guards make more robust, content-based judgments.

Method: 1) Dataset audit: Analyze WildGuardMix and GR-Train to measure label distributions for harmful prompts and detect over-association between refusal expressions and unharmful labels. 2) Empirical shortcut probing: Test various safety-guard models (including closed-training-data ones like LlamaGuard3 and Qwen3Guard) by inserting refusal cues into harmful responses at different positions and measuring detection failures, across model sizes in a family. 3) Intervention: Adapt sparse complementary masking, a post-hoc interpretability-based method, to identify specific attention heads and MLP neurons strongly associated with the refusal-cue shortcut. These components are then selectively suppressed at inference time without retraining the whole model. 4) Evaluation: Measure reduction in detection failures induced by refusal cues on primary benchmarks, test transfer to unseen response positions and datasets, and compare shortcut suppression to preservation of genuine refusal recognition.

Result: The authors show that (a) in WildGuardMix and GR-Train, harmful prompts whose responses contain refusal expressions are overwhelmingly labeled as unharmful, creating a strong statistical cue; (b) safety guards trained on these datasets, and even official guard models like LlamaGuard3 and Qwen3Guard, heavily rely on this refusal-cue shortcut, leading to harmful content being classified as safe when wrapped with refusal language; (c) smaller models are generally more vulnerable. Using sparse complementary masking to suppress a small set of shortcut-related internal components yields about a 79% relative reduction in detection failures caused by refusal cues at the start of the response, with standard detection performance largely unchanged. The mitigation generalizes to refusal cues at other positions and to new datasets, indicating that the relevant internal circuitry is shared across positions.

Conclusion: Safety-guard models learn a problematic refusal-cue shortcut from biases in common training datasets, causing them to misclassify harmful content as safe when it includes refusal-like phrasing. This shortcut is widespread, stronger in smaller variants, and not limited to models with known training data. However, it can be mitigated post hoc by selectively suppressing a sparse set of internal components tied to the shortcut, significantly reducing cue-induced failures while preserving overall detection quality and the ability to recognize genuine refusals. This suggests that shortcut behavior and legitimate refusal understanding are at least partially separable and that targeted internal interventions can improve the robustness of safety guards without full retraining.

Abstract: Safety guards are widely used to filter harmful content and are typically trained via supervised fine-tuning on labeled prompt-response pairs. We audit two widely used safety-guard training datasets, WildGuardMix and GR-Train, and find that among responses to harmful prompts, refusal expressions co-occur almost exclusively with unharmful labels. This imbalance motivates what we term the refusal-cue shortcut: inserting a refusal cue into a harmful response could flip the guard's verdict from harmful to unharmful. The shortcut affects not only guards trained on these datasets but also officially released models such as LlamaGuard3 and Qwen3Guard whose training data is undisclosed. It persists across response positions and is generally stronger in smaller variants within a family. To mitigate it, we adapt sparse complementary masking as a lightweight post-hoc intervention that identifies and suppresses a small set of shortcut-associated attention heads and MLP neurons without retraining. On two primary benchmarks, the intervention achieves an approximately 79% relative reduction in response-initial detection failures induced by refusal cues, while preserving standard detection performance. Although optimized using cues at a single response position, the suppression effect transfers to unseen positions and datasets, suggesting that shortcut manifestations across positions are partly mediated by shared internal components. Further analysis provides evidence that shortcut reliance and legitimate refusal recognition are partially functionally separable, as suppressing the shortcut broadly preserves the guard's ability to recognize genuine refusals.

</details>


### [124] [The Agent Operating System (AOS): A Reference Operating Architecture for Distributed Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03214)
*Ankur Sharma,Deep Shah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a vendor-neutral Agent Operating System (AOS) architecture to reliably and governably orchestrate distributed LLM-based agents and tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based agent systems are built with diverse frameworks, tools, and platforms, but lack a stable, implementation-independent operating architecture. This creates problems for governing system intent, managing delegation and authority, controlling uncertainty and risk, coordinating complex runtime behavior, and reconstructing why important actions occurred. The authors aim to define a common architectural layer that can unify heterogeneous components while enforcing governance, reliability, and observability.

Method: The authors define a reference architecture called the Agent Operating System (AOS). They decompose it into two main internal planes—Control & Governance, and Runtime & Coordination—and specify their responsibilities. They then delineate explicit interfaces between AOS and external infrastructure (platform services, OS, containers, hardware). The paper formalizes core concepts, invariants, interface objects, optimization objectives, deployment profiles, and reliability responsibilities, and discusses associated design tradeoffs and open research questions.

Result: The result is a conceptual, vendor-neutral operating architecture for agentic systems, comprising: (1) a Control & Governance Plane for managing intent, policy, trust, authority, confidence, auditability, observability, and human oversight; and (2) a Runtime & Coordination Plane for agent lifecycle management, workflow and routing, context/memory coordination, scheduling, traffic management, and runtime assurance. The architecture defines clear boundaries to external systems via explicit interfaces and highlights how disparate tools and frameworks can be composed into a coherent, governable system.

Conclusion: AOS should not replace existing frameworks or infrastructure but instead serve as the architectural layer through which they are integrated. By providing a stable, implementation-agnostic operating architecture with explicit governance and runtime coordination planes, AOS aims to make complex agentic systems governable, reliable, observable, and interoperable, while also surfacing key tradeoffs and research questions that remain open.

Abstract: Large language models have transformed artificial intelligence from isolated prediction services into components of long-running, distributed systems that reason, invoke tools, retrieve external state, delegate tasks, and act on behalf of users and organizations. The surrounding ecosystem has responded with agent frameworks, workflow engines, model-serving platforms, memory systems, communication protocols, and observability tools. These technologies improve execution, but they do not provide a stable, implementation-independent operating architecture for governing intent, selecting capabilities, preserving authority across delegation, controlling uncertainty, coordinating runtime behavior, and reconstructing why consequential actions occurred. This paper proposes the Agent Operating System (AOS), a vendor-neutral reference operating architecture for distributed agentic systems. AOS contains two internal planes: a Control & Governance Plane responsible for intent, policy, trust, authority, confidence, auditability, observability, and human oversight; and a Runtime & Coordination Plane responsible for agent lifecycle, workflow coordination, model and tool routing, context and memory coordination, scheduling, traffic management, and runtime assurance. Platform services, Linux or Windows, container runtimes, and physical infrastructure remain outside the AOS boundary and are integrated through explicit interfaces. The paper specifies AOS concepts, invariants, interface objects, optimization objectives, deployment profiles, and reliability responsibilities. It also identifies tradeoffs and unresolved research questions. AOS is not presented as a replacement for existing frameworks or infrastructure; it is proposed as the operating architecture through which heterogeneous components can be composed into governable, reliable, observable, and interoperable agentic systems.

</details>


### [125] [Reachability Is Not Realization: Tracing the Sources of LLM Benchmark Gains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03219)
*Yanchao Li,Wanhao Liu,Jiaqing Xie,Ben Gao,Yanbo Wang,Tianfan Fu,Yuqiang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper distinguishes between realized performance (what the model actually answers correctly under a fixed deployment procedure) and reachable performance (what the model could answer correctly if we searched its outputs under a fixed budget). It shows that benchmark gains can reflect changes in deployment, routing, or suppression rather than true expansion of what the model can, in principle, answer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Benchmark scores for LLMs are usually treated as evidence of increased capability, but the same overall score can arise from different underlying behaviors. Aggregate metrics do not say whether models are answering genuinely new questions or just surfacing answers that were already possible. The authors want a more fine-grained, question-level way to audit what has actually changed in a model’s abilities, and to separate potential capability (reachability) from realized deployment behavior.

Method: They define a question-level audit under strictly matched evaluation conditions (same budgets, temperatures, answer formats). For each question, they distinguish: (1) realization: the default deployment procedure directly produces the correct answer; and (2) reachability: a separate probing procedure can find the correct answer somewhere within a bounded inference budget. They then run three main empirical studies: (a) test whether inference-time layer routing (including random routes vs structured search) can increase reachability across 43 combinations of models and tasks; (b) diagnose why reachable answers fail to appear, by identifying and silencing specific MLP blocks whose ablation ‘repairs’ many failures across models from 0.5B to 31B parameters; and (c) compare how training methods affect realized vs reachable performance, including DAPO, by tracking both realized scores and the reachable ceiling over training.

Result: (1) Inference-time layer routing can significantly expand reachability, but random routing under a fixed budget matches or exceeds more structured search in all 43 model–task settings. However, when routing is made answer-blind (no access to the correctness signal), almost all of this gain disappears, implying that much of the reachability boost relies on access to the correct answer. (2) In six model cases from 0.5B to 31B, ablating a single identified MLP block fixes 68–92% of a curated failure set, indicating that particular components can actively suppress reachable correct answers. (3) In five of six matched evaluations of training, realized benchmark scores go up while the reachable ceiling stays flat or even decreases. For DAPO specifically, realized performance improves by 14.7 points but the reachable ceiling falls by 13.3 points. Overall, realized performance and reachability often diverge.

Conclusion: Realized benchmark scores and reachability are distinct dimensions of model behavior; improvements in observed performance do not necessarily mean that the model’s underlying space of possible correct answers has expanded. Inference-time interventions and training can change which answers are surfaced or suppressed without broadening reachability, and in some cases may even reduce it. Therefore, claims about capability expansion should always report both realized performance and reachable performance under matched conditions, using question-level audits rather than only aggregate scores.

Abstract: Benchmark gains are often treated as evidence of greater LLM capability. Yet the same gain can reflect different changes in model behavior. A model may reach new answers, or produce answers that were already within reach. Aggregate scores do not distinguish these changes question by question. We establish a question-level audit under fixed budgets, temperatures, and answer formats. A question is realized when the default deployment procedure produces the correct answer. A question is reachable when a specified probe finds that answer within a fixed budget. We first test whether inference-time layer routing can expand reachability. Under a matched budget, random routes match or exceed structured search in all 43 model and task settings. Answer-blind procedures retain almost none of this gain, which instead requires access to the correct answer. We then ask why reachable answers sometimes fail to appear. Across six cases spanning 0.5B to 31B, silencing one identified MLP block repairs 68 to 92 percent of a predefined failure set. We next test whether training closes the gap by expanding reachability. In five of six matched evaluations, deployed performance rises while the reachable ceiling remains flat or falls. For DAPO, the deployed score rises by 14.7 points while the reachable ceiling falls by 13.3 points. Across the settings we audit, realization and reachability therefore do not always change together. Claims of capability expansion should report both realized performance and reachability under matched evaluation conditions. Code is available at https://github.com/LiZaiyuan0619/reachability-not-realization

</details>


### [126] [UniNav: A Unified World-Action Diffusion Model for Visual Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03244)
*Changqing Zhou,Yueru Luo,Zeyu Jiang,Changhao Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UniNav is a unified diffusion-based model that jointly predicts future visual observations and continuous waypoint trajectories for image-goal navigation, achieving both accuracy and low-latency inference, with a fast variant that omits image prediction at test time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In image-goal navigation, existing policies can efficiently predict waypoint trajectories but lack explicit visual foresight, while world models with visual prediction capabilities typically require expensive planning rollouts. There is a need for a single model that can both foresee future visual states and generate effective navigation actions in a computationally efficient way, and that can exploit large video datasets that lack action/trajectory labels.

Method: The authors propose UniNav, a unified world-action model based on a single diffusion process that simultaneously generates future visual observations and continuous waypoint trajectories. The model takes as input history frames and a goal image, and jointly denoises visual and waypoint tokens using a single transformer backbone. Geometry-aware camera tokens are incorporated to strengthen spatial grounding. Training is performed on a mixture of trajectory-labeled navigation datasets and video-only data, enabling learning from unlabeled visual dynamics. Two inference variants are introduced: UniNav-Full, which outputs both future observations and trajectories, and UniNav-Fast, which drops future-image tokens at inference to speed up trajectory prediction while leveraging the same trained model.

Result: On standard navigation benchmarks, UniNav surpasses the strongest existing baselines in Absolute Trajectory Error (ATE) across all evaluated datasets. The UniNav-Fast variant achieves approximately 0.1-second latency with one-step inference, maintaining comparable navigation accuracy and showing only a minor performance drop relative to the full model.

Conclusion: A single diffusion-based world-action model can effectively unify visual foresight and waypoint generation for image-goal navigation, improving accuracy over prior methods while enabling efficient, low-latency inference. Leveraging geometry-aware camera tokens and training on both labeled trajectories and unlabeled video makes it possible to benefit from diverse data sources, and the proposed UniNav framework can flexibly trade off interpretability and speed via its Full and Fast variants.

Abstract: Image-goal visual navigation is a fundamental capability for embodied agents. Existing navigation policies efficiently predict waypoint trajectories but lack visual foresight, while navigation world models can anticipate future observations but often require costly planning rollouts. We present UniNav, a unified world-action model that generates future visual observations and continuous waypoint trajectories through a single diffusion process. Given history frames and a goal image, UniNav jointly denoises visual and waypoint tokens within a single transformer, unifying future prediction and action generation in a shared framework. To improve spatial grounding, we incorporate geometry-aware camera tokens. We also train on both trajectory-labeled navigation data and video-only data, enabling the model to benefit from diverse videos without waypoint annotations. Based on this unified framework, we introduce two variants: UniNav-Full jointly predicts interpretable future observations and their corresponding trajectories, while UniNav-Fast removes future-image tokens at inference for efficient trajectory prediction. Experiments on navigation benchmarks show that UniNav outperforms the strongest baseline in ATE across all datasets. With one-step inference, UniNav-Fast achieves a latency of 0.1s without a substantial accuracy drop. Code will be released.

</details>


### [127] [One Knob to Rule Them All: A Unified Optimal Transport View of Cold-Start Active Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03249)
*Ning Zhu,Xiaochuan Ma,Juntao Xu,Jingze Liang,Mengfei Zhao,An Chen,Liang-Jian Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They introduce a new active learning method for the cold-start setting based on optimal transport that automatically adapts to different datasets and tasks, achieving better accuracy and faster selection than prior work.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cold-start active learning must choose an informative subset of data without labels or prior knowledge, but existing heuristics (typicality, coverage, diversity) each embed fixed inductive biases and thus work inconsistently across tasks. The authors want a unified, principled and adaptive approach that can automatically adjust to the structure of the unlabeled data and the downstream task.

Method: They reframe cold-start active learning as an optimal transport problem and propose a generalized transport-based selection framework that unifies many existing methods via a common allocation structure. Using entropic regularization, they analyze the trade-off it introduces and derive a task-agnostic minimax bound for cold-start selection. Based on this analysis, they design a data-driven rule to choose the regularization strength and implement a new Sinkhorn-based algorithm called ε-Adaptive Selection (ε-AS) for efficient subset selection.

Result: Theoretically, they show how entropic regularization governs a trade-off in the selection process and obtain a minimax bound that is independent of the specific task. Practically, experiments on six public datasets and various annotation budgets demonstrate that ε-AS consistently outperforms state-of-the-art CSAL baselines. On ImageNet-1k, ε-AS improves average accuracy by 1.29% over ActiveFT and cuts selection time by 56.2%.

Conclusion: Viewing cold-start active learning through optimal transport yields a unified framework that subsumes existing approaches and enables principled adaptation via entropic regularization. The resulting ε-Adaptive Selection algorithm is both theoretically grounded and empirically superior, delivering robust state-of-the-art performance and faster selection across diverse datasets and budget settings.

Abstract: Cold-Start Active Learning (CSAL) aims to select a valuable subset from an unlabeled pool without any prior knowledge or human assistance. Existing methods take diverse routes based on typicality, coverage, or diversity. Each rests on its own inductive bias and therefore performs well on some tasks yet poorly on others. We argue that the real challenge is not to design yet another selection heuristic, but to make CSAL adapt automatically to the data and task at hand. To this end, we revisit CSAL through the lens of optimal transport. First, we propose a generalized transport selection framework that reveals the shared allocation structure of existing methods and exactly subsumes representative formulations. Second, we introduce a theoretical analysis that characterizes the trade-off controlled by entropic regularization and establishes a task-agnostic minimax bound for cold-start selection. These results provide a principled foundation for adapting the regularization strength to the unlabeled data. Third, we derive a data-adaptive regularization rule and present a novel Sinkhorn-based CSAL algorithm, termed $ε$-Adaptive Selection ($ε$-AS). Extensive experiments on six public datasets and multiple annotation budgets show that $ε$-AS consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance. On ImageNet-1k, it improves the average accuracy over ActiveFT by 1.29% while reducing selection time by 56.2%. Code will be released at https://github.com/Z-yiwei/OT-CSAL

</details>


### [128] [TaskPress: Query-Agnostic KV Cache Compression via Task-Guided Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03276)
*Wonpyo Park,Seung-won Hwang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TaskPress is a task-guided, query-agnostic framework for pruning KV caches in long-context LLM inference, enabling reusable compact memory across queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context LLM inference is expensive because the key-value cache grows linearly with sequence length. Existing pruning methods are query-specific and must recompute token importance for each new query, limiting efficiency and reusability. The paper aims to design a method that makes cache compression reusable across queries that share a task, reducing computation and memory costs.

Method: TaskPress introduces a task-guided, query-agnostic KV cache eviction framework. It conditions cache construction on a high-level task guide that acts as a meta-query during the prefill stage, filtering out task-irrelevant tokens before downstream queries are processed. Additionally, TaskPress uses quantization scale factors as a zero-cost signal to detect influential representation outliers, using them as an efficient proxy for token importance in deciding which tokens to retain or evict from the cache.

Result: On several long-context benchmarks, TaskPress is shown to build compact, reusable KV caches that work well across diverse queries for the same task. It maintains effective performance while reducing cache size and improving efficiency compared with prior query-specific pruning methods.

Conclusion: TaskPress offers a practical way to compress KV caches for long-context LLMs by leveraging task-level guidance and quantization-based importance signals. This produces a reusable, task-specific memory representation that supports multiple downstream queries efficiently, addressing scalability limitations of earlier query-dependent pruning approaches.

Abstract: Long-context inference with large language models is constrained by the linear growth of the key-value cache to sequence length. While pruning offers mitigation, prevailing methods determine query-specific token importance that cannot be reused across unseen queries. In contrast, we introduce TaskPress, a framework for task-guided, query-agnostic KV cache eviction. Instead of optimizing the cache for a single query, TaskPress constructs a reusable memory representation conditioned on a high-level task guide. The guide functions as a meta-query during prefill to filter irrelevant tokens before downstream queries are issued. In addition, TaskPress leverages quantization scale factors as a zero-cost signal for detecting influential representation outliers, providing an efficient proxy for token importance. Experiments on conducted on various tasks with long context input demonstrate that TaskPress efficiently creates a compact, reusable cache across diverse queries.

</details>


### [129] [AgentPanel: Toward a New Paradigm for Human--AI Collaboration in Exploring Scientific Questions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03283)
*Zhiyao Cui,Qianyi Wang,Haoyang Yan,Yiqun Zhang,Siyue Ren,Hangfan Zhang,Zelin Tan,Hao Li,Chunjiang Mu,Dexian Cai,Shao Zhang,Chen Zhang,Meng Li,Jianan Chai,Yuting Fan,Zichao Ye,Xiaolei Yang,Xinyao Lu,Yuyang Yu,Wenjie Lou,Xiaosong Wang,Fenghua Ling,Shiyang Feng,Mao Su,Qiaosheng Zhang,Bo Zhang,Yang Chen,Lei Bai,Shuyue Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces AgentPanel, a multi-agent, forum-style system that helps researchers explore and assess early-stage scientific ideas, showing it improves idea breadth and quality over baseline tools and is preferred to common LLM setups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current practices for finding promising scientific ideas—such as small-group discussions or querying a single LLM—limit researchers to narrow perspectives and may miss valuable directions. There is a need for scalable, structured human–AI collaboration that can surface diverse viewpoints, broaden exploration, and still remain efficient for researchers to navigate.

Method: The authors design AgentPanel, a platform where multiple heterogeneous AI agents asynchronously discuss user-submitted scientific questions in a forum-like environment. Researchers can browse, organize, and refine candidate ideas, interact with agents via follow-up prompts, and generate post-hoc summary reports. The system is evaluated via offline experiments comparing it to a centralized multi-agent debate baseline, and via a human-subject study with 20 participants who used AgentPanel and compared it to commonly used LLM tools based on their experience.

Result: Offline evaluations indicate that AgentPanel generates higher-quality ideas, supports broader exploration, and improves interaction effectiveness and candidate-selection efficiency compared to a centralized multi-agent debate baseline. In a user study, participants reported valuing AgentPanel’s diversity of perspectives and its support for exploring research directions; 65% of participants preferred it over their usual LLM tools for both the breadth of research directions and its suitability for early-stage exploration.

Conclusion: A multi-agent, forum-style approach can enhance early-stage scientific ideation by providing diverse perspectives and structured exploration support. AgentPanel improves over baseline multi-agent approaches and is generally preferred to standard LLM tools for ideation tasks, suggesting that such collaborative agent forums are a promising paradigm for human–AI scientific exploration. The system is publicly accessible for broader use and further study.

Abstract: Identifying promising scientific ideas remains an important challenge in research practice. Researchers commonly rely on small-group discussions or one-to-one interactions with a single large language model, yet these approaches often expose them to only a limited range of perspectives and directions. We present AgentPanel, a multi-agent forum for human--AI collaboration in scientific exploration. Heterogeneous agents asynchronously discuss scientific questions in a forum-style environment, while researchers can submit questions, browse and organize candidate ideas, engage agents in follow-up interactions, and optionally generate post-hoc summary reports. We evaluate AgentPanel in terms of idea quality, exploration breadth, interaction effectiveness, candidate-selection efficiency, and practical utility. Offline experiments show that AgentPanel outperforms a centralized multi-agent debate baseline. A human study with 20 participants further shows that users value AgentPanel for perspective diversity and exploration support. In experience-based comparisons with commonly used LLM tools, 65\% of participants favored AgentPanel for both breadth of research directions and overall suitability for early-stage exploration. The platform is publicly available at https://agentpanel.cc/.

</details>


### [130] [DocTrace: Towards Traceable Long Document VQA via Hierarchical Evidence Graph Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03292)
*Le Xiang,Zhicheng Guan,Hong Chen,Xiaocong Lin,Zhenghua Lei,Teng Hu,Bolei He,Long Zeng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DocTrace reframes long-document visual QA as explicit evidence graph reasoning, with a hierarchical pipeline and specialized training, yielding better accuracy and traceable reasoning than strong MLLM baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MLLM, RAG, and agent-based approaches for LongDocVQA must reason over many heterogeneous elements across pages but typically treat reasoning and evidence aggregation implicitly. This hurts answer accuracy and, crucially, makes it hard to trace or verify which document parts support an answer. The authors want a system that not only answers better but also exposes explicit, verifiable evidence provenance over long documents.

Method: They formulate LongDocVQA as constructing and reasoning over an explicit evidence graph rather than directly predicting answers. They introduce DocTrace, a hierarchical framework with three main stages: (1) evidence localization across long multi-page documents, (2) structured document parsing to turn located regions into structured elements, and (3) evidence graph reasoning that composes these elements into an explicit evidence graph used to derive answers. Training is done in two stages: joint supervised fine-tuning to initialize both localization and graph reasoning, followed by Group Relative Policy Optimization (a policy-gradient RL method) with tailored rewards to further refine evidence localization and reasoning behavior.

Result: On three LongDocVQA benchmarks (MMLongBench-Doc, LongDocURL, and SlideVQA), DocTrace significantly outperforms both open-source baselines and strong proprietary MLLMs. Using Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct as the base model, DocTrace yields absolute gains of 14.4, 11.3, and 11.7 points on the respective benchmarks, indicating substantial improvements in long-document understanding and QA performance.

Conclusion: Recasting LongDocVQA as explicit evidence graph reasoning and implementing it via the DocTrace hierarchical framework leads to more accurate and more interpretable long-document visual QA. The method not only improves benchmark scores but also produces traceable evidence graphs with node-level provenance, enabling transparent, verifiable reasoning over complex multi-page documents.

Abstract: Long Document Visual Question Answering (LongDocVQA) requires Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to locate, integrate, and reason over heterogeneous document elements distributed across multiple pages. Existing approaches, including end-to-end MLLMs, retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) pipelines, and document agents, often lack explicit mechanisms to represent and verify how grounded evidence is progressively composed during reasoning, limiting both answer accuracy and traceability. In this paper, we cast LongDocVQA as an explicit evidence graph reasoning problem rather than implicit answer prediction. To this end, we propose DocTrace, a hierarchical framework that progressively performs evidence localization, structured document parsing, and evidence graph reasoning to enable explicit evidence provenance. To effectively learn these capabilities, we develop a two-stage training framework: joint Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) first initializes evidence localization and graph reasoning abilities, followed by task-specific Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with dedicated rewards to further optimize these capabilities. Extensive experiments on MMLongBench-Doc, LongDocURL, and SlideVQA demonstrate that DocTrace consistently outperforms both existing open-source baselines and proprietary MLLMs. Compared with the Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct backbone, DocTrace achieves absolute improvements of 14.4, 11.3, and 11.7 points on the three benchmarks, respectively. Beyond competitive performance, DocTrace constructs traceable evidence graphs with explicit node-level provenance, enabling transparent and verifiable reasoning for long document understanding.

</details>


### [131] [Distractor-Aware Truncation: Disentangling Context-Length Effects from Signal Loss in Long-Context LLM Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03297)
*Mohsen Arjmandi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper tests whether shorter contexts help language models when relevant information is preserved, finding that naive truncation hurts because it drops answer-bearing content, while distractor-aware truncation preserves or improves performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many works on retrieval-augmented and memory-augmented language models assume that reducing context length (while keeping relevant information) improves performance. However, standard evaluation protocols often shorten context by naively truncating the middle of prompts, potentially confounding genuine context-length effects with the accidental removal of task-relevant information. The authors aim to disentangle true context-window effects from artifacts of how context is shortened, and to assess whether prior conclusions about shorter contexts being better are methodologically sound.

Method: The authors systematically evaluate long-context language model performance across different context-retention fractions (100%, 75%, 50%, 25%) using two truncation protocols on long-context benchmarks: BABILong and GraphWalks (BFS), and additionally MRCR v2 and Oolong. The naive protocol simply drops content from the middle of the prompt, mimicking common practice in prior work. The distractor-aware protocol first identifies task-relevant (answer-bearing) content for each sample and selectively removes only distractor content, guaranteeing preservation of the signal. They test three model sizes from the Claude family (Haiku 4.5, Sonnet 4.6, Opus 4.7) and one model from another provider (GPT-5.5), measuring performance changes and using paired Wilcoxon tests with Holm correction to assess statistical significance of performance differences across retention levels and protocols. They also analyze how frequently answer-bearing content survives under naive truncation at different retention levels.

Result: Under naive middle truncation, performance monotonically collapses as context-retention decreases across all evaluated cells in BABILong and GraphWalks, with statistically significant degradation (Holm-corrected p_adj < 0.05 in all eight cells). In contrast, under the distractor-aware protocol that explicitly preserves task-relevant content, performance is maintained or improves: the smaller Claude models (Haiku 4.5, Sonnet 4.6) achieve statistically significant gains on BABILong, while larger models (Opus 4.7 and GPT-5.5) reach a ceiling comparable to their full-context scores. The same pattern—naive-performance collapse and distractor-aware recovery—replicates on GPT-5.5, demonstrating that the effect is not specific to a single provider. Mechanistically, they show that with naive truncation at 25% retention, answer-bearing content survives in <1% of samples, whereas the distractor-aware protocol preserves it by construction.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that commonly used naive truncation protocols do not meaningfully measure the effect of context-window length on language model performance. Instead, they mainly reflect how often middle-removal happens to leave the answer-bearing content intact. When task-relevant information is reliably preserved through a distractor-aware scheme, shorter contexts do not inherently degrade—and can even improve—performance, especially for smaller models. Consequently, the authors argue that future research on context-length effects must explicitly define and implement a method for distinguishing signal from distractor; otherwise, conclusions about the benefits or harms of longer context windows are ambiguous and potentially misleading, as they confound context length with loss of relevant information.

Abstract: A standard claim in the literature on retrieval-augmented and memory-augmented language models is that shorter context is better when the relevant information is preserved. We test this claim by running every sample of two long-context benchmarks -- BABILong and GraphWalks (BFS) -- at four context-retention fractions (100%, 75%, 50%, 25%) under two truncation protocols. The first is the naive protocol implicitly used in much prior work: drop content from the middle of the prompt. The second is distractor-aware: identify the task-relevant content for each sample and drop only the rest. We evaluate three sizes of the Claude family (Haiku 4.5, Sonnet 4.6, Opus 4.7) and, to test cross-provider generality, GPT-5.5 from a different provider; we apply the same protocol to two further benchmarks (MRCR v2, Oolong). Under naive truncation, score collapses monotonically (paired Wilcoxon, Holm-corrected p_adj < 0.05 in all eight BABILong and GraphWalks cells). Under the distractor-aware protocol -- which preserves the signal by construction -- performance is preserved or improves: the two smaller Claude models show statistically significant gains on BABILong, while the larger models (Opus 4.7 and GPT-5.5) sit at their full-context ceiling. The naive collapse and its distractor-aware recovery replicate on GPT-5.5, ruling out a single-provider artifact. The mechanism is direct: under the naive protocol the answer-bearing content survives in fewer than 1% of samples at 25% retention; under the distractor-aware protocol it is preserved by construction. The naive protocol is therefore not a measurement of context-window effects; it is a measurement of how often middle-removal happens to spare the answer. We conclude that future studies of context-length effects must specify how they distinguish signal from distractor, or they are at best ambiguous between two opposite hypotheses.

</details>


### [132] [SeaSlides: Semantic Abstraction Layer for Agentic Slide Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03298)
*Shengjun Fang,Chenyang Wu,Zongzhang Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SeaSlides is an agentic slide-generation framework that uses a semantic abstraction layer and reusable components to create robust, rich-content presentations across multiple backends (HTML and Typst), outperforming SVG-heavy approaches in readability and rich-content quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agentic systems for generating slide decks either rely on rigid templates that limit adaptation or on low-level HTML/SVG authoring that forces models to make brittle rendering decisions, especially for technical slides with math, code, charts, and complex layouts. This mismatch leads to fragile, hard-to-maintain decks and poor separation between content and presentation.

Method: The authors introduce SeaSlides, which inserts a semantic abstraction layer between the model and the rendering backend. The model authoring interface consists of structured slide content built from reusable components and capability modules, rather than raw coordinates or SVG. Templates in each backend (HTML and Typst) control layout, styling, and rendering. SeaSlides-HTML uses DOM components; SeaSlides-Typst uses template functions and package-based modules. Specialized capability modules route equations, code, and charts to domain-specific renderers. Three feedback stages check and correct build errors, constraint violations, and visual defects before export. Both backends share the same authoring boundary but keep backend-specific syntax and contracts.

Result: Using UltraPresent (128 tasks) plus a new benchmark, SeaSlidesBench-Rich (32 tasks focused on math, code, pseudocode, tables, charts, diagrams), the authors compare SeaSlides-HTML and SeaSlides-Typst to SVG-heavy generation across four LLMs. Both SeaSlides backends generate more readable, content-centric source code than SVG-based pipelines. A SeaSlides backend achieves the best macro-average performance on rich-content tasks under three of four models while maintaining competitive overall qualitative scores.

Conclusion: Semantic abstraction—where models write structured semantic slide content while templates own layout and rendering—is an effective, backend-agnostic principle for agentic slide generation. SeaSlides demonstrates that this separation improves readability and robustness for rich technical content and generalizes across different presentation backends (HTML and Typst) while preserving competitive qualitative performance.

Abstract: Agentic presentation generation must preserve source content, maintain coherent visual design, render specialized objects, and produce usable artifacts. Existing systems meet only part of this requirement: templates preserve regularity but restrict adaptation, whereas free-form HTML or SVG gives models flexibility at the cost of low-level rendering decisions. This mismatch makes long technical decks brittle, especially when slides contain formulas, code, or data graphics. We present SeaSlides, an agentic slide-generation framework built around a semantic abstraction layer. Rather than authoring coordinates, inline styles, or raw SVG geometry, the model writes structured slide content through reusable components and capability modules, while templates own layout, style, and rendering. We instantiate this principle separately in HTML and Typst: SeaSlides-HTML uses template-defined DOM components, whereas SeaSlides-Typst uses template functions and package-backed modules. Capability modules route equations, code, and charts to dedicated renderers, and three feedback stages localize build errors, project-constraint violations, and visual defects before export. The two systems retain backend-specific syntax and contracts while sharing the same authoring boundary. For evaluation, we combine the 128-task UltraPresent validation setting with SeaSlidesBench-Rich, a new 32-task benchmark stressing mathematics, code, pseudocode, tables, charts, and diagrams. Across four generation models, both SeaSlides backends produce more readable, content-oriented source than SVG-heavy generation. A SeaSlides backend attains the highest rich-content macro-average under three of the four models while maintaining competitive overall qualitative performance. These results support semantic abstraction as a practical authoring principle across presentation backends.

</details>


### [133] [Screenshots or Tools? Eliciting Tool Use and Managing Multimodal Context in Hybrid GUI-MCP Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03327)
*Siqi Fan,Minghao Li,Xiaoqian Ma,Wenhui Tan,Xiusheng Huang,Juntong Wu,Liujie Zhang,Shuo Shang,Weihang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies when and how hybrid computer-use agents should use external tools versus GUI actions, finding that tools help only when models can decide to use and integrate them properly, and that current agents underuse tools due to training and semantic bottlenecks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hybrid agents can act via screenshots and via text-based tools, but simply giving models tools does not guarantee better performance; sometimes it helps, sometimes it hurts. The authors want to understand why tool availability has opposite effects on different models, what governs tool adoption and usage quality, and how to reduce computational cost while improving performance.

Method: They evaluate two models (a reasoning and a non-reasoning policy) under the same GUI-MCP harness on the OSWorld-MCP benchmark with identical tool access, analyzing how tool-decision behavior impacts success. They define and measure an 'adoption gap'—how often tools are available but not used. They run multi-turn reinforcement learning experiments, adding dense tool-use bonuses to encourage specific tool adoption (e.g., spreadsheets), and study how that affects behavior versus accuracy. They also modify the observation rules (e.g., dropping screenshots after successful tool calls and halving image history) to compress input, retrain agents under these new rules, and compare performance and input cost tradeoffs, including on a pre-registered degraded subset.

Result: With identical tools, the reasoning model gains +4.0 percentage points in accuracy, while the non-reasoning model loses -5.9 points, with both changes statistically significant. The non-reasoning model frequently ignores tools, misnames them, or terminates incorrectly when tools are involved, whereas the reasoning model largely avoids such failures but still employs tools on only 55 out of 309 tasks, using them in just 23.9% of cases where tools could help. A dense RL tool bonus increases spreadsheet tool adoption from 0.03 to 0.33 and this preference persists in greedy decoding, yet there is no corresponding improvement in held-out accuracy, showing that behavior can be steered without improving underlying competence. At the context level, dropping redundant screenshots after successful tool calls and halving image history reduces input tokens by about one-third with only a small accuracy loss, and retraining under this observation regime not only removes the loss but improves performance: the compressed agent reaches 37.8% accuracy versus 33.0% for the uncompressed configuration while using just 53% of the input tokens, and on a pre-registered degraded subset the performance gap between rich and lean contexts closes to zero.

Conclusion: Merely providing tools to hybrid agents does not ensure gains: both poor tool-decision policies and under-adoption limit benefits. RL incentives can increase tool usage but do not automatically raise task accuracy because the core bottleneck is in tool-call semantics and integration, not in aversion to using tools. Adjusting observation rules to exploit cases where tools make further screenshots redundant can substantially cut input costs, and with retraining, even improve accuracy. Overall, tools are beneficial only when models are trained to choose, interpret, and integrate them effectively, and current hybrid agents leave many valuable tool-usage opportunities unexploited.

Abstract: Hybrid computer-use agents can act through screenshots or call text tools. We find that having a tool available does not settle which way the effect goes. Under one identical GUI-MCP harness on the OSWorld-MCP benchmark (309 tasks), the same MCP tools improve a reasoning model by +4.0pp and degrade a non-reasoning model by -5.9pp (5 runs each, both beyond 2 SE). What separates the two is tool-decision behavior. The non-reasoning policy ignores, misnames, or falsely terminates around tools. The reasoning model avoids these failures, yet still calls a tool on only 55/309 tasks, 23.9% of the tool-reachable ones. We call this shortfall the adoption gap. Both levels of the problem share one cause: the model already has a cheaper route and is never trained to take it. Multi-turn RL probes that cause. At the action level, a dense tool bonus raises spreadsheet adoption 0.03 -> 0.33 and carries into greedy decoding, but held-out accuracy does not follow. Behavior is steerable; competence is not. The bottleneck lies in tool-call semantics. At the context level, a successful tool call often makes the next screenshot redundant. Dropping it and halving image history cuts input tokens by about a third, at a small accuracy cost. Retraining under the same observation rule removes that cost. The compressed agent then reaches 37.8% against 33.0% for the uncompressed operating point, at 53% of the input cost, and closes the rich-lean gap on a pre-registered degraded subset to zero. Tools help when the model chooses and integrates them, and current hybrid agents leave many such choices unused.

</details>


### [134] [Long-term Traffic Scene Prediction via Polynomial Representations in Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03330)
*Yue Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The thesis proposes polynomial-based representations for traffic scene prediction that are more efficient, generalizable, and behaviorally plausible than traditional sequence-based methods, validated theoretically and empirically on major autonomous driving benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sequence-based models for trajectory and scene prediction in autonomous driving struggle with noise, poor generalization under distribution shift, high computational cost, and limited ability to capture realistic motion plausibility. There is a need for representations that are both computationally efficient and better aligned with real-world vehicle dynamics, while maintaining or improving predictive accuracy.

Method: The work models both agent trajectories and map geometry using moderate-degree polynomials, justified via theoretical analysis of motion dynamics. On top of these representations, it builds: (1) a prediction model that uses polynomial parameterization instead of raw sequences; and (2) a diffusion-based generative framework that operates in the polynomial space to generate multi-agent future traffic scenes. The models are trained and evaluated on large-scale autonomous driving datasets such as Argoverse 2 and Waymo Open, comparing against standard baselines on accuracy, generalization, and plausibility metrics.

Result: Polynomial representations achieve near state-of-the-art prediction accuracy on standard benchmarks while substantially reducing computational cost. They generalize better under distribution shifts across datasets, produce smoother and more kinematically consistent trajectories, and yield more plausible multi-agent traffic continuations than conventional baselines. The experiments also show that commonly used in-distribution metrics and regression losses do not fully capture generalization and behavioral plausibility.

Conclusion: Polynomial trajectory representations constitute an efficient, expressive, and robust foundation for traffic scene prediction in autonomous driving. They improve computational efficiency, cross-dataset generalization, and motion plausibility while maintaining high predictive accuracy. The work also highlights limitations of current evaluation protocols and suggests that better measures of generalization and behavioral realism are necessary for safety-critical autonomous driving systems.

Abstract: This thesis addresses fundamental challenges in traffic scene prediction for autonomous driving by introducing robust and computationally efficient models based on polynomial representations. While conventional sequence-based representations often struggle with noise and generalization, this work demonstrates that polynomial representations offer significant advantages in computational efficiency, generalization, and prediction plausibility. Through theoretical analysis and empirical validation, this thesis demonstrates that moderate-degree polynomials capture real-world motion dynamics with high fidelity without constraining predictive performance. Building on this foundation, a prediction model representing both trajectories and map geometry with polynomial representations achieves near state-of-the-art accuracy on standard benchmarks while substantially improving generalization under distribution shift. Extending this concept, a diffusion- based generative framework enables multi-agent scene generation, producing traffic continuations that are more plausible and kinematically consistent than those generated by conventional baselines. Evaluations on the Argoverse 2 and Waymo Open datasets confirm that polynomial representations reduce computational cost, enhance cross-dataset generalization, and yield smoother trajectories and higher behavioral plausibility. The findings reveal that standard in-distribution evaluation and regression-based metrics may fail to reflect true model generalization and prediction plausibility. By providing theoretical justification and empirical validation, this dissertation estab- lishes polynomial trajectory representations as an efficient, expressive, and generalizable foundation for traffic scene prediction in safety critical autonomous driving.

</details>


### [135] [Traceable Multi-Agent System for Knowledge-Based Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03339)
*Junhyeok Kang,Sangjun Han,Hyeokjun Choe,Soonyoung Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces TraceMAS, a system that makes multi-agent, autonomous enterprise forecasting transparent and traceable through causal-loop visualizations connecting domain knowledge, data, and model changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As enterprises increasingly use autonomous agents for forecasting, their pipelines become opaque: it is hard for practitioners to see why forecasts change, what evidence supports those changes, and how data and modeling decisions evolve. The authors aim to improve transparency and inspectability of these autonomous forecasting systems without sacrificing their flexibility.

Method: The authors design and implement TraceMAS, an interactive demo system for multi-agent forecasting. The system structures agent outputs around two causal-loop diagrams: (1) an Ideal Causal Loop Diagram extracted from domain documents, representing domain factors and causal relations; and (2) a Data-Grounded Causal Loop Diagram that links those conceptual factors to internal variables, external datasets, or proxy measurements. These diagrams guide feature construction and model design while maintaining explicit links between textual evidence, data choices, and model revisions. A user interface allows exploration of forecast iterations, agent-level edits, causal maps, data-feature mappings, and model architectures.

Result: TraceMAS is demonstrated on crude oil price forecasting, showing that users can compare successive forecasting iterations, inspect how agents revised models and data, navigate causal maps, and relate scenario forecasts to market narratives. The demo illustrates that the system can maintain a flexible, autonomous forecasting workflow while exposing the chain from evidence and domain knowledge to final forecasts.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that traceable, causal-loop-based organization of multi-agent forecasting processes can make autonomous enterprise forecasting more transparent and inspectable, enabling practitioners to understand and audit how evidence, data, and modeling decisions lead to specific forecasts, without unduly constraining the autonomy of the agents.

Abstract: Enterprise forecasting increasingly relies on autonomous agents that interpret documents, search for data, generate code, and revise models. While this autonomy helps build adaptive forecasting pipelines, it also makes it difficult for practitioners to inspect why a forecast changed, which evidence supported the change, and how data and modeling choices were revised. We present TraceMAS, an interactive demo system for traceable multi-agent forecasting. TraceMAS organizes agent outputs around two causal-loop representations: an Ideal Causal Loop Diagram (Ideal CLD), which captures key factors and their causal relations extracted from domain documents, and a Data-Grounded Causal Loop Diagram (Data-Grounded CLD), which links those factors to internal variables, external data, or documented proxies. The Data-Grounded CLD guides feature construction and model design while preserving the connection between textual evidence, data choices, and model revisions. We demonstrate TraceMAS on crude oil price forecasting. The demo interface allows users to compare forecasting iterations, inspect agent-level revisions, explore causal maps, review feature-data mappings and model architecture, and connect scenario forecasts to market narratives. This demonstration shows how autonomous forecasting agents can retain flexibility while making the evidence-to-forecast process inspectable.

</details>


### [136] [MMLongBench-Doc-V2: A Corrected-Annotation, Semantics-Aware Revision of MMLongBench-Doc](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03397)
*Mingtian Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MMLongBench-Doc-V2 is a corrected and re-evaluated long-document QA benchmark that fixes annotation errors and improves the evaluation metric, making results more faithful to true model abilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original MMLongBench-Doc benchmark had two key issues that distorted evaluation: (1) a brittle string-matching metric that treated semantically identical but differently formatted numeric answers (e.g., '1,358,000' vs '1358000') as wrong, and (2) a substantial portion of ground-truth annotations that were wrong, ambiguous, or incomplete, especially for harder questions that capable systems could actually answer. These flaws made reported scores diverge from the underlying quantity of interest—true QA performance on long documents—motivating the creation of a more accurate and reliable benchmark version.

Method: The authors re-audit the benchmark and perform several corrections: (1) They manually fix 106 flawed annotations, documenting for each the exact page references and arithmetic used to determine the correct answer. (2) They replace the strict string-matching metric with an LLM-based judging protocol, where a pinned LLM determines whether a model’s answer matches the reference in meaning, not just in surface form. (3) They clean the dataset by removing 10 questions whose documents had incorrect filenames and one duplicated question, resulting in 1,071 questions over 134 PDFs.
