(4) They formalize and apply a decision procedure for when an empty set key in the data can be widened without compromising deliberate negative examples, and apply this to all 208 relevant rows, widening 14 of them.

Result: The outcome is a revised benchmark, MMLongBench-Doc-V2, that: (1) contains 1,071 questions over 134 documents instead of 1,082 over 135; (2) includes 106 corrected annotations with transparent per-entry correction records; (3) uses an LLM-based semantic equivalence metric instead of raw string matching; and (4) applies a principled rule for widening empty set keys, modifying 14 of 208 such cases. Because of these methodological changes, scores computed on V2 are not directly comparable to previously published results on V1.

Conclusion: MMLongBench-Doc-V2 provides a more faithful and reusable resource for evaluating long-document QA systems by fixing annotation errors, adopting a semantic evaluation metric, and clarifying dataset construction decisions. While it breaks backward comparability with V1 scores, it yields measurements that better reflect actual model understanding. The dataset, correction logs, and evaluation harness are publicly released for use by the community.

Abstract: MMLongBench-Doc is a long-document QA benchmark of 1,082 questions over 135 PDFs. Two properties of it push measured scores away from the quantity they are meant to capture: the reference metric compares extracted answers, so 1,358,000 loses to 1358000; and a non-trivial share of ground-truth annotations are wrong, ambiguous, or incomplete --- concentrated, because of how they were found, in exactly the questions capable systems answer correctly. MMLongBench-Doc-V2 corrects 106 annotations, each published with the page and arithmetic that settle it, and replaces the string metric with a pinned LLM judge asked whether a response means the reference. Ten questions whose document ships under the wrong filename are removed rather than counted wrong, along with one duplicated question, leaving 1,071 questions over 134 documents. The most reusable contribution is a decision procedure for when an empty set key may be widened and when widening would destroy a deliberate negative sample; applied to all 208 rows, it widened 14. V2 scores are not comparable with published V1 numbers. The corrected corpus, the per-entry correction record and the evaluation harness are available at https://github.com/VectifyAI/MMLongBench-Doc-V2.

</details>


### [137] [Towards Robust Tool Use in Agents via Experience-Driven Adaptive Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03403)
*Can Wang,Haoran Chen,Li Yu,Ding Hao,Bohai Zhao,Zhaoyang Liu,Zhiying Tu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes ExpG, a three-phase framework that learns from past tool-use experiences to guide LLM agents toward more robust and effective tool use across diverse runtime conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM agents increasingly rely on tools to interact with external environments, their performance bottleneck has shifted from model capacity to the robustness of their execution and tool-use processes. Existing approaches underutilize historical tool-use traces and rarely provide systematic, adaptive guidance on when and how to use each tool under varying conditions. There is a need for a principled way to capture tools’ capability boundaries and best practices so that agents can avoid common failures, select the right tools, and invoke them effectively, particularly in challenging scenarios or when using smaller models.

Method: The authors introduce ExpG, a mechanism that converts past tool-use experiences into adaptive guidance via three phases. (1) Experience acquisition: they analyze historical execution trajectories, assess the quality of each tool invocation, and attribute success or failure along multiple aspects (e.g., selection, arguments, timing), forming structured, learnable experience units. (2) Experience distillation: they maintain an effective experience pool by filtering low-quality or redundant experiences, grouping similar cases into equivalence classes, choosing representative examples, and summarizing them into compact, generalizable guidance that captures the capability boundaries and best practices for each tool. (3) Experience reuse: at inference time, the agent conditions its planning, tool selection, and invocation on the distilled guidance, applying it adaptively to new tasks and runtime contexts to improve robustness of tool selection, calling, and downstream response generation.

Result: Through extensive experiments on multiple tool-using benchmarks and tasks, ExpG consistently improves agents’ performance on tool selection, tool invocation, and final response generation. The framework helps smaller models, when equipped with ExpG-derived guidance, outperform larger models that lack such guidance. The gains are particularly pronounced in more challenging or noisy settings, demonstrating increased robustness of tool use and suggesting that systematic experience-guided tool use can partially compensate for smaller model capacity.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that robust tool use is a key determinant of agent performance and that ExpG offers an effective way to leverage historical execution trajectories to learn tool-specific capability boundaries and best practices. By structuring, distilling, and reusing experience as adaptive guidance, ExpG substantially enhances agents’ robustness and effectiveness in tool use, enabling smaller agents to rival or exceed larger ones. The strong improvements in difficult conditions indicate that experience-guided tool use is a promising direction for building more reliable, capable tool-using agents. The authors release their code and experimental resources to support further research in this area.

Abstract: The performance bottleneck of agents is increasingly shifting from model capability to the robustness of their execution processes. Tools play a central role as the primary interface through which agents interact with external environments, yet existing methods rarely focus on ensuring robust tool use across diverse runtime conditions. To address this problem, we propose ExpG, a mechanism that builds and refines adaptive guidance capturing each tool's capability boundaries and best practices, thereby enabling agents to use tools more robustly and effectively. ExpG consists of three phases: (1) experience acquisition, which analyzes tool invocation quality from historical execution trajectories, producing structured learnable experiences through multi-aspect attribution; (2) experience distillation, which keeps the experience pool effective by filtering unhelpful experiences, selecting representative ones with an equivalence-class-based method, and summarizing them into generalizable guidance; and (3) experience reuse, which applies the guidance adaptively during future task solving. Extensive experiments show that ExpG brings consistent improvements across the tool selection, tool calling, and response generation tasks, enabling smaller agents to outperform larger ones that do not use ExpG. Moreover, ExpG achieves particularly strong gains in challenging settings, suggesting a promising path toward more robust tool use. Our code, experiments, and results are available.

</details>


### [138] [Enactive Artificial Intelligence: A Decision-Centric Architecture for Complex Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03413)
*Zuojun Max Shen,Yuan Qu,Pujun Zhang,Anbang Liu,Yunhao Liang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a conceptual framework called Enactive AI for applying AI to complex enterprise and industrial systems, emphasizing decision intelligence and system-aware action rather than just model capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI progress, especially around LLMs and generative models, has not been sufficiently validated or structured for real-world, complex enterprise and industrial environments, which demand reliability, feasibility, resilience, and responsibility. There is a need for a framework that organizes how AI interacts with organizational structures, physical sites, and decision processes in a scalable and governable way.

Method: The authors synthesize adjacent research and introduce a conceptual framework named Enactive AI. This framework is organized around four roles: (1) Organizational World – a world model of operations management logic and organizational behavior at a strategic-institutional level; (2) Site World – a physically bounded world model for industrial optimization and execution at an operational level; (3) Schema Intelligence – the coupling mechanism that links the Organizational and Site worlds and integrates various AI applications across them; and (4) Enactive Decision Cycle – a self-evolving dynamic process for continuous updating and auditing of the entire framework.

Result: The paper presents Enactive AI as a structured way to connect AI capabilities with enterprise- and site-level decision-making, enabling AI to participate in reasoning, decision support, and execution feedback across complex systems. It shows how the four roles together can organize AI tools, agents, and harnesses into a coherent, governable decision intelligence system.

Conclusion: Enactive AI reframes AI progress from a focus on model generation and automation capabilities to system-aware action and decision intelligence in complex enterprise and industrial settings. The framework aims to support reliable, responsible, and socially valuable AI deployments at scale, proposing that such system-level decision support and governance will define the next frontier of AI research for enterprise and industrial complex systems.

Abstract: As artificial intelligence (AI) continues to evolve and mature, recent AI practices have moved beyond large language models (LLMs) and text or image generation tasks, increasingly integrating tools, agents, and harnesses to solve real business and industrial problems. However, the power of AI is not verified under these real-world complex systems for various reasons, considering reliability, feasibility, resilience, and responsibility requirements in real commercial and industrial operations. This study synthesizes adjacent research and introduces Enactive AI as a conceptual framework for enterprise and industry reasoning, site-level decision support, and execution feedback. Four complementary roles organize the framework: an Organizational World defines operations management logic and an organizational behavior world model behind an enterprise from a strategic-institutional horizon; a Site World defines a physically bounded industrial optimization and execution world model from an operational-realization horizon; Schema Intelligence provides the coupling mechanism between two world models to weave various AI applications via two models; and Enactive Decision Cycle triggers the self-evolving dynamic process to update and audit the entire framework. By foregrounding decision intelligence in complex systems, Enactive AI expands the frontier of AI from model capability to system-aware action, opening new possibilities for scalable, governable, and socially valuable AI deployment. Enactive AI points toward a future in which AI progress is measured not only by what models can generate or automate, but by how reliably intelligent systems can support consequential action, responsible governance, and durable social value in the complex systems that shape modern life, which we believe will define the next frontier of AI research for enterprise-level and industrial complex systems.

</details>


### [139] [AI World Cup 2026: Benchmarking Large Language Models for End-to-End Football Tournament Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03416)
*Jonaid Shianifar,Iias Faiud*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They run a standardized “AI World Cup” benchmark where 10 LLMs predict the full 2026 FIFA World Cup; GPT‑5.5 Thinking wins, and results show tournament-wide forecasting behaves differently from match-by-match prediction and is highly sensitive to scoring rules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used to forecast real events, but prior comparisons are messy because models get different inputs, tools, and evaluation rules, making it unclear which models are actually better forecasters and what skills these tasks measure.

Method: Design a controlled benchmark where 10 LLM assistants each make a single, pre-tournament forecast of the entire 2026 FIFA World Cup using an identical snapshot of information, a shared prompt, a fixed JSON output schema, and a unified scoring procedure. Forecasts include group match scores, group rankings, knockout brackets, final placements, confidence values, and brief rationales. After the real tournament concludes, compute points for different forecast components and analyze correlations between total score, phase-specific scores, match-level accuracy, and self-reported confidence.

Result: GPT‑5.5 Thinking scores highest overall (744), followed by GPT‑5.5 (717), Gemini (699), and Qwen 3.7 (687). GPT‑5.5 Thinking is uniquely correct in picking Spain as champion over Argentina. Overall ranking is almost entirely driven by knockout-stage performance (total score correlates strongly with knockout points, r=0.986) and is largely unrelated to group-stage match points (r=0.055), group-standing points (r=-0.103), or their combined pre-knockout score (r=-0.054). Match-level accuracy yields a different model ordering: Claude Sonnet 4.6 has the best group-stage outcome accuracy (63.89%) but is only sixth overall. Self-reported confidence has negligible or slightly negative correlation with both accuracy (r=-0.060) and total score (r=-0.067).

Conclusion: Comprehensive tournament forecasting exercises LLM capabilities that differ from predicting individual matches and produces rankings that are heavily shaped by how knockout stages are scored. Bracket-style leaderboards can therefore be highly sensitive to scoring design, and standardized, fully released benchmarks like this one are important for meaningful comparison and extension of LLM forecasting abilities.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are now regularly asked to forecast real-world events, but comparisons are often difficult because models receive different information, use different tools, and are evaluated under different rules. This paper reports the completed \emph{AI World Cup} benchmark, in which ten LLM-based assistants made a single pre-tournament forecast of the entire 2026 FIFA World Cup. Every submission used the same tournament snapshot, prompt, JSON schema, and scoring procedure. The forecasts covered group-stage scores, group rankings, the knockout bracket, final placings, confidence values, and short explanations. After all 104 matches had been played, GPT-5.5 Thinking finished first with 744 points, followed by GPT-5.5 with 717, Gemini with 699, and Qwen 3.7 with 687. GPT-5.5 Thinking was also the only model to select Spain, which defeated Argentina 1--0 in the final, as champion. The final ranking was driven mainly by knockout performance: total score was strongly correlated with knockout points ($r=0.986$), but showed little relationship with group-stage match points ($r=0.055$), group-standing points ($r=-0.103$), or their combined pre-knockout score ($r=-0.054$). Match-level accuracy produced a different ordering. Claude Sonnet 4.6 correctly predicted the largest number of group-stage outcomes (63.89\%) but placed sixth overall. Average self-reported confidence was also unrelated to either outcome accuracy ($r=-0.060$) or total score ($r=-0.067$). The results suggest that forecasting a complete tournament tests something different from predicting matches one at a time, while also showing how strongly a bracket-based leaderboard can depend on scoring design. The benchmark materials, raw responses, and scoring code are released to support replication and future extensions.

</details>


### [140] [Towards Improving Sequential Decision-Making in LLM Agents via Experience Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03420)
*Jakub Rada,Viliam Lisý*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates how well large language models handle sequential decision-making in two-player zero-sum games and proposes an agentic framework with experience memory to improve their play without changing model weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs perform strongly on single-shot reasoning tasks, their capabilities in sequential decision-making are unclear. Fully observable two-player zero-sum games provide a clean testbed with ground-truth evaluation, where outcomes and move optimality can be precisely measured without subjective judging. The authors are motivated by the observation that even simple games like tic-tac-toe and Connect Four require planning and credit assignment across multiple moves, making them suitable for probing and improving LLMs’ sequential reasoning abilities.

Method: The authors systematically evaluate LLMs of different sizes on simple two-player zero-sum games such as tic-tac-toe and Connect Four, comparing their play against Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) opponents and assessing move optimality using game-theoretic analysis. They introduce obfuscations that preserve the underlying game tree but alter the surface representation to test whether performance is due to memorized strategies. To address observed weaknesses, they propose an agentic framework that augments the LLM with an experience memory tailored to sequential decision-making, incorporating post-game reflection and rule extraction mechanisms, all without modifying model weights.

Result: The experiments show that across model tiers, LLMs play suboptimally in simple board games and are consistently outperformed by MCTS-based agents. Performance remains largely stable under game-tree-preserving obfuscations, suggesting the deficits are not primarily due to lack of memorized strategies or surface-form dependence. Using the proposed agentic framework with experience memory, post-game reflection, and rule extraction leads to measurable performance gains on tic-tac-toe without retraining or fine-tuning the underlying models.

Conclusion: LLMs, despite strong single-shot reasoning skills, are weak at sequential decision-making in even simple two-player zero-sum games and lose to search-based methods like MCTS. This weakness appears not to stem mainly from failure to recall memorized strategies but from more fundamental limitations in handling planning, credit assignment, and learning from interaction. An agentic wrapper with experience memory and reflective rule extraction can partially mitigate these issues and improve gameplay without altering model parameters, highlighting a promising direction for enhancing LLMs’ sequential decision-making abilities.

Abstract: Large language models have improved substantially on single-shot reasoning tasks, but their performance in sequential decision-making is less well understood. We study this on fully-observable two-player zero-sum games, which provide ground-truth evaluation: outcomes are determined by the rules, and optimality of individual moves can be computed or approximated, without relying on a judge model. Across model tiers, LLMs play suboptimally in simple games such as tic-tac-toe or Connect Four, and lose to MCTS opponents. Obfuscations that preserve the game tree but rewrite its surface form leave performance largely unchanged, indicating the gap is not fully explained by recall of memorized strategies. Motivated by this performance gap, we introduce an agentic framework enhanced with an experience memory designed for the sequential setting and addressing common challenges of sequential decision-making such as credit assignment. We show that post-game reflection and rule extraction yield measurable improvements on tic-tac-toe without modifying the model weights.

</details>


### [141] [State Propagation Also Satisfies: A Complex-Valued State-Space Model for Deterministic State Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03425)
*Xiaohe Li,Yang Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new recurrent architecture, Complex State Propagator (CSP), can solve deterministic state tracking tasks using only state propagation, without intermediate attention or output projections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformers with attention are powerful but potentially unnecessarily complex for simple deterministic state tracking tasks like parity checking or modular counting. The authors want to test whether a simpler, purely recurrent state propagation mechanism can match or exceed Transformers on such tasks.

Method: They design the Complex State Propagator (CSP), a minimal recurrent model where the hidden state is a complex-valued vector updated via input-dependent rotations in the complex plane. The model only propagates hidden states across layers, with no intermediate output projections. To enable deep state propagation and stable training, they add block-level skip connections, element-wise complex normalization, and SiLU activation at sequence boundaries. Training uses Focal Loss to handle class imbalance or hard examples.

Result: On canonical deterministic state tracking benchmarks (parity checking, modular counting, parenthesis matching), CSP reaches 100% accuracy and perfect F1 scores, showing it can fully solve these tasks.

Conclusion: State propagation alone, when implemented as complex-valued recurrent rotations with appropriate normalization and skip connections, is sufficient to solve a range of deterministic state tracking tasks, suggesting that attention is not necessary for such problems and that simpler architectures can be effective replacements for Transformers in this regime.

Abstract: Transformer-based architectures have dominated sequence modeling, largely due to the expressive power of attention mechanisms. However, for a class of deterministic state tracking tasks---such as parity checking, modular counting, and parenthesis matching---attention may be overkill. In this paper, we show that \textbf{state propagation alone is sufficient}.
  We propose the \textbf{Complex State Propagator (CSP)}, a minimalistic recurrent architecture that \textbf{only propagates hidden states} across layers without output projections at intermediate steps. The state is represented as a complex-valued vector, updated via input-dependent rotations in the complex domain. To enable deep propagation without gradient vanishing or degradation, we introduce a \textbf{block-level skip connection} alongside element-wise complex normalization and SiLU activation at sequence boundaries. Applied with Focal Loss, CSP achieves \textbf{100\% accuracy} with perfect F1 scores across canonical tasks.

</details>


### [142] [DataSpace: Benchmarking Data Agents for Verifiable Analytics over Heterogeneous Workspaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03451)
*Boyan Li,Zhuowen Liang,Yupeng Xie,Xiaotian Lin,Tianqi Luo,Xinyu Liu,Yizhang Zhu,Zhangyang Peng,Yuan Li,Zhengxuan Zhang,Jiayi Zhang,Nan Tang,Guoliang Li,Yuyu Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces DataSpace, a benchmark to evaluate data agents that answer natural-language questions by producing verifiable tables from heterogeneous, multimodal organizational workspaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks evaluate structured querying, retrieval, and open-ended analysis mostly in isolation and on homogeneous data, making them inadequate for testing realistic data agents that must discover heterogeneous evidence, integrate it, and output complete, verifiable tables. There is a need for a unified, deterministic benchmark that reflects real-world multimodal, multi-format analysis settings and enables reliable comparison of data-agent systems.

Method: They construct DataSpace, a benchmark of 410 tasks over 7,439 heterogeneous artifacts (CSV, JSON, SQLite, Markdown, PDF, video), where each task requires an agent to return a complete tabular answer from a given workspace. The benchmark is built via DataSpace-Builder, an execution-grounded pipeline involving cross-language transformation, constraint-aware relational sampling, modality routing and artifact rendering, and human review/repair by 11 experts. A deterministic evaluator performs header-invariant column alignment, type- and precision-aware normalization, and order-aware row comparison to score outputs. They evaluate six frontier multimodal models combined with five agent harnesses on the benchmark.

Result: DataSpace covers 15.01 GB of multimodal, heterogeneous artifacts and 410 cross-language tasks. In experiments, the best-performing combination of backbone model and agent harness attains 66.34% accuracy, with a 15.36 percentage point spread due solely to harness choice with the same backbone. Tasks requiring multimodal evidence integration and joins reduce performance across all six backbone models, indicating these capabilities are particularly challenging.

Conclusion: DataSpace is a large, execution-grounded, and deterministically scored benchmark that remains far from saturated, with top systems achieving only about two-thirds accuracy. The strong dependence on agent harness design and the systematic performance drop on multimodal and join-intensive tasks highlight key weaknesses in current data agents and provide concrete directions for improving their reliability in realistic, heterogeneous analysis scenarios.

Abstract: Data agents enable natural-language analytics over organizational workspaces, where relevant evidence may be scattered across databases, structured files, long documents, and multimedia. Existing benchmarks largely isolate structured querying, retrieval, or open-ended analysis, leaving heterogeneous evidence discovery, complete tabular outputs, and deterministic evaluation insufficiently unified. We introduce DataSpace, a benchmark in which data agents produce verifiable tabular results from task-local heterogeneous workspaces. It contains 410 cross-language tasks and 7,439 artifacts totaling 15.01 GB across CSV, JSON, SQLite, Markdown, PDF, and video. DataSpace also served as the official evaluation benchmark for the KDD Cup 2026 Data Agents for Complex Data Analysis competition. Each agent receives only a question and workspace and returns the complete requested tabular result. We construct DataSpace with DataSpace-Builder, an execution-grounded framework comprising cross-language transformation, constraint-aware relational sampling, modality routing and artifact rendering, and human review and task repair by 11 domain experts. A deterministic evaluator performs header-invariant column alignment, type- and precision-aware normalization, and order-aware row comparison. Across six recently released frontier multimodal models and five widely used agent harnesses, the best accuracy reaches 66.34%, while harness choice creates a 15.36-point spread with the backbone fixed. Multimodal evidence integration and joins consistently reduce accuracy across all six backbones. These results show that DataSpace remains unsaturated and identify key challenges for improving data-agent reliability.

</details>


### [143] [LLaDA MoE v2: Scaling Mixture-of-Experts Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03457)
*Fengqi Zhu,Shaoxuan Xu,Jingyang Ou,Zebin You,Yipeng Xing,Huabin Liu,Xiaolu Zhang,Jun Zhou,Zhenzhong Lan,Yankai Lin,Wayne Xin Zhao,Jianguo Li,Chongxuan Li,Ji-Rong Wen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how Mixture-of-Experts diffusion language models (MoE dLLMs) scale, and derives practical design and training rules, then validates them with a 30B-parameter MoE diffusion model (LLaDA MoE v2) that is competitive with strong AR baselines like Qwen3 and SDAR Chat.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While autoregressive language models and MoE architectures have well-studied scaling laws, diffusion-based language models—especially MoE variants—lack clear guidance on how to choose optimization hyperparameters, data/model compute allocation, and architectural design as scale increases. This gap makes it hard to train competitive MoE dLLMs efficiently. The paper aims to fill this gap by empirically characterizing the scaling behavior of MoE dLLMs and turning it into actionable rules.

Method: The authors conduct systematic scaling experiments with MoE diffusion language models, varying total compute, batch size, learning rate, data/model FLOP allocation, and MoE architectural knobs such as number of experts, expert granularity, and fraction of shared experts. They use IsoFLOP analysis to study trade‑offs between more data vs. more model computation at fixed compute. From the observed trends, they derive practical scaling laws for optimization and architecture. Finally, they apply these rules to design and train LLaDA MoE v2 (30B parameters, A3B configuration) on 23.5T tokens and evaluate it on knowledge, reasoning, and coding benchmarks against strong baselines like Qwen3 and SDAR Chat.

Result: They find that in MoE dLLMs: (1) the optimal effective batch size increases faster with compute than in reported AR scaling; (2) the optimal learning rate decreases more quickly with compute; (3) IsoFLOP analysis shows a mild data preference, with optimal token count growing slightly faster than activated model-side FLOPs; (4) at larger scale, performance increasingly benefits from having more experts while keeping activated capacity fixed, moderate expert granularity remains consistently good, and the optimal proportion of shared experts is roughly scale-invariant. Using these insights, they train LLaDA MoE v2 (30B-A3B) on 23.5T tokens. Despite using around 65% of Qwen3’s pretraining tokens, LLaDA MoE v2 approaches Qwen3 on multiple knowledge, reasoning, and coding benchmarks, and after only supervised fine-tuning, it beats SDAR Chat on 7 out of 8 reasoning/coding benchmarks, staying close to Qwen3 on several tasks.

Conclusion: MoE diffusion language models obey scaling behaviors that differ in important quantitative ways from autoregressive models. In MoE dLLMs, optimal batch sizes and learning rates scale differently with compute, data‑model trade‑offs mildly favor additional data, and larger expert pools with moderate granularity and stable shared‑expert fractions are preferred at larger scales. Using these empirically derived rules enables the design of competitive large‑scale MoE dLLMs such as LLaDA MoE v2, which matches or approaches the performance of state‑of‑the‑art autoregressive models with significantly fewer training tokens. The work thus provides practical scaling laws and architectural guidelines for efficiently training high‑quality MoE diffusion language models.

Abstract: Diffusion language models (dLLMs) offer an alternative to autoregressive (AR) language modeling, yet the scaling behavior of Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) dLLMs remains poorly understood. We systematically characterize how optimization hyperparameters, compute allocation, and architecture scale for MoE dLLMs, identifying quantitative differences from scaling trends previously reported for AR models. Specifically, for optimization, the optimal nominal batch size grows faster, while the optimal learning rate decays more rapidly with compute. For model--data allocation, IsoFLOP analysis reveals a slight data-side tilt: the optimal token budget grows faster than activated model-side computation. For MoE architecture, larger scales increasingly favor larger expert pools at fixed activated capacity, while moderate expert granularity remains consistently effective and the preferred fraction of activated capacity assigned to shared experts remains stable across scales. Guided by these findings, we train LLaDA MoE v2, a 30B-A3B dLLM, from scratch on 23.5T tokens. With approximately 65\% as many pretraining tokens as Qwen3, LLaDA MoE v2 approaches Qwen3 on several knowledge, reasoning, and coding benchmarks. After supervised fine-tuning alone, it outperforms SDAR Chat on seven of eight reasoning and coding benchmarks and remains close to Qwen3 on several tasks. These results establish practical scaling laws and design principles for MoE dLLMs.

</details>


### [144] [Solver-Aware Decompositions for Programming-by-Example: When Dividing Requires Knowing how to Conquer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03461)
*Janis Zenkner,Tobias Sesterhenn,Tim Grams,Christian Bartelt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They show that in program synthesis-by-example, using ground-truth human decompositions is not always best for a bounded solver, and propose Solver-Aware Decomposition (SAD), which chooses subgoals that the existing solver can actually handle, improving end-to-end success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing decomposition-based PBE methods assume that human-annotated or ground-truth decompositions are intrinsically optimal. However, for practical solvers with limited capacity and specific inductive biases, those decompositions may be logically correct but hard to search over, hurting performance. There is a gap between the way tasks are factored by annotators and what is tractable for the solver. The paper aims to align decomposition with the actual solver dynamics rather than with human factorization alone, and to understand when higher agreement with GT decompositions really helps synthesis.

Method: They introduce Solver-Aware Decomposition (SAD), a training framework for the decomposer in a decomposition-based PBE system. First, as in prior work, the decomposer is supervised to imitate ground-truth (GT) subgoals to provide a structural prior. Additionally, SAD optimizes the decomposer using direct feedback from a frozen synthesizer. For a candidate decomposition, they measure the synthesizer’s loss on the target program when conditioned on the proposed subgoals; this loss acts as a reward or training signal indicating the practical difficulty of the subgoals for that particular solver. The decomposer is then updated to propose subgoals that lead to lower synthesizer loss, even if these diverge from GT decompositions. The synthesizer itself is not changed during this stage and was originally trained on GT subgoal data.

Result: Experiments across two PBE domains show that maximizing agreement with GT decompositions does not necessarily lead to better synthesis performance, revealing an “accuracy paradox.” Even though the synthesizer was trained on GT subgoals, forcing the decomposer to closely mimic GT decompositions fails to improve, and can limit, end-to-end success. In contrast, SAD learns decompositions that are less aligned with GT but better matched to the solver’s search behavior, leading to consistent gains in both subtask synthesis success and overall task accuracy. Remarkably, SAD can solve tasks that remain unsolved even when an oracle GT decomposition is provided, indicating that alternative, solver-friendly decompositions can outperform human-annotated ones for bounded solvers.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that decomposition quality is not an intrinsic property of a task but is relative to the capabilities and biases of the solver. Ground-truth decompositions derived from human or annotator factorization are not universally optimal for bounded synthesizers and can yield intractable subgoals for a given solver. By incorporating solver feedback into decomposer training, Solver-Aware Decomposition (SAD) discovers alternative factorizations that are better suited to the solver’s search dynamics, leading to improved program synthesis performance. This reframes how decompositions for PBE should be designed and evaluated: they should be solver-aware rather than solely judged by alignment with human or GT structures.

Abstract: Decomposition-based Programming-by-example (PBE) scales performance by splitting tasks into subtasks that a learned synthesizer solves: a decomposer predicts intermediate subgoals, and a synthesizer generates programs conditioned on them. Current approaches train the decomposer to imitate ground-truth ( GT) subgoals, implicitly treating decomposition quality as intrinsic to the task. We challenge this assumption: for bounded solvers with fixed inductive biases, GT decompositions reflect the annotator's factorization choices - not the solver's search dynamics. A decomposer trained to match GT decompositions may therefore propose subgoals that are logically valid yet intractable for the solver. We propose Solver-Aware Decomposition (SAD), a training framework that retains supervised training on GT subgoals as a structural scaffold, while additionally optimizing the decomposer via direct feedback from a frozen synthesizer. Subgoals are rewarded based on the synthesizer's loss on the target program - a signal of subtask difficulty that encourages decompositions the solver can act on. Our experiments reveal an accuracy paradox: higher agreement with GT decompositions does not improve synthesis success - even though the synthesizer was trained on the very same GT data the decomposer is optimized to mimic. SAD instead learns decompositions that trade GT alignment for solver tractability, yielding consistent gains in synthesis and end-to-end task accuracy across two PBE domains. Moreover, SAD solves tasks that a GT decomposition oracle fails - empirical evidence that GT decompositions are not universally optimal for bounded solvers, and that decomposition quality is solver-relative, not intrinsic.

</details>


### [145] [LeanMem: Simple and Efficient Long-Term Memory for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03463)
*Yuxin Liao,Le Wu,Min Hou,Hao Liu,Han Wu,Zishu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes LeanMem, a lightweight, structured long‑term memory framework for LLM agents that treats different kinds of dialogue history differently to improve accuracy and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-term memory systems for LLM agents treat all historical dialogue in a uniform way, usually via generic summarization and retrieval. This causes either high token and computation costs or loss of important fine-grained evidence. There is a need for a memory framework that respects differences in compressibility, temporal dynamics, and fidelity needs across various types of dialogue content so that agents can sustain long interactions reliably and efficiently.

Method: The authors design LeanMem, which first filters out low-value dialogue content and then assigns remaining information to one of three tailored memory types: (1) compact profile memory for stable user or task attributes, (2) temporally structured event memory for evolving interactions, and (3) source-grounded record memory for immutable detailed evidence. Maintenance focuses only on updating event memory while keeping profiles and records largely fixed to avoid redundant consolidation. At inference, LeanMem dynamically chooses which memory types to query and how much retrieval budget to allocate based on what the current query requires, assembling an on-demand evidence set.

Result: On the LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S benchmarks, using models like GPT-4.1-mini and Qwen3-8B, LeanMem outperforms the strongest prior memory-based baselines in all tested settings. It achieves up to a 15.1-point gain in accuracy while also being at the lowest or near-lowest cost in terms of memory construction, inference token usage, and latency.

Conclusion: Differentiating and structuring long-term memory for LLM agents according to information type, temporal behavior, and fidelity requirements significantly improves both accuracy and efficiency. LeanMem demonstrates that selective storage, targeted updating, and query-aware retrieval of specialized memory types provide more effective long-term memory than uniform summarization pipelines, offering a practical path for scalable, long-horizon LLM agents.

Abstract: Long-term memory is essential for LLM-based agents to sustain interactions and reliably leverage distant history. However, existing memory systems typically process heterogeneous dialogue content through a uniform summarization and retrieval pipeline, leading to either excessive token consumption or irreversible loss of fine-grained evidence. We argue that historical dialogue content should be handled differently according to its compressibility, temporal dynamics, and fidelity requirements. Based on this insight, we propose LeanMem, a lightweight long-term memory framework. LeanMem first filters out low-value content, then stores informative segments as compact profile memory, temporally structured event memory, or source-grounded record memory, depending on the nature of the information. During maintenance, only dynamically evolving event memories are selectively updated, avoiding redundant consolidation of stable profiles and immutable records. During inference, LeanMem dynamically selects memory types and allocates retrieval budgets according to query-specific evidence demands, assembling relevant evidence on demand. On LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S with GPT-4.1-mini and Qwen3-8B, LeanMem improves accuracy over the strongest memory-based baseline in every setting, by up to 15.1 points, at the lowest or near-lowest construction cost, inference tokens, and latency. The code and datasets are included in the supplementary materials.

</details>


### [146] [ChartAnno: Evaluating MLLMs for Chart Annotation Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03464)
*Zhenghan Chen,Zekai Shao,Lidan Tan,Xin Lin,Xingchen Zeng,Yi Shan,Ziyue Lin,Xiaoliang Fu,Xinyuan Liu,Yuetong Guo,Fen Wang,Bongshin Lee,Siming Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ChartAnno, a benchmark to evaluate multimodal large language models (MLLMs) on the underexplored task of chart annotation generation, showing that this task is challenging and that current models, especially open-source ones, still struggle with inferring abstract intent and effective annotation design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While MLLMs have advanced in chart understanding, generation, and editing, their capability to annotate existing charts—adding explanatory text or graphical elements that convey intended messages—has not been systematically studied. Chart annotation is a common real-world communication task that demands models to interpret chart semantics, infer the author’s intent, and design suitable annotations. There is a lack of dedicated benchmarks to evaluate and compare MLLMs on this ability, which hinders progress and systematic analysis.

Method: The authors build ChartAnno, a benchmark consisting of 1,200 real-world charts, each paired with underlying chart code and annotation instructions at three different levels of specificity (from abstract to highly specific). They then evaluate 10 representative multimodal large language models under two main input settings: (1) chart code only, and (2) chart code plus chart image, and additionally run an ablation with chart image only. They use a set of metrics capturing correctness and design quality of generated annotations to compare proprietary and open-source models and to analyze the impacts of instruction specificity and input modality.

Result: Experiments show that proprietary MLLMs still outperform open-source models on chart annotation generation overall, though large-scale open-source models reduce the performance gap. More specific annotation instructions consistently improve the quality of generated annotations, while tasks requiring inference of abstract communicative intent remain particularly challenging. Adding chart images to chart code yields only modest overall performance gains; the benefits mainly appear in design-focused metrics, suggesting that visual input helps with layout and style more than with semantic interpretation.

Conclusion: Chart annotation generation is a difficult, underexplored capability for MLLMs that requires deep semantic grounding and strong annotation design skills. ChartAnno provides the first systematic benchmark for this task and reveals that current models—especially in open-source form—struggle with inferring abstract messages and producing high-quality annotations, even when given chart images. The work motivates future research on better multimodal reasoning, intent inference, and design-aware generation for chart communication tasks; the authors plan to release code and data to support such research.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made significant progress in chart understanding, generation, and editing, but their ability to annotate existing charts remains underexplored. Annotating charts is a common yet challenging communicative task, requiring models to infer intended messages, interpret chart semantics, and place appropriate textual or graphical elements. To address this gap, we introduce ChartAnno, a benchmark for evaluating MLLMs on chart annotation generation. It contains 1,200 real-world charts with paired code and annotation instructions across three levels of instruction specificity. We evaluate 10 representative MLLMs under two primary input settings: (1) chart code alone and (2) both chart code and chart image, and further include a chart image-only ablation study. Results show that proprietary models remain stronger overall, although large-scale open-source models narrow the gap. More specific instructions improve annotation quality, while inferring abstract intent remains most difficult for current MLLMs. Providing chart images brings limited overall gains, with improvements mainly appearing in design-related metrics. These findings highlight chart annotation generation as a challenging task requiring semantic grounding and effective annotation design. Code and data will be released in a future version.

</details>


### [147] [When Correct Solutions Repeat: Rarity-Aware Credit Redistribution for GRPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03467)
*Zhe Cao,Miaowen Wen,Fangjiong Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Cue-GRPO, a reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) method that redistributes credit among correct solutions based on their structural rarity to prevent overemphasis on frequently occurring solution forms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In RLVR methods like GRPO, each correct completion is treated equally at the completion level, which causes a bias: common solution structures get disproportionately more learning credit simply because they appear more often, while rare but valid structures receive little credit. This multiplicity-induced structure-level credit concentration can harm exploration and diversity of solution strategies, particularly in math reasoning tasks where multiple correct solution styles exist.

Method: The authors formalize the phenomenon of multiplicity-induced credit concentration and propose a partition-conditioned credit redistribution rule that reallocates positive advantages among correct solutions based on the rarity of their structural cluster. They instantiate this with Cue-GRPO, which uses deterministic "Strategy Cues" to create rollout-local partitions (clusters) of verified-correct traces without needing extra model inference. They also explore Credit Redistribution (CR) under Judge Partitions (JP), where partitions are derived from an external judge model, to test the generality of the redistribution mechanism.

Result: On Qwen2.5-Math-7B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, Cue-GRPO improves performance on AIME under repeated sampling, with especially strong gains at higher sampling budgets. The added computational cost over standard GRPO is limited to about 6% extra wall-clock training time. Experiments with CR under Judge Partitions indicate that the proposed redistribution mechanism works not only with cue-based partitions but also with judge-derived structure groupings.

Conclusion: Structure-level credit redistribution is an effective and practical new design axis for RL with verifiable rewards. By reallocating learning signal toward rarer yet correct solution structures, Cue-GRPO enhances math competition performance with minimal overhead, and Strategy Cues offer a lightweight way to realize rarity-aware credit redistribution without heavy auxiliary models.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) com- monly optimizes each correct completion as an independent learning signal. In GRPO, this completion-level uniformity creates structure-level skew: recurring correct solution forms accumulate positive coefficient mass in proportion to how often they are sampled, while rare forms receive limited credit. We formalize this behavior as multiplicity-induced structure-level credit concentration and introduce a partition- conditioned rule that redistributes positive advantages accord- ing to cluster rarity. Cue-GRPO instantiates this rule with- out auxiliary-model inference by using deterministic Strategy Cues to construct rollout-local partitions of verified-correct traces. Across Qwen2.5-Math-7B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, Cue-GRPO improves AIME repeated-sampling performance, with the largest gains at high sampling budgets. Credit Re- distribution (CR) under Judge Partitions (JP) further indi- cates that the proposed redistribution mechanism can oper- ate with judge-derived partitions. Cue-GRPO adds only 6% wall-clock training overhead over GRPO. These results sup- port structure-level credit redistribution as a practical design axis for RLVR, with Strategy Cues providing a low-overhead implementation for competition mathematics. Code is avail- able at https://github.com/CzZ12/When-Correct-Solutions- Repeat-Rarity-Aware-Credit-Redistribution-for-GRPO.

</details>


### [148] [ToolLIFT: Lifting Tool-Specific Trajectories into Function-Level Graphs for Generalizable Tool Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03468)
*Xiuhui You,Jiayi Luo,Zichao Shen,Qingyun Sun,Ziwei Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ToolLIFT proposes a function-level workflow abstraction to make LLM tool-use planning more transferable across different tool sets, using lifted trajectories, decoupled workflow planning and tool selection, and RL-based rewards for reliable dataflow, achieving strong ID and OOD performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM tool-use methods build graphs directly over concrete tools from past trajectories, which lock the learned structure to a specific tool set and generalize poorly when tools change. The authors observe that, across different tools, many tasks share similar high-level workflow structures at the functional level, suggesting that learning and planning at this more abstract level could yield better transfer and generalization to unseen tools and tool configurations.

Method: The authors introduce ToolLIFT, which (1) lifts historical tool-use trajectories into a function-level workflow graph (FWG) that encodes abstract workflow structures and shared collaboration patterns across tools; (2) performs decoupled workflow planning and tool selection, where the agent first plans at the function/workflow level using the global FWG structure and then chooses concrete tools that implement each function, aligning local choices with the overall plan; and (3) uses reinforcement learning with specially designed source-gated and skill-specific rewards to enforce reliable, source-traceable information flow through tool calls, improving the robustness of multi-tool dataflow.

Result: Across two in-distribution and three out-of-distribution benchmarks, ToolLIFT is reported to consistently outperform state-of-the-art baselines in tool-using tasks, particularly showing improved generalization when evaluated with previously unseen tool sets.

Conclusion: Abstracting tool-use trajectories into a function-level workflow graph and separating workflow planning from concrete tool selection allows LLM agents to better reuse past experience, maintain reliable information flow, and generalize more effectively to new tools and environments than existing tool-level graph approaches.

Abstract: Historical tool-use trajectories provide valuable experience for large language model (LLM) agents to plan and coordinate tool usage. Existing approaches directly construct tool-level graphs from these trajectories, but the resulting graphs remain tied to specific tools and are hard to generalize across tool sets. To tackle this challenge, we find that despite differences in the tools involved, analogous tasks often share a common function-level workflow structure, which serves as a potentially more transferable abstraction for tool planning. Based on this insight, we propose ToolLIFT, a framework that lifts tool-specific trajectories into a function-level workflow graph (FWG) for generalizable tool planning. Specifically, we first propose a trajectory-lifting mechanism that encodes workflow structures in the FWG and shares collaboration experience across tools. Then, building on the global structure of the FWG, we introduce decoupled workflow planning and tool selection to align individual tool choices with the overall workflow. Lastly, to ensure reliable tool dataflow, we adopt Reinforcement Learning (RL) and propose source-gated and skill-specific rewards to maintain source-traceable information flow across tool calls. Experiments on two in-distribution (ID) and three out-of-distribution (OOD) benchmarks show that ToolLIFT consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, demonstrating strong generalization to unseen tool sets.

</details>


### [149] [WeClawArena: An Auditable Sandbox and Benchmark for Cross-User Agents Collaboration and Security in Human-Centered Agent Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03499)
*Prince Zizhuang Wang,Aojie Yuan,Haiyue Zhang,Xiyang Hu,Yue Zhao,Shuli Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WeClawArena is a benchmark and sandbox for studying multi-agent, cross-user collaboration of personal AI agents over private digital workspaces, with both benign and adversarial scenarios and detailed auditing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personal AI agents that persist for each user and collaborate across social and task networks are becoming realistic. In such settings, agents work over separate personal workspaces (files, tools, policies) that are not mutually visible. Existing benchmarks test tool use and collaboration but lack an end-to-end, auditable environment where multiple user-owned agents coordinate over realistic, access-restricted workspaces, and where one can systematically study how harmful or adversarial actions might propagate through this network. The authors are motivated to create a benchmark that both reflects this realistic multi-party setting and enables rigorous evaluation of safety, privacy, and robustness.

Method: The authors design WeClawArena as a benchmark plus runtime sandbox. They formalize collaborative tool-use tasks in which personal workspaces act both as tools and as constraints due to access separation. They construct 124 base cross-user tasks spanning six domains; each base task is instantiated into five variants: one benign control scenario and four attack-vector scenarios, yielding 620 total instances. The sandbox infrastructure logs all relevant runtime data—peer messages between agents, tool calls, resource and file operations, governed policy decisions, and final states of workspaces—providing audit trails. They define metrics that separately measure task utility (benign success) and attack success, and introduce auditing procedures that infer whether an attack succeeded based on bounded runtime evidence.

Result: WeClawArena provides a functioning, auditable environment and dataset of 620 scenarios across six cross-user domains that systematically test multi-party agent collaboration over personal workspaces, including benign and adversarial conditions. The sandbox successfully records fine-grained traces of communication, tool use, and workspace changes to support verifiable assessment. It can detect and report outcomes such as task completion, privacy leaks, poisoned or manipulated evidence, and misuse of authority paths, enabling more precise diagnosis of where and how collaborations or defenses fail.

Conclusion: WeClawArena fills a gap in current agent benchmarks by offering an end-to-end, auditable sandbox that models realistic human-centered networks of personal agents with separated workspaces. By separating utility from attack success and providing detailed logs, it allows researchers to study both collaborative performance and security/privacy risks of cross-user agent interactions. This framework is positioned as a foundation for evaluating and improving the safety, robustness, and reliability of persistent personal-agent ecosystems.

Abstract: Recent advances in persistent personal-agent frameworks are making human-centered agent networks realistic deployment targets: each user can be served by an AI agent that acts on the user's behalf, maintains state, and communicates with other agents through social and task relations. In these networks, everyday tool use becomes multi-party owned-agent collaboration over personal workspaces, where files, records, tools, and policies are not directly visible across owners. Existing agent benchmarks study tool use and collaboration, but they do not provide an end-to-end sandbox for verifiable cross-user agent collaboration with realistic user digital workspaces or test how harmful actions can travel through the human-centered agent network. We introduce WeClawArena, an auditable benchmark and runtime sandbox for multi-party owned-agent collaboration over personal workspaces. WeClawArena targets collaborative tool-use tasks in which personal workspaces serve as both operational tools and personal constraints. The benchmark contains 124 base tasks across six cross-user task domains and expands them into 620 scenario variants, with one benign control and four attack-vector variants per base task. The sandbox records peer messages, tool calls, resource operations, governed decisions, and final workspace states. WeClawArena reports utility and attack success rate separately and audits attack success from bounded runtime evidence, supporting diagnosis of task breakdown, privacy leakage, poisoned evidence, and invalid authority paths.

</details>


### [150] [Can LLM design high-quality experiments? A Comprehensive and Systematic Benchmark on Autonomous Experimental Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03501)
*Zejun Liu,Jian Wu,Ru Peng,Yuliang Ji,Dongyuan Li,Renhe Jiang,Yue Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces SCOPE, a benchmark to evaluate how well AI (specifically LLMs) can design scientific experiments, and OptED, an agentic workflow that improves LLM-based experimental design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI-for-research tools largely focus on coding and running experiments, neglecting the crucial step of experimental design, and there is no systematic benchmark to measure AI’s ability to plan experiments. The authors aim to fill this gap and understand LLM limitations in this area.

Method: They build SCOPE from 300 recent, high-quality papers across 19 research domains from top venues (ICML, NeurIPS, ICLR). SCOPE evaluates LLMs on (1) high-level planning completeness (covering main, ablation, and analysis experiments) and (2) low-level configuration accuracy and rationality (datasets, baselines, metrics). They benchmark various LLMs under different modes, including search-based modes, then propose OptED, an agentic workflow that structures experimental design into stages, augments LLMs with tools, and applies rule-based constraints.

Result: Benchmarking shows: (1) most LLMs fail to directly produce high-quality experimental designs; (2) all tested LLMs hit a bottleneck on low-level configuration details such as datasets, baselines, and metrics; and (3) enabling search mode does not significantly improve design quality. OptED is shown to alleviate the low-level configuration bottleneck and improve experimental design quality.

Conclusion: LLMs currently struggle with end-to-end experimental design, particularly with detailed configurations. A dedicated benchmark like SCOPE exposes these weaknesses, and structured, tool-augmented workflows such as OptED can substantially improve AI-assisted experimental planning by isolating stages and constraining the design process.

Abstract: AI for Research (AI4Research) leverages AI to automate and improve scientific workflows. While experimental design is a critical stage of the research process, prior work has focused primarily on code implementation and execution, overlooking the importance of this stage, and no benchmark exists to evaluate AI's ability to conduct systematic experiment design. To bridge this gap, we propose SCOPE, a Scientific COmprehensive Planning Evaluation Benchmark constructed from 300 high-quality latest papers across 19 research domains from top-tier venues (e.g., ICML, NeurIPS, and ICLR),evaluating LLMs on two dimensions: High-Level planning completeness (main, ablation, and analysis experiments) and Low-Level configuration accuracy and rationality (datasets, baselines, and metrics). Benchmarking reveals three findings: (1) most LLMs cannot directly design high-quality experiments; (2) all LLMs exhibit a performance bottleneck in low-level configuration; and (3) search mode does not improve design quality. Furthermore, to address these challenges, we propose OptED, a novel agentic workflow to optimize LLM-based experimental design, that enhances LLM-based experimental planning through stage isolation, tool augmentation, and rule-based constraints, effectively alleviating the configuration bottleneck.

</details>


### [151] [Hybrid LLM-Augmented Reinforcement Learning Agents for Complex Sequential Decision Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03502)
*Christophe D. Hounwanou,John Emeka Eze,Yaé Ulrich Gaba*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a hybrid framework that combines large language model (LLM) planning with reinforcement learning (RL) for long-horizon sequential decision-making, achieving better performance than using RL or LLMs alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are good at high-level reasoning, planning, and decomposing tasks but perform poorly at precise, long-horizon action control. Traditional RL excels at low-level sequential decision-making but lacks abstract reasoning and task decomposition abilities, making it hard to solve complex, long-horizon tasks efficiently. The paper aims to bridge this gap by combining the strengths of both paradigms.

Method: The authors design an LLM-Augmented Reinforcement Learning Agent. The LLM component is responsible for generating subgoals, structured high-level plans, and contextual guidance based on the current task and environment state. The RL component then operates at a lower level, using interaction with the environment to optimize concrete actions that achieve the LLM-specified subgoals. The system iteratively refines behavior by coupling LLM-planned structure with RL-based control.

Result: On a set of sequential decision-making benchmarks, the proposed hybrid agent achieves higher success rates, better sample efficiency, and more coherent action sequences than both RL-only and LLM-only baselines. The experiments indicate that LLM-provided structure helps RL learn more efficiently and produce more organized trajectories.

Conclusion: Integrating LLM-based high-level planning with RL-based low-level control is an effective strategy for long-horizon decision tasks. The hybrid approach leverages complementary strengths of LLMs and RL, pointing toward a promising direction for developing more capable and efficient autonomous agents and suggesting that future systems should tightly couple symbolic/planning abilities with data-driven control.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently shown strong capabilities in reasoning, planning, and tool-use, enabling new forms of autonomous agents. However, LLM-based agents struggle with long-horizon sequential decision tasks that require precise action optimization and environment interaction. Reinforcement Learning (RL), while effective for sequential control, often lacks the high-level abstraction and task decomposition abilities needed for complex scenarios. This paper introduces an LLM-Augmented Reinforcement Learning Agent that integrates LLM-driven planning with RL-based action optimization. The proposed architecture leverages the LLM to generate subgoals, structured plans, and contextual guidance, while the RL agent refines low-level actions through interaction with the environment. Experiments on sequential decision tasks demonstrate improved sample efficiency, higher success rates, and more coherent action trajectories compared to RL-only and LLM-only baselines. This hybrid paradigm highlights a promising direction for building more capable autonomous systems.

</details>


### [152] [When Many Answers Are Valid, Voting Fails: Symbolic Verification for Best-of-K Causal Reasoning in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03506)
*Omatharv Bharat Vaidya,Connor Thomas Jerzak,Zayne Rea Sprague,Fangcong Yin,Nhat Ho*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose CALVER, a symbolic verifier that evaluates causal reasoning traces using Pearl’s criteria to pick causally valid answers, outperforming self-consistency and LLM judges on multiple causal reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-consistency and similar selection methods often fail on causal reasoning tasks because many sampled reasoning traces share the same confounding error, and valid answers can be split across several distinct solutions, allowing an invalid but frequent answer to win. There is a need for a selection method that can reliably identify causally valid reasoning traces without relying on majority votes or reference answers.

Method: They design CALVER (Causal Axiom-Level VERification), a training-free symbolic verifier. CALVER takes structured reasoning traces and scores them according to Pearl’s causal criteria, such as d-separation, backdoor adjustment, and interventions. It then chooses the trace with the highest causal validity score. CALVER operates without consulting ground-truth answers and only needs a causal structure, which can be given directly or inferred from text. They evaluate CALVER on multiple benchmarks, including CLEAR find-one-valid queries with multiple valid answers, Bayesian networks, tasks where the model must construct the causal graph from text, and logic tasks checked via truth tables.

Result: On CLEAR find-one-valid queries with multiple graph-valid answers, CALVER achieves 42.1% accuracy, while baseline methods like plurality voting, a reward model, an LLM judge, and model confidence stay around 30%, even when scaling the LLM judge to 72B parameters. In a curated subset, 11 of 21 CALVER-selected answers are graph-valid yet differ from the benchmark’s labeled solution, still satisfying the query. CALVER’s performance improves with more samples, generalizes across ten published Bayesian networks and to a second model family, and works even when the causal graph must be extracted from text. It also improves decisions about average treatment effects when compared to exact ground truth, and can score candidates in milliseconds on CPU.

Conclusion: CALVER provides an effective, training-free method for validating and selecting causal reasoning traces based on formal causal axioms. It outperforms standard selection mechanisms, remains efficient, and generalizes across different causal reasoning setups, including tasks requiring graph construction from text and logical reasoning checked via truth tables. Wherever a causal structure is available or can be derived, CALVER enables aggregation based on causal validity instead of majority frequency or heuristic confidence.

Abstract: Self-consistency assumes the most frequent answer among sampled reasoning traces is the most reliable, but this can fail in causal reasoning: samples often repeat the same confounding error, and votes fragment across multiple valid answers, letting an invalid answer win despite a valid minority trace. We introduce CALVER (Causal Axiom-Level VERification), a training-free symbolic verifier that scores structured traces against Pearl's causal criteria, including -separation, backdoor adjustment, and intervention, and selects the highest-scoring candidate without consulting a reference answer. On CLEAR find-one-valid queries that admit multiple graph-valid answers, CALVER reaches 42.1% where plurality, a reward model, an LLM judge, and model confidence remain near 30% on identical frozen pools. Scaling the judge to 72B does not close the gap. In an audited clean-core subset, 11 of 21 graph-valid CALVER selections differ from the benchmark's listed answer while still satisfying the requested predicate. The advantage widens with the sampling budget and reproduces across ten published Bayesian networks, a second model family, and settings where the model must build the graph from text. CALVER also improves thresholded average-treatment-effect decisions against exact ground truth, generalizes to logic under a truth-table checker, and scores each candidate in milliseconds on CPU. CALVER needs only a causal structure, supplied outright or built from the text; wherever that holds, selection can aggregate via causal validity.

</details>


### [153] [Reversing Arrows in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03512)
*Sefika Efeoglu,Adrian Paschke*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how well large language models understand directionality in inverse relations (like mother/child) using a dedicated benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although LLMs perform well on text-to-knowledge graph tasks, it is unclear whether they correctly model direction-dependent semantics in inverse relations where argument order changes meaning. This is important for accurate reasoning and knowledge graph construction.

Method: The authors build a benchmark of 5,457 instances covering 27 inverse relation types. They test five open-source LLMs with a multiple-choice prompting setup, and run controlled variants where original entities are replaced by synthetic or masked entities to probe the role of relation descriptions and entity representations.

Result: Across models there are systematic asymmetries in how inverse relations are classified. Adding relation descriptions does not reliably improve accuracy, and models’ predictions change noticeably when entity representations are altered, indicating sensitivity to surface forms rather than robust relational semantics.

Conclusion: Current LLMs do not consistently encode robust directionality for inverse relations. Their behavior is asymmetric, not reliably aided by explicit relation descriptions, and strongly affected by how entities are represented, suggesting limitations in their relational understanding for knowledge graph tasks.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved strong performance on text-to-knowledge graph generation and related tasks. Nevertheless, it is still unclear whether they accurately model the direction-dependent semantics of inverse relations, in which reversing the order of the arguments alters the meaning of a relation (e.g., \textit{mother} versus \textit{child}). To the best of our knowledge, this work presents the first systematic study of inverse relation directionality in LLMs, using a benchmark consisting of 5,457 instances spanning 27 distinct inverse relation labels. We evaluate five open-source LLMs under a multiple-choice prompting framework and further examine the influence of relation descriptions and entity representations by substituting the original entities with synthetic and masked entities. Our findings reveal systematic asymmetries in inverse relation classification across LLMs, indicate that relation descriptions do not consistently improve performance, and show that model performance can be sensitive to variations in entity representations.

</details>


### [154] [Dr. AGENTONOMICS: A Didactic Experiment of AGENTONOMICS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03524)
*Fengjunjie Pan,Alois Knoll*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces AGENTONOMICS, a framework treating AI agents as economic entities, and Dr. AGENTONOMICS, a lecture/tutor agent that operationalizes this framework in a university course, outlining its architecture, evolving roles, and implications for a polycentric AI economy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore how AI agents can be systematically designed, managed, and governed as economic actors, and to demonstrate, in an educational setting, how a single agent can both teach and embody a comprehensive framework for AI agents in business administration.

Method: Design and implementation of a web-based, retrieval-grounded lecture/tutor agent (Dr. AGENTONOMICS) embedded in a university course, defining an integrated architecture (interface, intelligence layer, tools, knowledge base, ecosystem connection, and an orchestrator) and mapping out how this architecture supports multiple cumulative roles: tutor, avatar lecturer, design consultant, and meta-agent for agent construction.

Result: A working prototype of Dr. AGENTONOMICS as a web-based retrieval-grounded tutor for the AGENTONOMICS framework, plus a conceptual demonstration that the same underlying architecture can be extended to additional roles (avatar lecturer, design consultant, and meta-agent) through orchestration of role-specific algorithms over shared components.

Conclusion: AGENTONOMICS, instantiated through Dr. AGENTONOMICS, shows how AI agents can be treated as economic entities within a unified management and governance architecture, and how a single agent can be used to teach, apply, and eventually reproduce the framework that defines it, with broader implications for the emergence and governance of a polycentric AI agent economy.

Abstract: AGENTONOMICS is a framework that treats AI agents as economic entities that can be designed, managed, and governed through an integrated management architecture. Dr. AGENTONOMICS is its first application: a lecture agent developed in the context of the TUM course on AI agents in business administration. Conceived during the winter semester 2025/26 and first introduced to students in the summer semester 2026, it serves as a didactic experiment in which the agent is both the object that students study and the medium through which they learn and apply the framework. The current prototype is a web-based, retrieval-grounded tutor that explains AGENTONOMICS concepts and supports student questions. This report argues that the same system can grow beyond tutoring into three additional cumulative roles: an avatar lecturer that delivers multimodal instruction, a design consultant that guides students through the AGENTONOMICS Design & Management Reference Framework (ADMRF), and a meta-agent that helps construct the agents students have specified. These roles are cumulative because they share the same interface, intelligence layer, tools, knowledge base, and ecosystem connection, while an orchestrator selects the role-specific algorithm required for each task. We present the architecture of the prototype, outline its development roadmap, and discuss its implications for a polycentric AI economy. This report is intended to invite further discussion on how agents can teach, apply, and eventually reproduce the frameworks by which they are designed.

</details>


### [155] [Behaviorally Adaptive Visual Diversion for Inclusive and Resilient Digital Assessment Delivery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03531)
*Gupta Lovi Raj,kaur Kamalpreet,Dama Sriram,Parali Prajithaa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Behaviorally-Adaptive Visual Diversion (BAVD), a theoretical framework that overlays adaptive, synthetic visual patterns on exam content to hinder illicit capture while preserving accessibility and assessment integrity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Digital high-stakes exams rely on mechanisms like browser lockdown and webcam monitoring, which are typically designed in isolation and can neglect accessibility needs. There is a need for a unified, mathematically grounded approach that both hardens exams against unauthorized screen capture/sharing and incorporates accommodations for learners with visual-processing differences.

Method: The paper introduces BAVD, in which a synthetic, non-semantic visual layer is composited with the unmodified assessment content. This diversion field is adaptively modulated in response to observed candidate behavior using a coupled dynamical-systems model. The framework comprises a Diversion Field Generator, Rendering Tensor, Behavior Tensor, Composite Integrity Functional, and Multi-dimensional Entropy Model. The authors formalize theoretical properties such as content fidelity, rendering stability, entropy boundedness, integrity tracking, and closed-loop adaptation stability, along with an accessibility-aware attenuation mechanism that reduces or suppresses the diversion for candidates with approved accommodations.

Result: The work yields a formal, dynamical-systems-based specification of BAVD and proves theoretical guarantees on system behavior: the original content remains intact, the composite display is stable, the randomization/entropy of the diversion field is bounded and trackable, and the closed-loop adaptation converges without destabilizing the user experience. It also embeds an explicit threat model, deployment assumptions, and limitations into the framework.

Conclusion: BAVD offers a rigorous theoretical foundation for behaviorally adaptive, accessibility-aware visual protection in digital exams. By mathematically characterizing how synthetic diversion fields interact with user behavior and accommodations, the framework sets the stage for implementation and empirical evaluation in real assessment platforms, and frames the trade-offs between accessibility and resistance to screen capture or sharing.

Abstract: Institutions increasingly rely on browser lockdown, webcam monitoring, and behavioral analytics to secure high-stakes digital assessments, yet these mechanisms are commonly designed and evaluated independently and often overlook learner accessibility. This paper introduces Behaviorally-Adaptive Visual Diversion (BAVD), a theoretical framework in which a synthetic, non-semantic visual field is composited with assessment content and adaptively modulated according to observed candidate behavior. The underlying assessment content is never altered; only its visual presentation is modified to reduce the usefulness of unauthorized screen capture or screen sharing while remaining minimally intrusive for legitimate candidates. The framework further incorporates an accessibility-aware attenuation mechanism that reduces or suppresses diversion intensity for candidates with approved visual-processing accommodations.
