We formulate the model using a coupled dynamical-systems representation comprising a Diversion Field Generator, Rendering Tensor, Behavior Tensor, Composite Integrity Functional, and Multi-dimensional Entropy Model, and establish theoretical properties for content fidelity, rendering stability, entropy boundedness, integrity tracking, and closed-loop adaptation stability. The framework explicitly states its threat model, identifies deployment assumptions and limitations, and discusses the trade-off between accessibility and capture resistance. This work provides a mathematically grounded foundation for behaviorally adaptive and accessibility-aware assessment delivery and offers a basis for future empirical validation in trusted digital assessment platforms.

</details>


### [156] [Soft Guidance Starts to Outperform CoT Prompting as LLMs Improve](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03550)
*Denys Pushkin,Albert Q. Jiang,Aryo Lotfi,Colin Sandon,Emmanuel Abbé*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper questions the continued usefulness of standard few-shot Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting for modern reasoning-focused LLMs, showing that simpler zero-shot prompts can perform better.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reassess whether traditional few-shot CoT prompting is still the best way to elicit reasoning from contemporary LLMs, given that many models now produce CoT-like reasoning without explicit examples.

Method: Evaluate several modern mid-sized LLMs, including both reasoning-specialized and general-purpose models, on the GSM8K math word problem benchmark, comparing performance across zero-shot, zero-shot CoT, and few-shot CoT prompting setups.

Result: Reasoning-specialized models perform better with simple zero-shot prompting than with few-shot CoT examples, substantially improving over previously reported results (e.g., Mathstral from ~77% to ~84% on GSM8K). For a general-purpose model, a zero-shot CoT prompt also outperforms the standard few-shot CoT baseline.

Conclusion: As LLMs become stronger and more natively inclined to produce step-by-step reasoning, standard few-shot CoT prompting can hurt rather than help, due to a guidance–distraction tradeoff arising from extra style and formatting constraints; simpler prompts are increasingly preferable.

Abstract: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting remains the standard baseline for evaluating models' reasoning abilities. Originally, this technique was introduced to elicit step-by-step reasoning from large language models (LLMs), which would otherwise tend to directly output the final answer. However, many modern LLMs produce CoT-style responses \textit{natively} when presented with reasoning tasks, which made us revisit the effectiveness of standard CoT prompting.
  We evaluate several modern mid-sized language models on a math problem-solving task and find that models specialized for reasoning achieve better performance in a simple zero-shot setting than when using few-shot CoT examples - significantly surpassing officially reported results at no additional cost (e.g., from $\sim$77\% to $\sim$84\% for Mathstral on GSM8K). For the tested general-purpose model, a zero-shot CoT prompt is also sufficient to outperform a few-shot CoT baseline. We attribute this to a `guidance-distraction' tradeoff: standard CoT prompting also demands style adaptation, formatting compliance, and potentially undesired contextualization, which can distract models from the core reasoning task.
  Our findings suggest that using standard CoT prompting increasingly acts as a source of distraction as models grow stronger.

</details>


### [157] [Enhancing Tabular Learners with Context-Aware Semantic Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03565)
*Günther Schindler,Maximilian Schambach,Johannes Höhne*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CASE introduces context-aware semantic embeddings that inject LLM-derived semantics into tabular learners, improving performance on semantically rich tabular datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tabular models treat textual features and column names as discrete tokens without leveraging their semantics, which is limiting on text-heavy or semantically rich tables, especially when data is scarce. There is a need to combine LLM semantic understanding with strong statistical tabular learners in a principled way.

Method: The authors propose CASE, which uses a custom Gemma 3-based Tabular Language Model. They pre-fill the model’s KV cache with a representative sample of rows from the dataset, creating a persistent semantic anchor. New rows are then embedded while the model is conditioned on this cached context, so embeddings are dynamically contextualized by the broader dataset semantics before being fed into downstream tabular learners.

Result: On multiple benchmarks (CARTE, TextTab, TabArena), CASE significantly boosts the performance of standard tabular learners, with the largest gains observed on datasets where textual semantics are important and in low-data settings.

Conclusion: Contextualizing row embeddings with a dataset-level semantic anchor via a Tabular LLM effectively transfers LLM semantic knowledge into tabular learning, addressing semantic ambiguity and improving performance, particularly for semantically rich and low-data tabular tasks.

Abstract: While modern tabular learners excel at capturing statistical patterns, they frequently operate in a semantic vacuum, treating textual features as discrete symbols, ignoring the rich semantics inherent in feature names or cell entries. We propose CASE (Context-Aware Semantic Embeddings), a novel framework that bridges the gap between the semantic understanding of Large Language Models (LLMs) and the statistical capabilities of tabular learners. Unlike existing methods that embed rows in isolation, CASE utilizes a contextualization strategy: we pre-fill the KV cache of a custom-trained Gemma 3-based Tabular Language Model with a representative sample of rows to establish a persistent anchor of the dataset's semantics. This ensures that generated row embeddings are dynamically contextualized, resolving semantic ambiguities and anchoring representations in domain-specific context. Our experiments across several benchmarks (CARTE, TextTab, and TabArena) demonstrate that CASE substantially improves the performance of tabular learners on semantically rich datasets, particularly in low-data regimes.

</details>


### [158] [Adversarial Fast-Moving Real-World Domains as Test Beds for Benchmarking AI Scientist Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03569)
*William Bolton,Philip Torr*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes and tests a new way to benchmark whether AI systems can generate genuinely novel, expert-level ideas by comparing them against real innovations in fast-moving domains like Formula 1 and Magic: The Gathering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks for “AI scientists” mainly use synthetic or retrospective tasks that are limited because models may have seen the targets during training and the tasks do not fully capture abilities like generating truly new, high-quality hypotheses. The authors want a more realistic benchmark that reflects how experts innovate in the real world, and that can better measure whether AI can propose novel, useful ideas rather than only reason about existing ones.

Method: They introduce an evaluation framework based on adversarial, fast-moving real-world domains where expert outputs are independently observable. They instantiate this in two structurally different domains: (1) Formula 1 car design for the upcoming 2026 season, where model-generated design ideas are compared against real pre-season innovations that later appear; and (2) Magic: The Gathering deckbuilding, where models propose decks given a recently updated card pool, and these are compared against 19 Pro Tour decklists. Multiple frontier models are run to generate many candidate ideas/decks, and then the overlap and alignment with real expert innovations is quantitatively assessed (e.g., how many real innovations are matched; correlation between card usage frequencies).

Result: In both domains, models generate outputs that look plausible, but they seldom match the actual innovations produced by human experts. In F1, the strongest model (GPT-5.2) matched 10 of 40 real innovations despite proposing 166 ideas across multiple runs. In MTG, the best deck generated by Gemini 3 Flash reproduced 5 of 7 new-set cards from the third-place Pro Tour deck, and overall, across 108 model-generated decks, the cards chosen most frequently by models were strongly correlated with those most widely played in Pro Tour decks (Spearman ρ = 0.74, p = 0.0003).

Conclusion: The study concludes that modern AI models can readily produce many superficially reasonable ideas, but they struggle with filtering, prioritizing, and assembling these into genuinely coherent and novel expert-level solutions. Thus the main capability gap for “AI scientists” is not raw idea generation, but the ability to discern which ideas are truly valuable and to structure them into high-impact innovations, and their proposed benchmarking framework helps expose this gap.

Abstract: Benchmarking the ability of AI scientists to generate novel ideas is notoriously difficult. Existing benchmarks in this field have made progress in evaluating scientific reasoning and research replication, but often rely on synthetic tasks or retrospective targets, which may be confounded by prior exposure. We hypothesize that complex, adversarial, fast-moving real-world domains where expert practitioners independently generate observable outputs can provide a practical solution to fill this gap and evaluate the capabilities needed for AI scientists, including reasoning, novelty, and hypothesis formulation. We instantiate this framework in two structurally different domains, Formula 1 (F1), where models ideate around car design concepts for the 2026 season, and real pre-season innovations provide a ground truth, and Magic: The Gathering (MTG), where models propose decks from a recently updated card pool and are evaluated against 19 Pro Tour (PT) decklists. Across both domains, models produce plausible outputs, but few align with real-world expert solutions. In F1, the best model, GPT-5.2 matched 10 of 40 real innovations with 166 ideas proposed across runs. In MTG, the best deck from Gemini 3 Flash recovered 5 of 7 new-set cards from the third-place PT deck, and across all 108 decks, the cards models selected most often were also the cards most widely adopted by PT decks (Spearman $ρ= 0.74$, $p = 0.0003$). These results suggest that a key capability gap for AI scientists is not idea generation, but filtering, prioritization, and coherent novelty.

</details>


### [159] [Policy Fragmentation or Institutional Alignment? Institutional Governance of AI in Universities and Business Schools](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03584)
*Lydia Manikonda,Dominique Outlaw*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how higher education institutions in the U.S. are creating and structuring AI policies, revealing a divergence between university-level and school-level focuses and a policy gap in business schools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI rapidly changes high-skilled work, higher education institutions must prepare students by integrating AI into curricula and governance. However, institutions struggle with how to structure AI policies, especially when policies exist at multiple organizational levels and may not align with discipline-specific needs or workforce expectations. The study aims to clarify what current AI policies contain and how they differ across levels to inform better policy design.

Method: The authors collected AI-related policies from higher education institutions across 34 U.S. states. They applied natural language processing (NLP) techniques to systematically analyze the content of these policies, comparing themes and emphases across university-level and school-level (particularly business school) documents.

Result: The analysis shows a clear split: university-level AI policies focus strongly on data security and risk mitigation, while school-level policies—when they exist—emphasize pedagogy and tool use in teaching and learning. Business schools in particular rarely have AI policies separate from the overarching university policies, leading to limited explicit consideration of business-specific learning goals and use cases.

Conclusion: There is a misalignment between institution-wide AI governance and discipline-specific educational needs, particularly in business schools. To better support faculty, students, and accreditation requirements, AI guidelines should be coherently aligned with university-wide frameworks while also being tailored to discipline-specific learning objectives and evolving labor market demands.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) is rapidly transforming high-skilled domains, requiring higher education institutions (HEI) to balance the teaching of foundational principles with the integration of emerging tools to ensure workforce readiness. While HEI are increasingly adopting AI, many continue to grapple with how it should be incorporated into curricula and governed through policy, especially when such policies are set at different levels of an institution. This research analyzes AI policies across HEI from 34 states in the United States to investigate what these policies entail and how policies set across institutions as well as within different levels at an institution differ. Using natural language processing (NLP) to analyze institutional AI policies, we find a clear divergence: university-level policies emphasize data security and risk mitigation whereas school-level policies, when present, focus on pedagogical applications and tool usage. When focusing on business school specific policies, relatively few business schools maintain AI policies distinct from university frameworks, creating misalignment with discipline-specific learning objectives. This gap poses challenges particularly for faculty and students as well as for accreditation purposes. Our insights suggest that guidelines should be aligned with broader institutional policies while addressing discipline-specific learning objectives and evolving workforce demands.

</details>


### [160] [From Social Coding to Agentic Coding: Productivity and Relational Reconfiguration in Open-Source Communities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03585)
*Mengying Zhou,Yongjie Yin,Yang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper simulates the introduction of generative coding agents into a large GitHub community and finds they significantly boost productivity but reduce public, reusable knowledge and human-human interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As generative coding agents become integrated into software development, much of the work may move from open, social collaboration to private human–agent loops. This could fundamentally change how open-source communities function as public digital infrastructure. The authors want to understand not just productivity impacts but also the consequences for public knowledge creation, collaboration patterns, and equity within developer communities.

Method: The authors build an LLM-based multi-agent simulation calibrated with real GitHub data from 1,084 active developers and their repository relationship network. They initialize agents based on historical behavior, run a warm-up phase using past commits, and then fork the simulated community into two parallel experimental conditions: one without coding agents (No-CA) and one with coding agents (CA). They simulate 4 weeks of activity in each condition, track task planning and completion, interaction patterns (human-human vs CA-involved), and then evaluate the resulting public knowledge artifacts using a standardized retrieval benchmark to measure coverage, retrieval steps, and success rate compared to a real-human corpus.

Result: With coding agents, planned tasks increase by 34% and completed tasks by 39%, and median task completion time drops from 45 to 20 minutes. However, only 26% of developers adopt CAs, and the benefits are concentrated among already active and well-connected developers. Interaction patterns shift: direct human-human interactions fall from 32.4% to 11.6%, while CA-involved modes rise to 57.3%, including 40.3% of tasks completed through CA-assisted self-loops. The public knowledge produced in the CA condition is substantially weaker as later support: in a retrieval benchmark, the CA-generated corpus covers only 22.3% of needed knowledge versus 81.1% for the real-human corpus, and it requires more retrieval steps with lower success.

Conclusion: Generative coding agents substantially increase short-term technical productivity but divert work into private or agent-mediated channels, reducing visible human-human collaboration and degrading the quality and usefulness of the public knowledge record. This creates a tension between efficiency and the long-term health of open-source as digital public infrastructure: without careful design and policy, coding agents may benefit already advantaged developers while leaving future contributors with sparser, less supportive public artifacts.

Abstract: Open-source software communities are a form of digital public infrastructure that not only produces code, but also generates public knowledge and interpersonal relationships through visible collaboration. Generative coding agents (CAs) are an advanced tool to improve development efficiency while shifting part of activities from public human interaction to private human-agent loops. We study this shift using an LLM-based multi-agent simulation initialized with real GitHub data from 1,084 active developers and their repository relationships. After a warm-up with historical commits, we branch the same community state into parallel No-CA and CA conditions for 4-week simulations. CA introduction increases planned and completed tasks by 34.0% and 39.0%, respectively, and reduces median completion time from 45 to 20 minutes. However, adoption reaches only 26.0%, and the gains concentrate among developers who are already more active and well connected. CAs also restructure task execution pathways. Direct human-human interaction declines from 32.4% to 11.6%, while CA-involved modes increase to 57.3%, including 40.3% completed through CA-assisted self-loops. Public knowledge generated under CA condition also provides less support for later tasks. On a standardized retrieval benchmark, the CA corpus achieves 22.3% knowledge coverage, far below the 81.1% achieved by the real-human corpus, and requires more retrieval steps with a lower success rate. These results reveal a productivity-public knowledge tension: coding agents increase technical production, but more work shifts to agent-mediated or private loops, leaving public records less useful to future contributors.

</details>


### [161] [FOUND-AF: Benchmarking ECG Foundation Models for Atrial Fibrillation Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03597)
*Amirhossein Taleshinosrati,Yangyang Wang,Atitaya Phoemsuk,Vahid Abolghasemi,Naser Hossein Motlagh,Sadasivan Puthusserypady,Daniel Teichmann,Abdolrahman Peimankar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces FOUND-AF, a unified benchmark to fairly compare ECG foundation models for atrial fibrillation detection, and finds that ECGFounder offers the best overall trade-off between accuracy and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many ECG foundation models for automated AF detection exist, but previous evaluations are inconsistent because they use different datasets, preprocessing pipelines, classifiers, and validation protocols, making it hard to know which model is actually best or most practical for deployment.

Method: The authors build FOUND-AF, a benchmarking framework that evaluates pretrained ECG models as frozen feature extractors under strictly identical conditions: standardized ECG preprocessing, model-native resampling, a fixed XGBoost classifier, and grouped cross-validation at the recording level to avoid data leakage. They test nine models from five model families on four heterogeneous ECG datasets and assess performance using multiple classification metrics, ROC analysis, statistical comparisons via paired bootstrap with Holm correction, embedding visualizations, and profiling of computational efficiency.

Result: Across all datasets and evaluation criteria, the ECGFounder model generally delivers the best overall AF detection performance and shows a strong balance between predictive accuracy and resource usage (model size, inference time, and memory).

Conclusion: FOUND-AF offers a reproducible, leakage-controlled, and deployment-aware framework for comparing ECG foundation models, and the study shows that compact encoders pretrained on clinical ECG data can enable robust and computationally efficient AF detection across varied acquisition conditions.

Abstract: Atrial fibrillation (AF) is the most common sustained cardiac arrhythmia and is associated with increased risks of stroke, heart failure, and mortality. Recent ECG foundation models offer transferable representations for automated AF detection. However, their relative effectiveness remains unclear because existing studies use different datasets, preprocessing procedures, classifiers, and validation protocols. This study presents FOUND-AF, a unified, leakage-controlled, and deployment-oriented benchmarking framework that evaluates the quality of pretrained ECG representations under identical experimental conditions. Nine publicly available foundation models from five families, including HuBERT-ECG, CLEF, ST-MEM, ECG-JEPA, and ECGFounder, were evaluated across four heterogeneous ECG datasets, namely AFDB, CinC2017, CPSC2021, and LTAFDB. All models were used as frozen feature extractors with standardized preprocessing, model-native resampling, a fixed XGBoost classifier, and recording-level grouped cross-validation. The evaluation included classification metrics, receiver operating characteristic analysis, paired recording-level bootstrap comparisons with Holm correction, embedding-space visualization, and computational efficiency profiling. The ECGFounder model consistently achieved the strongest overall performance across datasets while offering a favorable trade-off between accuracy, model size, inference time, and memory usage. FOUND-AF therefore provides a reproducible framework for selecting ECG foundation models and demonstrates that compact, clinically pretrained encoders can support robust and computationally efficient AF detection across heterogeneous acquisition settings.

</details>


### [162] [Large language models for partial differential equation workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03600)
*Han Wan,Rui Zhang,Hao Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper surveys how large language models can support end-to-end workflows involving partial differential equations (PDEs), highlighting current capabilities, limitations, and the need for better datasets and benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In scientific and engineering practice, PDEs are used within complex workflows, not as standalone equations. As LLMs become capable of handling natural language, code, and mathematical reasoning, there is an opportunity to integrate them into PDE workflows for modeling, simulation, and decision-making. However, it is unclear how well current LLMs perform across these stages and what challenges limit their impact, motivating a systematic examination.

Method: The authors conceptually analyze and organize recent advances in LLM-assisted PDE research into three main workflow stages: (1) discovering and formulating governing models, (2) generating and refining executable numerical solvers, and (3) using simulation feedback for control, design, and optimization. They review existing systems and characterize how LLMs function as workflow-level interfaces, as well as identify bottlenecks such as data scarcity and the simulation-to-reality gap.

Result: The review finds that LLMs have begun to successfully assist in PDE-related tasks like proposing models, writing and debugging solvers, and iterating on designs using simulation outputs. However, progress is uneven: systems mostly operate as high-level interfaces rather than autonomous scientific agents, and their effectiveness is constrained by limited high-quality datasets, benchmarks, and the difficulty of constructing executable tasks with expert annotations and feedback. The gap between simulation environments and real-world systems further hampers the practical deployment of LLM-derived solutions.

Conclusion: LLM-assisted PDE workflows provide a rich, demanding testbed for advancing scientific AI capable of linking language, computation, physical laws, and real-world decisions. Overcoming current limitations—especially data and benchmark scarcity and the simulation-to-reality gap—is essential for moving from interface-like assistance toward robust, practically useful AI systems in science and engineering.

Abstract: Partial differential equations (PDEs) become actionable in science and engineering not as isolated formulae, but as executable workflows that connect modelling assumptions, governing equations, numerical solvers, diagnostics, and decisions. Large language models (LLMs) are beginning to support such workflows by linking natural language, symbolic mathematics, code, solver outputs, and feedback. Here we examine recent advances in LLM-assisted PDE research across three stages: the discovery and formulation of governing models, the generation and revision of executable numerical solvers, and the use of simulation feedback to support control, design, and optimization. Across these stages, current systems act primarily as workflow-level interfaces. Despite this progress, the field remains limited by the scarcity of high-quality datasets and benchmarks, especially for knowledge discovery and real-world applications, where expert annotation, executable problem construction, and task-level feedback require substantial domain effort. A further challenge is the persistent gap between simulation-based results and real-world scientific and engineering systems, which limits the direct transfer of numerical simulations, control policies, and optimized designs to practical settings. These challenges make LLM-assisted PDE workflows a critical testbed for developing scientific AI systems that can connect language, computation, physical constraints, and real-world decision-making.

</details>


### [163] [FraQ: Efficient Coordinate-Space Recompression for Federated Low-Rank Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03605)
*Shenghui Li,Thiemo Voigt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FraQ is an efficient server-side method to recompress and aggregate LoRA adapters in federated fine-tuning of LLMs, preserving accuracy while cutting communication and computation costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In federated learning with LoRA adapters for LLMs, each client learns low-rank updates. Standard parameter averaging fails because averaging the LoRA factors (A,B) does not equal averaging the induced full-rank weight updates AB^T. The exact aggregate can be formed in full weight space, but then recompressing via SVD or similar is computationally and memory expensive at the server. There is a need for a scheme that (1) preserves the exact global update implied by all clients, (2) recompresses it back to a low-rank adapter efficiently, and (3) reduces downlink communication without heavy server overhead.

Method: FraQ begins by stacking client LoRA factors into a representation that exactly encodes the aggregated update in full weight space. Instead of explicitly forming or decomposing this dense matrix, it constructs an orthonormal basis for the column space and a compact coordinate matrix. It then computes a small Gram matrix from these coordinates to recover the singular values (spectrum) efficiently. Using this spectrum, FraQ selects the minimal rank that satisfies a chosen energy (variance) retention threshold. Finally, it maps the chosen low-dimensional coordinate subspace back through the orthonormal basis to form a global low-rank adapter, effectively performing an approximate SVD-like recompression with significantly reduced time and memory costs.

Result: On text classification and commonsense reasoning benchmarks, FraQ yields performance close to uncompressed baselines, meaning that the loss in task accuracy from recompression is minimal. At the same time, it significantly reduces downlink communication because the server sends back only the recompressed low-rank adapter rather than the full stacked representation. The method also incurs low server-side recompression overhead compared to naive full-matrix decomposition approaches.

Conclusion: FraQ provides a practical and efficient recompression scheme for federated LoRA-based fine-tuning of LLMs. By operating in coordinate space and using compact Gram-matrix-based spectral recovery, it resolves the aggregation mismatch of LoRA factors while avoiding the computational and memory costs of full-matrix decompositions. This enables communication-efficient, accurate, and scalable federated adaptation of large models.

Abstract: Federated fine-tuning with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) enables efficient collaborative adaptation of Large Language Models (LLMs) without centralizing private data. However, LoRA's two-factor parameterization creates an aggregation mismatch across clients: naively averaging the factors does not recover the average of their induced updates. This mismatch can be avoided by forming the exact aggregate in the full weight space and then recompressing it, but decomposing the resulting dense matrix is computationally expensive and memory-intensive. We propose FraQ, an efficient coordinate-space recompression method for federated LoRA. Starting from stacked factors that exactly represent the aggregate, FraQ factorizes it into an orthonormal basis and a compact coordinate matrix. It then recovers the singular spectrum from a small Gram matrix, selects the smallest rank satisfying a prescribed energy threshold, and maps the selected coordinate subspace back through the basis to construct the global adapter. Experiments on text classification and commonsense reasoning benchmarks show that FraQ achieves accuracy close to uncompressed baselines while substantially reducing downlink communication with low server-side recompression overhead.

</details>


### [164] [Learning Clinical-Trial Strategy: Offline Policy Training for Decision Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03606)
*William Bolton,Philip Torr*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper frames oncology clinical trial planning as an offline decision-making problem and shows that structured offline RL-style methods, especially reward-weighted behavioral cloning, can outperform LLM tool agents in predicting future trial portfolios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical development requires sequential decisions about which trials to run, under uncertainty and using heterogeneous data (registries, filings, reviews, etc.). Existing approaches are ad hoc and not systematically data-driven. The authors want to see if offline decision-making / RL methods can learn from historical oncology programs to help plan future clinical trial portfolios, and whether they can beat frontier LLM agents using retrieval over the same evidence.

Method: The authors build a temporal dataset aggregating 31.7k heterogeneous records (trial registries, regulatory reviews, sponsor filings, utilization, epidemiology) into 881 decision episodes across 45 oncology drug programs. They cast, for each decision date, the task of predicting the next six‑month portfolio of trials (indications, etc.) as an offline decision-making problem. They train and compare four offline-learning objectives—behavioral cloning, reward-weighted behavioral cloning, learned-reward training, and value-based implicit Q-learning. They also build four strong LLM-based tool agents, all using a shared date-gated retrieval scaffold, and evaluate performance on several challenging held-out splits (by drug, sponsor, drug-class, and time, including a post-Aug 2025 contamination-clean split).

Result: Offline-trained models outperform non-fine-tuned LLM tool baselines on predicting future trial portfolios, especially in the contamination-clean post-August 2025 temporal holdout. Among offline objectives, reward-weighted behavioral cloning is best, achieving 46.2% indication F1 and 14.2% strict F1, compared with 25.0% and 2.1% for the best tool agent on those metrics. This demonstrates a substantial gain from structured offline learning over generic LLM tool use.

Conclusion: The study concludes that clinical development decisions—here, oncology trial portfolio planning—can be effectively modeled as an offline decision-making problem. Structured offline learning, particularly reward-weighted behavioral cloning, can learn from historical heterogeneous evidence to produce superior trial-planning policies compared with frontier LLM tool agents. This suggests that data-driven offline RL-style approaches can meaningfully support or augment human planning of clinical experiments.

Abstract: Clinical development is sequential decision-making under uncertainty, where a sponsor must plan a portfolio of experiments from heterogeneous evidence. We study this setting by framing oncology clinical development as an offline decision-making problem in which an agent predicts the next six-month trial portfolio of an oncology drug program from information available at the decision date. To support this, we construct a temporal dataset that combines 31.7k heterogeneous public data records, including trial registries, regulatory reviews, sponsor filings, utilization data, and epidemiology, into 881 offline decision episodes across 45 historical programs. We compare four offline objectives: behavioral cloning, reward-weighted behavioral cloning, learned-reward training, and value-based implicit Q-learning against four frontier LLM agents that share a common date-gated retrieval scaffold across held-out drug, sponsor, drug-class, and temporal splits. Models trained offline outperform the non-fine-tuned baselines, particularly in the post-August 2025 contamination-clean holdout. Reward-weighted behavioral cloning performs the best, obtaining 46.2% indication F1 and 14.2% strict F1 against 25.0% and 2.1%, respectively, for the best-performing tool agent on each metric. These results suggest that structured offline learning can teach agents to plan clinical experiments.

</details>


### [165] [Formal Verification of Agentic Systems over Operational Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03609)
*Alejandro J. Mercado,Alessio Lomuscio*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a formal model (STEAD) for LLM-based agentic systems operating over relational data, studies their formal verification, and provides conditions and constructions that make verification decidable and practically applicable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents are increasingly embedded into real-world workflows that manipulate persistent data (e.g., business processes, case management). Organizations need guarantees that such systems satisfy business rules and temporal properties over data evolution, but current approaches only constrain the agent’s interface behavior and lack system-level, formal guarantees. This motivates a rigorous model and verification framework capturing both the agent and its tool-based interactions with operational data.

Method: The authors: (1) define Stateful Tool-Enabled Agentic Deployments (STEADs), a formal model of a single LLM with a tool orchestration harness over relational data; (2) give operational semantics for STEADs; (3) formulate the verification problem against First-Order Computation Tree Logic (FO-CTL) specifications; (4) prove in general that verification is undecidable; (5) identify structural conditions ensuring that FO-CTL properties are preserved under finite-domain restriction, under which verification becomes PSPACE-complete; (6) analyze when LLM-driven agents fail these conditions due to non-equivariant treatment of opaque identifiers; (7) design a canonical deployment wrapper that enforces equivariance for arbitrary base agents while preserving existing equivariant behaviors; and (8) show that computing the canonical forms needed for the wrapper is graph-isomorphism-hard, then instantiate the framework on a case-management agent example.

Result: Key results: (1) General FO-CTL verification of STEADs is undecidable. (2) Under a finite-domain restriction that exactly preserves FO-CTL properties, verification complexity drops to PSPACE-complete. (3) A crucial sufficient condition is equivariance: renaming opaque identifiers in the data must induce a consistent renaming in the agent’s tool calls. (4) LLM agents in practice may break equivariance, leading to verification unsoundness. (5) A canonical deployment wrapper is proposed that enforces equivariance for any base agent, while leaving already-equivariant behavior unchanged. (6) The computation of canonical representations underpinning this wrapper is proved graph-isomorphism-hard. (7) The overall framework is demonstrated on a realistic LLM agent managing a case-management workflow.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that while verifying realistic LLM-based agentic systems over relational data is undecidable in full generality, it becomes tractable (PSPACE-complete) under well-justified structural conditions centered on identifier equivariance and finite-domain restriction. The proposed STEAD model and canonical wrapper provide a principled path to obtaining sound, system-level guarantees for LLM agents orchestrating tools, albeit at a complexity cost linked to graph isomorphism. This establishes both the theoretical limits and a practical methodology for verifying business-critical workflows driven by LLM agents.

Abstract: Agentic systems driven by large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in real-world workflows where they act on persistent operational data. Before deployment, these systems need to be verified against business requirements that govern workflow execution and data evolution. However, existing approaches do not provide such system-level guarantees, as they mainly constrain or analyse behaviour at the agent's interface level. We study here the verification of agentic systems comprising a single LLM and a tool orchestration harness over relational operational data. We formalise them as Stateful Tool-Enabled Agentic Deployments (STEADs), give their semantics, define the problem of verifying them against First-Order Computation Tree Logic (FO-CTL) specifications, and show that it is undecidable. We identify sufficient conditions for exact preservation of FO-CTL specifications under a finite-domain restriction, over which verification is PSPACE-complete. The key requirement is that renaming opaque identifiers in the data must correspondingly rename the selected tool calls. We show that LLM-driven agents can violate this condition and introduce a canonical deployment wrapper that guarantees it for arbitrary base agents while preserving already-equivariant behaviour. We prove that computing canonical representations required by this construction is graph-isomorphism-hard. Finally, we illustrate our framework on an LLM agent orchestrating a case-management workflow.

</details>


### [166] [Rethinking Modality Reliability in Multimodal Sentiment Analysis with Incomplete Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03611)
*Chunlei Meng,Jacqueline J. Pang,Pengbin Feng,Zhenyu Yu,Chun Ouyang,Zhongxue Gan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes MRCF, a modality reliability-calibrated framework for multimodal sentiment analysis with missing modalities, explicitly modeling how trustworthy each modality is to improve performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world multimodal sentiment data (text, audio, vision) are often partially missing or degraded. Existing methods either reconstruct missing modalities or directly learn from incomplete inputs, but they only handle modality reliability implicitly. This leads to problems when different samples or missing rates change how much affective evidence each modality truly carries, and when low-quality modalities negatively influence cross-modal fusion. The authors aim to explicitly model and use modality reliability to make incomplete-observation MSA more robust.

Method: They introduce MRCF, which has three main components: (1) a Reliability-Aware Branch that estimates sample-wise reliability scores for each modality by combining intra-modality quality indicators and cross-modal semantic consistency; (2) a Reliability-Guided Interaction Branch that uses these reliability scores to gate and modulate cross-modal message passing, reducing harmful influence from unreliable modalities; and (3) a Reliability-Calibrated Fusion Module that fuses both semantic features and estimated reliabilities into a joint representation for sentiment prediction. The framework is designed to work under incomplete-observation settings without requiring full data at test time.

Result: On three benchmark datasets for multimodal sentiment analysis—CMU-MOSI, CMU-MOSEI, and CH-SIMS—MRCF achieves strong performance under commonly used incomplete-observation protocols, outperforming or matching state-of-the-art baselines that do not model reliability explicitly.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling modality reliability in incomplete-observation multimodal sentiment analysis can alleviate reliability mismatch and reliability propagation bias, leading to more robust cross-modal interaction and fusion. The proposed MRCF framework demonstrates that reliability-aware design is an effective way to improve sentiment prediction when some modalities are missing or degraded.

Abstract: Multimodal Sentiment Analysis (MSA) integrates text, audio, and vision to infer human affect, yet real-world multimodal observations are often incomplete. Existing methods for incomplete-observation MSA mainly follow two paradigms. Reconstruction-based methods recover missing information from observed modalities, while joint-representation methods learn directly from incomplete inputs. Although effective, these methods usually treat modality reliability only implicitly within representation learning or fusion design rather than modeling it explicitly. We argue that modality reliability is a central variable in incomplete-observation settings. Failure to model it explicitly gives rise to two related issues. The first is reliability mismatch, in which the affective evidence retained by each modality varies across samples and missing rates. The second is reliability propagation bias, in which messages from degraded modalities may adversely affect cross-modal interaction and predictive performance. To address these issues, we propose MRCF, a Modality Reliability-Calibrated Framework for MSA with incomplete observations. MRCF contains a Reliability-Aware Branch that estimates sample-specific modality reliability from intramodal quality cues and cross-modal semantic consistency, a Reliability-Guided Interaction Branch that uses the estimated scores to modulate cross-modal information flow, and a Reliability-Calibrated Fusion Module that integrates reliability and semantic cues for final prediction. Experiments on CMU-MOSI, CMU-MOSEI, and CH-SIMS show that MRCF achieves strong performance under standard incomplete-observation protocols. Further analyses provide evidence that explicit reliability modeling helps mitigate reliability mismatch and reliability propagation bias during interaction and fusion.

</details>


### [167] [Unequal Verdicts: Investigating Gender Bias in LLM-Based Fake News Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03627)
*Razieh Chalehchaleh,Reza Farahbakhsh,Noel Crespi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates gender bias in large language models used for automated fake news detection by augmenting the LIAR dataset with gendered speaker job titles and evaluating six LLMs, finding significant instability and directional gender bias that harms reliability and fairness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly applied to socially impactful tasks like automated fact-checking, there is concern that these systems may encode or amplify gender bias, but this issue has not been systematically studied in the specific context of fake news detection. Understanding whether and how gender presentation alone influences veracity judgments is crucial for ensuring fairness, trustworthiness, and responsible deployment of LLM-based fact-checking tools.

Method: The authors augment the LIAR fake news benchmark by creating three gender variants (Neutral, Male, Female) of the speaker job titles for each statement, holding all other content constant. They then evaluate six state-of-the-art LLMs on this augmented dataset, measuring how often veracity labels differ across gender variants and quantifying bias using multiple fairness and bias metrics. They analyze two forms of bias: instability (label inconsistency across variants) and directionality (systematic favoring or disfavoring of a given gender) and assess statistical significance of directional effects.

Result: All six evaluated LLMs show gender sensitivity in their fake news judgments. Between 9.79% and 35.13% of statements receive inconsistent labels across the three gender variants, and direct Male-Female comparisons yield label flip rates between 6.5% and 23.6%. Two key patterns emerge: (1) instability, where the same content gets different veracity labels solely due to gendered job titles, and (2) directionality, where five models show statistically significant bias, with the strongest pattern reflecting greater skepticism toward male-presenting speakers (male-skeptic bias).

Conclusion: The study concludes that LLM-based fake news detection is affected by substantial gender bias that manifests both as unreliability (instability of predictions) and unfairness (directional favoritism). This undermines the suitability of current LLMs for automated fact-checking without explicit bias controls. The authors argue for bias-aware evaluation protocols, the development of mitigation strategies, and provide their gender-augmented LIAR dataset as a public resource to facilitate further research on fairness and robustness in LLM fact-checking systems.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used for automated fact-checking, yet their susceptibility to gender bias in this context remains underexplored. This study presents the first systematic investigation of gender bias in LLM-based fake news detection using real-world data. We augment the LIAR benchmark with three gender variants of speaker job titles (Neutral, Male, Female) for each statement to test whether veracity judgments vary solely based on gender presentation. Six state-of-the-art LLMs are evaluated across multiple bias and fairness metrics. All models exhibit gender sensitivity: 9.79%-35.13% of statements receive inconsistent labels across the three variants, with Male-Female comparisons showing 6.5%-23.6% flip rates. Two primary bias manifestations are identified: instability (inconsistent judgments) and directionality (systematic favoritism). Five models show statistically significant directional effects, with the strongest effects displaying male-skeptic patterns. These findings demonstrate that gender bias undermines both reliability and fairness in LLM-based fake news detection, highlighting the need for bias-aware evaluation and mitigation strategies. The augmented dataset is publicly released to support future research.

</details>


### [168] [Cross-Layer Interaction under Weight-Space Ablation: A Closed-Form Attention Jacobian Bound and a Test on a Real Pretrained Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03629)
*Abdallah Khemais*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper extends theoretical analysis of when activation patching and weight ablation agree in transformers, moving from a single residual block on synthetic models to multi-layer settings and real LLM weights, and empirically explores an emergent indirect-object-identification circuit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mechanistic interpretability tools like activation patching and weight ablation are widely used but their relationship is not fully understood. A companion paper provided a rigorous result only for a single residual block and small synthetic transformers. This paper aims to generalize those guarantees across layers, understand cross-layer interactions, and test them on real model weights and a realistic emergent circuit, thereby strengthening theoretical and empirical foundations for interpreting transformer behavior.

Method: 1) Mathematically extend the conditional-computation-in-residual-stream model to multiple layers, deriving an exact decomposition of the interaction from ablating carriers across several layers into per-layer terms plus a cross-layer remainder. 2) For two layers, express the cross-layer remainder as a double integral of a mixed second derivative and identify the Jacobian bound for the attention sub-block as the missing piece to control this term. 3) Derive a closed-form Jacobian bound for attention and verify it empirically on Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct’s weights. 4) Provide a closed-form expression for the curvature constant left implicit in the companion paper. 5) Empirically discover an emergent indirect-object-identification circuit in the model using activation patching and probe it with collapse, dissociation, and interaction tests across layers.

Result: 1) Theoretical extension: The interaction from ablating carriers across multiple layers decomposes exactly into a sum of same-block terms plus a cross-layer remainder; for two layers, this remainder is characterized exactly as a double integral over mixed second derivatives. 2) Identification and solution of the missing bound: they show that controlling the cross-layer term requires a Jacobian bound for the attention sub-block, derive this bound in closed form, and verify it without violations for Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct. They also provide a closed-form expression for the curvature constant in the companion bound. 3) Empirical circuit study: they find a shared carrier circuit for indirect object identification across five tested instances. Collapse and dissociation mostly hold but not universally, and a measurable nonzero interaction appears on three out of five case studies at layer pairs beyond the same-block regime where the original theorem applied.

Conclusion: Activation patching and weight ablation can be related by more general and rigorous formulas than previously shown, extending from single-block synthetic settings to multi-layer decompositions and real LLM weights, but cross-layer interactions introduce residual terms whose full control still requires chaining newly derived attention Jacobian bounds. Empirically, an emergent indirect-object-identification circuit partly satisfies theoretical expectations (shared carrier, approximate collapse and dissociation) but exhibits non-negligible cross-layer interactions, highlighting both the utility and the limitations of current mechanistic interpretability assumptions.

Abstract: A companion paper studies when activation patching and weight-space ablation agree, inside an idealized model where a conditional computation is carried additively through a residual stream. For the one composition in that model where two carriers are architecturally dependent, an attention head and its own layer's normalization-MLP composition, it derives an exact first-order interaction formula, zero when only the MLP is ablated and second-order bounded when the head is also ablated. That result is confined to a single residual block and checked only on small transformers on a synthetic task.
  This paper extends the result past both limits. First, the interaction from ablating carriers spanning several layers decomposes exactly into same-block terms, one per touched layer, plus a cross-layer remainder on which the decomposition makes no claim of smallness. Second, we isolate that remainder exactly, for two layers, as a double integral of a mixed second derivative, and name the missing ingredient needed to bound it: a Jacobian bound for the attention sub-block. We derive this bound in closed form and verify it, without a single violation, against Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct's real weights, though we do not yet chain it across layers. We also give, in closed form, the curvature constant the companion paper's bound leaves unexhibited.
  Third, on that same model, we search for and find an emergent circuit for indirect object identification, never designed into it, using the original activation-patching method for this task, and test collapse, dissociation, and interaction on it. The result is mixed: a shared carrier emerges across all five tested instances, collapse and dissociation hold on most but not all, and a nonzero interaction is measurable on three of five, at layer pairs outside the same-block case the companion theorem covers.

</details>


### [169] [When Teachers Mislead: Spurious-Signal-Aware On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03632)
*Yinuo Jiang,Yongjie Ye,Zhou Tao,Xiang Zhuang,Qiang Zhang,Huajun Chen,Tiankai Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes SA-OPD, a spurious-signal-aware on-policy distillation framework that filters misleading token-level teacher signals using an input-groundedness proxy and optimization impact, improving over standard and selective OPD on LLM and VLM benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing On-Policy Distillation (OPD) methods transfer knowledge from a teacher model to a student by supervising student-generated trajectories with dense token-level teacher signals. Recent selective OPD variants try to choose more useful signals based on confidence, informativeness, or learnability. However, these approaches assume token-level teacher judgments are reliably grounded in the input, which is often false: language models can produce high-confidence predictions driven by generic language priors, formatting habits, or stereotyped reasoning rather than evidence from the specific input. Such spurious yet optimization-relevant signals can create strong gradients that fail to improve task performance. There is a need to explicitly detect and mitigate these spurious token-level signals during OPD.

Method: The authors introduce SA-OPD (Spurious-Signal-Aware On-Policy Distillation), which augments OPD with a mechanism to identify and filter spurious token-level supervision. They design a lightweight input-groundedness proxy that estimates how much each token-level distillation signal depends on the input rather than on generic priors. In addition, they measure the optimization impact of each token via its distillation divergence, capturing how strongly it would influence the student update. SA-OPD then selectively filters out only those tokens that have both low input-groundedness and extreme distillation divergence, i.e., signals that are weakly grounded yet would cause large parameter updates. This yields fine-grained control over which tokens contribute to the OPD gradient. The framework is applied in both LLM and vision-language model settings without heavy architectural changes.

Result: Across extensive experiments on language and vision-language tasks, SA-OPD consistently outperforms vanilla OPD and several competitive selective OPD baselines. The improvements indicate that many high-impact teacher signals in standard OPD are indeed spurious and that explicitly filtering them leads to better downstream performance. The method proves robust across different model scales and modalities, demonstrating its generality.

Conclusion: Spurious, weakly input-grounded token-level signals are a key failure mode in on-policy distillation and are not adequately addressed by existing confidence- or informativeness-based selection schemes. By introducing an input-groundedness proxy and combining it with optimization impact to filter high-impact spurious tokens, SA-OPD offers a simple, effective, and broadly applicable way to improve OPD. The findings highlight input-groundedness as a crucial dimension in supervising student models and suggest that future distillation methods should explicitly account for how tightly supervision is tied to the input evidence.

Abstract: On-Policy distillation (OPD) transfers teacher capabilities by supervising student-sampled trajectories with dense token-level teacher signals. Recent selective OPD methods improve this process by prioritizing signals that are confident, informative, or learnable. However, the assumptions overlook a fundamental failure mode of language models: their token-level judgments can be driven by input-agnostic language priors, formatting conventions, or stereotyped reasoning templates rather than task-specific evidence. We refer to such optimization-relevant but weakly input-grounded supervision as spurious signals in OPD, which may produce large gradients while contributing little task-improving direction. To mitigate this issue, we propose SA-OPD, a Spurious-Signal-Aware On-Policy Distillation framework that identifies and filters misleading token-level supervision based on input-groundedness and optimization impact. SA-OPD introduces a lightweight input-groundedness proxy estimating whether a token-level distillation signal truly depends on the input. It then filters only tokens that simultaneously exhibit low input-groundedness and extreme distillation divergence, thereby removing high-impact spurious updates and achieving fine-grained OPD optimization. Extensive experiments on both large language model (LLM) and vision-language model (VLM) settings demonstrate that SA-OPD consistently outperforms Vanilla OPD and competitive selective methods. These results establish input-groundedness as a key dimension for OPD supervision selection and offer a simple, effective strategy for mitigating spurious updates.

</details>


### [170] [Is Inter-Seed Cross-Play Enough? Evaluating the Robustness of Zero-Shot Coordination Algorithms to Implementation Details](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03644)
*Maksymilian Wolski,Nicholas Hoernle,Johannes Forkel,Jakob Foerster*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how robust zero-shot coordination (ZSC) algorithms are to differences in how they’re implemented, using a new cross-implementation cross-play scheme applied to the Other-Play algorithm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of ZSC algorithms typically rely on a single code implementation with different random seeds, and sometimes small architecture changes. This ignores the real-world situation where independently engineered agents may differ due to ambiguous specifications and diverse implementation choices. The authors want to know whether current evaluation practices actually reflect robustness to such variations, or if they give an overly optimistic view of performance.

Method: The authors propose an evaluation protocol called cross-implementation cross-play. They create multiple, meaningfully different implementations of the same ZSC algorithm (here, Other-Play), varying implementation aspects previously shown to influence MARL performance (e.g., architectural choices, training details). They then measure how well independently implemented agents coordinate with each other, compared both within and across implementations, and compare this to standard ZSC evaluations that only vary seeds or architectures within a single implementation.

Result: Applying cross-implementation cross-play to the Other-Play algorithm, they find that its performance under this stricter, more realistic evaluation broadly matches what is seen under the standard ZSC evaluation protocol. That is, the algorithm’s ability to coordinate generalizes well across independently engineered implementations, despite variations in implementation details known to affect MARL outcomes.

Conclusion: For the case of Other-Play, the commonly used ZSC evaluation practice—testing a single implementation across random seeds (and occasionally architectures)—appears to be a reasonable surrogate for a more thorough, multi-implementation robustness evaluation. This suggests that, at least for this algorithm, concerns about hidden brittleness to implementation differences may be less severe than feared, and that standard ZSC benchmarks can provide informative assessments of cross-agent coordination ability.

Abstract: AI agents deployed in real-world settings must be capable of coordinating with humans and other AI agents they have not encountered before. Zero-shot coordination (ZSC) algorithms aim to achieve this by specifying high-level learning rules such that independently engineered agents can coordinate with each other at test time. Rigorous evaluation of ZSC algorithms remains difficult: ideally, multiple independent implementations of each proposed algorithm must be used, reflecting the variation that arises when independent parties interpret and implement the same specification. In practice, however, ZSC algorithms have almost exclusively been evaluated using a single implementation trained across different random seeds, with only a handful of works additionally varying the neural network architecture. This leaves open questions about robustness to specification ambiguities and implementation details. In this work, we provide the first systematic evaluation of this robustness. We introduce a new evaluation scheme, cross-implementation cross-play, varying implementation details that prior work has shown to affect the performance of multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) algorithms, and we evaluate Other-Play, a popular ZSC algorithm, with this scheme. Our findings are encouraging and suggest that, for Other-Play, the standard ZSC evaluation is, in fact, a reasonable proxy for this more thorough cross-implementation evaluation.

</details>


### [171] [AutoSND: From Execution Evidence to Structural Policies for Automated Network Dismantling Heuristic Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03653)
*Zhijing Hu,Changjun Fan,Yufan Deng,Zhiguang Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces AutoSND, an automated framework that uses tree search and large language models to design effective and efficient heuristics for network dismantling, outperforming previous methods and yielding interpretable strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Network dismantling is essential for understanding and controlling the robustness and vulnerability of complex systems (e.g., infrastructures, social or biological networks). Designing good dismantling heuristics is challenging because they must be both effective and computationally efficient, and existing methods rely heavily on manual expert design. Recent LLM-based heuristic design approaches can generate many candidate heuristics but struggle to systematically incorporate feedback from execution failures or performance signals back into the heuristic generation process at a structural level. This limitation motivates a more principled, automated framework that can iteratively learn from execution evidence and produce stronger, interpretable dismantling programs.

Method: The authors propose AutoSND, a three-stage tree search framework for automatically generating complete network dismantling programs. In Stage I, the system broadly explores the space of simple heuristic programs and executes them, archiving both successful and failed runs as execution evidence. In Stage II, it analyzes the collected candidate records and distills them into higher-level structural policies, focusing on three aspects: local structural signals used for decisions, how much of the neighborhood the program can access, and how far state updates are allowed to propagate. In Stage III, the framework continues tree search but now conditions generation on these learned structural policies, guiding the search toward promising regions of the program space. This yields two final types of heuristics: quality-prioritized (AutoSND-Q) and speed-prioritized (AutoSND-S).

Result: On 12 real-world networks and 3 larger real-world networks, AutoSND shows improved search performance and stability over baselines. It discovers dismantling programs that are more competitive (better tradeoffs between dismantling effectiveness and efficiency) and more structurally interpretable than those produced by prior LLM-based or manually designed heuristics. The learned heuristics consistently exhibit a common structural pattern that the authors characterize in detail.

Conclusion: AutoSND demonstrates that combining tree search with structured analysis of execution evidence can automatically produce strong, interpretable heuristics for network dismantling. The resulting programs share a clear structure: they primarily use residual degree (the degree after previous removals) as the main ranking signal, refine node selection with additional but bounded local structural features, and limit the range over which state updates are propagated after each removal. This shows that automatic heuristic design can match or surpass expert-crafted strategies while revealing general structural principles for effective network dismantling.

Abstract: Network dismantling is fundamental to analyzing the robustness and vulnerability of complex systems, yet practical heuristics must balance effectiveness and computational efficiency, and are usually designed manually by researchers. Existing large language model based automatic heuristic design methods can generate and screen candidates, yet they have difficulty further transforming candidate quality or failure states during execution into structural-level guid- ance for subsequent generation. We propose AutoSND, a three stage tree search framework for complete network dismantling pro- grams. Stage I broadly explores from simple heuristics and archives execution evidence. Stage II compiles candidate records into struc- tural policies concerning local signals, neighborhood access, and state update ranges. Stage III continues tree search conditioned on these policies and obtains the final quality prioritized and speed prioritized candidates, AutoSND-Q/S. Experiments on 12 real world networks and 3 large real world networks show that AutoSND achieves better search performance and stability and discovers more competitive and structurally interpretable network disman- tling programs. The final candidates form an interpretable structure that uses residual degree as the backbone, adjusts node order with bounded local signals, and restricts the state update range. Code is available at https://github.com/MirrorNew/AutoSND.

</details>


### [172] [Taming the Implicit: Dual-Channel Risk-Aware Reinforcement Fine-Tuning for Continual Multimodal Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03660)
*Yibei Liu,Jiajun Chen,Qianle Zhang,Tangyue Jin,Mengying Zhu,Meng Xi,Yangyang Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces RAPO, a risk-aware, plug-and-play framework to reduce catastrophic forgetting in continual reinforcement fine-tuning of multimodal LLMs, achieving large forgetting reduction without hurting new-task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement fine-tuning is popular for post-training multimodal LLMs and is thought to naturally resist catastrophic forgetting. However, under large task distribution shifts, existing RFT methods still suffer sharp forgetting, because their implicit reward-variance regularization cannot adequately control optimization risk. There is a need for an explicit, principled way to manage optimization risk across tasks in continual RFT, without requiring costly cross-task memory or changing existing algorithms.

Method: The authors propose Risk-Aware Policy Optimization (RAPO), a dual-channel risk governance framework for continual RFT. On the policy channel, they introduce Risk-Aware Policy Scaling, which adaptively scales per-sample policy updates based on rollout reliability and a Fisher-information-inspired measure of local predictive sensitivity, limiting risky updates. On the data channel, they design Risk-Aware Dynamic Bucket Sampling, which dynamically stratifies samples into risk-based buckets and constructs training batches that emphasize informative yet stable samples. RAPO is designed as a plug-and-play wrapper that can be combined with arbitrary RFT algorithms without architectural changes or storing cross-task data.

Result: On the public MLLM-CL continual learning benchmark for multimodal LLMs, plugging RAPO into an RLOO-based RFT backbone yields a 79.8% reduction in final catastrophic forgetting while maintaining competitive performance on new tasks. This empirically validates that explicit, risk-aware policy and data control substantially mitigates forgetting under strong distributional shifts in continual post-training.

Conclusion: RAPO shows that catastrophic forgetting in continual reinforcement fine-tuning is not inherently solved by standard RFT and that explicit risk governance is crucial under large distribution shifts. By jointly controlling per-sample update magnitudes and risk-aware batch composition, RAPO can be seamlessly integrated into existing RFT algorithms, greatly reducing forgetting without sacrificing new-task performance and without requiring cross-task memory. This positions RAPO as a practical and general solution for more stable continual post-training of multimodal LLMs.

Abstract: Reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT) is widely believed to inherently resist catastrophic forgetting in continual post-training of multimodal large language models. Under pronounced task distributional shifts, however, forgetting across representative RFT algorithms escalates sharply. This stems from the implicit reward-variance regularization inherent to RFT, which proves incapable of suppressing uncontrolled optimization risk. We propose Risk-Aware Policy Optimization (RAPO), the first dual-channel framework for explicit risk governance in continual RFT. On the policy channel, Risk-Aware Policy Scaling adaptively calibrates per-sample update magnitude via rollout reliability and Fisher-inspired local predictive sensitivity; on the data channel, Risk-Aware Dynamic Bucket Sampling reorganizes training batches through dynamic risk stratification, steering optimization toward informative yet stable samples. As a plug-and-play strategy requiring no cross-task memory, RAPO generalizes to any RFT algorithm without modification. On the public MLLM-CL benchmark, RAPO reduces final forgetting by 79.8% relative to its RLOO backbone while retaining new-task competitiveness.

</details>


### [173] [Shielding for Higher-Order Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03662)
*Filip Cano,Thomas A. Henzinger,Konstantin Kueffner*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a method to build runtime safety shields that can enforce constraints on higher-order derivatives (like speed, acceleration, jerk) by modeling them in a finite-state safety-game framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical safety shields only consider whether the current state is safe or unsafe, which is too coarse for many cyber-physical systems such as vehicles that must satisfy constraints on speed, acceleration, and jerk. There is a need for shield synthesis that can reason about these higher-order smoothness constraints in a rigorous, automatable way.

Method: The authors define differential safety properties using finite differences over a discretized state space, capturing constraints over derivatives of the state. They construct a finite-state safety game over an expanded history-based state space, where states encode the last k system states for properties of order k. Shield synthesis is then reduced to solving this ordinary safety game. They also design an iterative synthesis procedure that handles a hierarchy of derivative constraints by solving them in increasing order and pruning the state space based on earlier solutions.

Result: They obtain a synthesis algorithm that produces shields which store exactly k past states for safety properties of order k and prove that this memory requirement is tight. The iterative, hierarchical approach enables more efficient computation by avoiding exploration of state-space regions that are already determined to be unsafe under lower-order constraints.

Conclusion: Finite-state safety-game techniques can be extended to enforce high-order smoothness constraints in cyber-physical systems by encoding derivative requirements via finite differences and history-based state spaces. The resulting shields are provably memory-optimal and can be synthesized more efficiently using an iterative, hierarchical algorithm that incrementally prunes unsafe parts of the state space.

Abstract: Safety shields are runtime enforcement mechanisms that restrict the actions of a controller to guarantee safety. Classical shields are usually synthesised for state predicates: the current physical state is either safe or unsafe, and the shield disables precisely those actions that can force the system into an unsafe state in the future. In many cyber-physical applications this view is too coarse. A vehicle approaching an obstacle should not only avoid collision, but also respect speed regulations, force limits induced by acceleration, and jerk limits to prevent injuries. From a physical perspective, these requirements are predicated over the derivatives of the state. This paper develops a finite-state safety-game construction for such high-order smoothness constraints. We define differential safety properties using finite differences over a discretised state space, characterise their expressiveness, and reduce shield synthesis to an ordinary safety game over a history state space. We give a synthesis algorithm whose shields store exactly $k$ past states for properties of order $k$ and prove that this memory is necessary. We describe an iterative synthesis procedure for a maximally permissive shield that operates over hierarchies of derivative constraints. The algorithm solves constraints iteratively in increasing order and uses the solution at each iteration to prune the state space for the next constraint. This makes shield synthesis more efficient in practice, as the algorithm refrains from exploring large regions of the state space that are known to be unsafe.

</details>


### [174] [PhyAI: Real-Time Physical AI at the Edge, Scalable Rollouts in the Cloud](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03682)
*Chenghua Wang,Daliang Xu,Dongqi Cai,Duojin Sun,Hao Zhang,Haoze Qian,Huaiyuan Zhang,Jinshuo Cui,Kezhao Zhao,Longxi Gao,Mengwei Xu,Rongjie Yi,Tianyue Zhang,Weikai Xie,Xiyuan Tan,Xuanzhe Liu,Yingying Qin,Yiwen Lu,Yuan Yao,Yuezhi Zu,Yunhan Guo,Ziqi Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PhyAI is a unified, high-performance inference engine for Physical AI policies that runs multiple model types across cloud, edge, and onboard hardware with competitive latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physical AI pipelines currently use separate, specialized inference programs for evaluation, cloud rollouts, edge serving, and onboard deployment, even though they share checkpoints and action formats. This fragmentation complicates development, slows model integration, and makes it hard to systematically optimize performance across diverse hardware and use cases. The authors aim to provide a single, general runtime that simplifies deployment while remaining fast enough to be practical for real-time control.

Method: The authors design PhyAI, an inference engine that factors model- and architecture-specific logic into pluggable adapters while centralizing common runtime components. Adapters encapsulate conditioning, solver choice, caching, and output logic for each model, whereas the shared core handles graph execution, kernels, memory management, and parallel services across GPUs. The system supports both vision-language-action (VLA) and world-action models (WAMs) at various scales and deployment locations (onboard, edge, cloud). They evaluate PhyAI by integrating existing policies such as pi0, pi0.5, GR00T N1.7, MiniCPM-Robot, and Cosmos3-Nano-Policy-DROID, profiling performance under different batch sizes and GPU configurations. They also propose a control-time Roofline model that separates inference-bound from environment-bound scenarios in embodied control benchmarks.

Result: PhyAI can run multiple state-of-the-art Physical AI models via its adapter interface, including rapid integration of newly released models like MiniCPM-Robot. Across several policies (pi0, pi0.5, GR00T N1.7, MiniCPM-Robot), it achieves speedups of 1.40x–4.65x compared to official implementations. On Cosmos3-Nano-Policy-DROID with eight H20 GPUs (CFG=2, TP=4), PhyAI reduces latency from 2.46 s to 1.18 s, a 2.08x speedup. Profiling shows that different models have distinct performance bottlenecks: for pi0.5 at batch size 1 on a Hopper GPU, an action expert comprising only 8.8% of FLOPs contributes 57.2% of latency, but at batch size 32 its FLOP share is 13.5% and throughput reaches about 100 samples/s. For Cosmos3, generation dominates runtime and batching from 1 to 16 yields only a 14.3% throughput gain. The control-time Roofline analysis shows pi0.5 policies on four LIBERO suites are environment-bound, while Cosmos3 remains inference-bound.

Conclusion: A single, adapter-based inference runtime can support a wide variety of Physical AI models and deployment contexts while remaining competitively fast, though not always faster than specialized runtimes. PhyAI’s design demonstrates that centralizing execution, memory, and parallelism while modularizing model-specific logic yields both performance and maintainability benefits. The introduced control-time Roofline framework helps characterize whether control tasks are limited by model inference or by interaction with the environment, guiding when further inference optimization will meaningfully improve end-to-end control performance.

Abstract: Physical AI policies require inference throughout their lifecycle, including model evaluation, cloud reinforcement learning rollout, edge GPU serving, and onboard deployment. Although these settings share the same checkpoint and action semantics, they often rely on separate inference programs. To unify them, we build PhyAI, a Physical AI inference engine with a single runtime that keeps architecture-specific conditioning, solver, cache, and output logic in model adapters while sharing graph execution, kernels, memory management, and parallel services. The same codebase runs vision-language-action (VLA) models and world-action models (WAMs) on single or multiple GPUs across onboard, edge, and cloud deployments.
