We used the adapter interface to add MiniCPM-Robot on the day of its release. PhyAI achieves 1.40x-4.65x speedups over the official implementations of pi0, pi0.5, GR00T N1.7, and MiniCPM-Robot. On Cosmos3-Nano-Policy-DROID it reduces latency from 2.46 to 1.18 s on eight H20 GPUs (CFG=2, TP=4), a 2.08x speedup. Specialized runtimes remain faster in several configurations, so our goal is one runtime with competitive latency rather than the fastest result in every case. Detailed profiles reveal why different models need different execution policies: on a Hopper-series GPU at batch size one, the pi0.5 action expert accounts for 8.8% of FLOPs but 57.2% of latency; at batch size 32 its share drops to 13.5% and throughput reaches about 100 samples/s. Cosmos3 remains generation-dominated and gains only 14.3% throughput as batch size increases from 1 to 16. We further introduce the control-time Roofline, which distinguishes inference-bound from environment-bound control; the measured pi0.5 points on four LIBERO suites are environment-bound while Cosmos3 stays inference-bound. Code and benchmarks: https://github.com/mingti-org/phyai.

</details>


### [175] [LiveEvalBench: Toward Open-World Evaluation for Web Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03689)
*Yiyao Wang,Zhen Wen,Yinghao Tang,Yixiao Fu,Lin Yuan,Xiaolau Zhang,Jun Zhou,Wei Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces LiveEvalBench, a new dynamic benchmark for evaluating large language models on web UI generation by simulating a multi-role, interactive review process instead of static tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing web-generation benchmarks evaluate models with static, rigid pipelines that do not capture the interactive nature of frontend applications, the diversity of valid implementations, or the fast evolution of web technologies. The authors want an evaluation framework that better reflects real-world frontend development workflows and provides more faithful, fine-grained assessments of models’ capabilities.

Method: They design LiveEvalBench, an automated evaluation framework that models an agentic workflow with three main roles: a Build Engineer that handles deployment and build issues, a Code Engineer that inspects and reasons about source code, and a UI Tester that interacts with the running web app in the browser. These agents follow shared rubrics for comparability but also apply implementation-specific criteria. The protocol is adaptive, allowing it to adjust to each artifact and to incorporate new evaluator roles or assessment dimensions without redesigning the whole pipeline.

Result: Applying LiveEvalBench to a range of realistic web-generation tasks, they find that its scores and judgments closely track those of human experts while producing more detailed diagnostics about where models succeed or fail across the frontend lifecycle (build, code quality, and interactive behavior).

Conclusion: Reframing web-generation evaluation as an interactive, multi-agent, and extensible process yields a benchmark that better matches human assessment and captures nuanced aspects of frontend capability. LiveEvalBench therefore offers a more robust and adaptable standard for evaluating large language models on real-world web UI generation tasks.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly capable of synthesizing executable frontend projects, yet existing benchmarks still treat web generation as a static evaluation problem. We argue that frontend artifacts demand a different paradigm: they are interactive rather than static, admit diverse yet equally valid implementations, and evolve faster than rigid pipelines can accommodate. To address these gaps, we present LiveEvalBench, an automated framework that reformulates web-generation evaluation as an agentic, adaptive, and extensible process. LiveEvalBench instantiates evaluation as a collaborative review workflow, in which a Build Engineer, a Code Engineer, and a UI Tester collectively gather evidence across the full lifecycle of a frontend project, from deployment and code inspection to browser-based interaction. To handle implementation diversity, an adaptive protocol couples shared rubrics for cross-model comparability with implementation-grounded criteria tailored to each artifact. The framework further supports incremental integration of new evaluator roles and assessment dimensions without pipeline redesign. Experiments across diverse real-world web-generation scenarios show that LiveEvalBench aligns closely with human expert judgment and provides fine-grained insights into frontier models' web generation capabilities. Code is available at https://github.com/wyysteelhead/LiveEvalBench

</details>


### [176] [TARL: Transaction-Aware Reliable Ledgers for Executable Memory Management in Long-Term Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03699)
*Han Xiao,Hongjun Xu,Xin Zhang,Yidong Chen,Xiaodong Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes TARL, a framework that updates long-term memory in agents using five nuanced actions instead of a binary write/hold decision, reducing memory errors and corruption.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-term memory systems for agents typically use a simplistic binary Write/Hold decision when updating memory. This cannot distinguish between adding new facts, revising outdated beliefs, ignoring irrelevant data, rejecting unreliable information, or deferring uncertain claims. As a result, a single incorrect update can repeatedly distort future retrieval and reasoning, leading to memory pollution and cumulative corruption. The authors aim to design a more fine-grained and reliable memory update mechanism.

Method: The authors introduce TARL, a memory state update framework that maps each incoming statement to one of five executable actions determining how memory should be modified. TARL includes mechanisms to: (1) identify which existing memory item is affected; (2) resolve the temporal scope of the update; (3) compare the reliability of different information sources; and (4) maintain and update separate ledgers of accepted, pending, and rejected information. The system is trained by contrasting the memory states produced by alternative update operations, encouraging it to choose the operation that yields the correct next memory state. They also create TARL-Mem, a benchmark dataset with fine-grained action labels and explicit next-state supervision for training and evaluation.

Result: On the TARL-Mem benchmark, including in-domain, cross-source, temporal, counterfactual, and sequential tasks, TARL outperforms baselines in both predicting the correct update action and recovering the correct memory state after updates. It reduces memory pollution, preserves conflicting but relevant evidence instead of overwriting it, and limits the cumulative corruption of memory over many updates.

Conclusion: Fine-grained, action-based memory updates significantly improve the robustness and fidelity of long-term memory in agents compared with binary write/hold schemes. By explicitly modeling how to add, revise, defer, or reject information and tracking accepted, pending, and rejected ledgers, TARL reduces error propagation and cumulative memory corruption. The TARL-Mem benchmark supports training and evaluation of such systems, and the released implementation facilitates further research on reliable memory for long-term agents.

Abstract: Persistent memory helps long-term agents retain knowledge, yet a single update error can repeatedly distort future retrieval and reasoning. Most existing systems reduce memory updating to a binary Write/Hold decision, which cannot distinguish whether new information should be added, ignored, used to revise an outdated belief, rejected as unreliable, or deferred for verification. These choices may share the same binary label while producing fundamentally different memory states. We introduce TARL, a memory state update framework that maps each statement to one of five executable actions. TARL identifies the affected memory, resolves its temporal scope, compares source reliability, and updates accepted, pending, and rejected ledgers. It is further trained by comparing the memory states produced by alternative update operations, encouraging the model to select the operation that leads to the correct result. We also introduce TARL-Mem, a benchmark with fine-grained action labels and next-state targets. Across in-domain, cross-source, temporal, counterfactual, and sequential evaluations, TARL improves action prediction and state recovery, reduces memory pollution, preserves conflicting evidence, and limits cumulative corruption. The complete model implementation is provided in the supplementary material.

</details>


### [177] [Less Traffic, Better Outcomes: Competition-Aware Request Dispatch in Real-Time Ad Exchanges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03705)
*Jonaid Shianifar,Blaz Mramor,Fangda Zou,Matthieu C. Martin,Xingsheng Guo,Zhihua Zhu,Rong Zhou,Bichen Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They design and deploy a smarter way for ad exchanges to decide which bid requests to send to which DSPs, cutting unproductive traffic while increasing revenue.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In RTB ad exchanges, nearly all ad requests are broadcast to all DSPs, even though most get no bids. This wastes DSP computation, forces them to throttle under budget/compute limits, and weakens auction performance and revenue. A better request routing mechanism is needed that accounts for competition and DSP constraints.

Method: They propose a competition-aware request dispatch framework that: (1) uses distributional models of DSP bids to estimate the value and likelihood of bids for each request–DSP pair; (2) applies probabilistic forwarding based on these predictions to decide which DSPs receive each request; and (3) adapts per-DSP forwarding thresholds over time using lightweight online policy optimization to handle non-stationary markets. They test this via four sequential online experiments on a large-scale production ad exchange.

Result: In a full deployment across multiple DSPs on a platform handling 20B+ daily requests, the new policy reduces the number of requests sent to DSPs by 34.2% while increasing net revenue by 4.6% during a 14-day evaluation period, with statistical significance (p<0.001). Further analysis shows strong differences across traffic segments and DSPs, indicating that the policy reallocates traffic in a way that surfaces each DSP’s comparative advantage rather than uniformly reducing volume.

Conclusion: Competition-aware, probabilistic request dispatch—driven by distributional bid prediction and adaptive per-DSP thresholds—can substantially lower unproductive DSP traffic while improving exchange revenue. Aggregate averages can hide important heterogeneity, so segment-level and per-DSP evaluation is crucial; the framework appears to better match requests to the DSPs most likely to monetize them, without increasing total request load.

Abstract: Real-time bidding (RTB) ad exchanges typically forward nearly all incoming requests to demand-side platforms (DSPs), even though only a small fraction receive bids. This over-distribution weakens auction outcomes: DSPs throttle participation under compute and budget constraints, reducing the effective use of limited bidding capacity. We present a competition-aware request dispatch framework that uses distributional bid prediction and probabilistic forwarding to decide whether each request should be sent to each DSP. The system adapts per-DSP thresholds over time through lightweight policy optimization to track non-stationary market conditions. We evaluate the framework through four sequential online experiments on a production platform serving over 20 billion daily requests. A full multi-DSP deployment reduces DSP request volume under the policy by 34.2% while increasing net revenue by 4.6% (p<0.001) in a recent 14-day window after an initial DSP adaptation period. Further analysis highlights strong heterogeneity across traffic segments and reveals that aggregate metrics can be misleading. Segment-level and per-DSP analyses suggest that the policy surfaces comparative advantages among DSPs, improving monetized outcomes without increasing overall request volume.

</details>


### [178] [When Outputs Disperse, Does Epistemic Revision Follow? A Black-Box Coupling Diagnostic for Machine Collectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03722)
*Molood Arman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that in LLM collectives, visible disagreement (dispersion in text/embeddings) does not always mean genuine change of mind, and introduces a black-box metric system (CI + MPCS/RDP) to measure whether higher dispersion actually corresponds to epistemic revision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In human and multi-agent systems, disagreement is often used as a proxy for epistemic diversity and the capacity to correct errors. For LLM collectives, however, models may produce arguments that look diverse while still clinging to the same mistaken premise or conclusion. This breaks standard assumptions in collective intelligence and makes it hard to know whether interventions that increase output diversity are really improving truth-tracking. The paper is motivated by the need for a black-box, text-only diagnostic to distinguish genuine stance revision from mere rephrasing in LLM collectives, especially when using diversity-inducing interventions.

Method: The authors define and operationalize 'dispersion-revision coupling'—the extent to which an intervention that provably increases dispersion of outputs in embedding space is accompanied by actual revision of the group’s epistemic stance. They build a black-box diagnostic with two independent channels: (1) an output channel using a Coherence Index (CI) to verify that an intervention changed dispersion, and (2) an epistemic channel using per-turn stance annotations to track whether the agents revise their stance or merely reformulate it. They implement this via the Meta-Predictive Clarity System (MPCS), which triggers a Re-Differentiation Protocol (RDP) whenever outputs over-converge, and apply it to five-agent collectives of two LLM configurations (gpt-4o-mini and gemini-2.5-flash) across 310 paired episodes per condition. They also perform mechanism tagging to classify whether post-RDP responses preserve the original premise or concede/revise it.

Result: For gpt-4o-mini collectives, conditional dissent induced by MPCS/RDP substantially improves recovery from false premises by +17.7 percentage points (p<1e-6), whereas static persona diversity actually harms recovery (-8.1 points, p=.007). For gemini-2.5-flash collectives, applying the same intervention with a comparable budget produces no significant gain (26.1% vs 27.1% recovery, p=.84), even though CI confirms a drop in dispersion; the difference in treatment effects between models is statistically significant (z=3.79, p<.001). Mechanism tagging reveals that Gemini tends to maintain the false premise via intra-framework dissent: after RDP, 94% of tagged responses merely reformulate the original stance instead of conceding, compared to only 24% on GPT. This shows that increased dispersion does not necessarily entail epistemic revision, and that models differ systematically in their coupling regimes.

Conclusion: The study concludes that dispersion in LLM collective outputs is an unreliable standalone proxy for epistemic diversity and corrective capacity. Interventions that increase apparent disagreement can either help or hurt false-premise recovery depending on how tightly dispersion is coupled to actual stance revision in a given model family. The authors advocate for routinely reporting per-intervention stance shift and premise-preservation rates, alongside accuracy, to properly characterize collective behavior and avoid over-interpreting diversity-inducing methods. Their CI + MPCS/RDP framework offers a reusable, black-box way to measure dispersion-revision coupling across different LLM collectives.

Abstract: Collective intelligence research treats disagreement as evidence of epistemic diversity: if agents express different views, the group should retain capacity to revise. In LLM collectives this proxy can break: agents can produce diverse-looking arguments while preserving the same conclusion. We operationalize dispersion-revision coupling: the degree to which an intervention that verifiably increases the dispersion of a collective's outputs in embedding space is accompanied by genuine revision of its epistemic stance rather than premise-preserving reformulation. The diagnostic is black-box: it operates on generated text alone and makes no claims about the internal representations of the generating models. Two channels are measured independently: an output channel, the Coherence Index (CI), verifies that the intervention changed output dispersion; an epistemic channel, per-turn stance annotation, measures whether the collective revised. We propose CI with the Meta-Predictive Clarity System (MPCS), which inserts a Re-Differentiation Protocol (RDP) when outputs over-converge, as a reusable method for estimating this coupling regime. We evaluate five-agent collectives from two configurations (gpt-4o-mini and gemini-2.5-flash; 310 paired episodes per condition). On gpt-4o-mini, conditional dissent improves false-premise recovery by +17.7 points (p<1e-6) while static persona diversity harms recovery (-8.1, p=.007). On gemini-2.5-flash, the same intervention at a comparable budget yields no gain (26.1% vs 27.1%, p=.84) despite a verified dispersion drop; the two treatment effects differ from each other (z=3.79, p<.001). Mechanism tagging shows Gemini preserves the false premise via intra-framework dissent: 94% of tagged post-RDP responses reformulate rather than concede (vs 24% on GPT). We recommend reporting per-intervention stance shift and premise-preservation rate alongside accuracy.

</details>


### [179] [SAT-Edge-Agent: Hardware-in-the-Loop Edge-Agent Orchestration for Onboard Satellite Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03728)
*Longji He,Jeto Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces and evaluates SAT-Edge-Agent, a hardware-in-the-loop satellite edge-agent system that orchestrates onboard AI tasks and measures its performance on an ARM-based edge SoC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable intelligent onboard satellite tasking that can autonomously translate high-level mission intent into local AI tool invocations and return structured, machine-consumable products, all under tight communication, power, and compute constraints. The authors seek a reproducible, observable testbed to study edge-agent orchestration rather than just model accuracy.

Method: Implement SAT-Edge-Agent on a commercial ARM-based heterogeneous edge SoC with a browser workspace and FastAPI-based agent that coordinates a local OpenAI-compatible LLM service and a YOLO-style oriented object detector trained on FAIR1M. Define two fixed FAIR1M detection workloads (single-image and two-image serial requests), run each workload 20 times in a hardware-in-the-loop setting, and instrument the system to collect latency, CPU utilization, and NPU load along with request-level artifacts in a public evidence package.

Result: Both workloads completed successfully in all 20/20 trials. Mean end-to-end Full-Agent latencies were about 29.4 s (single-image) and 60.9 s (two-image), with empirical P95 latencies of ~31.2 s and ~66.9 s. The detector itself was fast (mean 861 ms and 1511 ms), accounting for only ~2.5–3% of end-to-end latency. Mean CPU utilization was ~20.5%. A 200-ms sampled NPU-load field reported 100% for both workloads, though it reflects a shared-accelerator metric rather than calibrated detector utilization. The authors release sanitized logs, redacted JSON outputs, SSE examples, and scripts to reproduce statistics.

Conclusion: SAT-Edge-Agent provides a concrete, reproducible hardware-in-the-loop boundary to study and observe satellite edge-agent orchestration performance, revealing that orchestration and overheads dominate latency rather than the detector itself. However, the work does not address or validate detection accuracy, novel geolocation capabilities, true energy efficiency, or actual flight readiness, so it should be viewed as an orchestration and benchmarking framework rather than a full operational solution.

Abstract: Onboard satellite intelligence requires a task layer that translates mission intent into local tool calls, exposes execution state, and returns machine-consumable artifacts under communication and power constraints. We present SAT-Edge-Agent, a hardware-in-the-loop (HIL) edge-agent system deployed on a commercial off-the-shelf ARM-based heterogeneous edge system-on-chip. A browser workspace and FastAPI agent coordinate a local OpenAI-compatible language service with a project-internal YOLO-style oriented-object-detection endpoint that returns FAIR1M metadata-backed structured results. Two fixed FAIR1M workloads, one single-image and one serial two-image request, were repeated 20 times each and completed 20/20 attempts. Mean Full-Agent latency was 29.353 s and 60.937 s, with empirical P95 values of 31.166 s and 66.882 s. Mean detector time was 861.386 ms and 1510.920 ms, only 2.93% and 2.48% of the corresponding Full-Agent means. Profiling indicates that most visible latency occurs outside detector execution. Mean CPU utilization was 20.761% and 20.482%. A 200-ms NPU-load field averaged 100% for both workloads, but it represents a shared-accelerator software field rather than detector-only occupancy or calibrated utilization. The public evidence package provides sanitized request-level records, redacted JSON, normalized SSE examples, and scripts reproducing the reported statistics. These results establish a reproducible HIL boundary for observable satellite edge-agent orchestration, but do not establish detector accuracy, a new geolocation method, calibrated energy efficiency, or flight readiness.

</details>


### [180] [CARE-Bench: Benchmarking Patient-Facing LLM Triage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03731)
*Yining Hua,Hongbin Na,Cyrus Ayubcha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CARE-Bench is a benchmark to evaluate how safely medical LLMs advise patients on what to do next, showing current models often mistime actions even with prompting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As patient-facing medical LLMs increasingly provide triage advice before clinician contact, there is a critical safety need to understand whether these systems recommend the correct next action at each step of a conversation, not just final diagnoses or static decisions.

Method: The authors build CARE-Bench, a source-grounded benchmark of 500 patient cases with 1,059 partial-disclosure prefixes reconstructed from real medical dialogues, consultations, and follow-up questions. Each step is labeled with one of four possible recommended current actions. They then test 11 language models on 269 held-out rounds using both unprompted and minimally prompted open-ended settings, and use a fixed GPT-5.5 model to map free-form responses into the four action labels, allowing macro-F1 evaluation per turn.

Result: Across 11 models, unprompted macro-F1 on the four-action triage task is low (31.2–50.4). Minimal prompting improves performance for 10 of 11 models, with macro-F1 rising to 46.9–63.4, but substantial errors remain, particularly in the timing of actions. Models frequently jump to recommending care prematurely; when the correct next step was to ask for more information, only 33.5% of prompted responses maintained that information-gathering step.

Conclusion: Patient-facing triage with LLMs is not solvable by simple prompting tricks, as models systematically mistime recommended actions, often failing to ask necessary follow-up questions before suggesting care. Therefore, explicit evaluation of action timing—such as with CARE-Bench—is necessary before deploying medical LLMs or agents that provide triage guidance to patients.

Abstract: Patient-facing medical LLMs and agents increasingly answer symptom questions before clinician contact, where the key safety question is what action the user should take next. We introduce CARE-Bench, a source-grounded benchmark that evaluates sequential patient-facing triage as a four-label per-turn current-action task. CARE-Bench contains 500 cases and 1,059 evaluated patient-disclosure prefixes reconstructed from medical dialogue, consultation, and follow-up-question sources. We evaluate 11 models on 269 held-out rounds under unprompted and minimally prompted open-ended protocols, using a fixed GPT-5.5 mapper to code each response into the four-label action space. Unprompted macro-F1 remains low, ranging from 31.2 to 50.4. Prompting improves 10 of 11 models, with prompted macro-F1 ranging from 46.9 to 63.4, but substantial threshold errors remain. Prompted models often recommend care before needed clarification is obtained; when the correct action was to ask for more information, only 33.5% of prompted outputs preserved the step. The persistence of these errors after prompting suggests that patient-facing triage is not a simple prompting problem and supports explicit evaluation of action timing before deployment.

</details>


### [181] [Failure-Informed Image Self-Augmentation for Multimodal Large Language Model Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03733)
*Chunyang Jiang,Pingping Zhang,Yuzhi Zhao,Wenao Ma,Zhijian Hou,Mengyang Wu,Yiyang Cai,Senkang Hu,Sitong Cheng,Chi-Min Chan,Wei Xue,Yike Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces FISA, a failure-informed image self-augmentation framework that uses a model’s own visual failure cases to synthetically generate harder yet label-preserving images, improving multimodal LLM performance on VQA tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal LLMs need large amounts of high-quality, annotated multimodal data, which are expensive to obtain. Existing self-augmentation methods mostly focus on text, while image-side augmentation is generic, handcrafted, and not tailored to the model’s specific weaknesses. The authors aim to create an automatic, model-aware way to expand training images that directly target the model’s visual failure modes while preserving ground-truth answers.

Method: The authors propose FISA, which: (1) mines the model’s failure cases on visual question answering examples; (2) constructs image complications—visually more challenging variants of original images—that are designed to keep the original answer valid; (3) uses model-based self-examination to check whether the augmented images are useful for learning; and (4) applies a dual fidelity filtering strategy to remove samples that likely distort the original semantics or labels. The resulting curated augmented images are then used to continue training the MLLM.

Result: On standard VQA benchmarks, FISA-trained models show consistent performance gains in both in-distribution and out-of-distribution evaluations. The synthesized data provide better data efficiency than generic image augmentation baselines, and FISA can be combined with existing textual self-augmentation methods to deliver additional improvements. Ablations indicate that the dual fidelity filtering is important for practical effectiveness and for avoiding harmful augmentations.

Conclusion: Failure-informed, model-centric image self-augmentation is an effective way to improve multimodal LLMs without costly new annotations. By generating answer-preserving but visually harder images from the model’s own failure cases and carefully filtering them, FISA yields superior gains over generic image augmentation and is complementary to textual self-augmentation strategies for VQA.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have achieved remarkable performance across vision-language tasks, but their progress depends heavily on large-scale, high-quality multimodal data that are costly to annotate. Self-augmentation offers a promising alternative by enabling models to expand their own training data without external supervision. However, existing MLLM self-augmentation methods are largely text-centric, while image augmentation remains underexplored and typically relies on generic or handcrafted transformations that are weakly aligned with the model's actual incapability. We propose Failure-informed Image Self-Augmentation (\textbf{FISA}), a framework for MLLM self-improvement that constructs augmented images from the model's own failure cases. Our method generates visually challenging yet answer-preserving image complications, verifies their utility through self-examination, and applies dual fidelity filtering to avoid semantic distortion. Experiments on visual question answering benchmarks show that the proposed method consistently improves performance across both in-distribution and out-of-distribution settings. Further experiments validate the compatibility of FISA with existing textual self-augmentation approaches, the superior data efficiency of the synthesized samples over generic image augmentation baselines, and the practical effectiveness of the proposed filtering strategy.

</details>


### [182] [AgenticECO: An Agentic Framework for ECO on 3D Integrated Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03738)
*Shuo Ren,Yaohui Han,Libo Shen,Zhiqiang Jia,Rongliang Fu,Bei Yu,Tsung-Yi Ho*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces AgenticECO and EcoRoute, an AI-agent-based, minimal-disturbance ECO routing workflow for 3D-IC design that makes each repair attributable and significantly improves defect clearance rates over traditional full reroute and stock repair methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With the slowdown of Moore’s law, 3D-IC integration is increasingly important, but current ECO (engineering change order) flows for 3D-ICs are manual, depend heavily on expert effort, and suffer from the problem that edits are entangled with global rerouting. This makes it impossible to attribute signoff-quality results to specific ECO edits, and 3D-ICs introduce bond-level defect modes that lack simple 2D analogues. There is a need for an automated, accountable ECO workflow that works specifically for 3D-ICs and can be evaluated rigorously.

Method: The authors build AgenticECO, a tool-using agent workflow that interacts with the open-source TaiWei 3D-IC flow, and pair it with EcoRoute, an ECO-routing layer that constrains a standard pinned router to make minimal, localized changes. This preserves most of the existing routing while implementing ECO edits, enabling attribution of signoff metrics to individual edits. They evaluate on nine matched natural defect cases under equal resource budgets, perform controlled ablation-style studies (e.g., testing necessity of repair moves under preservation, examining occupancy-aware choices), and conduct preregistered visual and blind diagnostic studies on a pixel-level instrument used for localizing defects. All results are validated through standard signoff checks, including routing, extraction, timing, DRC, and structural-equivalence checks.

Result: Under identical budgets across nine natural defect cases, AgenticECO successfully clears seven defects, compared with only two cleared by full reroute and two by stock repair. For the cases it clears, it achieves on average 0.66% routing disturbance and touches no clock nets. A cross-backbone rerun under the same fixed conditions clears all nine. Controlled experiments show (1) that the specific repair moves are necessary when routing preservation is enforced, (2) that occupancy-aware landing-site choices improve the legality of landings but not raw repair success rate, and (3) that when clock constraints are tightened, the minimal-disturbance nature of the method becomes decisive in whether a repair is accepted. Preregistered visual studies show the pixel instrument accurately localizes defect-related areas, and a preregistered blind diagnostic experiment perfectly restores all held-out injected defects with no incorrect edits, uniquely among compared approaches.

Conclusion: AgenticECO together with EcoRoute provides an automated, evidence-gated, and minimally disturbing ECO workflow for 3D-ICs that makes ECO edits attributable and significantly improves defect clearance under realistic constraints. The method is robustly validated—both algorithmically and via preregistered human/diagnostic studies—and passes comprehensive signoff gates for all accepted repairs. The release of code, environment, and detailed audit logs supports reproducibility and suggests this framework can serve as a new standard for accountable ECO in advanced 3D-IC design flows.

Abstract: As Moore's law slows, the industry is turning to three-dimensional integration; yet in merged 3D-IC flows, routed designs expose bond-level defects with no 2D analogue, and post-route engineering change orders (ECO) remain manual, expertise-bound work. Worse, the standard edit-then-fully-reroute practice entangles a repair with router churn, so a signoff number cannot be attributed to the edit that motivated it. We present AgenticECO, an evidence-gated tool-using agent workflow for 3D-IC ECO on the open-source TaiWei flow, paired with EcoRoute, a minimal-disturbance ECO-routing layer that drives the unmodified pinned router so a repair is attributable to its edit. Across nine matched natural defect cases under identical budgets, AgenticECO clears seven versus two for both full reroute and stock repair, at 0.66\% mean disturbance over cleared cases and zero clock nets touched, and a cross-backbone rerun under the same sealed contract clears all nine. Controlled studies show that the repair moves are necessary under preservation, that occupancy-aware choice buys legal landings rather than repair success, and that under tightened clocks minimal disturbance flips accept versus reject. Three preregistered visual studies localize the pixel instrument's edge to contested landing sites, and a preregistered blind diagnostic exactly restores every held-out injected defect, the only arm with zero wrong edits. Every accepted result passes routing, fresh extraction, max/min timing, DRC, and structural-equivalence gates. Code, environment, and per-episode audit artifacts are released as supplementary material.

</details>


### [183] [MissClick: Exploiting Digit-Serialized Coordinates to Attack GUI Grounding Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03740)
*Yu Ran,Wentao Zhao,Xin Zhang,Yi Pan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MissClick is an adversarial attack framework that exploits the digit-wise, place-value structure of coordinate outputs in GUI visual grounding models to cause severe misclicks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GUI grounding models output click coordinates as sequences of digit tokens, but attacks and defenses typically treat these outputs like generic text and ignore their numerical, place-value structure. Small changes in high-order digits can cause large spatial misclicks, posing security risks that are not yet systematically studied.

Method: The authors formalize coordinate generation as token-level prediction with strong place-value semantics and design two white-box adversarial objectives that operate directly on this structure. MissClick-U (untargeted) maximizes a soft measure of coordinate displacement from the original click location, while MissClick-T (targeted) minimizes a loss that weights target-digit errors by their positional significance (e.g., hundreds vs ones place). They integrate these objectives into gradient-based attacks against GUI grounding models and evaluate them on OS-Atlas and UGround across desktop, web, and mobile settings.

Result: MissClick-U attains significantly higher untargeted attack success rates than existing methods: 75.07% and 72.93% on OS-Atlas and UGround, improving by 16.62 and 30.72 percentage points. MissClick-T similarly boosts targeted hijacking success to 44.86% and 62.67%, gains of 31.73 and 47.06 percentage points over prior attacks. Ablations show that displacement-based objectives are best for untargeted attacks, while place-weighted digit objectives are best for targeted attacks.

Conclusion: Adversarial attacks on GUI grounding models are substantially more potent when they exploit the numerical and place-value structure of coordinate tokens rather than treating them as plain text. Different attack goals (untargeted vs targeted) demand distinct objective designs—soft-coordinate displacement for generic disruption and place-weighted target-digit loss for precise hijacking—highlighting the need to rethink robustness for coordinate-generating models.

Abstract: Recent GUI visual grounding models generate screen coordinates as sequences of digit tokens that are parsed into numerical values and mapped to executable clicks. The security implications of this coordinate generation process have been largely overlooked. We observe that each coordinate digit is predicted as a categorical token, yet after parsing, changing a hundreds-place digit by one changes the corresponding numerical coordinate component by 100 units, which can induce a large displacement of the executed click. This observation motivates attack objectives that account for the numerical and place-value structure of coordinate outputs rather than treating them as ordinary text. Moreover, untargeted and targeted attacks impose different success conditions--displacing the click outside the correct region versus into an attacker-specified region--and therefore benefit from different objectives. We propose MissClick, a simple and effective white-box adversarial attack with two goal-specific objectives: MissClick-U maximizes soft-coordinate displacement for untargeted disruption, while MissClick-T minimizes a place-weighted target-digit loss for targeted hijacking. Compared with existing attacks against GUI grounding models on OS-Atlas and UGround across desktop, web, and mobile platforms, MissClick-U achieves untargeted success rates of 75.07\% and 72.93\% (+16.62 and +30.72 pp), and MissClick-T achieves targeted success rates of 44.86\% and 62.67\% (+31.73 and +47.06 pp). Attack objective comparison further shows that soft-coordinate displacement yields the highest untargeted attack success rate, whereas place-weighted target-digit optimization yields the highest targeted attack success rate, revealing distinct objective preferences for the two attack goals.

</details>


### [184] [Agents Catching Agents: Shortcut Cascades and Benchmark Gaming in Clinical Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03744)
*Sebastián Andrés Cajas Ordóñez,Agastya Munnangi,Aldo Marzullo,Felipe Ocampo Osorio,Quang Bui,Mohammad Shahin,Armaan Grewal,Emmanuel Paul Kwesiga,Anqi Peter Li,Josephine Nanyonjo,Aaditya Panchal,Arshnoor Bhutani,Nikhil Jaiswal,Milit S. Patel,Maximin Lange,Leo Anthony Celi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how multi-agent language model committees used for clinical decision support can be manipulated by socially plausible shortcuts, and evaluates oversight mechanisms to detect such gaming.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As clinical decision support moves toward using committees of language-model agents to deliberate on cases, there is a risk that these systems can be exploited by cues or shortcuts that lead to incorrect but socially plausible decisions, undermining safety and reliability. The authors aim to understand how vulnerable such committees are to gaming and what forms of oversight are effective.

Method: The authors evaluate Gemini-based multi-agent committees across seven cohorts on six public clinical datasets covering text (MedQA-USMLE, MedMCQA, MIMIC-CXR reports), imaging (NIH ChestX-ray14, MIMIC-CXR-JPG, CheXpert), and tabular ICU data (SUPPORT2). They inject various shortcut cues—including peer agents asserting the same wrong answer and false pre-screen flags—and measure how often a designated holdout agent adopts these shortcuts. They then test three oversight agents (a gate, a transcript-only judge from the same model lineage, and an independent referee that re-queries the holdout) for their ability to detect when the holdout has been socially influenced rather than reasoning honestly.

Result: Gemini committees show modest vulnerability to isolated shortcut cues (5–16% answer flipping), but a socially plausible shortcut—two peers agreeing on the same incorrect answer—drives much higher contagion: the holdout adopts the wrong answer in about 38% of cases, as does a false pre-screen flag, across capability tiers. A simple gate oversight mechanism fails completely, with a 100% false-positive rate in separating genuine agreement from social adoption. A same-lineage judge that reads only the dialogue transcript works well for text tasks (100% precision, 93% recall) but degrades to gate-like behavior in imaging tasks. An independent referee that privately re-queries the holdout generalizes better to imaging, achieving 77–88% precision with a 13–21% false-positive rate. Increasing the visual salience of cues does not significantly increase contagion, but adding another agreeing peer voice does. Most manipulated agents do not explicitly mention the hidden rubric they shift toward, indicating that the gaming is largely implicit and not revealed by self-report.

Conclusion: Multi-agent language model committees for clinical decision support are especially vulnerable to socially plausible forms of gaming, where consensus-like signals from peers or system flags can strongly bias decisions even without overt cues. Simple oversight based on gates or same-lineage transcript judges is insufficient, especially in imaging settings. More robust oversight requires independent referee agents that can re-assess decisions without relying on self-report from the influenced agent. Designers of clinical AI systems must treat social plausibility as a core attack surface and build oversight that does not depend on models accurately explaining their own reasoning or susceptibility to shortcuts.

Abstract: Clinical decision support is moving toward committees of language-model agents deliberating on a shared workspace. We ask whether such committees can be gamed by shortcuts, cues a benchmark rewards but a clinician would ignore. Across seven cohorts on six public datasets spanning text (MedQA-USMLE, MedMCQA, MIMIC-CXR reports), imaging (NIH ChestX-ray14, MIMIC-CXR-JPG, CheXpert) and tabular ICU records (SUPPORT2), Gemini committees resist these cues in isolation (flip 5-16%), yet a socially plausible shortcut spreads: when two peers assert the same wrong answer, the holdout under test adopts it in 38% of cases, as does a false "pre-screen" system flag, on both capability tiers. Of three oversight agents, a gate cannot separate adoption from honest agreement (false-positive rate 100%); a same-lineage judge reading only the transcript flags adoption on text (precision 100%, recall 93%) but collapses onto the gate in imaging; a referee that privately re-queries the holdout transfers to imaging (77-88% precision, 13-21% false-positive rate). Tripling a cue's visual salience does not move contagion, whereas a second peer voice raises it by half again. Gaming a hidden rubric is near-silent: only 1/10 text and 1/134 imaging drifters name the rubric they moved toward. What games a committee is social plausibility, and only a referee independent of self-report catches it. Code: https://github.com/criticaldata/benchmaxxing

</details>


### [185] [Risky Business: Measuring The Faithfulness-Safety Tension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03745)
*Dominik Meier,Luca Joshua Francis,Marco Bernhard Kaiser,Terry Ruas,Jan Philip Wahle,Bela Gipp*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how reliable chain-of-thought reasoning is for monitoring large reasoning models, finding a tension between being faithful to internal reasoning and being robust to unsafe thoughts, and proposes a method and dataset to quantify and mitigate this tension.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chain-of-Thought reasoning is being used to monitor AI models by inspecting their intermediate reasoning traces, but this only works if outputs are faithful to those traces. At the same time, aligned models are supposed to detect and refuse unsafe or illogical reasoning, which can require *not* following the literal chain of thought. The authors are motivated to understand and measure this alignment tension and to find ways to make models both monitorable and safe.

Method: They introduce HazMart, a human-written dataset framed as an autonomous AI shopkeeper making decisions. Instead of hint-based faithfulness tests, they propose Targeted Reasoning Replacement (TRR), which directly edits intermediate reasoning steps to insert unsafe or incorrect thoughts and then checks whether the final answer follows those edits. They evaluate two Large Reasoning Models (DeepSeek-R1-Llama-70B and QwQ-32B) on HazMart and TRR, and perform mechanistic analysis on QwQ-32B to identify internal representation directions corresponding to faithfulness and safety, especially around the token where the model commits to an action. They then apply representation steering to selectively strengthen the safety direction and measure effects on behavior and capabilities.

Result: DeepSeek-R1-Llama-70B shows very high faithfulness (97.5%) but poor robustness to unsafe reasoning (only 12.3% of unsafe chains are rejected). QwQ-32B, in contrast, is less faithful (74.7%) but safer (73.9% safety). Mechanistic probing of QwQ-32B finds anti-correlated internal directions that encode faithfulness and safety, with strongest influence at the action-commit token. By steering the safety direction in representations, they increase safe behavior by 9 percentage points without degrading the model’s overall performance on base tasks.

Conclusion: There is an inherent alignment tension between faithfulness to chain-of-thought and robustness against unsafe reasoning in current large reasoning models. Directly manipulating reasoning traces with TRR reveals this trade-off more sharply than prior hint-based approaches. Safety and faithfulness are partially separable at the level of internal representations, and targeted representation steering can improve safety while preserving capabilities. This suggests that effective model monitoring will require architectural or training approaches that jointly optimize for both faithful reasoning and robust rejection of unsafe thoughts.

Abstract: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning offers a promising window into model monitoring. However, monitoring relies on faithfulness, i.e., the model output strictly derives from its reasoning trace. We identify an alignment tension where a model must be faithful enough to be monitored, yet robust enough to reject unsafe reasoning. We demonstrate that this counterbalance exists in current Large Reasoning Models (LRMs), and show ways in which it can be addressed. We introduce HazMart, a human-written dataset set in an autonomous AI shopkeeper scenario. Unlike prior work that relies on providing hints in prompts to test faithfulness (e.g., "A Stanford professor said it should be Answer A"), we propose a novel replacement-based technique, which we call Targeted Reasoning Replacement (TRR), that directly intervenes in the reasoning chain to substitute in unsafe or illogical thoughts (e.g., "Wait, the answer must be Option B [was Option A] because it is the most fitting"). DeepSeek-R1-Llama-70B exhibits high faithfulness (97.5%) but fails to reject Unsafe Reasoning (12.3%), while QwQ-32B is more robust (73.9% safety) at the cost of lower faithfulness (74.7%). Mechanistic analyses of QwQ-32B reveal that these properties are represented by anti-correlated internal directions peaking at the action-commit token. Finally, we demonstrate that representation steering can independently amplify the safety direction, increasing safe behavior by 9 percentage points while maintaining base capabilities.

</details>


### [186] [GDPevo: Evaluating Agent Self-Evolution on Real Business Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03764)
*Leijun Zhou,Zhihao Liu,Xiang Qu,Chenxu Liu,Yifei Liu,Yanke Yu,Jingzhe Xu,Xuejun Wu,Buyue Qian,Xi Chen,Yaowei Zheng,Junhao Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GDPevo is a fully automated, contamination-resilient benchmark to rigorously evaluate agent self-evolution on realistic, GDP-related enterprise workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent self-evolution—updating an agent’s persistent state from past experience to improve future performance—is a key capability for practical, economically relevant AI agents. However, current benchmarks have three major issues: (1) they cover few real-world, high-value enterprise domains; (2) they don’t clearly separate training and test tasks so that improvements at test time can be confidently attributed to prior experience; and (3) they are susceptible to data contamination from pretraining corpora. The paper aims to build a principled, scalable benchmark that directly targets these issues and enables robust measurement of self-evolution in agents.

Method: The authors introduce GDPevo, an “evolution-native” benchmark constructed via a fully automated data pipeline. They start from realistic GDP-related enterprise workflows and decompose each workflow into atomic business rules. Through a mechanism called rule hybridization, subsets of these rules are distributed across multiple training tasks and then recombined into held-out test tasks so that any test-time gains must rely on integrating knowledge acquired during training. The benchmark spans several enterprise domains (CRM, ERP, finance, healthcare, legal, data-centric workflows) and is organized into groups, each with multiple training and test tasks. The pipeline allowed them to generate GDPevo V1 (120 tasks in 12 groups) and scale to V2 (240 tasks in 24 groups) within two days to mitigate contamination. They then evaluate four different agent systems (each defined by a harness+model combination) under four supervision regimes, with and without self-evolution, to quantify performance gains.

Result: Self-evolution reliably boosts performance: across the evaluated agents and supervision settings, held-out test accuracy improves by as much as 16.44 percentage points when agents are allowed to update and reuse their persistent state. Nonetheless, even the best evolved agents perform far below an oracle that has full access to all business rules, which reaches 91.6% accuracy. This gap shows that current agents only partially exploit the available structure and that the benchmark is non-trivial. The pipeline itself successfully scales the benchmark from 120 to 240 tasks rapidly, demonstrating its practicality for responding to contamination concerns.

Conclusion: GDPevo provides a rigorous, scalable, and contamination-resilient benchmark specifically designed to measure agent self-evolution on realistic enterprise workflows. The rule hybridization strategy ensures that test-time improvements can be causally linked to training experience. Empirical results confirm that self-evolution can significantly improve performance but also reveal a large gap to an oracle upper bound, implying that existing agents are far from saturating the benchmark and that substantial headroom remains for future research. By releasing the pipeline, benchmark, and full results, the authors aim to catalyze systematic progress on self-evolving AI agents in economically meaningful domains.

Abstract: Agent self-evolution updates an agent's persistent state from prior experience and reuses it to solve related tasks more effectively. Evaluating self-evolution is difficult: existing benchmarks provide limited coverage of economically valuable task domains, do not always design training and test tasks such that test-time gains can be attributed to training experience, and remain vulnerable to data contamination. We present GDPevo, an evolution-native benchmark grounded in GDP-related enterprise workflows, together with the fully automated data pipeline that generates it. Its core mechanism, rule hybridization, decomposes each enterprise workflow into atomic business rules, distributes subsets of these rules across training tasks, and recombines them in held-out test tasks so that test-time gains are attributable. GDPevo spans CRM, ERP, finance, healthcare, legal, and data-centric workflows. Its V1 release contains 120 tasks in 12 groups, with five training and five held-out test tasks per group. Full automation enables the pipeline to expand the suite to 240 tasks in 24 groups (V2) within two days, providing a practical response to contamination. Using GDPevo, we evaluate four agents, each comprising a harness and a model, under four supervision types. Self-evolution consistently improves held-out accuracy by up to 16.44 percentage points. But the best evolved agents remain far below the fully informed oracle ceiling of 91.6%, indicating that the self-evolution ability of current agents remains far from fully realized. We publicly release the pipeline, benchmark, and full evaluation results at https://github.com/Prism-Shadow/GDPevo.

</details>


### [187] [ContinualSkillBench: Can LLM Agents Truly Evolve Their Capabilities?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03874)
*Tianyi Guan,Yiding Wang,Haotong Yang,Siyuan Cao,Shirui Liu,Yi Hu,Jiaqi Li,Muhan Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ContinualSkillBench, a benchmark to study how LLM agents acquire and reuse skills over time across ordered subtasks, revealing that current systems adapt from context but struggle to form robust, reusable skill abstractions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic LLM systems now use external tools and skills but it is unclear whether, and how well, they can continually evolve these skills and whether learned skills truly improve later task performance. There is a need for a systematic way to measure in-context continual skill learning, skill reuse, and consolidation across tasks of growing difficulty.

Method: The authors build ContinualSkillBench, a dynamic evaluation framework with five domains, each containing 100 subtasks arranged by difficulty and designed for cross-task skill reuse. They then run experiments where models solve these tasks sequentially, comparing different mechanisms of skill evolution: pure in-context learning (adapting from prior interactions) versus explicit skill maintenance (storing and reusing discovered skills). Performance is analyzed across models and domains, with attention to how many and what kind of skills are learned and reused.

Result: Sequential task execution generally improves performance across domains, but the magnitude of improvement differs by model and domain. In-context learning alone performs, on average, similarly to approaches that explicitly store and reuse skills, indicating that much of the benefit comes from adapting to the immediate prior context and feedback rather than from clean, reusable abstractions. Explicit skills nonetheless help specifically on tasks that need stable procedures or very precise outputs. Less capable models tend to accumulate many more narrow, fragmented skills rather than compact, general ones.

Conclusion: Current in-context skill evolution mechanisms in LLM agents are capable of continual adaptation and yield performance gains when tasks are solved sequentially. However, they often fail to reliably distill experience into robust, transferable skills, especially in weaker models that collect fragmented, task-specific skills. Explicit skill representations offer targeted benefits for procedural or precision-heavy tasks, but in general, more work is needed to achieve consistent skill consolidation and effective long-term skill reuse.

Abstract: Modern agent frameworks equip large language models with external skill libraries to solve complex tasks. However, it remains unclear whether these systems can effectively evolve their skills and whether the resulting skills improve task-solving capabilities. To bridge this gap, we introduce ContinualSkillBench, a dynamic evaluation framework for in-context continual skill learning. It covers five representative domains, each containing 100 interconnected subtasks ordered by increasing difficulty and opportunities for cross-task skill reuse. Our experiments show that sequential execution generally improves performance, but the gains vary substantially across models and domains. Moreover, in-context learning performs comparably to explicit skill maintenance on average, suggesting that much of the improvement arises from adaptation to prior context and feedback rather than reusable skill abstraction alone. Explicit skills nevertheless provide selective benefits for tasks requiring reusable procedures or precise outputs. We further find that less capable models tend to accumulate larger, more fragmented collections of task-specific skills. These findings show that current in-context skill evolution mechanisms can support continual adaptation, but still struggle to consistently consolidate experience into robust and transferable skills.

</details>


### [188] [Computing Actual Causes for Neural Network Predictions under Structured Causal Inputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03772)
*Jannick Strobel,Muqsit Azeem,Stefan Leue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a causality-based method to explain neural network predictions that explicitly models dependencies among input features, achieving scalable and more accurate explanations compared to existing approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most explanation methods for neural networks, such as feature attribution or minimal sufficient subsets, implicitly assume that input features are independent. In realistic settings, inputs often have structured dependencies, so independence assumptions can lead to misleading or spurious explanations. There is a need for an explanation framework that can account for these dependencies and provide principled, causally grounded explanations with formal guarantees.

Method: The authors formalize explanations as Halpern-Pearl (HP) actual causes within Boolean Structural Causal Models (SCMs) that capture dependencies among input variables. They design an algorithm to compute all minimal HP actual causes for a given prediction by combining bound propagation with a branch-and-bound search strategy. The algorithm systematically prunes the search space while maintaining guarantees of completeness (all minimal causes are found) and minimality (no superfluous elements in each cause). They compare against brute-force, integer linear programming (ILP), and heuristic search baselines on SCMs of varying graph sizes.

Result: The proposed method scales to SCMs with up to 28 nodes and extremely large search spaces, handling up to approximately 2.3×10^13 candidate (cause, contingency) pairs within a 180-second time budget per instance. It substantially outperforms brute-force and ILP-based methods in scalability and also outperforms heuristic search as the graph size increases. Empirically, the algorithm successfully enumerates all minimal actual causes under these challenging conditions.

Conclusion: Modeling input dependencies via Boolean SCMs and defining explanations as HP actual causes yields more principled and reliable explanations of neural network predictions. The proposed algorithm makes exact causal explanation computationally feasible at moderate scales while offering formal guarantees. A case study demonstrates that traditional methods that ignore dependencies can over-report causes, with about 14.9% of those being spurious when evaluated under the SCM, highlighting the practical importance of accounting for feature dependencies in explanation methods.

Abstract: Explaining the predictions of neural networks is a central challenge in trustworthy AI. Existing explanation methods, such as those based on feature attribution or minimal sufficient sets, typically treat input features as independent, which can yield misleading explanations when inputs exhibit structured dependencies. We address this by formalizing explanations as Halpern-Pearl (HP) actual causes, modeling input dependencies using Boolean Structural Causal Models (SCMs). We compute HP causes by applying bound propagation and branch-and-bound techniques, while providing formal guarantees of completeness and minimality. Our experiments show that we substantially outperform brute-force and ILP baselines in scalability, and outperform heuristic search as graph size grows, computing all minimal actual causes on instances with search spaces of up to $2.3\times10^{13}$ candidate (cause, contingency) pairs, on SCMs with up to 28 nodes, within a 180s per-instance budget. In a case study, we further show that ignoring input dependencies inflates the number of reported causes, 14.9% of which are spurious under our SCM.

</details>


### [189] [KnowHal: A Knowledge-Driven Benchmark for Comprehensive Multimodal Hallucination Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03782)
*Ruihan Li,Jiyang Tan,Kailin Jiang,Huining Li,Hengyang Lu,Yu Huang,Qian Li,Yuntao Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KnowHal is a benchmark to systematically evaluate multimodal LLM hallucinations, especially knowledge-related ones, across four unified dimensions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal hallucination benchmarks mostly focus on perceptual aspects (entity, attribute, relation) and treat knowledge hallucinations separately, lacking a unified framework to compare different hallucination types and distinguish perception errors from knowledge errors and false-premise acceptance.

Method: The authors build KnowHal, a benchmark with 1,800 human-verified samples over 10 domains and 50 categories. For each image and entity, they construct paired positive (true) and negative (false-premise) questions spanning four hallucination dimensions: entity, attribute, relation, and knowledge. The dataset is created via a semi-automated pipeline combining LLM-assisted question generation, CLIP-based filtering to align images and text, and human verification. They then evaluate 14 representative multimodal LLMs and analyze performance patterns, particularly on knowledge and negative questions.

Result: Across the 14 evaluated MLLMs, the knowledge dimension is consistently the hardest, with the lowest performance compared to entity, attribute, and relation hallucinations. Models also show large performance drops on negative questions, indicating poor robustness to false premises and a tendency to accept them.

Conclusion: KnowHal provides a unified, four-dimensional benchmark and paired-question design that fills an important gap in hallucination evaluation for MLLMs. It shows that knowledge hallucination and handling of false premises are major unresolved challenges, and offers a more comprehensive testbed for future model and mitigation method development.

Abstract: Hallucination remains a critical challenge for developing trustworthy Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). While existing benchmarks mainly focus on entity, attribute, and relation hallucinations, knowledge-related failures are often investigated separately, lacking a unified evaluation framework across different hallucination dimensions. To overcome this, we propose \textbf{KnowHal}, a benchmark that explicitly incorporates knowledge hallucination into multimodal hallucination evaluation spanning four dimensions: entity, attribute, relation, and knowledge. KnowHal constructs paired positive and negative questions over shared images and entities, enabling controlled comparisons among perceptual errors, knowledge-related errors, and false-premise acceptance. The benchmark contains 1,800 samples across 10 domains and 50 categories, constructed through a semi-automated pipeline combining LLM assistance, CLIP-based filtering, and human verification. We evaluate 14 representative MLLMs on KnowHal and conduct extensive analyses. Results show that the knowledge dimension consistently presents the greatest challenge for nearly all evaluated models, while most models exhibit substantial performance degradation on negative questions, revealing limited robustness to false premises. By unifying four hallucination dimensions with paired question design, KnowHal addresses an important gap in existing evaluation frameworks and enables a more comprehensive assessment of hallucinations in MLLMs.

</details>


### [190] [Does Forgetting Transfer Across Modalities? A Real-World Benchmark for Cross-Modal Knowledge Unlearning Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03791)
*Chunlin Liu,Junnian Chen,Haitong Jiang,Jianyu Zhao,Yingsen Pang,Jingchen Li,Jiabiao He,Youming Lu,Jinhe Bi,Yuntao Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents UNLINK-VL, a benchmark to study how well vision-language models can forget real-world knowledge across text and images, showing that text-only unlearning fails to transfer effectively to visual and cross-modal settings while multimodal unlearning does.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models can memorize sensitive or copyrighted real-world knowledge that should be removed for safety and compliance. Existing unlearning research mostly considers a single modality (e.g., text only) and often assumes access to the original training data. There is little understanding of how forgetting in one modality transfers to another (e.g., from text to images) in realistic conditions where original corpora are unavailable. This work aims to systematically measure and understand cross-modal knowledge unlearning in VLMs under these constraints.

Method: The authors build UNLINK-VL, a post-hoc unlearning benchmark using visually identifiable real-world entities as forget targets. For each target, they collect associated images and structured facts (one-hop and multi-hop) from Wikidata. They organize these into four subsets that test: (1) direct forgetting of target knowledge, (2) propagation of forgetting through relational knowledge, (3) preservation of related non-target knowledge, and (4) robustness to semantically equivalent query rephrasings. They then train VLMs with both text-only and multimodal unlearning methods and evaluate forgetting and utility across textual, visual, and cross-modal tasks on this benchmark.

Result: Experiments show a strong asymmetry in cross-modal transfer: multimodal unlearning (using both text and images during unlearning) successfully reduces the targeted knowledge when evaluated with textual prompts, while text-only unlearning fails to adequately remove the same knowledge when evaluated with visual or cross-modal queries. Despite these differences in forgetting behavior, the models’ general capabilities remain mostly intact under the tested unlearning methods.

Conclusion: Unlearning evaluated only within one modality—especially text-only evaluation—can significantly overestimate how much knowledge has actually been removed from vision-language models. Effective and trustworthy unlearning in VLMs requires both multimodal unlearning procedures and cross-modal evaluation protocols, as knowledge can persist in other modalities even when it appears forgotten in text.

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs), like Large Language Models (LLMs), may memorize sensitive, copyrighted, or harmful knowledge from their pretraining corpora. Removing such knowledge is essential for building trustworthy AI systems. However, existing studies primarily focus on forgetting within individual modalities. Although recent work has begun to explore cross-modal consistency in unlearning, the cross-modal transfer of real-world knowledge unlearning remains insufficiently studied. To address this gap, we introduce UNLINK-VL, a real-world benchmark for cross-modal knowledge unlearning in VLMs. Under a post-hoc unlearning setting in which the original forget and retain corpora are unavailable, UNLINK-VL selects visually identifiable real-world entities as unlearning targets and associates them with corresponding images and one-hop and multi-hop facts derived from Wikidata. The benchmark comprises four complementary subsets that evaluate direct forgetting of target knowledge, the propagation of forgetting through relational knowledge, the preservation of related non-target knowledge, and robustness to semantically equivalent queries. We train models under text-only and multimodal unlearning settings and evaluate forgetting effectiveness and retained utility across textual, visual, and cross-modal scenarios. Extensive experiments reveal a pronounced asymmetry in cross-modal transfer: multimodal unlearning remains effective under textual evaluation, whereas text-only unlearning transfers poorly to visual and cross-modal scenarios. Meanwhile, the evaluated methods largely preserve the models' general capabilities. These findings demonstrate that relying solely on intra-modal evaluation, particularly text-only evaluation, may substantially overestimate the effectiveness of knowledge unlearning in VLMs, underscoring the need for cross-modal unlearning and evaluation.

</details>


### [191] [LatentGuard: Efficient and Inspectable Latent Reasoning for LLM Safeguards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03838)
*Zhinan Liu,Jie Li,Mingyu Kang,Jiayi Ji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LatentGuard is an efficient, inspectable safeguard framework that replaces explicit textual reasoning with compact latent reasoning states while retaining auditability via an auxiliary decoder.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing reasoning-based guard models provide strong safety moderation by generating explicit rationales, but this makes them expensive to deploy due to long generations. Latent-reasoning approaches are more efficient, yet are underexplored for safety tasks and typically lack a way to inspect their internal reasoning, which is crucial for trustworthy deployment. The paper aims to achieve both efficiency and inspectability for LLM safety guards.

Method: The authors introduce LatentGuard, which employs a staged curriculum to train a guard model to progressively compress task-aligned textual rationales into low-dimensional latent states. The model is trained so that safety decisions (verdicts) can be predicted directly from these continuous representations instead of explicit text. To maintain inspectability, they add a separate auxiliary decoder that, when needed, can reconstruct compact textual audit artifacts from the latent states, but this decoder is not used on the main inference path, keeping cost low.

Result: On benchmark safety moderation tasks, LatentGuard-8B outperforms the prior reasoning-based guard model GuardReasoner-8B, improving mean weighted F1 from 83.95 to 84.91. At the same time, it dramatically reduces reasoning cost along the critical path from an average of 268.56 generated rationale tokens to just 1.60 latent reasoning tokens. The auxiliary audit decoder can generate useful audit traces, with an audit utility score of 85.75, indicating that inspectors can still understand and evaluate the model’s reasoning when required.

Conclusion: LatentGuard demonstrates that continuous latent reasoning can be effectively applied to LLM safeguard models, achieving better safety performance while significantly lowering inference cost. By decoupling latent reasoning from on-demand textual audits, it preserves inspectability without burdening normal operation. This offers a practical and scalable route for deploying powerful, transparent safety guards for LLMs.

Abstract: Reasoning-based guard models improve LLM safeguards, but decoding explicit rationales for every interaction makes them costly to deploy. Although latent-reasoning methods reduce token generation by moving reasoning into continuous states, they remain underexplored for safety moderation and lack an inspection interface for deployment. In this paper, we propose LatentGuard, an efficient and inspectable safeguard framework that brings continuous latent reasoning to guard models. LatentGuard uses a staged curriculum to progressively compress task-aligned textual rationales into compact latent states, enabling safety verdicts to be predicted directly from continuous representations. To preserve inspectability, an isolated auxiliary decoder generates compact audit artifacts on demand, keeping rationale generation off the standard inference path. Experiments show that LatentGuard-8B improves mean weighted F1 from 83.95 to 84.91 over GuardReasoner-8B, while reducing critical-path reasoning cost from 268.56 generated rationale tokens to 1.60 latent reasoning tokens. Its audit decoder achieves an audit utility score of 85.75, demonstrating an efficient and inspectable path toward deployable LLM safeguards.

</details>


### [192] [Oilbird: Training-Free Speculative Decoding with Keys the Verifier Already Computes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03839)
*Tao Jin,Phuong Minh Nguyen,Zhenzhu Yan,Teeradaj Racharak,Naoya Inoue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Oilbird, a training-free speculative decoding method that adds a semantic draft source to existing lexical drafters, significantly increasing accepted draft length and decoding speed, especially on tool-calling benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing training-free speculative decoding methods rely on exact suffix matching against a context pool, which fails frequently on tool-calling and similar traffic. Many correct drafts are present in the pool but cannot be retrieved due to addressing (exact-match) limitations, not lack of coverage, causing inefficiencies and lower speedups.

Method: Diagnose where and why exact-match speculative drafters fail across ten benchmarks, showing that many missed correct continuations exist but are unreachable. Introduce a second, semantic draft source built from the same context pool but re-keyed by the verifier model's hidden states at each committed token. Integrate this semantic source into the tree of an existing lexical drafter via a merge procedure so it remains training-free and reuses prior computation.

Result: On a dense tool-calling benchmark, about half of the drafts missed by the best exact-match drafter are actually present but unretrievable via exact matching. Adding the semantic draft source to three existing training-free drafters, with the same pool size and computational budget, increases accepted draft length by 24–29%. The resulting system, Oilbird, achieves 4.4x autoregressive decoding speed on the API-Bank benchmark, compared to 3.9x for the strongest training-free baseline under the same harness and 2.0x for EAGLE-3.

Conclusion: The bottleneck in training-free speculative decoding on challenging workloads like tool-calling is primarily an addressing problem, not a coverage problem. By using verifier hidden states to semantically address candidates in an existing context pool and merging this semantic drafter into standard lexical drafters, Oilbird significantly improves draft acceptance and decoding speed without extra training, setting a new state of the art among training-free methods on benchmarks such as API-Bank.

Abstract: Training-free speculative decoding drafts by matching an exact suffix of the context against a pool of earlier context. That lookup misses correct drafts already in the pool, most visibly on tool-calling traffic, where a request repeats almost everything but the few values minted for it, and where one rejected token discards the correct continuation behind it. We diagnose the failure position by position across ten benchmarks and find it to be a problem of addressing rather than of coverage: on our densest tool-calling benchmark, about half of what the strongest exact-match drafter misses is present in the pool yet unreachable by exact matching. We therefore propose a second, semantic draft source: the same pool, re-keyed by the hidden state the verifier has already computed at each committed token, together with a merge that lets it ride inside an existing lexical drafter's tree. In three published drafters, at matched pool and budget, it lifts accepted length by 24-29%. Oilbird reaches 4.4x autoregressive decoding speed on API-Bank, against 3.9x for the strongest training-free baseline in our harness and 2.0x for EAGLE-3.

</details>


### [193] [MAFIA: Query-Only Memory Attacks via Probing and Factual Injection against Audited LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03844)
*Jiaming Chen,Yisen Gao,Yanping Li,Zifan Liu,Yumeng Zhang,Jun Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces MAFIA, a query-only framework that can reliably poison memory-augmented LLM agents even when they have large benign memories and active input auditing, achieving high attack success with low detection rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory-augmented LLM agents need long-term memory for complex reasoning and acting, but these memory modules are a persistent attack surface: once poisoned, harmful information can repeatedly influence the agent. Existing memory-poisoning attacks assume simpler settings and break down when memories are large and benign entries dominate or when systems deploy strong semantic audits on new memory entries. The authors are motivated to study a stronger, more realistic threat model and to design attacks that remain effective under high retrieval competition and strict auditing, thereby exposing practical security risks of current agentic memory designs.

Method: They propose MAFIA, a query-only Memory Attack framework designed for extended, realistic threat settings. MAFIA has two main components: (1) a placement strategy that makes malicious memories retrieval-competitive by probing the victim’s memory behavior, allocating an injection budget across many entries, and scheduling insertions for maximum influence; and (2) a payload design using compact factual cloaks—malicious content wrapped or encoded in benign-seeming, factually correct statements—to evade semantic audits while preserving harmful downstream effects. The attacker interacts only via normal queries/inputs, without direct access to memory internals, and iteratively refines where and how to inject records based on retrieval behavior and audit feedback.

Result: Through extensive experiments on various memory-augmented LLM agents, the authors show that MAFIA can reach up to 90.7% attack success rate despite the presence of large benign memory pools and active input auditing. At the same time, MAFIA greatly reduces the chance of being filtered by audits, driving detection rates down from as high as 83.3% in baseline attacks to at most 7.4%. These results are consistent across different agentic memory systems, suggesting that the method is robust and widely applicable.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that current memory-augmented LLM agents are critically vulnerable to sophisticated, query-only memory poisoning attacks even under realistic defenses like large benign memories and semantic audits. MAFIA demonstrates that an attacker can strategically place and cloak malicious records to dominate retrieval and evade detection, achieving high control over agent behavior.
