The authors argue that these findings call for rethinking the design of agent memory, retrieval, and auditing mechanisms, and they release code to facilitate further research on defenses and secure memory architectures.

Abstract: Memory-augmented LLM agents rely on rich context for long-horizon reasoning and acting, yet their memory modules expose a persistent attack surface for malicious records, making the study of memory poisoning threats imperative. However, existing query-only attacks often fail to remain effective in two realistic and prevalent settings: large-scale benign memory pools and active input auditing. Consequently, current approaches fall short when facing the dual challenges of high retrieval competitiveness and rigorous semantic checks. To overcome these limitations, we propose MAFIA, a query-only Memory Attack framework via probing and Factual Injection against Audit, tailored to this extended threat model. Specifically, MAFIA introduces: (1) a placement strategy that ensures retrieval-competitive injection via memory probing, budget allocation, and scheduling; and (2) a payload design that bypasses audits using compact factual cloaks, preserving malicious effects while maintaining high semantic similarity. Extensive evaluations reveal that MAFIA achieves up to a 90.7% attack success rate while suppressing audit detection from a peak of 83.3% to at most 7.4%, exposing critical vulnerabilities across agentic memory systems. Code will be made publicly available at https://github.com/JiamingChen1234/MAFIA.

</details>


### [194] [ADMITBench: A Safety-Governed Reference Framework for Evaluating the Admissibility of Industrial LLM Advisories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03866)
*Yash Misra,Javal Vyas,Siddharth Gutta,Mehmet Mercangöz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ADMITBench is a benchmark framework to evaluate industrial LLM recommendations at the level of concrete actions, under explicit safety and plant-specific constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial use of large language models to advise on operational actions in plants requires a way to systematically evaluate whether model-generated recommendations are safe, authorized, and evidence-based. Existing evaluations often focus on generic correctness or helpfulness rather than plant-specific operational constraints. The paper aims to provide a structured, repeatable, and safety-aware way to benchmark such advisories before real-world deployment.

Method: The authors design ADMITBench as a reference framework that encodes a versioned, safety-governed evaluation contract. For each LLM recommendation, the framework checks three core aspects: (1) whether the proposed action is supported by the available evidence; (2) whether it is permitted under the explicitly stated authority and procedure; and (3) whether it passes plant-specific consequence checks defined in a selected evaluation profile. These constraints are encoded via a versioned plant profile and non-compensatory eligibility rules. They release version 0.1.0 as a public implementation meant for technical and research evaluations.

Result: The initial release (0.1.0) provides a working public reference implementation of ADMITBench that can be used to systematically evaluate LLM-generated industrial advisories against safety-governed criteria. It operationalizes the concepts of evidence support, procedural authority, and plant-specific consequence checks in a concrete benchmark setup, showing that such structured evaluation is feasible.

Conclusion: ADMITBench offers a structured, safety-governed framework for evaluating industrial LLM advisories at the action level, focusing on explicit eligibility checks tied to plant-specific profiles rather than generic correctness. While it is not a safety certification or authorization for real-world action execution, it serves as a reference tool for technical and research evaluation, enabling more disciplined assessment of LLM use in industrial settings.

Abstract: This white paper presents ADMITBench, a reference framework for evaluating industrial LLM advisories at the level of the proposed action. The framework implements a versioned, safety-governed evaluation contract that checks whether a recommendation is supported by the available evidence, permitted under the stated authority and procedure, and acceptable under the plant-specific consequence checks encoded in the selected evaluation profile. In this report, \emph{safety-governed} means that eligibility is determined through explicit, non-compensatory checks derived from a versioned plant profile; it does not mean that the evaluator, model, or plant has been safety-certified. Release 0.1.0 is a public reference implementation for technical and research evaluation, not an authorisation for physical execution.

</details>


### [195] [Intertemporal Preference Steering in Qwen3 via Contrastive Activation Addition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03892)
*Michal Mráz,Justin Shenk*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that a large language model’s time preferences (short-term vs long-term) can be measured as linear directions in its activations and then actively steered to change its preferences and planning behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI systems increasingly give advice or make recommendations that involve trade‑offs over time, such as short‑term costs versus long‑term benefits. Understanding and controlling a model’s implicit time preferences (e.g., favoring immediate rewards vs delayed rewards) is important for building safer, more reliable assistants, especially for long‑horizon planning. The authors want to know whether such intertemporal preferences are encoded in a structured way inside the model and whether we can systematically modify them without full retraining.

Method: They analyze the 32B-parameter Qwen3 model’s internal representations by: (1) collecting teacher-forced answers to temporal-choice questions that pit short-term against long-term options; (2) training contrastive linear probes on the model’s residual-stream activations to identify a direction that separates short-term from long-term choices; and (3) using contrastive activation addition (steering) along this learned direction at inference time to shift the model’s behavior. They then evaluate this steering on three tests: a held-out binary temporal-choice task similar to the training data, an out-of-distribution monetary intertemporal-choice task that manipulates reward size and delay, and a TravelPlanner benchmark that measures planning-related capabilities.

Result: They successfully find a low-dimensional direction in the residual stream that corresponds to temporal horizon (short-term vs long-term). By adding or subtracting activations along this direction, they can reliably change the model’s answers: on the monetary intertemporal-choice task, steering shifts the indifference point between smaller-sooner and larger-later rewards substantially in both the short-term‑seeking and long-term‑seeking directions. On the TravelPlanner capability benchmark, moderate steering toward long-term preferences yields improved performance on planning metrics, suggesting that the steering not only changes preferences but can enhance long-horizon task performance.

Conclusion: Intertemporal preferences in a large language model are linearly measurable and steerable: a simple contrastive linear probe can uncover a temporal-horizon direction in activation space, and activation-addition along that direction can produce large, bidirectional shifts in apparent time preferences and improve some planning capabilities. This has implications for the design and safety of AI systems that must reason about delayed costs and benefits or perform long-horizon planning, since their temporal preferences can potentially be tuned post hoc rather than being fixed by pretraining.

Abstract: We study linear representations of temporal horizon in the large language model Qwen3-32B and use them to change the model's time-related preferences, recommendations, and capabilities. We train contrastive linear probes on teacher-forced temporal-choice answers to find a short-term versus long-term direction in the model's residual stream, and evaluate contrastive activation-addition steering on a held-out binary temporal-choice task, an out-of-distribution monetary intertemporal-choice task, and a TravelPlanner capability benchmark. The central result is that temporal-horizon directions can be identified with simple contrastive linear probes and then used for steering to induce large, bidirectional preference changes. On an out-of-distribution monetary choice task that varies reward size and delay, steering strongly shifts the model's indifference threshold between smaller-sooner and larger-later rewards in both directions. We further show improvements on a planning-related capability metric under moderate temporal steering. These results suggest that model intertemporal preferences are measurable and steerable, which is relevant for AI systems that give advice involving delayed costs and benefits, and for safety questions about long-horizon planning.

</details>


### [196] [When Efficiency Becomes Fragility: Exploiting Dynamic Routing Vulnerabilities in Adaptive UAV Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03902)
*Shaofeng Liang,Runwei Guan,Wenshuo Chen,Jiemin Wu,Bowen Tian,Haozhe Jia,Kaishen Yuan,Songning Lai,Daizong Liu,Yutao Yue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies a fundamental instability in adaptive Transformer-based UAV trackers caused by discontinuous gating decisions and exploits it with a new attack framework, Adversarial Path-Inversion (API), which imperceptibly alters computational paths to severely degrade tracking while remaining efficient.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: UAV platforms have strict resource constraints, pushing tracking models toward adaptive, input-dependent computation to balance accuracy and efficiency. While these Adaptive Transformer Trackers are attractive, their robustness and security properties are poorly understood. The authors aim to uncover whether the dynamic routing/gating used for adaptive computation introduces unique vulnerabilities beyond standard adversarial attacks, and to provide a formal characterization and practical exploitation of any such weaknesses.

Method: The authors mathematically analyze the gating mechanism in adaptive tracking architectures, focusing on layer-skipping decisions and showing that the local Lipschitz constant at decision boundaries is unbounded, creating a Lipschitz singularity. They interpret this as an unstable, discrete topological path space of possible computational routes. Building on this, they design the Adversarial Path-Inversion (API) framework, which crafts small, imperceptible input perturbations that precisely steer the gating modules to choose drastically different computational paths (“inverted” paths) from the original. This is done to maximize the discrepancy between original and manipulated paths, thereby collapsing the model’s representation quality while preserving low perturbation norms and high efficiency.

Result: Theoretical analysis proves that the gating decisions in adaptive trackers are highly sensitive to small perturbations near decision boundaries, leading to unstable inference topologies. Empirically, on several state-of-the-art adaptive Transformer-based UAV tracking models, API generates adversarial examples that cause substantial tracking degradation while keeping perturbations imperceptible. Compared to baseline attacks, API delivers stronger performance degradation (more effective attack), improved stealthiness (lower visible distortion or smaller perturbation norms), and faster inference, indicating that exploiting path-space vulnerabilities is both practical and efficient.

Conclusion: Adaptive Transformer Trackers possess an inherent structural vulnerability due to Lipschitz singularities at their gating decision boundaries, exposing a novel attack surface in the space of computational paths. The proposed API framework successfully exploits this weakness by inverting computational paths with tiny input changes, dramatically undermining tracking performance while maintaining efficiency. This work highlights that dynamic routing, while beneficial for resource-constrained tracking, introduces serious security risks and must be carefully reconsidered in the design of future robust adaptive tracking architectures.

Abstract: Resource constraints on UAV platforms have driven a paradigm shift in aerial tracking, from pursuing performance toward balancing accuracy with efficiency. Adaptive Transformer Trackers, which leverage an input-dependent dynamic routing architecture, have emerged as a representative solution to this challenge. However, we reveal that behind this computation-on-demand flexibility hides a critical structural flaw: the Lipschitz singularity of computational path decisions, which has an unbounded local Lipschitz constant at discrete layer-skipping decision boundaries. This mathematical discontinuity renders adaptive tracking networks inherently unstable: tiny input perturbations can be amplified at the gating modules, causing dramatic changes in the inference topology. We formally characterize this singularity in the context of adaptive tracking architectures and, for the first time, identify it as a directly exploitable new attack surface. This insight reveals a previously overlooked and highly vulnerable topological path space attack surface. Based on this, we propose the Adversarial Path-Inversion (API) framework. API generates imperceptible perturbations to precisely manipulate the gating decisions, forcing the inference onto altered computational paths. The severe inconsistency between the original and the inverted paths dismantles the representation capability of the model. Extensive experiments on state-of-the-art adaptive trackers demonstrate that API achieves superior perturbation stealthiness, more effective attack, and faster inference speeds. This work opens a new dimension for the security analysis of dynamic tracking networks and provides a theoretical warning for constructing robust adaptive tracking architectures in the future.

</details>


### [197] [Socially Grounded Agentic AI: Coordinating Plural Perspectives through Social Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03910)
*Matt Ratto,Abhishek Moturu,Daniel Silver*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that AI alignment must handle multiple legitimate social perspectives and proposes using social theory to design pluralistic, role-sensitive AI systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI alignment work implicitly assumes a single, unified set of values, which fails in diverse social contexts where many legitimate perspectives coexist and often conflict. Current pluralistic alignment approaches tend to treat plurality as just output diversity and lack a grounded account of how values are actually organized, contested, and coordinated in real societies. The authors want to fill this gap by importing tools from social theory to better model and manage value pluralism in AI systems.

Method: Conceptual and theoretical analysis drawing on established traditions in sociology. The authors reinterpret AI alignment challenges through concepts such as social roles, interaction, power, and expertise. They then translate these sociological insights into design principles for AI systems, such as role-based representations of perspectives, structured coordination mechanisms among them, and context-sensitive evaluation schemes. For agentic systems, they extend this to internal processes like role activation, deliberation, aggregation, and feedback loops.

Result: A reframing of pluralistic alignment as a problem of socially grounded coordination rather than mere output diversification. The paper yields a design space and set of high-level design implications: use role-based representations of perspectives; coordinate perspectives via structured mechanisms; evaluate system behavior in a context-sensitive way; and align not only final outputs but also the internal processes (deliberation traces, aggregation rules, feedback) that generate them. It also identifies specific directions for empirical implementation and evaluation in real-world contexts.

Conclusion: Pluralistic alignment requires modeling how perspectives are socially structured and coordinated, not only generating varied outputs. Social theory offers key concepts to do this, emphasizing roles, interaction, and fields of power and expertise. AI systems should therefore be designed to represent and coordinate multiple perspectives in structured, accountable, and context-sensitive ways, with alignment applied to internal agentic processes as well as final outputs. This socially grounded framing opens a research agenda for building and testing such systems in practice.

Abstract: As AI systems are deployed across increasingly diverse social contexts, alignment can no longer be framed as the optimization of a single, unified set of values. Instead, systems must be able to recognize, represent, and respond to multiple legitimate perspectives. This has led to growing interest in pluralistic alignment, which seeks to move beyond one-size-fits-all models of appropriate behaviour. However, current approaches often lack a clear account of how values are socially organized, contested, and coordinated in practice. In this paper, we argue that social theory provides essential conceptual and design resources for addressing these challenges. Drawing on established traditions in sociology, we show how perspectives can be understood as structured by roles, shaped through interaction, and distributed across fields of power and expertise. We translate these insights into concrete implications for AI system design, including role-based representations, structured coordination among perspectives, and context-sensitive evaluation. For agentic systems, this requires aligning not only final outputs, but also the role activations, deliberative traces, aggregation rules, and feedback loops through which those outputs are produced. Our contribution is to reposition pluralistic alignment as a problem of socially grounded coordination rather than output diversification. We outline a design space for systems that engage multiple perspectives in structured and accountable ways, and we identify directions for future work to implement and empirically evaluate these approaches in real-world settings.

</details>


### [198] [Implementing Causal Perception: Competing SCMs and Situated Fairness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03917)
*Jose M. Álvarez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper implements and tests the theoretical framework of causal perception, showing how disagreements between experts’ causal models translate into different fairness and accuracy outcomes in decision-making.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although the causal perception framework formally characterizes how different agents’ causal models induce different probabilistic views and fairness judgments, it has so far lacked a concrete, operational implementation that can be used in practice or empirically studied.

Method: The authors formalize and implement two types of causal perception—structural (different causal graphs) and parametric (same graph, different parameters)—for agents modeled with Structural Causal Models. They design algorithms to compute interventional and counterfactual distributions for each agent’s SCM and introduce distance measures to quantify disagreement between these distributions. They then apply the framework to the German Credit dataset in a multi-expert decision scenario, systematically varying distance metrics and thresholds that determine when perceptions are considered meaningfully different.

Result: The study finds that the degree and even the presence of disagreement between agents’ causal perceptions are highly sensitive to the chosen distance metric and threshold. These disagreements lead to different accuracy and fairness evaluations across experts using different SCMs on the same data and interventions. The empirical analysis shows that the same situation can be judged differently in terms of fairness depending on each agent’s causal model and the formal notion of perceptual distance used.

Conclusion: Implementing causal perception in practice reveals that fairness and bias assessments are not objective properties of the data alone but are situated in, and contingent on, each agent’s SCM. Competing causal worldviews materially affect accuracy, fairness metrics, and threshold-based decisions, implying that fairness analysis must explicitly account for disagreements in causal modeling choices and the metrics used to compare induced distributions.

Abstract: Causal perception occurs when agents with competing Structural Causal Models (SCMs) of the same system infer different probability distributions, including the hypothetical distributions implied by each agent's SCM under the same set of interventions. It shapes how agents reason about the system and how they perceive its fairness. Causal perception is a promising probabilistic framework, but it has remained purely theoretical. This work provides the first implementation of the causal perception framework of Álvarez and Ruggieri (2025). We operationalize structural (agents disagree on the causal graph) and parametrical (agents agree on the causal graph but disagree on its weights) causal perception. We design algorithms for computing interventional and counterfactual distributions and propose suitable distance measures to quantify the disagreement. Using the German Credit dataset, we illustrate how causal perception affects accuracy and fairness in a multi-expert decision setting. We show that the perception verdict is sensitive to the choice of distance metric and threshold. We also show that causal perception changes fairness assessments and threshold-based decisions. Bias proves situated with respect to the agent's SCM, demonstrating that competing worldviews in fairness problems cannot be ignored.

</details>


### [199] [The Transformer Revolution, Part 1: Dynamic Processing through Output- Weight Interconnections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03921)
*Marco Giunti,Fabrizia Giulia Garavaglia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper reconceptualizes Transformers as systems that dynamically construct prompt-dependent transformations during inference, rather than just replaying learned statistics, and links this mechanism (SIDPP) to both AI interpretability and possible neural implementations in the human brain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is an ongoing debate about whether large language models are merely "stochastic parrots" that regurgitate statistical patterns from training data or whether they perform richer, structured computations. Existing accounts often underplay the role of prompt-specific computation and struggle to explain strong prompt sensitivity and emergent behaviors. The authors aim to provide a more mechanistic, conceptually rich interpretation of Transformer inference that clarifies how prompts shape internal processing and what this implies for understanding, control, and biological plausibility.

Method: The paper offers a conceptual and mechanistic reinterpretation of the Transformer architecture. It analyzes the network in terms of two kinds of concepts: token vectors (concepts to be transformed) and parameterized transformations implemented by matrices and vectors (transforming concepts). It distinguishes static transformations (fixed by training) from dynamic transformations (parameters constructed from the input sequence at inference time). The authors introduce the notion of SIDPP (Sequence-level Interactive Dynamic Parallel Processing) to formalize how output-weight and output-input interconnections allow the model to build prompt-dependent transformations and apply them in parallel across the sequence.

Result: The analysis shows that, due to output-weight interconnections in the Transformer, parts of the network can generate new, prompt-dependent transformation parameters during inference. This dynamic processing becomes more important as prompt length increases and can match or exceed the contribution of static, trained weights—termed strong prompt sensitivity. The Transformer is thus characterized as a system that transforms concepts using other concepts, with dynamic transformations playing a central computational role. The framework clarifies how attention and related mechanisms instantiate SIDPP within standard Transformer blocks.

Conclusion: Transformers are better understood as SIDPP systems that dynamically construct and apply prompt-dependent transformations, rather than as mere statistical parrots. This view has implications for interpretability (distinguishing static from dynamic processing), predictability and control (understanding how prompts shape internal transformations), and the design of smaller, more efficient models that exploit dynamic computation. Because cortical circuits plausibly possess the mechanisms needed for SIDPP, the authors conjecture that human language processing may implement a similar functional architecture, making Transformer-like SIDPP a candidate model for aspects of cortical computation.

Abstract: This paper offers a new interpretation of the Transformer during inference. Against the "stochastic parrot" view that large language models merely reproduce statistical regularities learned in training, we argue that Transformers construct and apply prompt-dependent transformations whose parameters are generated during inference. We call this form of computation SIDPP: Sequence-level Interactive Dynamic Parallel Processing. The Transformer is interpreted as a system that transforms concepts by means of concepts. Token vectors are the concepts to be transformed; parameterized transformations defined by matrices and vectors are the transforming concepts. These may be static, when fixed through training, or dynamic, when generated from the input sequence. Mechanically, they correspond to groups of simple neural networks. The Transformer's architectural novelty lies in output-weight interconnections, through which the outputs of some networks determine the weights of others, alongside ordinary output-input interconnections. By means of these interconnections, the system constructs transformations from the prompt and uses them to modify token representations. The contribution of dynamic processing grows with prompt length and may equal or exceed that of static processing, a phenomenon we call strong prompt sensitivity. This account bears on interpretability, predictability, control, and the design of smaller, more sustainable systems. Finally, since the human neural system possesses the mechanisms required to implement SIDPP, we argue that a form of SIDPP may, in principle, be neurally realized in the cerebral cortex. We therefore conjecture that human language processing may itself be a form of SIDPP produced by a functional architecture relevantly similar to that of the Transformer.

</details>


### [200] [TACT: Taxonomy-Aligned Post-Training for Pedagogically Adaptive English Tutoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03952)
*Dongjie Yang,Siyan Lin,Leixian Shen,Rui Sheng,Huamin Qu,Zixin Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes TACT, a human-grounded framework, corpus, benchmark, and post-trained LLM (TACTutor) to make ESL conversational tutors pedagogically adaptive using tutoring taxonomies and RL-style post-training, achieving substantial gains over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based ESL conversational tutors mostly optimize for fluent language generation or reference imitation, not for pedagogically adaptive behavior grounded in human tutoring research. While education literature provides principles for adaptive support, these are scattered, task-specific, and not systematically integrated into the training and evaluation of LLM tutors. There is a need for a principled, human-grounded framework, data, and metrics that explicitly connect learner behavior, tutor strategies, and model optimization so that LLM tutors can better scaffold ESL learners in realistic dialogues.

Method: 1) Derive two taxonomies from human-tutoring literature: (a) a 13-category Tutor-Strategy Taxonomy describing distinct pedagogical response strategies; (b) a Student-Move Taxonomy characterizing learner behavior by move type and status. 2) Annotate 260 authentic teacher-student ESL conversations using these taxonomies to build TACTCorpus, yielding 32,379 high-quality annotations and additional quality-controlled augmented training data. 3) Take Qwen3.5-4B as a backbone model and post-train it in two stages: supervised fine-tuning on taxonomy-annotated data, followed by taxonomy-aligned Group Relative Policy Optimization that directly optimizes for scaffolding quality (i.e., appropriate strategy selection and adaptation to student moves) rather than simple imitation. 4) Construct TACTBench, a 78-example, strategy-balanced diagnostic benchmark based on authentic tutoring contexts, to evaluate models on their ability to select effective tutor strategies. 5) Conduct automatic evaluations on TACTBench and standard educational benchmarks, plus a blinded human study with 50 ESL learners comparing TACTutor against proprietary and backbone models under a shared interaction protocol.

Result: On TACTBench, TACTutor improves over its Qwen3.5-4B backbone by 20.30% and outperforms all evaluated proprietary LLM tutors under the same evaluation protocol, specifically in terms of pedagogical strategy selection and scaffolding quality. It preserves the backbone’s performance on other external educational benchmarks, demonstrating no loss of general capability. In a blinded user study with 50 learners, TACTutor receives the highest overall mean rating among all compared tutors, indicating that its taxonomy-aligned training translates into better perceived tutoring quality in real interactions.

Conclusion: Taxonomy-aligned post-training using human-grounded tutoring principles can substantially improve the pedagogical adaptivity of LLM-based ESL conversational tutors without degrading their general educational performance. By providing (1) tutor- and student-move taxonomies, (2) an extensively annotated corpus, (3) a diagnostic benchmark, and (4) an open post-trained model that outperforms strong baselines, the work establishes an open foundation for future research and development of pedagogically aware LLM tutors. The results suggest that optimizing for scaffolding quality—rather than mere reference imitation—is an effective direction for building better language-learning assistants.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to provide conversational practice for English-as-a-second-language (ESL) learners. Effective ESL tutoring, however, requires more than fluent response generation: a tutor must select an appropriate pedagogical action based on learner behavior and dialogue context. Human-tutoring research offers principles for adaptive support, but they are often task-specific and remain insufficiently integrated into LLM-based ESL tutor training and evaluation. We present TACT (Taxonomy-Aligned Conversational Tutor), a human-grounded framework for post-training and evaluating pedagogically adaptive ESL tutors. Drawing on established literature, we develop two complementary taxonomies: the Tutor-Strategy Taxonomy with 13 tutor response strategies and the Student-Move Taxonomy characterizing learner behavior by move type and status. Using these taxonomies, we construct TACTCorpus, which enriches 260 authentic teacher-student conversations with 32,379 annotations and quality-controlled augmented training data. We then post-train Qwen3.5-4B through supervised fine-tuning followed by taxonomy-aligned Group Relative Policy Optimization, producing TACTutor and optimizing it for scaffolding quality rather than reference imitation alone. On TACTBench, a strategy-balanced diagnostic benchmark comprising 78 authentic tutoring contexts, TACTutor improves over its backbone by 20.30% and outperforms all evaluated proprietary baselines under the same protocol, while maintaining backbone performance on established external educational benchmarks; in a blinded study with 50 learners, it also receives the highest overall mean rating among the evaluated tutors. We release the data, benchmark, and model weights, providing an open foundation for developing pedagogically adaptive ESL tutors.

</details>


### [201] [A game theory for foundation models shows new paths to rational cooperation through similarity inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03958)
*Alexander Meulemans,Maciej Wołczyk,Marissa A. Weis,Rajai Nasser,Roberta Rocca,Seijin Kobayashi,Guillaume Lajoie,Angelika Steger,Blake Richards,Marcus Hutter,James Manyika,Rif A. Saurous,João Sacramento,Blaise Agüera y Arcas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that foundation-model-based autonomous agents, when doing optimal planning and modeling themselves as part of the environment, tend to cooperate stably in social dilemmas, contrary to classical game-theoretic expectations of defection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical game theory assumes decoupled agency, where each agent treats its own decisions as independent from others and from the environment. However, modern AI agents based on foundation models predict their own future actions jointly with the environment, violating this assumption. There is a need to understand and formalize the collective behavior and interaction principles of such embedded agents, especially for safety and cooperation in socio-economic systems.

Method: The authors introduce a theoretical construct called the 'embedded Bayesian agent' to model foundation-model-like agents that treat themselves as part of the universe and maintain uncertainty over their own decision algorithms. They analyze stylized social dilemma games and derive a new solution concept, the 'embedded equilibrium', to capture how such agents infer similarity between themselves and others. Using this framework, they explain why optimal planning with embedded agency leads to cooperative outcomes.

Result: They find empirically (or via stylized simulations) that foundation model agents using optimal planning in social dilemmas stably converge to mutual cooperation, contradicting classical Nash predictions of mutual defection. The embedded Bayesian agent framework and the embedded equilibrium concept mathematically formalize how agents’ self-modeling and similarity inference lead to cooperation as the equilibrium behavior.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that classical game theory, built on decoupled agency and Nash equilibrium, is inadequate for describing interactions between modern foundation-model-based agents. Instead, an embedded-agency perspective, in which agents reason about their own algorithms and infer behavioral similarity, naturally yields stable cooperation in social dilemmas. The proposed embedded equilibrium offers a new foundational game-theoretic solution concept for predicting and designing the social behavior of advanced AI agents.

Abstract: As autonomous agents powered by foundation models are increasingly integrated into social and economic systems, understanding the principles governing their collective behavior is essential for ensuring safety and cooperation. Classical game theory, the dominant framework for modeling rational interaction, is built upon the assumption of `decoupled agency,' where agents treat their own decision-making as independent of the environment and other actors. Modern AI agents, however, jointly predict their own future actions alongside external observations. Here, we report a striking finding: when interacting in stylized social dilemmas, foundation model agents engaging in optimal planning consistently converge to stable cooperation, directly contradicting classical game-theoretic predictions of mutual defection. To understand this phenomenon, we introduce the `embedded Bayesian agent,' a theoretical model for foundation model agents. By shifting from decoupled to embedded agency, these agents model themselves as part of the universe they inhabit, maintaining epistemic uncertainty about their own decision-making algorithms. We show that by inferring whether others are behaviorally similar, an embedded agent treats its own deliberation during planning as evidence: a decision to cooperate predicts a similar decision by a similar partner. We formalize this mechanism of similarity inference through the `embedded equilibrium,' a novel solution concept replacing the Nash equilibrium to provide a foundational game theory for the social behavior of modern AI agents.

</details>


### [202] [Interpretable Adaptive Sampling for LLM Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03961)
*Mobina Kashaniyan,Ali Jannesari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes an adaptive test-time scaling method for LLMs using a fuzzy controller to allocate variable sampling budgets per query, improving efficiency while maintaining reasoning performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing test-time scaling methods use fixed compute budgets per query, wasting resources on easy prompts and under-serving hard ones, while also being opaque and hard to interpret. There is a need for an interpretable, per-query adaptive compute allocation mechanism based on prompt difficulty and model confidence.

Method: Introduce a lightweight fuzzy logic controller that takes interpretable signals—such as estimated prompt complexity and model confidence—and outputs a per-query sampling budget. The controller dynamically adjusts the number of samples: fewer for easy/high-confidence prompts, more for hard/low-confidence ones. The approach is evaluated under a fair-alignment protocol with matched decoding settings and controlled answer selection, and compared to best-of-N, compute-aware scaling, and self-certainty baselines on QA and mathematical reasoning tasks.

Result: Across different models and datasets, the adaptive fuzzy control strategy outperforms several standard baselines, and its performance remains close to that of a system using a full, fixed budget while consuming fewer samples on average.

Conclusion: Interpretable adaptive sampling via fuzzy control is an effective and practical way to make test-time scaling for LLM reasoning more compute-efficient and transparent, suggesting it as a promising direction for future inference-time optimization methods.

Abstract: Test-time scaling improves LLM reasoning by generating and aggregating multiple candidate answers, yet many pipelines use fixed per-query budgets that spend the same compute on easy and difficult prompts. These fixed budgets are also difficult to inspect because they do not explain why a given prompt receives a particular number of samples. We propose adaptive} test-time scaling with a lightweight fuzzy controller that maps interpretable signals, including estimated prompt complexity and model confidence, to a per-query sampling budget. The controller assigns fewer samples to easier or more confident prompts and more samples to harder or less certain prompts, making inference-time compute inspectable rather than fixed or opaque. We evaluate under a fair-alignment protocol with matched decoding settings and controlled answer selection, and compare against best-of-$N$, compute-aware scaling, and self-certainty-based baselines on question-answering and mathematical reasoning tasks. Across models and datasets, adaptive fuzzy control improves over several standard baselines and remains close to a selector-matched full-budget control while reducing the average number of samples. These findings suggest that interpretable adaptive sampling is a practical direction for more efficient test-time reasoning in large language models.

</details>


### [203] [Should We Type or Talk to LLM Agents? A Comprehensive Study of Voice and Keyboard Input Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03970)
*Zizhao Hu,Nathan Elijah Segura,Mohammad Rostami,Jesse Thomason*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces HIVE, a framework to systematically perturb human inputs (voice transcriptions and keyboard text) to test LLM robustness, finding that structural distortions of input—especially in voice-like transcriptions—significantly degrade performance, primarily when tokens are destroyed and when answers must be composed rather than selected.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As language models are increasingly used via keyboards and voice interfaces, their inputs carry characteristic errors: typos from keyboards and disfluencies or restructuring from speech transcription systems. These differences may systematically affect model performance, but have not been rigorously quantified or compared. The authors aim to understand how robust LLMs are to realistic channel-specific noise and what aspects of that noise actually hurt model accuracy.

Method: The authors build HIVE (Human Input-Variation Engine), which generates controlled perturbations mimicking (a) voice transcription artifacts (fillers, disfluencies, structural changes) and (b) QWERTY keyboard errors (typos and related distortions). They apply these perturbations to benchmark questions and evaluate multiple instruction-tuned LLMs. By varying perturbation types and intensities, and comparing tasks (constructed answer vs. multiple choice), they disentangle which properties of the perturbations—such as token deletion vs token insertion—most impact accuracy. They also test whether fine-tuning or giving models more "thinking" time mitigates the harm.

Result: Seven main empirical findings emerge: (i) Voice-style transcription perturbations reduce accuracy across all tested instruction-tuned LLMs, with structural changes causing more harm than added filler words. (ii) QWERTY-style keyboard perturbations are less damaging; models tolerate many typos before accuracy degrades. (iii) The primary factor driving performance loss is token destruction—removing or corrupting existing tokens—rather than adding extra tokens. (iv) The discrepancy between voice and keyboard channels appears mainly on tasks requiring generation or reasoning; multiple choice tasks show little gap. (v) The observed harm is not merely due to test-set contamination issues. (vi) Lightweight adaptation or fine-tuning does not eliminate the vulnerability. (vii) Allowing extra "thinking budget" (e.g., longer reasoning) greatly restores performance for keyboard-perturbed text but not for spoken-style inputs, and compressed speech-like inputs fare even worse under such budgets.

Conclusion: LLMs are significantly more brittle to realistic voice-transcription-style perturbations than to keyboard-like typos, particularly on tasks requiring constructed or deduced answers. The main vulnerability stems from the loss or corruption of key tokens, not from extraneous additions. These problems are not easily fixable by small-scale adaptation, and extra reasoning capacity mainly helps with keyboard noise, leaving spoken-style inputs problematic. Designing more robust models and interfaces will require explicitly addressing structural distortions characteristic of speech transcription, especially where compression or aggressive restructuring is involved.

Abstract: Human input reaches language models by typing or speaking, and each channel leaves a distinct signature: orthographic noise for keyboards; for voice, disfluency from conventional transcription and restructuring from AI-backed dictation tools. How do they impact an LLM's performance? In this paper we present HIVE (Human Input-Variation Engine), a suite of voice transcription perturbations and QWERTY keyboard perturbations. We use HIVE to evaluate how robust models are to these perturbations. We present seven findings. (i) Voice transcription perturbations lower accuracy across every instruction-tuned model we test, and it is the structure of the transcription rather than its fillers that carries the cost. (ii) QWERTY keyboard perturbations cost less, and a model absorbs a lot of them before accuracy falls away. (iii) Both trace back to one cause, how many of the question's tokens survive the perturbation: destroying a token is what hurts, while adding new ones alongside it costs little. (iv) The gap between the two channels appears only where the answer must be constructed or deduced; on multiple choice there is none. (v) The harm does not solely come from test-set contamination. (vi) It cannot be trained away with lightweight adaptation. (vii) A thinking budget recovers the keyboard channel almost entirely but leaves the spoken registers untouched, and compressed speech is worse with it.

</details>


### [204] [ReflectRL: Learning from Golden Negative Trajectories via Reflective-to-Direct Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03972)
*Jinhe Bi,Chennan Zhou,Zengjie Jin,Aniri,Shuo Lu,Wenke Huang,Hu Cao,Xun Xiao,Zhihong Zhu,Volker Tresp,Fei Shen,Yunpu Ma,Tat-Seng Chua*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They introduce ReflectRL, a method that leverages failed expert trajectories (Golden Negative Trajectories) in on-policy training to improve LLM reasoning via reflection and policy transition, achieving consistent gains across multiple benchmarks and backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing on-policy training uses successful expert trajectories as supervision, but discards failed ones, losing potentially valuable learning signals. On hard problems, experts often fail, so supervision becomes sparse and ineffective. The authors hypothesize that these failures can still be useful if models are trained to reflect on them rather than imitate them, especially because critiquing a flawed solution may be easier than solving the problem from scratch.

Method: They define Golden Negative Trajectories as failed expert trajectories on hard problems. ReflectRL is a plug-and-play framework that: (1) uses these failed trajectories to elicit Reflective Reasoning, where the model analyzes and critiques the flawed reasoning; and (2) performs a Reflective-to-Direct Policy Transition, transferring the reflection-induced reasoning patterns into the model’s Direct Reasoning policy used at inference. ReflectRL is integrated into standard on-policy RL-style training and can work with multiple base LLMs and training algorithms.

Result: Across 9 reasoning benchmarks, 4 LLM backbones, and 4 different on-policy training methods, ReflectRL consistently yields better reasoning performance than baseline on-policy methods that either ignore or naively penalize failed trajectories, with minimal computational or implementation overhead.

Conclusion: Failed expert trajectories, if reinterpreted as objects for reflection rather than imitation, are a rich supervision source for on-policy training. ReflectRL operationalizes this via reflective reasoning and policy transition, improving reasoning robustness and performance across diverse setups while remaining lightweight and easy to plug into existing pipelines.

Abstract: On-policy training has emerged as a powerful post-training paradigm for improving the reasoning capabilities of large language models, and is often enhanced by golden trajectories from stronger expert models. However, when the expert fails on harder problems, existing trajectory-guided methods lose their main source of supervision, and these failed trajectories are typically discarded as negative samples. We argue that such failures, which we call Golden Negative Trajectories, can still provide valuable reasoning signals when treated not as demonstrations to imitate, but as flawed trajectories to reflect upon. We identify a Reflection Advantage: for hard problems, reflecting on a flawed trajectory can be easier and more effective than solving the problem directly from scratch. Motivated by this, we propose ReflectRL, a lightweight plug-and-play framework that learns from Golden Negative Trajectories during on-policy training. ReflectRL first uses these trajectories to elicit Reflective Reasoning, then applies Reflective-to-Direct Policy Transition to transfer the acquired reasoning behavior back to Direct Reasoning. Experiments across 9 benchmarks, 4 LLM backbones, and 4 on-policy training methods show that ReflectRL consistently improves reasoning performance with minimal overhead.

</details>
